% ===== TEXT =====
% --- p. 1 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\anthsection{} {GENERAL INTRODUCTION}\opage{1}

\begin{intro}

In my work ``Danske Filosofer'' (1909) I have sought to show that in the way in
which philosophizing has been done in Denmark, no less than in the other forms
in which Danish intellectual life has revealed itself, there is expressed a
national peculiarity, which appears partly in the treatment of the currents of
thought coming from abroad, partly in the continuity that Danish life of
thought exhibits despite the frequent interruption by foreign influences,
partly in the problems that above all set thought in motion.

As regards the currents of thought coming from abroad, characteristic
oscillations take place. The founders of Danish thought,
\textsc{Holberg} and J. S. \textsc{Sneedorff}, were most influenced by English
and French literature, a circumstance that has certainly been of great
importance for the whole subsequent development. Then there came
--- in connection with the great movement in German philosophy, at the same
time as a philosophical ebb in England and France --- a whole series of German
influences (the thoughts of Wolff, Kant, Schelling and Hegel). In the century
from Sneedorff's death to the last quarter of the nineteenth century German
influence prevails; only a few (such as Joh. Boye, Treschow and F. G. Howitz)
show a decisive influence of English thought. It was the period of rationalism
and romanticism. In the last quarter of the nineteenth century new Western
points of view and theories (positivism, Darwinism) make themselves felt.

That there is nevertheless, despite the repeated outward influence, an inner
continuity in Danish thought can be seen above all from the direction that
Danish life of thought took through the appearance of \textsc{Treschow} and
\textsc{Steffens} at the beginning of the nineteenth century, a direction that,
despite the great differences which the altered intellectual and especially
scientific conditions brought with them, presents many points of similarity
with the line of thought of Holberg and Sneedorff.

Treschow's thought was characterized by an interest in psychological
observation and by the endeavor to bring the human sciences into as close a
connection with natural science as possible. Of special significance for the
subsequent Danish philosophy was
% --- p. 2 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{2}his strong emphasis on the richness and inexhaustibility of individual
phenomena as against German thought's tendency toward the universal and the
abstract. He lays great weight on the fact that there is more in the individual
than in the general concept, so that the transition from the former to the
latter entails a loss. The treatise in which he develops these thoughts
influenced A. S. Ørsted's ethical and legal-philosophical conception, as well as
Sibbern's emphasis on the sporadic and the individual in all mental development
and Howitz's assertion of the transitions, mocking all absolute distinctions,
between the healthy and the diseased mental life. Sibbern in particular, not
only in his younger years but with strong emphasis also in his old age,
stressed Treschow's significance.

Steffens' activity did not have the clear and sober character of Treschow's. He
came at the beginning of the century as the prophet of romanticism, an awakener
not only of philosophers but also of poets. But in his poetic, often fantastic
philosophy of nature he laid chief weight on the fact that through nature, this
prehistory of spirit, there runs an individualizing tendency, a striving to
develop peculiar forms of existence and of life, which reaches its culmination in
the human world. He found the measure of a natural form's stage of development
in the degree to which it appears with a pronounced individual peculiarity. This
line of thought gives Steffens a special position in the history of romantic
philosophy.

Since \textsc{Sibbern} took up influences from both Treschow and Steffens and
worked them over in his own peculiar way, he came to occupy a central position
in Danish philosophy in the nineteenth century. Through Poul Møller an
influence runs from him to \textsc{Kierkegaard}, in whom the individualistic
philosophy of personality reaches its culmination through passionate
intensification. The whole way in which Kierkegaard conceives philosophy and
its significance, in opposition to German speculation, coincides in essential
points with Sibbern's. However brilliant and personal Kierkegaard's life of
thought may be, --- however much it seems to defy every classification under a
heading or in a series, --- it is nevertheless in its growth and its formation
determined in many ways by the soil in which, and the conditions under which,
it came to be. And his influence also makes itself felt not only upon older
and younger contemporaries (R. Nielsen, H. Brøchner), whose thought it partly
bent in new directions, but also upon those who philosophized in Denmark in the
last part of the century.

% --- p. 3 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{3}Hegelian philosophy, with its trust in abstract derivations and in the
possibility of systematic completion, was met with independent and sharp
criticism by Sibbern and Kierkegaard. Its greatest influence it exerted --- through
Heiberg and Martensen --- on aesthetics and theology. R. \textsc{Nielsen} and
H. \textsc{Brøchner}, who received their philosophical initiation through the
study of Hegel, were compelled, partly through Kierkegaard's appearance, partly
through the increasing influence of natural science after the middle of the
century, to stress the opposition between the universal laws of thought on the
one side and the spiritual and material realities on the other side far more
sharply than German speculation allowed. ---

Characteristic of Danish thought from Holberg's time onward has not least been
the choice of problems. It was above all psychological and ethical questions that
interested the thinkers in Denmark. In the treatment of the problem of knowledge
one took, on the whole, a critical attitude toward far-reaching speculations. A
real ``metaphysician'' Denmark has not produced.

When in the last quarter of the nineteenth century the empirical sciences made
themselves felt with such great authority, it was bound to be natural for Danish
life of thought to take up the points of view
that it asserted. Not only the influence of positivism from abroad, but also the
interest in realities and the critical sense that awoke in Denmark with Holberg
and Sneedorff, led those who in the last generation philosophized in Denmark to
take up the problems mentioned. In descriptive and analytic psychology
(Høffding, Kroman) the work of Treschow and Sibbern is continued under new
conditions and on the basis of more manifold experience, and energetic work was
done in the field of experimental psychology (A. Lehmann). Efforts were made on
behalf of mental botanizing and of a feeling for nature (L. Feilberg). In close
connection with psychology, aesthetics (Wilkens) and the philosophy of religion
(Høffding) were worked out, as well as ethics (Høffding, Kroman), and
contributions were made to the epistemological discussion (Kroman, Høffding).
Even though one cannot speak of a distinctive Danish school, and even though
more or less significant oppositions make themselves felt among those who
philosophize, the present representatives of philosophy in Denmark too may
nevertheless be said to be working in the direction that Holberg and Sneedorff
in their time opened up in Denmark.
\end{intro}

% --- p. 4 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\anthsection{I.} {HOLBERG AND THE EIGHTEENTH CENTURY}\opage{4}

\begin{intro}

1. \textsc{Ludvig Holberg} (1684---1754), the true founder of Danish
literature, was not only poet and historian but also philosopher. His endeavors
in all three directions are closely connected, although the philosophical
interest, which has become less known than the poetic and the historical, is
perhaps most closely connected with his personality as a whole. On his travels
in his youth he became acquainted with the currents of thought of the time and
was confronted with problems that occupied him throughout his whole life.
John Locke and Pierre Bayle in particular had an influence on him.
Characteristic of him is that he liked a sharp formulation of the problems,
with emphasis on the reasons for and against, and that he would rather leave a
riddle unsolved than rest content with an imagined solution. He had a keen eye
for the limit of human knowledge, and it was his conviction that as experience
advanced the riddles would rather increase than decrease, because many of our
explanations are due only to limited experience.

Among the questions that occupied Holberg must be mentioned the strife between
freethinkers and the orthodox, the compatibility of evil with belief in an
almighty and all-good God, the possibility and necessity of a purely human
morality. The pieces here selected from his writings concern these questions.
We begin with some statements in his autobiography on studies and sciences in
general. Holberg's autobiography was written in Latin, but was already
translated into Danish in 1745, and it is believed that Holberg himself
looked over the translation, which in any case bears the stamp of his language
and humor.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. ON STUDIES AND SCIENCES. (Levned.)}


Some have imagined that I am a strong hater of the metaphysical study, which
they have judged from the oration that I delivered some years ago in praise of
metaphysics.
% --- p. 5 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{5}For I seemed to have delivered rather a funeral sermon over it than a
speech of praise. But by Metaphysica I understand the manifold terms and subtle
distinctions that one misuses in disputations to entangle one's opponent in.
But if it is taken in another sense, I hold that it is a study that becomes a
philosopher. I often read metaphysical books myself, although I must admit that
I make but poor progress in that science. For there occur so many hidden things
that will always remain hidden. I know well that such things seem to some of our
theologians and philosophers to present no difficulty, for they describe to us
quite boldly both the nature of God and that of spirits. Hence Tertullian
has said: Every Christian artisan can both find and show us God. But to me
Simonides seems to judge more reasonably about this; for when he was commanded
by Hiero, king of Sicily, to describe God to him, he asked first for one day's
respite, then for 2 days' respite, and as he thus kept doubling the days for a
long time, and Hiero wondered and asked him the reason for it, he answered:
Because the longer I take it into consideration, the more difficult a thing it
is for me to answer. It is to be wished that all who study that science would
use the same caution that Newton uses in his Mathesis, namely to refrain from
demonstrations \textit{à priori,} and let go the descriptions that they make of
souls and spirits: I should wish that they would not ask whether, or how,
spirits are, or what the shape of souls was, or what their effect was, or in
what manner they exist, or how they are propagated, how they reason when they
are outside the body, or what their figure is, whether it is such as is painted
for children in \textit{Orbis Pictus,} or whether they are alone, like little
indivisible motes in the sunbeam, or whether they have height, breadth and
length, and so on. I should wish, I say, that they would let all these
questions go, and show only the effects that lie before everyone's eyes.

% --- p. 6 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{6}The effect would be that instead of so countless uncertain things we
should have at least a few certain ones. For if one seeks by one's reason and
understanding to make those things certain that are in themselves uncertain,
one does nothing other than rave out of sheer understanding.

Mathematics I have not applied myself to. But \textit{Physica
Coelestis} I have always taken great pains with, and with great eagerness I
read everything I come across that has been written on those matters. Between
the Principia of Descartes and of Newton I have always stood in doubt, so that
I cannot say which of them I approve most. Yet I cannot get Descartes's
\textit{vortices} or whirls out of my head, since this opinion about these
whirls is so easy and so evident that even children can grasp that they must
necessarily follow from the revolution of the sun, whereas unlearned people must
regard Newton's opinion about the sun's power to draw to itself as a kind of
witchcraft, and cannot possibly grasp how the sun, which casts so many floods
of fire from itself, can at the same time draw planets to itself, since it is
against nature to blow and to suck at once. Therefore, since Descartes's opinion
is both easier to grasp and also more probable, and one cannot find more
probable causes to ascribe to this effect, I hold that one can most safely
stay by that opinion, and, if anything occurs that seems to conflict with it,
as for example the oval course of the comets and other such things, then it may
have its origin in the nature of the comets, which we do not know. More I will
not say of these things, since I am not appointed to be a judge in that matter.
This only I will add, that the irregular course of the comets conflicts just as
strongly with Newton's Principia as with Descartes's. For if the question is
asked how it comes that the comets go straight down, whereas the other planets
roll in a circle around the sun, then the Newtonians have just as hard a time
when they are to answer the question why the comets, when they have come near
the sun, go the same way
% --- p. 7 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{7}back again: for if the sun draws to itself, then the comet, when it came
near the sun, would be drawn straight into it. Newton foresaw well the
objections that would be made to him concerning the \textit{principia
Gravitatis,} and therefore he also left it free to everyone whether they would
give them the name of a pulling or a pushing \textit{(attractio} seu
\textit{impulsio),} since it came to the same thing. One must praise this great
man's modesty, for, although he had so great an insight into it, he
nevertheless admits that these were hidden things that no one could grasp. The
mathematicians who have come after him have built on the foundation that he
laid, and since that time many of Descartes's opinions have been rejected.

\sectionrule

Some reproach me that I do not speak respectfully enough about academies and
learned societies, especially since I am myself a member of the Academy, so
that it seems to fit me, as one commonly says: It is a bad bird that fouls its
own nest. But it is not about studies themselves that I quarrel, but only about
the manner of studying. Disputations I have in my Subterranean Journey turned
into stage plays. Some academic ceremonies that arouse laughter among the
spectators I do not approve of, and such vexatious tattle and useless studies as
hold sway in a number of gymnasia I laugh at; yet it is not the literary arts
themselves, but the faults of the students, that I attack with this critique.
For my censure shows rather that I cultivate studies with reverence, and shows
that I am careful lest the literary arts, by a wrong manner of studying and by
some ridiculous ceremonies, should be laughed at and despised. It is a great
prejudice against a good many academic foundation charters that they were issued
in the dark ages, at whose ignorance we now wonder, and whose manners and
ceremonies we cannot read of without laughter.
% --- p. 8 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{8}All things are indeed in full work and uproar enough, both in lecture
halls and in professorial chairs, but when everything is done, nothing is done.
Therefore I have often wished that there might occur a reformation that could be
becoming to our century; I should wish that all uproar be changed to silence,
all chatter to meditations. I should wish that instead of so many doctors and
masters who give lectures, some be chosen who should not be permitted to take up
and explain whatever they pleased, but should only answer what was asked of
them. From such an arrangement two benefits would flow: first the hearers could
learn what they desired to know: second, no one would take it upon himself to be
a teacher before his studies had become ripe in the science that he gave himself
out for.

It is to be wished that at the time when studies came to life again, together
with that Luther who reformed theology, there had also arisen another Luther,
who could have reformed such learned matters, who could have wholly removed all
the old leaven of that time, and cured the faults that the darkness of that age
had introduced; for there are still many things that remain and ought to be
reformed. The academies that have lately been founded in France and England show
how necessary a reformation is, since their founders, contrary to the charters
of other universities, where the young students are required to believe
everything, whatever their
% print: alt,, (misprint)
teachers tell them, and where the students' heads are not sharpened or polished,
but rather their taste is spoiled, and where they must learn something that they
afterwards have the trouble of forgetting again. Those who come to the
universities, as to the place where they are to acquire all good arts, indeed
come away from them more learned, but not better, or more sharpened; for instead
of their old prejudices being removed, which is what distinguishes the learned
from the common man, they are on the contrary the more increased. The old
prejudices ought to be wholly eradicated, and the young men's brains quite
cleansed, before they come to
% --- p. 9 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{9}study. For if one does not heed this, they believe nothing to be true except
what agrees with the old sect they have once adopted.

From this one can see that when, on the occasion of the jesting that is found
here and there in my writings, one would accuse me of hating studies, they do me
great wrong. My unceasing labor, my many published writings, the prizes with
which I have lured others to write, show rather zeal for studies than coldness,
rather reverence than contempt. They also greatly err who imagine that I write
for gain, for other opportunities have been offered me by which I could, with
less labor, make greater profit: the trouble I have had in working out my
writings gives sufficient notice that it has not been avarice that drove me to
write; I have let the most profitable opportunities slip out of my hands,
because I regarded them as a hindrance to my studies; for all the time, I have
held, I have wasted that I have not employed on my studies.

With all this zeal I still do not think that I have done anything, and, although
some give me the name of very diligent, I hold myself to have been very lazy;
for if I compare myself with others, I am ashamed that I have all but slept my
time away; the perpetual illnesses with which I am plagued greatly hinder my
eagerness to study. In particular I am hindered by headache, which is indeed
very inconvenient for all people, but especially for a studying person.
Therefore, since illnesses are unavoidable for human beings, if I were allowed
the power to distribute illnesses, I should send headache either to women alone,
and to those whose business requires no brains, or else to unreasonable
scribblers and authors, of whom we have far too many in the world.

If I have any merits in studies, they consist in this, that I have sought to
cure people's common and
% --- p. 10 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{10}pedantic taste.
For since I came in good time to study at the French and English academies, I
learned not so much to write learnedly as neatly to express my meaning. And this
I can say without boasting, that I am almost the first among my countrymen who,
instead of meager and confused chronicles, have given us tolerably well-arranged
histories, and who have set the moral study on its feet again, which up to this
time lay as if buried here in the North. There are also those who appreciate this,
and praise me for my diligence; this only they wonder at, why a man such as I,
who am constantly sickly and so advanced in years, will concern himself with
such comic poems, and expend my powers on merry fables. But just the weakness of
my body and mind drives me to this, which I find to be to me as a medicine: and
I will not advise others against making use of the same remedy. There was one
who once took me to task for it with these words: How can you write such jests,
when you are yourself so decorous. But I answered: How can you write such
serious things, when you are yourself such a comedian.


\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. ON FREETHINKERS (Epist. II).}


The English writing against \textit{Warburton,} composed by a \textit{Deistic
society} in England, I have received together with your consideration of the
\textit{Deists} of the same country, who attack \textit{revelation} with such
vehemence that \textit{Hobbes,} Spinoza and others are not to be compared with
them. You condemn the boldness of these new writers, and with justice. For one
can, and ought, do no other than condemn the people who make it an honor to
overthrow \textit{religion,} and to blacken the clergy. I hold that one ought to
labor with all one's might to refute them. I only wish that such labor were
entrusted solely to those who could do it with
% --- p. 11 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{11}acumen. Yet one does not always notice that this is heeded; for one often
sees young and half-learned people undertake such \textit{refutations,} and
fight more with abusive words than with arguments: which has the effect that the
unbelieving are strengthened in their unbelief, and that one is more scandalized
by the \textit{refutations} than by the writings themselves.

So it has gone with me. I have read most of the new \textit{Deistic} writings
without any notable temptation: But I have been scandalized by several of the
replies; For as to the writings in themselves, since I have noticed that nothing
is so ill-grounded that it cannot get a semblance of truth when it is carried
out by a \textit{skilled} pen, I have indeed often been brought up short by them,
but yet not \textit{despaired} of the loosing of the knots: whereas when I have
read the answers of certain writers who have undertaken to do battle for
\textit{religion,} and seen that they arm themselves with the prophecies of the
\textit{Sibyl,} with Josephus's testimony about Christ, and other such
\textit{arguments,} which are yet no \textit{arguments,} then I have often lost
courage. It is better to answer nothing than to answer in a manner that makes
the truth suspect. Since I have always with great concern investigated the matter
of my salvation, nothing has gladdened me more than to find out upright proofs
and demonstrations with which the mouth might be stopped of those who seek to
deprive men of the comfort which they have from \textit{revelation,} and of the
hope of the other life, which strengthens them in the adversity of this life. I
admit only that the \textit{Deists} mentioned are such enemies as cannot be
fought with every kind of weapon, but that one must meet them with helmet,
shield and armor, armed from head to foot. A \textit{Collins}, a
\textit{Tyndal}, a \textit{Morgan}, a \textit{Chub} etc. are such writers as I
condemn, but do not treat with contempt, and, after the example of certain
clergymen, hold to be foolish, injudicious and confused heads. I have found in
their writings \textit{objections} at which I have groaned, and knots which I
have not so easily been able to loose. Such a thing, however, has not
% --- p. 12 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{12}taken my courage from me, but rather encouraged me to examine everything
carefully; which I still do daily, advising you and everyone to do the same. My
thoughts are that these terrible champions who challenge the whole of
Christendom to battle can be fought, but not by everyone; and my advice would be,
instead of allowing everyone to enter the lists with \textit{foils} against such
exercised and armed fencing-masters, to set certain \textit{prizes} for those who
could most thoroughly defend the truth, so that a good cause might not be ruined
by unsuitable \textit{advocates.} This could be done by \textit{renouncing}
\textit{trivial} and ill-founded arguments, and keeping to those alone that hit
the mark: For the state of things is now such that one, according to the
reminder of a certain writer, must abandon the outworks in order to defend
oneself the better in the main fortress. I know well that such
\textit{precaution} is commonly ascribed either to cowardice or to ignorance, and
that many think to show proof of their understanding and Christianity by despising
such books. I for my part have just as bad thoughts of those who see none as of
those who see too many difficulties, of those who can resolve all knots as of
those who hold them to be unresolvable. Both may arise from ignorance: The
former go straight ahead with boldness, because they see no stones on the way;
And the latter turn back, because they hold the way
\textit{impracticable.} I hold him a reasonable Christian who sees stones, but
strives to clear them out of the way.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{C. THE PROBLEM OF EVIL (Epist. CCXXV).}


Our common friend \textit{B. S.}, whose chief study for some time has been to refute the
\textit{Manichaeans,} and to loose the knots which \textit{Monsr. Bayle} has tied,
and which he has thought to be insoluble, pretending that however much one
twists
% --- p. 13 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{13}and turns, one cannot but adopt the hated doctrine of two
\textit{principia,} since the natural as well as the moral evil things that are
seen in the world cannot flow from one good \textit{principium,} or from a
righteous, good and merciful Creator. I say, our common friend thinks that one
could extricate oneself from this \textit{labyrinth} by \textit{positing} two
\textit{principia,} without lapsing into the \textit{Manichaean} heresy. The two
\textit{principia} which he thinks might be admissible, without impairing God's
absolute power or the \textit{unity} of his essence, are, with the ancient
philosophers, to \textit{posit} the eternity of matter, and thus to assume two
\textit{principia}, an \textit{activum}, which is the Creator, and a pure
\textit{passivum,} which is matter. In this doctrine he thinks nothing offensive
to be, since by \textit{positing} a dead and formless \textit{matter's
preexistence} before the creation, the Creator's honor, omnipotence and
\textit{unity} are not denied, as in the \textit{Manichaeans'} system; who by
\textit{positing} two equally eternal \textit{active Principia} deny God's unity.
He thinks by such innocent doctrine first to prevent one's coming into collision
with the commonly accepted \textit{philosophical} \textit{axiom}: \textit{Ex nihilo
fit nil}; that is: From nothing comes nothing. Next to refute the
\textit{Manichaeans' duality} or doctrine of two \textit{active} principles;
since by adopting such a doctrine one can conceive how the many evil things can
\textit{exist}, without ascribing their cause to a good and righteous God.
Almost all ancient \textit{philosophers}, except \textit{Democritus} and his
followers, have assumed two eternal \textit{principia}, namely God and
\textit{matter}; and it seems that they held such a doctrine necessary, since
otherwise one cannot conceive why so many evil things, \textit{moral} as well as
\textit{physical}, that are found in the world, can be reconciled with God's
goodness. The Stoic \textit{philosophers} speak thus about this: \emph{There are
two \textit{principia} of all things; the one is \textit{activum,} the other
\textit{passivum:}} (τὸ ποιοῦν καὶ τὸ πάσχον.) \emph{The \textit{passive
Principium} is \textit{matter}, which is unfit for the least}
% --- p. 14 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{14}\emph{Action, and the \textit{active Principium} is God;} (λόγος και Θεός.)
\textit{Seneca} says that there are in the world two things of which it is
composed, namely the efficient cause \textit{(causa efficiens)} and
\textit{matter}. In which the greatest \textit{philosophers} agree, by whom God is
called a living and rational being, who by his action and labor on the
\textit{preexistent matter} has formed the world. \textit{Matter} is thus,
according to their saying, a \textit{substance} which is without all life and
motion, but such as can receive all the shapes that can be set upon it. And, when
that is so, it is, notwithstanding its eternity, a \textit{dependence} of God,
who is the only living and acting \textit{principium}, and does not
\textit{enter} into the creation otherwise than as the \textit{materials} for a
building, and clay for a vessel. God, they say, has shown his omnipotence, wisdom
and goodness in the created things; that everything has not become good must be
ascribed solely to \textit{matter}, in which the evil things have lain hidden,
but not to the Creator, who in that way must be beyond all \textit{critique;} and
the objections that are made against his goodness are thereby refuted and brought
to nothing. God, says \textit{Plato,} wills all that is good. With regard to the
evil things, he does not will them; but they are effects of \textit{matter:}
which, since it is eternal, cannot be brought to nothing; but \textit{form} and
shape alone are set upon it. Against this it may be said that, since this
doctrine aims at refuting the objections that are made from the evil things
against God's goodness, they collide on the other hand with his omnipotence; for
it may in that case be said that he would have made everything good if he had
been able; for \textit{Plato} explains this plainly with these words: Βουληθεῖ ὁ
Θεὸς ἀγαθὰ μὲν πάντα, φαῦλα δέ μηδέν εἶναι κατὰ δύναμιν. But our common friend
thinks that this does no injury to God's omnipotence and wisdom, since nothing
else follows from it but that \textit{form} had to conform to \textit{matter;} the
objections, on the other hand, that are made against God's goodness are thereby
refuted, when one says that \textit{matter} with the evil and good that
% --- p. 15 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{15}lay hidden in it is eternal and uncreated. For to wish in that way to
\textit{criticize} the creation is the same as to wish to blame a great artist
who had worked out a great wooden figure because it was not as strong as one of
\textit{marble}; for to that one could answer: It cannot be otherwise, since the
material is wood. To say my plain thoughts about this, it seems to me that this
\textit{argument} could bring \textit{Monsr. Bayle} to sweat, if he had to deal
with a heathen \textit{philosopher.} But, since his \textit{antagonists} are
Christians, who know from \textit{revelation} that the \textit{axiom: Ex nihilo
fit nil,} does not hold water, and who admit that Holy Scripture testifies that
God made everything out of nothing, he lets himself not be troubled by this
\textit{philosophical} \textit{argument;} it is therefore best not to make use of
it, especially since we have an \textit{argument} which was unknown to the
heathen, which is taken from the Fall; so that we can say that all that God
created was good, but all the evil that is now seen in the world flows from the
transgression of our first parents, whereby man was subjected to vices, diseases
and death, and the whole earth was cursed, and lost the glory in which it had
been set at the first creation.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{D. MORALS AND THEOLOGY (Moralske Tanker. 1744).}

Studies may be divided into necessary, useful and harmful. The necessary studies
are those that deal with man's duty toward God and his neighbor, and comprise
theology and morals. In the necessity of these two sciences all nations are
agreed, though they differ from one another in manner and practice. For most
commit herein a \textit{hysteron proteron,} and begin with theology by impressing
on small children the mysteries and secrets of religion, before they make them
rational creatures. For it is as if in schools one were to begin in languages
with
% --- p. 16 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{16}the oracles of \textit{Zoroaster} or the most difficult Latin poets.
Children must be made human beings before they become Christians: As matter must
be prepared and smoothed before the shape is made, and the paper made level
before one writes upon it. It is therefore to be wished that the children's
examinations were made two out of one, and that in the first they were
questioned and confirmed as human beings, and in the last as Christian human
beings. But one begins first with the catechization of divine mysteries, whereby
it is brought about that everyone defends with utmost obstinacy the sect in which
he has been brought up, and will afterwards receive no arguments. For the fold
that is laid in childhood is not easily effaced. Man's brain must first be
planed, before any shape can be made upon it. For if one begins first with the
form, it is afterwards too late to level the matter, and if a prejudice takes
root in a man's mind, it becomes a stain that can almost never be effaced. One
must first learn to doubt before one may learn to believe; as one must first
taste the food and wine before one eats and drinks: for if that is not done, one
goes to work backwards with the instruction, and thereby authorizes all
religions, indeed lays no foundation for learning truth, but only for that alone
which an instructor holds to be truth, which is commonly the doctrine of the
sect in which he himself has been brought up.

If one went about this in proper order, began with morals and ended with
theology, that is, if one undertook to make people first into human beings before
one tried to make them Christians, the many religions' Sects would be reduced to
very few, and the bitterness that reigns among them would wholly cease. I will
not speak here of those who have an interested religion: for these begin
intentionally first with the theology of their sect, before they instruct in
morals, since their aim is not to train youth in the right truth, but in a
\textit{veritate locali,} that is: in such principles as among them are by laws
% --- p. 17 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{17}authorized. Therefore the Roman Catholics, especially Jesuits, who are all
interested teachers, begin by impressing on youth the doctrine of the Church's
infallibility, since they are assured that, when this doctrine has taken deep
root, truth can by no means find a place in the hearts of men. Therefore the
Muslims also take good care not to begin with philosophy, knowing well that
no one could get their theology into his head who had first been instructed in
the rules which a rational soul ought to use in order to derive truth.

I say that I will not speak of those who have a self-interested faith, but of
those who have sounder principles, of those who hold that one ought to taste
before one believes, and to examine before one signs. It is to them alone that
such a reminder is addressed, since in the education of youth they transgress
their own principles, in that they teach them catechisms and symbolic books by
heart, before they have made \textit{preparations} for it. \textit{Skilled
Physicians} proceed herein more in order, they begin with the body's purgings,
so that
the right \textit{medicine} may \textit{operate} the better. If teachers did the
same, theology would \textit{operate} the more powerfully. But one begins by
crying: \emph{Believe! Believe!} before one shows what ought to be believed and
what not. It is to go to work like what is told of a certain judge, who began
with the execution, and afterwards had the case examined. I should therefore
rejoice at the school ordinance by which it was decreed that in the first and
second lesson moral catechizations should be pursued, and that Luther's
Catechism should not become a \textit{precept} until the third lesson, so that
the first institution should consist in forming the mind, and arming it against
all prejudices, in order afterwards the better to impress upon it the true and
sound theology. For, if one learns theology before he learns to become a human
being, he never becomes a human being. Moral philosophy has been much neglected,
in the Jewish as well as in the Christian Church. The effect of it
% --- p. 18 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{18}has been that small things have been piously observed, and important things
neglected. In error one has transgressed all the commandments in order to uphold
one of the least. One has persecuted and murdered one's neighbor on account of
disagreement in a speculation or ceremony. One has been zealous in the outward
worship of God, and at the same time wallowed in all sins and pleasures: Indeed,
one has meddled with describing God, angels and spirits, and never troubled
oneself about self-Knowledge. Freebooting, for example, which was practiced among
the Northern Christians, could not have been counted as heroic virtues, if they
had learned to become human beings before they became Christians. The same may
also be said of many other sins, which for the same cause were in vogue, and
were not held to be sins.

One sees from this that a \textit{hysteron proteron} must be made in the
instruction of youth, and that one must lay the foundation by Christ's morals and
a sound philosophy before the \textit{dogmata} and mysteries of Christianity are
learned. The French King \textit{Clovis,} after he had become a Christian, and
heard the Passion story read, became so hot in the head that he said: \emph{if
I had lived at that same time, I would with my French troops have cut down
all the Jews with the saber, and saved Christ}; which he would hardly have said
if the \textit{converters} of those times had sought to have him molded before
they had him baptized. % Danish „døde“ (PRINTED AS IS), read as døbe, the rhyme with støbe
Our missionaries proceed herein like our schoolteachers.
They commonly begin with the mysteries of religion, at which the unbelieving at
once take offense, and will hear no more about it. I have shown in another place
what effect the Spanish Bishop's sermon had upon the Peruvian King, namely that
the King judged from the Bishop's speech that he could not be right in the head,
and therefore at once broke everything off short: which perhaps would not have
happened if he had not begun the conversion backwards. The same is seen from the
examples of many others, who have either taken offense at
% --- p. 19 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{19}the doctrine, or immediately after their acceptance have deserted again, as one
notes the newly converted Indian youth doing. One must therefore first lay the
foundation by shaping and caulking the vessels before one pours the wine into
them: one must impress on people sound morals, show them what they ought to
believe before they are bidden to believe, and instruct them in a kind of
\textit{arte critica,} so that they do not take the shadow for the body. Thereby
the catechist first acquires an opinion of honesty and impartiality among the
young proselytes, since they see from it that his sole design is to impress on
them a disinterested saving truth; thereby they are also themselves made suitable
instruments for receiving the doctrine and for keeping it.

When one goes to work thus in order, and shows in general what a rational soul
ought to believe, and what it ought to reject, what agrees with the law of nature,
and what conflicts with it, and afterwards comes forward with the revealed
doctrine, which one thereby shows to be wholly conformable, and that certain
mysteries do not conflict with it, one may expect that conversions will be lasting,
and no doctrine can become an article of faith unless it is thorough and rational:
whereas experience among all nations otherwise shows that, since this rule is not
heeded, one can impress upon youth the very strangest and most inhuman opinions,
and which afterwards can by no means be rooted out again. To illustrate this, I
will adduce only two examples. One undertakes to instruct another in Christianity.
He prepares the way for it, first by explaining to him the chief commandments of
nature: he gives him a general \textit{idea} of virtues and vices: he represents to
him what equity, justice and goodness are, that these are virtues among God's
created creatures, and therefore consequently must be the divine being's own
qualities. After such a foundation has been laid, he goes on to religion, and
shows from the symbolic books, which his sect has subscribed, that God has
% --- p. 20 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{20}of free will and pleasure created most men to damnation. The proselyte then
necessarily balks at it, and refuses to be able to adopt a doctrine which conflicts
with the former, which has been impressed on him as introduction. Further, a Roman
\textit{converter} begins to catechize with philosophy and morals, saying that a
human being ought to believe nothing that is against \textit{notiones communes},
that is, what he and all men see, hear and feel to be in conflict with what is
pretended. Thereupon he advances to the doctrine and shows, when he comes to the
article on Transubstantiation, that bread is flesh. The disciple is then
scandalized by a doctrine which conflicts openly with the former truth, saying:
\emph{if I may not believe common sight and senses:
if I must deny seeing what I see, and hearing what I
and all others hear, then a perfect Pyrrhonism is thereby
established, and no certainty remains any more in the world;
indeed then one may doubt Scripture and Miracles
themselves, since one has no benefit from sight and hearing}.

One sees from this that, when by such preparations one is first made into a
human being, the mind is thus formed to receive a true and to reject a false
doctrine. Enough has been said about the necessary studies, whose further
utility I will leave to others to describe. In this dissertation I have only been
content with this to note down an error that is daily committed by teachers in the
order of instruction: for it is at such things that my morals chiefly aim.

In this first class, which comprises theology and morals, may also be placed
histories, although most philosophers allow histories no place except among the
useful studies, so that they commonly place them after Mathesis. But this comes
from the fact that few regard histories with right eyes, and do not consider that
morals is chiefly learned from historical examples, and that religion must show
its warrant and title from histories.

% --- p. 21 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{21}Among the useful studies may be placed Mathesis and Physica. About this I
will say nothing, since it is known to all what use Mathesis brings with it. I
will only remind here of an error among the oldest philosophers, in that they had
formed a far different \textit{idea} of the dignity of mathematics than one does
nowadays: for they held that it was unbecoming to a philosopher to meddle with
anything but theory, and regarded Praxis or mechanics as common handicrafts; so
that Mathesis among them consisted solely in reasoning; whereas one nowadays
proceeds more in order, and lays the foundation with mechanical experiments.
Therefore the mathematicians of our times have this device: \textit{Nullius} in
Verba.

Philosophy was in olden days divided into sects, as theology is nowadays. Each
of these sects sought not so much to derive truth as to defend what the founder
or establisher had taught. This philosophy, which may be called \textit{philosophia
carbonaria,} lasted until somewhat after the time of Christ, when some, of whom
the first was \textit{Potamon,} undertook not to swear by anyone's word any more,
but to extract the best from all sects. This philosophy was called
\textit{philosophia eclectica.} I do not know whether it is against orthodoxy to
wish that there were also a \textit{theologia eclectica,} since one sees
Christians, after the plan of the old philosophers, not troubling themselves so
much to derive truth as to defend each his own sect. I do not venture to make such
a wish, although I admit only that if anyone made a proposal for a
\textit{theologia eclectica,} I would not make war on him for it.

One can say the same of Physica as of Mathesis, that its importance consists in
Praxis and experiments, and, when that is so, one must not blame him who places
peasants and skilled farmers among the professors of \textit{philosophiae
naturalis;} the more so as agriculture is the noblest and most important part of
Physica or knowledge of natural things. I
% --- p. 22 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{22}hold him a learned man who thoroughly understands an important science,
whether he has learned it in Greek, Latin or his mother tongue, whether he has
acquired it by reading or experience. I know well that such an opinion is held to
be peculiar and unreasonable. But all that is common is not always reasonable.
If knowledge of agriculture became an academic study, the 9 Muses would, in my
thoughts, bear no resentment over it. For academies are founded to cultivate those
studies by which Church, state and civil societies are built up. It does not
depend on who has studied most, but who has studied best. And, when that is so, I
cannot see but that one could with just as great right give a Master's or
Doctor's hat to a farmer as to a grammaticus, unless one will say that it is more
important to arrange words and glosses than to improve a piece of land, or more
important to cleanse an old poet of copyist errors than to cleanse a field and
make it fruitful. Therefore the peasant boy in the Comedy reasoned not so ill when
he held Peder Christensen to be the most learned man in the town, since he had
taken over a dilapidated farm and made it one of the best. I do not know whether
one can place rhetoric among the useful studies. Ancient Greeks and Romans
regarded it as the most important science. But against this great objections can
be made. A certain philosopher compares rhetoric to women's rouge. The latter
blinds the eyes; the former blinds the mind. \textit{Socrates} calls it an art of
deceiving people. One sees that in well-ordered states, as in \textit{Crete} and
\textit{Lacedaemon}, rhetoric has been held in no regard. In Rome, on the other
hand, Greece and other republics, where everything was in confusion, it has been
regarded as a chief study. In this, however, I will not decide.

I will also pass over speaking of other useful studies, since I have no particular
remarks to make about them; but turn to the useless ones, that is, those which are
most
% --- p. 23 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{23}in use. For most so-called learned dissertations are made either on what no
one understands, or on what no one has any concern to know. Thus one hears
disputations daily about God's essence, about the nature of souls and angels, and
other such things which are, and always will remain, hidden from men, or about
obscure and unimportant things in antiquities, such as about \textit{Homer's}
fatherland, about \textit{Aeneas's} mother, about \textit{Romulus's} father.

Such studies may not only be called useless, but even harmful, in so far as time
is thereby wasted. One may liken those who study thus to children who incessantly
leaf through books, but only to look for pictures.

\sectionrule

I have on various occasions shown that one of my chief \textit{principles} in
religion is this, namely to accept nothing that conflicts with God's goodness
and holiness. The same \textit{principle} causes me to reject all absolute
predestination: the same also causes me to judge more mildly of Jews, heathens and
Muslims than many others. The foundation of this my temperament is that I
cannot reconcile their damnation with God's goodness. For what can be more
contradictory to it than to let a human being's salvation and damnation depend on
birth, upbringing, ignorance and certain accidents to which he is subject. To damn
a Chinese or Muslim solely with regard to the false faith which they profess
is to damn them because they are born in Peking or Constantinople. Nothing can in
my thoughts give a worse \textit{idea} of God's economy, and no opinion is
therefore less admissible. What would one judge of the magistrate of that city who
without distinction declared all those citizens unworthy of honor and offices who
were born in certain quarters of the city? What would one judge of the Church law
which forbade that anyone who had
% --- p. 24 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{24}black hair might come into Christian soil, or that any child that was born
between Yule and Easter might be admitted to the congregation by baptism. One
would say: what can the persons do about it? why should they be punished for
things which they have not done themselves, or which were not in their power to do
or leave undone.

Just as it is contrary to justice and goodness to damn one on account of his
birthplace, so unfair is it also to pass such a judgment with regard to one's
\textit{education} and upbringing; for a child cannot itself choose nurses and
teachers, but must content itself with those it gets by accident, since the
tender years require that it stand under others' \textit{direction.} Now daily
experience shows us that the \textit{principia} which one receives with the
nurse's milk, and which are impressed by the first teachers, become
\textit{characteres indelebiles,} stains that cannot be washed off, and folds that
cannot be smoothed. One sees daily tender Christian children carried off to
Turkey, and there brought up in the Muslim religion; one sees the same ever
afterwards cling to it with such zeal that they stake life and limb for its
defense. To damn such children is to damn them solely on account of the
misfortune they had of being carried off in their tender years; and to adjudge to
their brothers or sisters salvation after this life is the same as to open to them
the gates of Paradise, because they had the good fortune not to fall into robbers'
hands.

It is no less unfair to punish on account of ignorance. Everyone must admit that
there are a million people who have never heard of the Christian faith, indeed
that they are in the \textit{situation} or in the countries where no opportunity
has been to hear anything about it. Among them may be counted certain nations in
\textit{America} or the inhabitants of the southern land: among whom to find
\textit{orthodoxy} might be held a miracle; for if there are innumerable people
who hear and do not believe, how would one then expect belief among those who have
never
% --- p. 25 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{25}heard.
% print: fodre (misprint)
No one can with fairness demand account of those to whom nothing has been entrusted,
and no one can punish for the transgression of a law before it is promulgated. One
of \textit{Caligula's} most misshapen undertakings was this, that he had a law
drawn up in small characters and set it in a high place, so that no one could read
it. We ought to have other thoughts of God, whom Holy Scripture likens to a pious
father of the household, and calls men his dear children. We must hold that, as a
good judge looks into all the circumstances of the case in an action before he
pronounces a judgment, so also God, who is justice himself, takes all such
matters into account, and takes into \textit{consideration} time, place, the
circumstances of persons, and in what \textit{situation} each one is, which he
also gives us to understand by parables, in which a difference is shown between
those who are entrusted with more and with less. They deceive themselves greatly,
says \textit{Tillotson,} who do not imagine that an ungodly life is the greatest
heresy: for it is such as directly conflicts with the aim of the Christian
religion; indeed they go astray who do not reflect that God would rather forgive
100 faults which arise from misunderstanding than one single offense that flows
from the will. \textit{Plato} therefore says quite reasonably: \textit{pœna errantis
est doceri,} that is: the punishment of him who errs is instruction.

For my own part I am willing to correct my opinions, and often let myself be
moved by thorough arguments. But if the whole world agreed in making God an
\textit{arbitrary} judge, who judges solely according to his pleasure without
regard to men's conduct, then I hold fast to this \textit{principle,} in which I
think I cannot go astray, and even if I did go astray, the error is such as cannot
but be pleasing to God himself, just as a ruler could not be angered at the
subject who denies that he does anything that conflicts with the laws of the
land, or that he punishes and condemns anyone by sheer will and pleasure; which
happens when one condemns on account of birth, insuperable ignorance and pure
accidents: indeed I \textit{extend} this in
% --- p. 26 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{26}the same respect so far that I hold that God even takes into
\textit{consideration} more and less innate frailty, so that for the same
misdeed he passes a milder judgment on a \textit{choleric} than on one who is
born with a temperate \textit{complexion,} and demands more of one whom he has
\textit{distinguished} with great gifts of nature than of a thick-headed person,
just as laziness is less blamed in a man racked with pain than in one who has
nimble and brisk limbs.

I do not know whether it is heresy to have such thoughts of God, that he also
reflects on the circumstances of a human being's end. We are all
sinners, and all need to do penance, especially at our last. Some die suddenly
and have little time to prepare: others die of long-lasting infirmities, so that
they enjoy sufficient opportunity to atone for their sins. Some die of violent
diseases and raving: others of wasting infirmities, whereby they retain their
senses to the last breath. The first are commonly judged ill of: the last, on the
contrary, are praised as blessed. I do not venture to \textit{decide} herein; but
say only that God, as a just judge, has all circumstances before his eyes when he
pronounces judgment, so that a serious sigh of him who is carried off suddenly
must balance against a whole year's penance of him who dies of a long illness:
for one would otherwise have to say that those who die of strokes and sudden
diseases are commonly damned; those, on the other hand, who die of consumption,
commonly become blessed: which is to let damnation and salvation depend on the
kind and quality of the disease. One must believe that, as a \textit{superior}
who bids his subordinate at once to get ready for a journey demands less
attentiveness than of him to whom he gives a long time for it, so God also takes
such things into account herein, since to die is nothing other than an order to
depart, which each must obey. I otherwise submit this to the judgment of others,
but hold fast to my \textit{thesis,} that God damns no one on account of those
things which are not within a human being's power.

% --- p. 27 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{27}This seems to me so clear that I have often wondered how not only the common
people, but even the greatest part of enlightened theologians can fall upon the
thought that God judges the human race, not with regard to its conduct and deeds,
but according to absolute will and pleasure. They indeed deny having such a
doctrine, but must admit that it is a consequence of it. They say that
God does not damn a heathen or Muslim because they are born of heathen and
Muslim parents, but because they do not use their natural understanding, and the
means that God has given them to search out truth, and because they neglect the
opportunities that are given them to hear the Word. But daily experience shows
that all, even the best and most moralized nations, cling to the religion in
which they are brought up, and no theologian ventures to deny that, if he had been
born in another place, he too would have had the faith of that place; for to deny
such a thing would be to speak against experience, and to dispute against
experience is to renounce one's senses, to make everything uncertain and
wavering, indeed to introduce a pernicious Pyrrhonism. Several have
noticed this, and therefore abandoned this argument, grounding their
thesis solely on certain expressions found in Holy Scripture: as, that
there is no salvation except through faith in Christ, item, that he who
does not believe is damned, etc. But, since these same people themselves admit
that in the interpretation of Scripture one often may and ought to depart from the
letter: item, that one must confer one passage of Scripture with
another, in order to derive its right meaning, there is no greater need for this
than herein, where it is a matter of conserving the thoughts one ought to
have of the divine attributes. As often therefore as Scripture says that there is
no salvation outside the orthodox, we must by unbelief and errors understand none
but wilful ones, and not extend it to insuperable errors. And, as often as
it is found that God can by sheer pleasure create to damnation and salvation, we
must
% --- p. 28 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{28}not believe that he does so, nor that his divine attributes permit him to do
so; but that the manner of speech gives us to understand only that if God did so,
or could do so, creatures must not dispute against it and say: \emph{Why hast thou
done this?} The necessity of such interpretations is shown by innumerable other
passages of Scripture, where there is talk of God's love toward the whole human
race, and his purpose to open the door of salvation to all. Thus one passage of
Scripture is reconciled with another, and God's holy attributes
are conserved.

Some have been found who have wished to extricate themselves from this in another
way, by pretending that one must not limit God's omnipotence, item, that
one must not judge of divine virtues by human ones; indeed, that God's justice
consists in doing what he finds pleasure in. But God's omnipotence is not limited
by saying that he cannot renounce his holiness and deny himself; and to
make into virtues in God what he himself condemns, what he blames in men, is not
only to overthrow all natural religion, but even the revealed one, which expressly
says that what he recommends to man as virtues is the property of his
own nature. For it says: \emph{Be ye merciful, as your Father}
\emph{is merciful.} One may hope that these harsh opinions will with time be more
and more softened; and one sees daily one and another stop at the
objections that are made against them, and by sighs give us to
understand that it is a labyrinth from which they would gladly be able to extricate
themselves, which may happen when the knots are loosed in the same way as has been
said. I for my part hold that if the knots could not be loosed, then one should
use \textit{Alexander's} sword to cut them across. For no doctrine ought to stand
in force that impresses on us a bad idea of the divine being.

\begin{intro}

% --- p. 29 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{29}2. \textsc{Jens Schielderup Sneedorff} (1724---1764) was, like Holberg,
influenced by French and English literature. In a pure and beautiful Danish, which
makes an epoch in the history of our language, he has discussed philosophical and
political-science topics. He emphasizes the great significance of experience, and
expects that with advancing experience many riddles and problems that now plague
us will vanish. Holberg saw the matter from the opposite side, a characteristic
difference of standpoint, which points to a difference of temperament. In the piece
chosen here, Sneedorff speaks for a philosophy grounded on experience and maintains
the reality of the single, individual things in opposition to all general
concepts.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{PHILOSOPHY OF EXPERIENCE \\ (Den patriotiske Tilskuer Nos. 14 and 47).}


No reasonable person can doubt that the mathematical method is necessary in the
sciences from which it has its name. They are grounded on concepts that we
ourselves have drawn from things, and which therefore let themselves be determined
in such a way that both the definitions themselves, and all the propositions that
are drawn from them, admit of no exception; but in other sciences, where it is a
matter of depicting things as they really are found in nature and with all the
properties that make them comprehensible to our senses and awaken the movements of
our minds, how difficult is it not often therein to determine both the definitions
and the propositions in such a way that they, without any exception, can fit all
single things of every kind, as surely as it is true in all possible circles that
every point on the circumference is equally far from the center, and what real
circle is made, even on paper, with such exactness that the roundness of the
circle in nature corresponds perfectly to the roundness that it ought to have
according to the definition in our representation? The humblest artist knows how
often this difference between the real and the represented makes the application
of the general rules as uncertain in the mathematical sciences as in others.

Nor do I deny that an orderly way of thinking, and the mathematical or
philosophical is undeniably the most orderly of all, is indeed very necessary to
direct our power of thought in all sciences; but if we are to put these sciences
% --- p. 30 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{30}into practice, we must carry it further than outlines. The best painting
without a correct drawing is deformed, but when the drawing is done, the brush
ought to hide it so well that the eye sees nothing but free, unconstrained and
playing nature. A description that is to represent a thing as it really is, a
speech that is to move the heart and awaken movements of the mind, would in the
strict philosophical method have as strange an appearance as a mathematical proof
if it were set in verse and sung to notes. If we lived in a world of people who had
neither imagination nor movements of the mind, a world that truly would not be the
best, even if these people all had as much understanding as Newton, I would admit
that mathematical sermons, mathematical addresses, mathematical descriptions and
mathematical letters would have more effect than all the eloquence of the Greeks
and Romans. I would despise Demosthenes and Cicero, because in their speeches they
have not used the same method as Euclid, and regard it as a great political fault in
England that Addison and not Newton was chosen to speak in the House of Commons.

I do not say this to excuse myself; for however many lovers the mathematical method
may have among us, I am yet sure that my readers demand just as little mathematical
weekly papers as mathematical plays. We once had a philosophical Spectator in this
country, and however much the mathematical method was in repute at that time, this
work did not have such success among my countrymen that I could feel inclined to
choose his method. Mathematics is a science that does the human understanding much
honor and society great benefit, but the method alone is not so great an art, and
to wish to make it general in all sciences is a pedantry at which the
% --- p. 31 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{31}great mathematicians themselves laugh. In all parts of human knowledge it
depends more on the matter than on the form, more on wealth of thoughts and words
than on skill in making syllogisms. A practiced and fruitful power of thought
easily finds the most convenient order in which to set forth its thoughts, whereas
a dry and methodical genius wearies both itself and its readers with circumlocutions
that serve neither to convince nor to move.

The great art of working on one's own perfection is to cultivate the powers of the
mind in such a way that the one facility in the soul does not limit the other.
Flightiness will never be the chief fault of the Nordic geniuses, as pedantry will
never be that of the French. If our countrymen, instead of fettering their power of
thought by all too many methods, cultivated more the sciences that could encourage
them and procure them a facility in thinking quickly and lightly, this mixture
would without doubt in time give them a great advantage over the southern nations,
who fall into another extreme, and whose works often lose much of their value by
their not sufficiently uniting philosophy with the belles lettres.


\sectionrule

As certain as it is that everything that contains a real contradiction is
impossible, so certain it is also that the human understanding can in very few
cases prove such a contradiction. As to that kind of impossibility that the
philosophers call the definitive or \emph{necessary} \textit{impossibilitas
absoluta,} which is grounded on a real contradiction and conflict between the
concepts that constitute the essence of the thing, we could not prove from it the
impossibility of other things than those that are mere chimeras, e.g. a square
circle. The other impossibility, which is called the \emph{arbitrary}, and which
consists in the thing's conflicting with certain fixed conditions, is subject to
many limitations. We can indeed conclude that
% --- p. 32 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{32}such a thing cannot be under certain conditions; but, when we have
enumerated all such conditions, and determined them as exactly as possible, they
are so small a part of the general connection of things, and of the infinite chain
of causes, that there may be a thousand other cases in which hidden causes cancel
the conditions under which the thing was impossible.

We daily discover so many new forces in nature; we notice upon closer examination
so many exceptions to the general propositions; experience convinces us of the
reality of so many things that we had formerly held impossible, and all this is
still so little of that which is hidden from us, that we ought not easily to
venture to say that anything is impossible in the present connection of things,
much less that it is so in itself, in all possible connections, and for the Being
for whom nothing is impossible except what is impossible in itself. Who would
have believed a few hundred years ago that a stone could show us the way on the
wild sea, and can one well tell anything that could seem to us less credible than
what must occur to the southern peoples on hearing talk of lands where water
becomes so solid that it can bear whole armies? The multitude is inclined to hold
everything impossible that conflicts with the few concepts it has; but a
philosopher ought by reflection and experience to have learned to be more
cautious. The more of a philosopher he is, the more possibilities he sees, and
the less ought he to build on the few concepts and experiences that a human being
can have, the slower ought he to be to say that anything is impossible.
Nevertheless philosophy often has quite another effect than it ought to have. A
conceit of a high degree of certainty, a trust in methods and demonstrations, a
habit of thinking by certain rules, and an infatuation with one's own opinions,
cause those who call themselves philosophers to be often both hastier and more
vehement in finding impossibilities in all that is strange and new to them than
other people.

% --- p. 33 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{33}We need go no further than to ourselves to be convinced of the truth that a
reasonable man, a true philosopher, ought not easily to hold anything impossible.
Let us try, if we can, to determine how far a single faculty of the soul can be
brought, what a high degree of insight and skill, and how many inventions are
possible in a single art? Let us go through the history of a people, and see how
many things in every age have become not only possible, but also real and common,
which previously were held impossible. Let us consider what great things a few
people, a single human being, have accomplished, how many alleged impossibilities
they have overcome, and how often they have brought into reality such things as,
not only according to the moral qualities of men, but also according to the
natural character of the country, its size, climate and fertility, could seem
impossible. Let us go through the history of our own fatherland, from the time
when we had neither apples, pears nor cabbage in the country, and when we held it
impossible to brew beer. It is still not many hundred years since. Let us
consider how much has in that time become possible in the sciences, in the arts,
in the cultivation and settlement of the country; and if we do not always feel
like working for posterity, let us at least for the sake of our own honor not give
them cause to laugh at us, as we now laugh at the ancients, because they held so
many things impossible that we now find very easy.

I hardly need to say more to prove how much these alleged impossibilities can
harm religion and the state. The inclination to find impossibilities, which comes
from our limited concepts, is bad enough as it shows itself in the raw multitude,
in whom it comes mostly from laziness; but it is ten times worse when it is
united with the pride that an undigested school philosophy breathes into those who
have a limited power of thought and little experience. As natural as it is that
much must seem impossible to those who know little, so natural it is also that
those
% --- p. 34 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{34}who would know everything must hold impossible all that they cannot
themselves comprehend, and this must be much, when they are not accustomed to
think outside a system.

It is not enough for a philosopher, in order to accept something as true, that it
is not impossible; but it is enough for him not to reject it without sufficient
proof, especially when it is a proposition that could have important consequences
if it were true. He examines such propositions not so much by looking for
impossibilities and contradictions, for which he knows that reason is commonly
inadequate, as by testing the grounds and experiences on which such propositions
are built; and if he is truly a philosopher, he knows that the highest ground of
human certainty is in most things a high degree of probability, what is called a
moral certainty. If it is a theoretical truth, he is content with this certainty;
if it is a practical one, he does not despair after the first attempt, and when
he has made many, he still does not dare to say that it is impossible for others
to find what he himself has sought in vain.

I say that the highest ground of human certainty is in most things a high degree
of probability. This important truth leads me to a consideration of the second
ground of our knowledge: that we ought not to accept anything without sufficient
ground. Several philosophers explain this in such a way that one would believe
that they meant that this ground was not sufficient unless one could prove from
its concept all the properties that one ascribes to a thing. They extol the
method of proving everything from general propositions, from the concepts of
things (definitions), what they call \textit{demonstratio a
priori,} so highly that they are ready to place all those who
infer from analogy, from an agreement of experiences in single cases, in the same
class with quacks and cattle. Nevertheless the most important part of human
knowledge rests on such proofs. It is not from general grounds, not from the
definitions of things, but from an experience in single
% --- p. 35 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{35}cases, that we know that a human being must die, that fire burns, that iron
sinks in water, that wood floats, etc., and what philosopher would presume to
prove from the essence of these things that what has happened in so many millions
of cases could not even once be prevented by causes that are hidden from us? The
general concept that we form of man is that he is a rational animal. But how many
things do we infer from this concept? Truly, no more than what man has in common
with all spirits and with all animals. Whence have we the most important
knowledge of man, except from what we experience, chiefly in ourselves? On this
inward feeling we build most of the knowledge that we have of the essence and
properties of other human beings, and what is that but analogy, an inference that
is grounded on likeness between single things? On this empirical knowledge, as
our proud philosophers call it with a kind of contempt, we build all that is
important, and when our mathematicians want to carry out the great things that
they have sketched in imagination and on paper; when they want to build houses
and ships, lay out fortresses, measure heaven and earth; must they not then
descend just as deep as all other people? must they not build their surest and
most important inferences on a long and consistent experience in single cases?
must they not admit that this experience with a little theory does mankind greater
and wiser service than a great theory without this experience?

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

3. \textsc{Frederik Christian Eilschow} (1725---1750) stands in a certain
opposition to Holberg and Sneedorff through the significance that he ascribes to
abstract (``drawn-off'') reasonings, especially in the religious discussion. It was
his conviction that the freethinkers could be refuted with the help of metaphysics.
He adheres to the German philosopher Wolff. Eilschow's significance rests
especially on his so strongly asserting the duty to philosophize in Danish, and on
his having contributed a series of Danish designations in place of the foreign
expressions.

\end{intro}

% --- p. 36 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{ON PHILOSOPHIZING IN DANISH \\ (Philosophiske Breve over adskillige nyttige og vigtige Ting. \\ 1748.)}\opage{36}

Since so much has been written in the world, it has been calculated, says
\textit{Dr. Swift,} that there is not so much new matter left in the whole of
nature that one could write a single proper book about it. I do not know whether
many of my readers are likely to be of the same opinion; but I can very well
assure you that there is still much unknown and hidden from us, without therefore
assuming that \emph{matter}, in the opinion of some, \emph{is infinitely divisible.} I will say in a
few words both what has moved me to publish this little work, and what one may
count on finding in it. What I have written here is as little all old as it is
all new. The prudent must judge whether my undertaking needs other, or more,
excuses than these.

We have no superfluity of philosophical writings in the Danish language. We have
some, and among these some choice ones; but few; so that he who publicly
philosophizes in our mother tongue has no need to complain of the multitude of his
predecessors. Most of our Danish philosophers are moreover \emph{moralists} only,
and one reads only very little, or nothing, in other parts of worldly wisdom in
the language of the fatherland. Now the whole of philosophy contains many high and
important things which it is useful for all people to learn and to be convinced
of: and for that reason in other countries, and in most European ones, it has long since
begun to set forth all sorts of sciences in one's mother tongue, in order to open
an all the easier way to knowledge to those who are ignorant of the language of
the learned. Nothing is more serviceable for separating the chaff from the wheat,
things from words, wisdom from gossip, than to set forth one's thoughts and
philosophical doctrines in a language that all understand.
% --- p. 37 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{37}A \emph{Latin wisdom,} which gets lost in the translator's hands when it is to
be set forth in another tongue, cannot please any but those who despise all that
is clear, and may be regarded as a kind of \emph{learned}
\emph{night-owls,} who feel
best of all in the darkness of concepts and words. I have on the contrary held
that a philosophical truth has no need to fear the light, and that its inward
excellence never pleases spectators more than when it is surrounded by an outward
clarity. I admit that the most excellent things are written in Latin; but I
maintain besides that they could also be written in our language, and that this
is no more unsuitable for it than \emph{English,} \emph{French} or
\emph{German.} The sciences, as they are taught in these, go out, as it were,
beyond the narrow bounds of the schools and academies among the people in the wide
world to more general use. \textit{Socrates} was, according to \textit{Cicero's}
account, the first who moved philosophy down from heaven, to let it dwell in
cities and houses. I know well that this Roman \emph{consul} means to say that the
said \emph{Greek} was the first who left the doctrine of nature in order to devote
himself the more to the doctrine of morals, and concerned himself more with
happiness than with knowledge of the stars; but nothing prevents us from also
saying: \textit{Socrates} was the first among the Greeks who made that wisdom
common among the people which the \emph{Sophists} until his time wished to reserve
to their disciples alone. This great philosopher conceived that what was useful in
philosophy was useful for all, and he took every pains that more than those who,
in the manner of those times, went about in \emph{long cloaks}
\emph{with a long beard} might get to know it. Long after that time the sciences were shut up within the
learned walls, and so long as they were wrapped in the Latin language, so long as
the scholastic darkness lasted, all non-Latin people understood not a word of this
wisdom; but had to admit, when a \textit{Scotus}, an \textit{Occam}, a
\textit{suárez}, or other good honest Aristotelians opened
% --- p. 38 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{38}their blessed mouths, with great astonishment and in the deepest ignorance,
that they were dreadfully learned people. Since then, when philosophical slavery
went out of fashion, since \textit{Aristotle} was gradually dethroned, one began to
express oneself in other living languages, so that more people also might have
benefit from it; though in the beginning it did not go off without much
contradiction. Everyone stared and gaped when \textit{Thomasius} for the first time
posted a German Programma on the academic blackboard. \emph{Just think,}
says this Socrates of the Germans, \emph{a German Programma on a Latin board!} But
gradually one paid more and more heed to the sound reason which said, \emph{that
what was sound and wise in one language remained so and necessarily
in another.}

So I concluded when I first resolved to write in \emph{Danish.} Some learned
friends entered into a society with me, to publish, each separately, a monthly
issue on one and another philosophical doctrine. I was chosen to compose the first
number; but before the second was to go out, the society was already dissolved
between us, so that I alone went on writing the following numbers, which all
appeared under the supervision of Prof. \textit{Kofod.} When I had laid down the
pen at the beginning of last year, it occurred to me from time to time to make
