important remarks on the matters that I had treated in these monthly writings. In
the course of it there also occurred to me some objections which several of my
readers had made against the same. I undertook to set down the former and to
answer the latter, and that was the occasion of this work, which comes to light in
the form of \emph{Philosophical Letters.} I have kept the same order of the
matters as in my monthly writings; but everywhere interspersed various remarks,
which run now into one, now into another science; among others also some on
various passages of Scripture, which, I hope, will not be unwelcome to those who
make either a duty
% --- p. 39 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{39}or a pleasure of searching the Holy Scripture, and of gaining gradually
greater and greater insight into important doctrines of the religion to which they
profess. I for my part have always had a pleasure in recognizing the agreement of
reason and revelation, and I have rejoiced whenever I have been able to find it.
Although the order sometimes seems to be confused, and the reader's attention
perhaps will be divided among too many things at once, so
this can yet easily be remedied, when in reading through one will set about
thinking over the one after the other in order. Everyone will excuse me herein who
considers that letter-writers are granted greater freedom than those who sit down
to write \emph{coherent textbooks.} The former can at pleasure sometimes give the
thoughts the rein, and allow them at their own discretion to stroll about in all
parts of learning; but the latter are bound to keep them gathered on the science in
hand in such a way that the reader's attention is maintained, and one always keeps
the chain of inferences with the coherence of the doctrines in view. In very few
places have I made use of proofs which consist of formal inferences of reason.
Although I know well that these are the strongest, they are yet for most people at
the same time the most disagreeable, and people of taste complain of the dryness
and coarseness of the schools when on every second line of a writing a
\emph{Consequently} meets them. Where I have used inferences of reason in their
express form, I have used them solely with the intention that I might gather my
preceding considerations into a short concept, and in few words unite all the
grounds of proof which I had explained more fully in what precedes.

\begin{intro}

% --- p. 40 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{40}4. \textsc{Johannes Boye} (1756---1830) built --- in polemic against Kant
and his school --- ethics on the fact that every force presupposes resistance, and
that it is through struggle and friction that the human will develops into purity
and nobility of mind. It is an anticipation of the Darwinian doctrine of the
struggle for life in its application to the ethical. From his remarkable book
``Statens Ven'' (1792 ff) a characteristic piece is set down here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{DEVELOPMENT THROUGH STRUGGLE}


Undeniably it is according to the procedure of nature, according to that of the
single raw human being, and according to that of states among themselves, that the
weaker is everywhere despised. Nature herself gives in all her kingdoms support to
the stronger. Life and death are disdained; and whether fewer or more shall exist
seems left to a luck of chance, which does not always know how to value. When a
trial of strength is to decide the dispute it is a trifle that a thousand weak are
sacrificed so that one stronger may gain superiority. Chance it was that gave
strength, and chance it is that gives right. Our fate under the dominion of nature
as well as of men seems almost not to be better than that of the nightingale in
the eagle's power in Hesiod. The poor creature, he tells, caught in the violent
claws, sends forth indeed through its tender throat its complaint over the wrong,
but the eagle, striving up toward the sun, hearing neither song nor sigh, devours
greedily its prey in flight; and innocent as the creation it sang of, the helpless
one ends at one stroke its life and its Creator's praise on high. Must not we
wretched, thus ill-treated humans cry with Gloster in Shakespeare: What flies are
to wanton boys, that are we to the gods. They kill us in sport!

\begin{quote}
As flies to wanton boys so are we to the Gods,\\
They kill us for their sport.
\end{quote}

In such tumult and uproar throughout all nature, what
% --- p. 41 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{41}use is it to talk of duty? of a virtue that is to be practiced by angels
without regard to consequences? What use is it to preach of virtue and forms of
virtue which man neither knew nor could yet learn from experience? --- Will man
know the world better than the Creator, or will we teach him how it ought to be?

And we must know things better, when we dare assert that there is a difference
between \emph{ought} and \emph{is.} The whole doctrine of innate forms of duty:
the doctrine of human corruption: the whole system of atonement is only complaints
against nature and her master-workman. Man in these systems insists on defects, on
misfortunes and miseries, on the great misery that weighs oppressively over the
whole world, without considering the presumptuous assertion he thereby makes, that
he, like the youngster, will know the thing better than the father: he will know
that the arrangement could be otherwise than it is. Had he been consulted when the
world was created, it would surely have become better.

It is of no use here to take refuge in an evil principle. It does not excuse the
Creator at all. If nature or man is not as he ought to be, then the Creator (whose
will is this \emph{ought)} either was unable to attain the good he desired, or he
did not know it or did not will it. Perhaps a stronger one came and played him a
nasty trick!\dots{}. But why did he begin on that whose outcome he could not
calculate? In such a case he was either short-sighted or powerless.

Better it is to take things as they are: and, instead of blaming man as an
apostate, to show what strikes everyone's eye: that nature is not at all better
than man. Better to admit that nature everywhere favors the right of the stronger,
than painfully to invent that there is an \emph{Ought} in the human brain different
from the \emph{Is} in nature. Certainly it seems disheartening when the investigator
brings home the truth that the Lord of nature gives violence and superior power his
support: For to whom shall the wronged seek, when the Sovereign himself loves
wrong? --- But when I find
% --- p. 42 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{42}this truth as it were forcing itself upon me, when I in the whole of nature,
in all annals, on the whole stage around me, find this truth written in such
glowing letters that one cannot possibly pass them unseen; shall I then with
closed eyes imagine, and make others imagine, that there is nothing at all to read?

O! what use is it to deceive oneself? Just as one is afraid of nature one hangs a
curtain of symbols between oneself and her, and sits down in this darkness and
writes theodicies that quite conflict with experience. One excludes the bodily
nature, though so closely bound to man, from the consideration of the ways of
Providence. Daily she makes our calculations false, and daily we push her
corrections from us. She shows us frequently her threats: and though the danger
hangs in the cloud above our heads, we nevertheless make the people in that holy
dimness believe there is nothing to fear, if only they atone for sin according to
the prescription. So also act the weak who shut windows and shutters in
thunderstorms, and in this darkness let a tallow candle outshine the lightning of
the Almighty. The philosopher as well as the teacher of the people is afraid for
the cause of the Almighty. To excuse it they cry Man has sinned! But has nature
also?

Yet, that neither nature nor man has sinned; that all is glorious as it is; that in
the short prelude of this life we have nothing to complain of but our own
pampering: of this we have spoken little in the first volume, and if in our
continued inquiry we can reach God's economy, the matter shall be laid before the
eyes with still greater clarity. So much of what has been adduced is already clear,
that this right of the stronger, favored by nature, not only agrees well, but is
even necessary for man's being brought up to greatness. Nature is implacable toward
sloth. It pushes aside the lazy and weak as unfit to conduct themselves there where
strife between forces is what maintains order. In the raw elements as well as in
organized classes down here, and no less between the
% --- p. 43 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{43}worlds up there, struggle and the equilibrium of forces is what mechanically
brings forth the wonderful harmony. And so long as man belongs to this world, and
human societies no less, so war and the right of violence among the half-cultivated,
and superiority of the power of spirit among the enlightened, will surely always
keep the work alive. An eternal peace will surely never be allowed to lull us into
a sleep that again would tumble us back to the cattle from whence we came. By
strife and labor man is helped forward to what he is. By strife and labor he shall
be preserved rational. If it were possible that war, and fear of war, and wakeful
interest, which preserves the nation's prosperity, could for a time cease among the
states, soon would we see voluptuousness and sloth stifle all the soul's manly
activity, and mere stupid dwarfs relieve industrious fathers. Nature rears her
children severely, but it happens in order to produce heroes. --- ---

However often man is defeated in this struggle, it is yet a blessed means to
awaken us to greatness and virtue. For virtue, which, as we have seen, consists in
the sacrifice of need, is the weapon by which the spirit-world gains victory over
the bodily world, and without strife and challenge to strife man surely would not
come to use this weapon, and perhaps he would never learn to know it. One single
simple plan has been followed by the Creator in the whole world. Strife and mutual
violence preserve and develop the bodily world. Strife and violence preserve
likewise the spirit-world: and even between these two worlds strife and violence is
the means which promotes the growth, nobility and greatness of both. As untrue as
it is that one can conquer where there is no enemy to overcome; so unreasonable is
it also that virtue and sacrifice can take place where society has no needy who
need our goodness, no offender who demands forbearance, no suffering and
opposition that can test our patience. Can the pampered one who from mother's lap
to the bed of earth is carried through the world on hands, without knowing the
% --- p. 44 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{44}harshness of the ocean and the atmosphere in nature, or the severity of
hospitals and the working class in society, can he in this cradle of ignorance
become a strong-souled enduring hero; then also can man become great and godlike in
a world where the combativeness of nature and of the soul ceases, where there is no
movement or rivalry among the peoples, but where everything in dumb and still
slumber rots away like the herbs at Lethe's bank. A water goes into putrefaction
that is not stirred by the wind: a soul that is not moved by opposition falls asleep
in its body, and even the body must in the armchair be dissolved by its eating
fluids, when it will not trouble to work itself to health. --- ---

It was this acquaintance with nature and her laws that abolished prejudices
and made man free. Man himself gained in nobility as he understood nature more
thoroughly: but this nobility too had to be won first by long and many endeavors,
by struggle with superstition and ignorance. Nor is it possible to comprehend nature
all at once. We must spell and decipher before we learn to read and interpret. For
the suddenly great man is not fitted here. Our weak soul and still weaker body
shrinks back before an unexpected specter. No mortal can bear the sight of the
Godhead, and just as little a sudden survey of the machine of nature. And even if a
mighty conviction came, and mastered the soul at once, could man suddenly lay aside
error, and unprepared get the great work of art of creation to survey from the
heights; he would be lost in the amazement; he would forget his origin and abode;
and almost insanely imagine that it already belonged to a higher sphere.

% --- p. 45 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\anthsection{II.} {STEFFENS AND GRUNDTVIG}\opage{45}

\begin{intro}

1. With the beginning of the nineteenth century the currents of Romanticism broke
in over Danish intellectual life. Imagination and feeling were emphasized in
opposition to the striving for enlightenment of the preceding period, and the need
for poetry, for religion and for science was to find satisfaction at one stroke,
not to be directed to wholly different paths. The wholeness of intellectual life
was emphasized in opposition to the critical separation of the various spiritual
domains that modern culture had brought with it. It was \textsc{Henrik}
\textsc{Steffens} (1773---1844) who in his lectures in Copenhagen
1802---1803 proclaimed the new doctrine. Of these lectures three pieces are set
down here; the first indicates Steffens's conception of philosophy and its method;
the second characterizes the romantic conception of nature; the third voices the
presentiment of the glorious primeval age that the longing of the Romantics would
call back.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. THE GOSPEL OF ROMANTICISM}


The common prejudice against philosophy, which seems, at least among the great
mass, to stand in direct proportion to the energy with which the greatest geniuses
of our time seek to bring it nearer to its perfection, compels me --- for my own
sake and for yours, gentlemen! --- to a brief preliminary declaration.

What I presuppose in my hearers is --- on the side of \textit{knowledge} ---
nothing but the general culture that must be presupposed in every educated
scientific person; on the side of \textit{interest} --- the inward need to
% --- p. 46 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{46}know the real nature of things, to resolve the riddle of existence; on the
side of \textit{capacity} --- the inward intuition that grasps every single part
together into a whole, posits the absolute whole, \textit{the One,} as the
\textit{Real,} and through this comprehensive intuition displaces every alien
representation that need not necessarily be thought in connection with the whole.
Every philosophical lecture is necessarily a continuum, an organic whole, not an
aggregate of single sentences. Everything single is understood only in its
connection with the whole. What binds and orders is the unalterably same in each
single proposition, the identical, which organizes all different objects. The
expression of this identity is thoroughgoing, absolute consistency. Therefore
philosophy, when it is what it ought to be, must become a living system, not a dead
classification. The more perfectly I should succeed in attaining the systematic
unity, the more unintelligible, paradoxical, indeed ridiculous every proposition
must seem in every other connection. The wretched expedient of tearing propositions
out of their context, of judging them singly, or in a fabricated combination ---
an expedient that the flattest triviality is always ready to use --- must always
succeed the better, the more consistent, the more perfect the philosophical system
is.

Without knowledge speculation is empty; without interest knowledge is a meaningless
aggregate of materials; without that faculty of intuition interest remains standing
at a soft longing, an indeterminate presentiment --- and philosophy itself a mere
pium desiderium for weak souls.

To lead you to the idea of philosophy's problem is the aim of these my first
lectures. I proceed from facts. I reason, I do not demonstrate. I awaken
presentiments; but these give no evidence. I will open to you a more significant
view over life and existence than that to which common experience, daily life,
hemmed in by finite need, leads us. From the deeper view over nature,
% --- p. 47 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{47}history and our inmost heart, the philosophical problem springs as from its
proper source. Everyone must give himself this problem before it can be solved in a
satisfying way. Everyone must solve his own problem, not another's.


\sectionrule

The sciences seek the infinite by a piecemeal accumulation of the finite. The
historian seeks it by an aggregate of facts, whose connection, when it is there,
again produces only something finite. But nothing in history, no single age's, no
single nation's individuality, can be fully understood except through its
necessary connection with the whole of history. An intuition of the whole must
therefore, as it seems, precede every single judgment. The investigator of nature
heaps phenomena upon phenomena; if he even attempts a theoretical connection, it is
only finite, limited. But no single phenomenon is fully understood except in the
necessary connection with \textit{all.} An intuition of the whole of nature thus
seems to have to precede every single investigation. I reserve to myself in what
follows to make this clearer and more vivid to you.

Indeed, it would be inconceivable what moved the investigator of nature to his
unceasing industry, if a presentiment, though obscure in many, of an infinite
connection were not the deep moving principle of their inquiry. This presentiment
never wholly forsakes any human being. Even in the most limited it slumbers deep in
his soul. It binds us to the whole of nature. It gives everything single a higher,
nobler significance. It is what, with the coming morning, opens the glowing, living
nature to everyone, as if an inward sun involuntarily followed that of heaven; it
lifts the infinite swarm of shapes out of the general darkness; through it the
eternal life of nature speaks, as through a mystic cipher, to our spirit, and
inwardly we understand it. It
% --- p. 48 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{48}covers the whole swarm of shapes, when the inner light, like an eternal darkness,
casts itself behind all the stars, and an alien light only makes the infinite
darkness visible to us.

This presentiment joins us likewise to history. Ages whose way of thinking, whose
outward existence had a quite different sweep from ours become comprehensible to us
through it. If we surrender to it, we resign that general postulate of the
understanding, to make our time and its way of thinking a norm for all; it bestows
on us the organs of the times that lie hidden in the past. Through it giants are
awakened from their graves, gods and goddesses wander about among us, every sound
from long-vanished times echoes back with its peculiar tone, it conjures the most
glorious ages into the most deeply sunken. It is as if the germ of all the epochs of
history slumbered in each. This presentiment, whose object is always infinite, is
called, when the living, creative reveals itself in its glory in an excellent
heart --- \textit{poetry.} No human being is wholly devoid of poetry. No age, not
even the rawest, has ever \textit{wholly} lacked it. It is as if nature's own
eternal productivity awoke with the poet. Noble, lofty shapes step forth, a divine,
golden world, quickened by an eternal sun, is opened to us, as by a sudden stroke of
magic. An infinite sense seems to have hidden behind every shape and beams mystically
toward us. We are surrounded by a lofty, glorious splendor, a deep longing awakens
in our inmost being, and joins us irresistibly to the wondrous, illusory world ---

It vanishes ---

Time holds us captive, its narrow need \textit{demands} our attention; but the
eternal longing that the poet awoke, \textit{nothing} can extinguish. What is it that
enchants us, when the poet sings?

% --- p. 49 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{B. ROMANTICISM'S CONCEPTION OF NATURE}\opage{49}

The investigators of nature, who so often felt themselves almost involuntarily
driven by an obscure presentiment of an inward connection between all the phenomena
of nature to many a step that they themselves could scarcely explain to themselves,
also assumed, although everything seems to speak against their assertion, a gradual
transition from one form to the other. Donati, and with him a great part of the
naturalists now living, imagines that the animal species pass over into one another
like the threads in a net. Bonnet and several others believed that all animals form,
by gradual transition, a linear series. Whoever can believe that merely the use that
the investigators of nature could expect from such hypotheses for the methodology of
natural description could bring them to defy even outward probability, certainly does
them wrong. It was a ground that lay far deeper. It was a presentiment of a
progressive formation running through the whole organization, a representation (that
never let itself be dismissed) of a general schema, by which nature, even under the
most apparent condition, had to be guided, that led them astray to so many false
hypotheses.

It is generally known how wholly all attempts to arrange the animals in the form of
a net or along a line have failed, and how strikingly they betray what is forced and
artificial; for what can well be more contrived and more unnatural than the
transition from flying fish to bird, from bat to the same animals, from Manis and
Dasypus to lizards? Even if one cannot deny that these animals point to the points
at which the classes \textit{approach} one another. --- But is then even the
\textit{thought} that underlies those attempts really to be rejected? Does not one
forming force run connecting through all shapes? --- One would have to try to
explain the discontinuity of the forms from a continuity in the functions
themselves. Comparative physiology was long since on the way to finding a continuity
of the organic functions.
% --- p. 50 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{50}Kielmeyer proved them, and yet it seems as if the physiologists still constantly
hesitate to make use of so glorious a discovery as their science almost against
their will forces upon them.

From our point of view the discontinuity of the forms of the higher animals is
absolutely necessary. These forms do not form a straight series, nor any net. The
latter representation, which doubtless has something right in it, holds only for
the lowest animal stages, --- holds here not only for the species but also for the
individuals; but if animalization on the lowest stages represents a net, then the
threads in this net are constantly separated more and more from one another. The
higher the animals stand, the greater and more striking is this separation, and now
there arise little by little not only different, mutually separate
\textit{genera;} but also natural classes --- like different worlds, in which nature
tries itself. Precisely because what steps forth as more significant is the
\textit{centripetal} tendency of nature, it was necessary that the outward points of
union of the classes should recede, as the forms of animalization became more
individual.

\sectionrule

Compare our physical and chemical theories with eternal nature itself --- leave the
chemical laboratories to survey the globe and its life. The sun glows at the center
of the planets, light embraces with eternal splendor all that lives, the whole globe
seems a flower that opens its many-colored petals, breathes out its life by day and
closes by night, warmth is an eternal alternation of expansion and contraction,
between which everything oscillates, magnetism has penetrated the whole globe and
points silently to a secret rule, electricity opens and closes the momentary life of
the chemical interplay, the air, constantly changing, and in all change constantly
the same, the globe's wondrous breath, is inhaled, bound, loosed again and encloses
all.
% --- p. 51 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{51}Water --- the indifferent, the formless, from which all form seems to have
arisen, circulates, ever surging, among the solid. Meanwhile the wholly
precipitated, inorganic mass (the deposited skeleton of the globe) ferments with the
last spark of life, still flaming under the equator. An eternally changing life, more
uniform under the poles, more variegated, more glowing under the equator, wells
forth with rising energy --- and in the midst of this variegated swarm there rises
little by little through all the shapes a mystic center, ever deeper and deeper
hidden in the innermost being of the shapes; as it reveals itself more clearly, the
single separates from the whole and becomes a whole in itself, the isolated
organization becomes more perfect; the energy with which it struggles against the
mass becomes mightier, the impressions it imposes on it more significant. At last,
with the most individual creature, nature's most invisible, holiest center awakens
--- reason. Even the mass is ennobled by it, and art and poetry are able to produce
shapes higher, nobler, more infinite, more significant than even nature.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{C. THE PRIMEVAL AGE AS THE IMMEDIATE \\ REVELATION OF THE ETERNAL}


With the gods all history begins. Behind all history lies mythology; that is a fact
no one can deny. Call this mythological time what one will, the fabulous, the raw,
the childhood of the human race, expressions that always prove that one makes one
time the yardstick for the other, our own time in particular the yardstick for all
the others --- this \textit{fact} stands unshakable. It is as if all history began
with the immediate emerging splendor of the eternal, as if everything lying nearest
that glorious period partook of the glory of the eternal. To reduce the ideas of the
noblest, most excellent nations concerning the Godhead to the most wretched and
% --- p. 52 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{52}flattest absurdities, to remove all higher sense from their religiosity, in order
thereafter to be able to explain their peculiar nature, may be permitted to anyone,
only not to a philosopher. To him it is, among all possible contradictions, the
hardest, that the most splendid existence could be built on a foundation of
unreason --- and no philosopher has ever doubted that the source of all existence is
religiosity. Besides, it is not my aim here to explain, merely to relate. The purity
of the narrative does not permit the admixture of any --- whatsoever
--- presupposition. We must take the oldest time as it shows \textit{itself,} not as
we believe we must understand it.

The mythological age, to which I will here briefly draw your attention, is the
ancient oriental, the mother of Greek mythology; for not to speak of the fact that
oriental mythology might contain the germ of the Nordic, which shows itself in
history only in a later period, it seems \textit{itself} --- like the higher sense of
the human race, its eye gazing with consciousness, to see from \textit{a} point movable from
region to region. Here, in the significant line in which this gleaming point moves,
we must find the deep trace of the sense of history. --- ---

That which in particular distinguished that age was the remarkable identity
of what we afterwards must separate as word and action. In living action everything
was expressed that we now seek by word, all the revolutions and developments of
nature, all the mysteries of religion. Life itself, fresh as it sprang from the
Godhead, had immediately the significance that we now only sense. Therefore all
their representations were living. No trace of the dividing death which in more
recent times has made sciences and poetry and, through them, religion itself so
monstrous. It is known that the oldest peoples represented the whole living universe
as a single animate being, which with infinitely various limbs moved and stirred in
the most manifold ways, that sacred matters keep remarkable fragments of the history
of the earth's formation, that many
% --- p. 53 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{53}myths of the oriental and Nordic primeval peoples preserve, in the most profound
images, the metamorphoses of nature, that many strange pieces of knowledge (as e.g.
in astronomy, in metallurgy), which we could not explain without deep insights,
indeed often not without acquaintance with the most artful instruments invented only
in our own times, convince us that also there, where the allegories and images are
unintelligible, something far deeper lies at the bottom of these than arbitrarily
spun-out dreamings. It is certain that the whole life of the earliest human race was
religion, that all their representations and actions had an immediate regard to the
eternal, in short: that they, as also one of the most glorious and profound Greeks
says of them, were nearest to the gods of all men. Therefore nature seems, though in
a manner inconceivable to the present physics, to have opened her holiest to them,
and many of the allegories and myths of that age seem to contain nature's own
highest oracles about its innermost being. Therefore their life itself was more
significant, all passions higher, all shapes nobler. Yes, nobler, although they
lacked the superficial glitter and meaningless trumpery of our time; our words,
which with form have lost their most proper significance and explain everything
except that which alone is worth the trouble to understand, our sciences, which have
closed nature to us, and make our existence itself senseless.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

2. According to his own testimony \textsc{N. F. S. Grundtvig} (1783
--- 1872) received from Steffens the germ of his historical-poetic conception.
The romantic harmony between religion, poetry and science did not, however, satisfy
him. Even before his actual breakthrough he was led not only to assert the
independence of religion in relation to the other spiritual domains, but even to
assert its supremacy over them. In the remarkable treatise on religion and liturgy
(1807), of which a piece is communicated here, he dissolves in this way the romantic
doctrine of the unity of intellectual
% --- p. 54 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{54}life by impressing the distinction between the spiritual powers.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{POETRY, PHILOSOPHY AND RELIGION}


\begin{center}\emph{Do we need religion}?\end{center}


This question would have to seem strange, especially to those familiar with
history, if it were not known that our time prides itself on denying what all the
past ages have confirmed, on shrinking itself down to the earth, as if it were man's
highest triumph to maintain the nullity of his existence. Now, on the other hand,
one will rightly demand it answered here.

First we will seek to bring life and its problem before contemplation, not in order
thereby to avoid the answer; but in order to put the readers at our standpoint.

We live in a valley, bounded by an unsurveyable, billowing sea. Awakened by
traditions, and driven by an inexplicable compulsion, we dimly sense that on the
other side of the sea there must be a land, with fairer plants and purer air ---
our original home. Involuntarily we stare thither, involuntarily we strive in our
breathing to catch the air that seems to stream out from that unknown. Thereby they
remain standing who, as intoxicated by the valley's grapes, or chained to this
earth, cannot see its end. Thereby we all perhaps would have remained standing, if
the waves of the sea did not gradually swallow us; but now we can no longer be
content with the indeterminate presentiment. We demand certainty. Ourselves we dare
not venture away, since we must perish by the attempt, and yet do not know whether
it is permitted us to leave the valley; but two messengers we have, \emph{Poetry},
and \emph{Philosophy}, and on them we build our hope. \emph{Poetry} soars out over
the sea, and loses itself among the clouds. Only at times we glimpse it in the
distance, and, our staring notwithstanding, we shall never discover other than that
it is surrounded by a passing splendor, which we sense to
% --- p. 55 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{55}be the effect of the rays of the fairer sun, which we place in the unknown.

\emph{Philosophy's} way is another. First it searches the valley closely, and
follows it in all its windings, in order, if possible, to find there the place
whence that presentiment issued; but is driven, after wasted effort, by
necessity --- to conclude that it must have come across the sea. With greatest care
it fits out its ship for the voyage over the deep: but scarcely a stone's throw from
land it is stopped by the cloud-high, rolling waves. Either it must raise itself
above them, and become poetry, or anchor, exposed to the storming winds, or, weary
of vain struggle, must turn back, bid us regard the valley as our home, and content
itself with giving rules for the most convenient walk on the winding road that
leads to the sea.

Either we must now again take refuge in that presentiment, which philosophy at most
only made more determinate, or we must acknowledge our need of instruction from the
other side of the sea.

\sectionrule

As soon as man has brought his faculties to the degree of development that he can
contemplate himself, there immediately meets him the wondrous riddle of existence.
He sees the other animals of the earth content with the enjoyment of the moment;
but in himself he finds a need that goes beyond the finite, without being conscious
of its object. He believes he solves the knot by declaring himself essentially
different from the other animals, and raised above them; but he only ties it
thereby the tighter, for if he will not adopt as his goal to be born, enjoy, and
die, then he is forsaken by the whole analogy of life; has torn himself out of the
only known chain, and stands as a single link, without points of contact. If he is
an everyday man, or a clever animal, then he hastens to cut the knot, turns back to
his unreasoning kinsmen, and finally makes enjoyment his highest goal. But not even
thereby has he become at one with
% --- p. 56 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{56}himself, for he has prepared for himself a new struggle against the suppressed
but never conquered presentiment of the higher, which proclaims itself in him
through what we call conscience. He can often evade the struggle, but never end it
as victor, for he must always flee from the overpowering enemy, whose attacks become
stronger and swifter, just as age makes him weaker, and more unfit for flight. At
last he is squeezed in between it and death, and throws himself trembling into the
cold arms of the latter, without daring, however, to hope there for deliverance from
the pursuing demon.

Man, who is more familiar with the presentiment of his higher nature, looks down
proudly on his slumbering brother, and reproaches him that he himself is the cause
of his anxious, tormenting dreams. He himself joins even more closely to his
presentiment, and hopes to get it, through poetry or philosophy, potentiated into
certainty. Sometimes he believes he has reached his goal, but whether it is a
disappointment or not, the following consideration shall teach.

Poetry knows well how to construct a higher existence out of the most brilliant
traits, in which man for a moment moves with intoxicating delight, but it cannot
bestow any lasting life in the same, nor attribute to existence a value of its own,
as including the condition of a higher being. If its tendency is to tear man out of
existence, it can neither solve the riddles of this, which on the contrary step
forth more strongly the nearer it comes to its goal. We know indeed that it several
times, especially in the later times, strove to attach itself to life, but we also
know that it either thereby wholly despised its nature, and became the seductive
painter of animal enjoyment, or sank under the heavy burden of existence, which it
wanted to raise with it to the eternal.

One should not expect here a detailed criticism of the philosophical systems, which
would not be in place here at all, even if we could give it; but only the
presentation of the highest point that philosophy, according to its nature, can and
dare climb!
% --- p. 57 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{57}It can lead man to consciousness of the strife of existence, and thus with
determinateness put forward the highest problem. It can motivate its assertion that
its solution must either be impossible, or lead beyond existence; but to solve it
itself, it cannot.

It can appeal to the inner experience of every developed human being, when it
asserts that our reason recognizes a higher law than that of nature; but it can
never explain the phenomenon, why that, which yet proclaims itself as the highest,
cannot appear in existence with the same necessity as this. It cannot explain the
origin of this disharmony from a harmony, any more than its return to such. Everyone
who thought for himself will find that both \emph{Fichte's}, and \emph{Schiller's},
and Schelling's attempts, by their unmistakable stamp of extraordinary genius, have
failed, and from here it is only a small step to see why, and that they necessarily
had to fail. All the solutions that philosophy permits itself of those high riddles
must either be a denial of the existence of the riddles, or rest on a hypothesis
that cannot be proved, but is sometimes so ingeniously hidden that it seems to be
the result of the inner experience itself.

\emph{Schelling} is without doubt the one among the recent philosophers who most
worthily characterized the eternal, and most consistently derived the strife of
existence from it, as merely apparent; but what does the strictest consistency help,
when the first link hangs in the air, so that we cannot approach it without poetry?
Under \emph{Schelling's} hand a glorious new creation arose, but just therefore the
inference to the present one was lacking. The assumption of the eternal, we are
tempted, misled by our need, easily to deny the name of hypothesis; but it does not
become so the less, because it is shown as necessary, when philosophy is to be able
to say something illuminating about the riddle.

Therefore we chose \emph{Schelling's} philosophy as an example, because we see in it
philosophy's confession of its
% --- p. 58 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{58}inability to explain the riddle of existence, and thus a pointing to an
unconditional need of something higher.

If we cannot attach the highest link of existence to the eternal, then knowledge of
their mutual relation must necessarily come from above, or we must do without it.
The latter we cannot, when we have raised ourselves above animality, and feel that
need which goes beyond the finite, and we must then earnestly long for the former.

If we succeeded, even if only in part, in presenting the foregoing ideas with light
and truth, then surely none of our readers will regard religion as a bond on the
unenlightened, or a support for the tottering structure of the moral system, which
is to be removed as soon as this has attained its proper firmness, but will follow
us with interest when we seek to answer the question:

\begin{center}\emph{What is religion}?\end{center}


The common definition says it is knowledge of the supersensible, and when one
assumes the existence of several religions, one must surely say so, since it seems
to be the only thing they have in common. When, however, without regard to any
definite religion, we want to state the concept as it has dimly hovered before all
founders of religions, and as it must be in order to include the determining ground
of all more cultured peoples' religions, then it cannot satisfy us. The imperfection
of the definition was necessary, since it has come about by merely logical
abstraction from a Something that without proof is assumed to be what one wanted to
define.

If it is possible to get a satisfactory answer to this important question, then it
must be sought in history, which, unconcerned about itself, discloses in its calm
course the highest and the deepest. If we now consult this, we shall find that
although its ciphers also include zeros, which, little as they lack significance for
us, yet by their as it were isolated appearance were meaningless, nevertheless
religion was generally recognized as the divine power that lifts man
% --- p. 59 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{59}above all the surroundings of the earth, to the highest, the supersensible.
Already thereby much is gained, for religion is now no longer for us a mere
knowledge, but the golden chain in Jupiter's hand, which is to lift the earth to
heaven.

If we take but one step further, we find that the history of Greeks and Jews and
Scandinavians alike loses itself in a golden age, when the gods walked on the earth,
when the heavenly and the earthly coincided in one idea. Here rests the cause of all
religions, here we get the answer: \emph{Religion is the communion of the finite
with the eternal}.

% --- p. 60 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: III without a stop (p. 45 has II.; the Contents has III.)
\anthsection{III} {THE ØRSTED BROTHERS}\opage{60}

\begin{intro}

1. While Grundtvig dissolved the romantic harmony by asserting the independence of
religion, \textsc{Hans Christian} \textsc{Ørsted} (1777---1851) took a step in the
same direction by asserting the independence of science in relation to the other
sides of intellectual life and its decisive influence on these. In his work
``Aanden i Naturen'' (1849---1850) he has in his old age collected speeches and
treatises from various times in his life, all borne by the thought that natural
science is called to bring forth significant changes in philosophy, poetry and
religion. The pieces chosen here will illuminate this conception from different
sides. The last two pieces are answers to Bishop Mynster's ``Bemærkninger'' on the
first part of ``Aanden i Naturen''.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. THE LAWS OF NATURE ARE THOUGHTS OF NATURE \\ \textit{(Fragment of a Conversation)}.}


\emph{Alfred}. --- Nature is a whole and not a piecework. The first step in our
inquiry will be to convince ourselves that the laws of nature, according to which
everything proceeds in each single object, constitute not merely a manifoldness, but
also a manifold unity, a unity, a whole. Will it not tire you, if we again go
through what we said about the Sarpfossen, in order thereby to point out this inner
necessary unity?

\emph{Sophie}. I beg you to believe of me that I am not tired or bored by important
inquiries, if only I can grasp them.

\emph{Alfred}. The fundamental thought, so far as the fundamental thought in such a
natural thing can be expressed, is a falling river. The water, constantly renewed by
the inflow, hurries down from
% --- p. 61 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{61}a great height. It obeys the same law of fall as every body, and therefore
gains incessantly a greater and greater speed in its fall; as water it has the
property that its parts easily roll over one another and are easily dispersed, and
that the freely floating parts form drops. By the ever-increasing speed those parts
whose fall began earlier gain a head start, which tends to separate them from those
following, and thereby a mighty dispersion is produced; at each impact countless
drops are sprayed up in the most various directions; there forms itself, if I may
so say, a world of drops, full of movements, which in spite of all changing
circumstances preserves a certain peculiarity. The air that must mix with the
falling water forms foam, countless air bubbles enclosed by films of water, whose
incessantly changing, uneven and white surface is as peculiar as it is well known.
The pitch of the sound that each falling part produces is determined by its height
of fall; its strength not only thereby, but also by the number of the falling parts.
The impression of all the combined sound can therefore well have some differences,
but must in the essential always remain the same. The foaming fall of its noisy
roaring bears witness to us of its crushing power, as it also shows when something
breakable comes into its gullet. This and much else that I have probably forgotten
to enumerate, and still far more things that go on in it without my knowing it,
constitute an intimately connected whole, in which every link is produced according
to laws of nature, or in other words: all the thoughts of nature therein are
inseparable from the main thought. Its distinctiveness from all other waterfalls it
gets from its peculiar natural position. The manifold variations under which it
shows itself without changing its peculiarity, rest on variations in the outer
world: changes in the direction and strength of the illumination, changes in air
movement, warmth, and the like. Thus it stands before us almost as a being with a
character of its own, that fills the imagination with the representation of a
mighty, though
% --- p. 62 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{62}unconscious giant, a slave of nature, with a firm, untameable force.

\emph{Sophie}. All this seems to me very clear; but it fills me with dread. I am
dizzier from looking down into this empty nothingness of existence that you show
me than from looking down into the deep gullet of water.

\emph{Alfred}. You will surely neither abandon yourself here nor believe that I
would abandon you in this desert.

\emph{Sophie}. Then hasten to my aid.

\emph{Alfred}. My chief aid will consist in my encouraging you to help yourself. It
was no doubt the conspicuous lack of independence of the contemplated object that
frightened you; but I beg you to consider that you, by contemplating the same
object without any scientific reflections, would have had to regard it as just as
lacking in independence.

\emph{Sophie}. I admit that you are right in this; but I fear that all other
existences would in the same way dissolve for us into mere thoughts.

\emph{Alfred}. Not into mere thoughts. It was the acting forces of nature that
presented to us a unity of thought. The ground of your misgiving lay elsewhere.

\emph{Sophie}. I believe so myself; but tell me where.

\emph{Alfred}. The unity of thought did not present itself to us as a little world
of thought closed in itself, but only as a fragment of a greater whole of thought.

\emph{Sophie}. So it seems to me to be.

\emph{Alfred}. But you must be prepared that something similar, though not in equal
degree, shows itself in every existence; for every thing that is not the whole All
itself is again only part of the greater whole.

\emph{Sophie}. I fear that the independence of things will thus vanish under our
hands after all.

\emph{Alfred}. Your fear is not wholly unfounded, and yet I do not fear on your
behalf for the continuation of our investigations.
% --- p. 63 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{63}\emph{Sophie}. You are bold on my behalf.

\emph{Alfred}. I must show the fear out of our inquiry to its proper home.

\emph{Sophie}. Where is this?

\emph{Alfred}. In existence itself. Just take any object in existence and look it
properly in the eye, and you meet everywhere dependence and perishability. This
complaint is as old as the human race, you surely know that; if there is to be
found something imperishable in things, it must not be the sensible in them.

\emph{Sophie}. I notice well that through thoughtlessness I was on the way to
demanding the impossible. Forgive!

\emph{Alfred}. I would not have spoken of it, if I had not had to fear that you
would be tempted to accuse our inquiry of making our view of existence less
powerful and living than it is in reality. We must then keep firmly in mind that
every question that is raised about something constant in things, without one's
raising oneself from the standpoint of sensible existence, leads us to a despairing
feeling of nothingness. It is then only by a consideration of reason that we can
find something constant in them.

\emph{Sophie}. But those who are not able to carry out a consideration of reason?


\emph{Alfred}. They are nevertheless sustained by the rays of rational existence
that religion sends down to them.

\emph{Sophie}. That is true. Now I am ready to go further.


\emph{Alfred}. I will now try to give a compressed presentation of the view that we
must next appropriate. That besides the fundamental forces of nature, the creating
forces, there is nothing else constant in things than the laws of nature according
to which everything in them proceeds, and that these laws of nature may rightly be
called thoughts of nature, on that we are agreed. The fundamental forces themselves
are found in all bodies; but their difference rests on the laws of nature prevailing
in them. That which gives a thing its
% --- p. 64 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{64}constant peculiarity, its essence, is then, as we have already established before,
the manifold unity of laws of nature by which it is produced and maintained; but the
laws of nature are thoughts of nature, the essence of things rests then on the
thoughts of nature that are expressed in them. In so far as something is to be a
self-coherent being, all the thoughts of nature expressed in it must belong together
in one thought of essence; which we call its idea. The essence of a thing is then
its living idea.

\emph{Sophie}. But then the essence of a thing becomes a mere thought.

\emph{Alfred}. Let us not forget that I said its living idea, by which I mean the
idea realized by the forces of nature.

\emph{Sophie}. But I have often heard and read that the idea can never be realized.

\emph{Alfred}. In the strictest meaning of the word this is also perfectly true. In
each single being the idea is found realized only in certain directions with
certain particular determinations. This happens also in art. No sculptor tries to
present in one work the idea of all humanity; but in each particular work he
presents it with a peculiar stamp. In \emph{Jupiter} with that of power and
independence, in \emph{Apollo} with that of youthful mobility and fieriness, in
\emph{Venus} with that of the allurement of love, in \emph{Minerva} with the stamp of
a vigorous thoughtfulness, but along with it in union with that of virginity. Do not
believe that by these expressions I have wanted to say anything exhaustive about
these objects; but that I have only wanted to direct attention to this, that an
idea, if you will fundamental idea, can assume manifold particular shapes, which one
can regard as the expression of as many more closely determined ideas.

\emph{Sophie}. But are the ideas then realized as perfectly in nature as in art?

\emph{Alfred}. If we take the works of all artists together as a realm of the arts,
then I may well say that the realm of nature here
% --- p. 65 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{65}does not stand back; but we will not hold too strictly to the comparison.
Nature carries out each of her ideas in countless modifications and in works whose
production proceeds through unsurveyable times; in the manifold unity of all the
whole idea is to be expressed. As a thinker forms for himself one fundamental
thought under the most various forms, as a musician does the same when he varies a
text, so nature, only in an unspeakably greater manifoldness. Each Enster\footnote[1]{The word was proposed by \emph{Eilschov} and used by \emph{Risbrigh}. It is no doubt old and was found in the word \emph{Eensterskilling}, which in the previous century was still used to denote a single shilling in silver coin.}
(individual) is such a peculiar execution of the fundamental idea of the object. But
rich nature does not confine herself to showing us executions in which the thoughts
stand as it were concluded before us: no, she shows them to us with countless
variations of relations of finitude, which a one-sided observer will call the most
conspicuous imperfection; but which \emph{he} who thinks the conception of nature brought
to the height to which it shall develop in the whole human race, must find destined
to reveal the idea of the thing in its whole fullness to a mighty, clear-seeing
spirit. But also in the present state of things, where even the greatest human
spirit has not reached so high a stage, the investigators of nature can have an
insight into this, if ever so much less profound.

\emph{Sophie}. I understand now your meaning in this respect, but allow me another
question, which occurred to me before, without my wishing therefore to interrupt the
discourse. You said that only the laws of nature constitute the distinguishing
feature of the objects; but after all that I know, great differences rest on the
substances of which the thing is formed. The rose has its scent from oil of roses,
the grape its taste from sugar and various acids, as I have been told, and you
surely know better than I countless other examples.

\emph{Alfred}. But all these substances are only compositions of more simple
substances, and the composition is produced
% --- p. 66 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{66}in accordance with the laws of nature. However, you touch here on a matter that
could lead us into inquiries whose limits our age has not yet reached. Allow me then
to point out to you that science has shown us the same constituents in highly
different plants and parts of plants, so that poisonous plants and those that serve
us for wholesome nourishment are not recognizably distinguished by the elementary
substances of which they are formed, but by the manner in which this has happened,
that is: the thoughts of nature that are realized in them.

\emph{Sophie}. Hereby this doubt seems to me removed.

\emph{Alfred}. All objects are then realized ideas, yet in such a way that each
single one expresses the idea only in a highly limited shape, whereas it is the
whole of the products of nature belonging under one idea that realize the whole idea
in all its fullness; but each idea thus realized in the finite world is again only a
member of a higher, more comprehensive one. Thus the idea of each animal species is
only a member in the idea of the whole animal kingdom; the animal kingdom's again of
the still more comprehensive idea in which both the animal and plant kingdoms are
comprised; this again a member in the idea of the terrestrial globe, which presents
to us a little world closed in itself, which yet again is a member of a still higher
system.

\emph{Herman}. But is this connection a reality and not merely a product of our own
thinking?

\emph{Alfred}. Nature herself shows it to us as her own. Our investigations into the
formation of the earth have, as we have already discussed before, shown that it has
developed in a long series of ages, and that at each new stage of development new
species of plants and new species of animals have formed, and that these in
% print: ogenlunde (misprint for nogenlunde, in the footnote)
structure and Skabelunde\footnote[1]{\emph{Skabelunde} seems to be the right word for the misshapen \emph{Skabelon}. It corresponds then to the Swedish \emph{Skaplynne}. The ending \emph{lunde} is sufficiently known in \emph{hvorlunde} \emph{nogenlunde} etc.} so resemble the products of the present
age of the earth as different executions of one fundamental thought must resemble one
another. Further it is important
% --- p. 67 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{67}to take note of the order of development. Nature has in the development of the
globe begun with the animal and plant species that stand on the lowest stages, and
has gradually in the following ages advanced to higher formations, which yet at all
earlier stages of formation constituted a less highly developed realm of creation
than that which the surface of the earth now bears. Add now to this that the higher
animal species in their embryonic state begin from lower stages of development,
related to those on which the lower animals stand, and run through from there a
series of stages of development, before they reach that to which they are destined.


\emph{Herman}. You need not conclude; I admit the weight of your reasons.

\emph{Alfred}. I then build further on what has been admitted. The terrestrial globe
is thus a member of our solar system, with which it has developed, and stands in an
unceasing reciprocal action. The idea of the globe is thus enclosed in that of the
solar system; but in the same way this is again a member in the next higher system:
this system of suns that the Milky Way shows us, and in which our artificial
instruments of sight and our inferences built on the laws of nature have let us see
as much as must remain hidden from the mere man of the senses. This system, immense
for our ordinary representations, is again only a member of a still higher one and
so on beyond all bounds. Thus the boundless extension forms an infinite whole, which
contains all ideas realized in existence; but this infinity of ideas is at the same
time comprised in one operative idea, an infinitely living reason.


\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. SCIENCE AND RELIGION}


In my book, Aanden i Naturen, I have with full conviction paid homage to the old
doctrine of the immutability of the laws of nature, and have striven to show how the
same truth, in my
% --- p. 68 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{68}opinion irrefutable, by no means leads to the denial of religion
and morality, as many through a false conception of natural necessity have let
themselves be misled to believe; but that a thorough natural science shows that
these laws are precepts of reason, springing from the divine reason itself; and that
this doctrine, rightly understood, is in the happiest harmony with true morality and
religion. The esteemed author of the Bemærkninger does not find himself convinced by
my presentation, but disputes the immutability of the laws of nature. The
fundamental objection is set forth in the following words:

\begin{smallquote}

That the laws of reason, namely --- if we may use a theological expression --- the
ad intra, the laws that constitute the essence of reason, are eternal, no one will
doubt, for reason cannot deny itself. However, a law can be wholly rational and yet
hold only for a time, since the actions, as the author says, when one under
different circumstances acts according to the same principles, must become
different. It was according to laws of nature that the animal and plant world of the
past came forth, yet it has now perished, replaced by other animals and plants,
which likewise come forth according to laws of nature, which thus are not the same
as those that nature in that antiquity obeyed. Only differently modified --- one
will say --- according to times and circumstances, for those laws were, as mutable,
not fundamental laws. Undeniably we must in the end come to immutable laws, the
fundamental laws of all existence; but the question is how high we shall have to
ascend to find laws that are independent of time and circumstances, that cannot be
changed. Yes, why does one not dare to venture the question whether the whole nature
of this world, however far even its duration extends, is not --- if I may so
say --- a temporary arrangement, which can and shall be transformed, while
presumably the reason that revealed itself therein remains the same.
\end{smallquote}

It is undeniably true that ``\emph{the actions, when one under the different
circumstances acts according to the same principles, must become
different}''; but \emph{when the principle continues to be the same, then it
has surely not been changed}! The actions are surely not the principle, but events
that happen according to the law!

% --- p. 69 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{69}Yet the matter is far too important to be left to be decided by anything that
could have even the faintest appearance of a dispute about words. As far as I
understand the author, he wanted to say that the changed circumstances were not
produced according to immutable laws, and that one must conclude from this that the
effects gradually developed were not necessary consequences of the fundamental laws.
But this opinion cannot be reconciled with \emph{that} which nature teaches us; our
investigation of it shows us that the changed circumstances are themselves
consequences of laws of nature. In the Bemærkninger an example has been chosen of
the changed circumstances by which the animals and plants produced in the various
ages of the earth's globe have received their dissimilarities. Since the reader, in
order to grasp the illumination of this example with the proper clarity, ought to
keep several examples in quite lively view, from which it is seen how the laws of
nature stand in the most intimate connection with one another, and how an
unspeakable manifoldness of singular phenomena can arise from the fact that effects
which proceed according to different laws of nature interfere mutually with one
another, I will introduce the matter by a presentation belonging here.

It is a law of nature that all bodies and all bodily parts attract one another
mutually, so that the attraction between two points therein is inversely as the
squares of the distances. The force with which the same two points attract each
other will then at 10 feet's distance be 100 times less than at 1 foot's distance.
The distance can undergo countless changes: the law remains the same. But from this
law it follows further that all bodies fall vertically toward the surface of the
earth, namely the surface the earth would have if all its unevennesses were gone,
which surface every water level shows us. If one now proves that the fall very near
a great mass of mountain deviates slightly from this, does this teach that the law
is changed? or does it not teach much rather that the deviation occurs, in
accordance with the law, through the mountain's attraction? Further we find that the
fall at the surface of the earth does not everywhere
% --- p. 70 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{70}occur with the same velocity, although it is the same mass of earth that attracts
the falling body; but does a change in the law of nature show itself here? No, but
another, equally certain law of nature intervenes, namely the law of the centrifugal
force, which itself is only a link, necessary to reason, in the manifold unity of
the laws of motion. From this law it has been calculated in advance that the
velocity of fall is the greater the nearer one comes to one of the poles of the
earth; and it must not be forgotten that one had calculated this inequality long
before one had found it in experience. I say not a deviation, but an inequality,
because it has its ground in a unity, namely a unity of law, formed, and formed with
necessity of reason, by the laws here prevailing together. The law of attraction is
further comprised in many other events of nature under many shapes. If one gives a
body a motion, then the attraction acts every moment just as much upon it as if one
had not given it this motion; but by the union of these two influences there then
arises a new velocity and path following from the laws of the imparted motion and
of the fall. Thus an oblique ascending motion imparted to the body will give it a
parabolic path. Meanwhile I here disregard in the presentation for some moments
various co-operating circumstances and in particular the resistance of the air. This
brings about a change in the form of the path, but precisely such a one as the laws
of nature of resistance demand. Even when currents of air change the path of the
thrown body, this does not happen because the laws of attraction and of motion and
of air resistance are now changed, but because an effect is added that is governed
by the laws of the air-currents. Let us now, with the same laws in view, imagine a
body that has received a motion high above the earth, outside its atmosphere, then
one can prove mathematically from the same fundamental laws that it must describe an
ellipse, whose size and deviation from the circular figure is determined by the
velocity and the distance from the earth. One can then
% --- p. 71 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{71}thus calculate this ellipse for a body that has the moon's distance, and see, it is
this path that the moon actually describes! A couple of hundred years ago the
irregularities discovered in the moon's course seemed so inconceivable that they
were compared with whims. The laws of attraction discovered by \textit{Newton} put
him in a position at once to give an account of many of these irregularities, indeed
to find out the existence of several that observation had not yet found, but later
confirmed; and now one has by the greater development of mathematics brought it so
far that one can calculate all those irregularities in advance, and so that the
calculation satisfies a far finer art of observation than that which one formerly
had.

But our thought cannot yet remain standing here. According to the same laws the
moons of the other planets move each about the planet concerned, and the earth with
all the planets about the sun. I will not here develop this more closely, but only
remark that in this whole manifoldness one finds countless inequalities in
distances, directions, velocities etc., which all follow the same laws. Science
defies the objections that ignorance now and then has advanced against it, by its
predictions; these are not few or obscure or indeterminate or only accidentally
coming true, but countless, clear, determinate with regard to time and place,
constantly coming true.


One sees here a great and yet, in comparison with its rich content, only very little
developed example, which can illuminate for us the important truth that the
contemplation of nature gives us a coherent view of the divine reason's constantly
present revelation in the finite. Here it is not a matter of hints and
presentiments, but of spiritual vision.

After this great example I can for the most part treat the following shorter ones,
and especially use them to throw light from various sides on the essence of the laws
of nature.

Chemistry says that the natural action by which iron
% --- p. 72 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{72}rusts, is a combustion. But it is surely a law of nature that combustion
produces heat; do we not meet here an exception? It only seems so; for this
combustion takes place so slowly that the heat developed in each minute is far too
slight for our measuring instruments to show it. The answer is perfectly
satisfactory; but it can nevertheless receive a support --- if I may so
say --- from outside. By chemical experiments aimed at bringing the iron rust back
into the metallic state, the metal has been obtained in a powdery form. When the
countless small surfaces of this iron dust come to touch the air, they pass over
into the state of rust, with a velocity that is many thousand times greater than
that which would take place with the coherent mass of iron; and behold! now a
powerful development of heat is not lacking either.

It is a well-known law that fire heats; but if one brings water into a glowing
crucible in which there is liquid sulfurous acid, it is transformed into ice. This
seems to him who is not familiar with science an immense exception; but the man of
science could name it as a great triumph. He knows that here effects take place that
stand under different laws: the one is that of the communication of heat, according
to which the water should get a higher degree of heat, the other is that of
evaporation, which produces cold. The liquid sulfurous acid evaporates with a
greater velocity than the vast majority of bodies, and produces here a cold so great
that the water thereby not only loses all the heat it gets from the hot crucible,
but far more, so that thereby the effect astonishing to the imagination occurs, that
the water in the midst of the fire passes over into ice. Experts will see that I
here do not go into all the closer circumstances; but they will also know that these
are not of such a nature that thereby any exception is made from the constancy of
the laws of nature.

Such a concurrence of several forces, each acting according to its law, is so far
from being a rarity that it is much rather the quite usual thing in nature;
% --- p. 73 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{73}hereby an infinite manifoldness of effects is produced; but under all this
manifoldness in time and in space it is only the effects that change, the laws of
nature according to which they occur remain the same.

It is with the thought of this that one must regard the more complicated natural
actions, e.g. plant life. The plant is nourished by certain substances, whose
circulation and chemical combinations are promoted by warmth and light. The laws
according to which warmth and light act in the plant are immutable; but the effects
thereby produced have a great manifoldness.

Warmth produces many changes in the chemical interaction of substances; and this
applies naturally also to the chemical natural actions that go on in the plant
kingdom. Both these effects and the evaporations occur through warmth and according
to definite laws of nature, which are the same in the plant kingdom as in all the
rest of nature. In the rainless sun-time of the torrid zone the evaporation that
warmth produces both in the soil and in the plants gets such a desiccating
preponderance that the chemical effect comes to lack the dissolving water by which
the nourishing substances should be carried around in the parts of the plants. When
now the chemical effects in these plants cease for a time, it does not happen
through a suspension of those chemical laws --- these remain unchanged --- but
because one of the conditions of the chemical effects had been removed in accordance
with the laws of nature of warmth. When a new season brings the needed fluid, the
interplay of the dissolved substances begins again; that even these seasons are
produced according to laws of nature, surely scarcely needs to be said. It would
lead to too great prolixity if I were here to discuss the laws according to which
light acts on the plant, according to which the carbonic acid of the air is absorbed
by the leaves, and these enrich the atmosphere with oxygen gas, and many more laws
that hold for the forces acting in plant life; I believe I have set forth enough to
show that it is not the laws of action that suffer changes, but the effects, in so
far as they meet together in accordance with the laws governing the whole.

% --- p. 74 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{74}All this preparation I found necessary before I dared to go on to the example
emphasized in the Bemærkninger; for this is taken from a branch of science which is
far from having reached the development of either the doctrine of motion or
astronomy or the observations on plant life on the surface of the earth; thereby it
would become possible that someone who saw fit to interfere in the present
discussions could use the points of contention that present themselves here to
confuse the whole matter. It is here nearest a matter only of showing how it was
possible that the numerous changes that took place during the development of the
globe could happen right through according to the same laws; for this a survey of
the doctrine of the earth's development, accepted in our time by most, will be
sufficient. I hold it to be in the main correct; but if we did not from so many other
places have such indisputable proofs of the immutability of the laws of nature, an
example taken from a less developed branch of science might easily be obscured, not
for the real investigator of nature, but for the many who rightly wish to share in
the explanations that can be given here. One will now see that it would not destroy
the aim of the following presentation, if one were to dispute that the heavenly
bodies had been in a vapor state before the liquid; that they have been in a liquid
state before the solid, is on the other hand easier to prove. If one gave up the
thought that the vapor state had gone before, one could indeed not account for the
earlier higher heat; but that there had been such a one, thus a progressing cooling
in the sequence of time, was nevertheless secured by other proofs. Even if other
influences now and then should have produced some interruption in the progressing
cooling, our development of the example at hand would retain its illuminating force,
and by relying on our surer knowledge of the immutability of the laws of nature not
lack its convincing effect. The development of the earth took place according to
laws that were never changed; but its condition suffered unceasing changes.
% --- p. 75 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{75}In the many thousands or perhaps even millions of years that preceded the
formation of the first organic bodies on the earth, it had, through a series of
condensations, passed from an immense sphere of vapor to a much more limited size,
not very unlike the present. These condensations had, in accordance with a
well-known law of nature, developed much heat, which far exceeded that which in the
same time was lost by radiation to space. When the contraction had accomplished the
greater part of its work, and the earth had almost been brought to the density it
now has, it found itself in a highly heated state. The condensing activities were
now no longer great enough to give it as much heat as it radiated; its surface
solidified, a very great part of the vapors surrounding it condensed; it became now
a sphere surrounded by a solid crust, liquid within, covered with a sea so hot that
as yet no plant, no animal could come forth in it. But the cooling continued
further; and when the surface had been brought down to a warmth that perhaps only
little exceeded that now found in the torrid zone, plants and animals began to
develop. The oldest strata, which show us these remains, teach us that it was as yet
only the least developed organic forms that were produced. In that age the
atmosphere was far differently constituted than it afterwards became; it was rich in
carbonic acid, and poor in the oxygen gas serviceable for respiration, whose basic
element for a great part was bound in the carbonic acid by chemical attraction. The
atmosphere was besides, by reason of the strong heat, filled with a great quantity
of water vapors; in its uppermost parts these vapors condensed by giving off heat to
celestial space, and had thereby to form far denser clouds than those we now know,
so that also less sunlight could penetrate through. But in accordance with the
unchanged laws of heat the cooling now continued: a very great part of the vapors
condensed hereby: the atmosphere became then clearer, so that the sunlight could act
more powerfully on the crust of the earth, and thereby produce its unequal
% --- p. 76 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{76}effects on the different parts of the surface. Under the manifold changes of
condition produced in consequence of all this, there developed constantly more
organic forms: the sunlight, so favorable to plant life, increased: the atmosphere's
great quantity of carbonic acid gave off gradually more and more its carbon for
plant nourishment, whereby its oxygen was set free as oxygen gas; and the atmosphere
became thus more fit for the respiration of animals. One comprehends from this that
each age of nature had to prepare a new one. The remains preserved in the earth show
us a series of more and more developed formations, which followed upon one another,
until at last the condition was prepared in which man and the animal and plant world
suited to man could thrive. It is probable that this occurred when the conditions of
heat had come to the point that the globe did not annually radiate more heat to
celestial space than the sun gives back to it, or that at least this equilibrium was
so nearly attained that our observations have not been able to show us any
perceptible change, and that this holds as far as our knowledge of the products of
the plant kingdom in the different countries is communicated to us by history, of
this we have a rich experience.

The thought, set forth and further developed in the passage of the Bemærkninger here
concerned, of the present world as a temporary arrangement, and a coming world of a
wholly different nature, I do not find myself called upon to discuss here; it will
suffice me if one will find with me that it stands firm that the world in which the
human race has come into being has developed and still continues to develop, in
which it has received so many revelations of the all-pervading, all-governing,
divine reason, and where reason awakens presentiments of the many mansions that are
promised us in the Father's house, that this world, I say, is governed by an eternal
reason whose mode of working is known to us as immutable laws of nature.

I am well aware that there are many who think that the conception of the world here
set forth, which indeed is old,
% --- p. 77 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{77}but yet still very little developed, leads to dreadful consequences, and threatens
to rob them of many representations in which they formerly found comfort. I do not
regard it as worthy of the matter to set forth \emph{that} which could remove this
fear, \emph{without first} reminding them that our wishes ought not to determine
what we shall accept as truth. Should we not be ashamed in our inmost being, when we
caught ourselves wanting a different truth from the real one? And what folly it
would be, if one let oneself be determined by one's wishes to adopt an opinion! Our
wishing and willing could not make it truth, after all! No, let us give truth the
honor! with it the good is indissolubly bound. The full truth brings its own comfort
with it.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{C. DEVELOPMENT FROM THE LOWER TO THE HIGHER}


In the Bemærkninger (p. 299.) it is asked: ``But why is it then a law of reason that
everything shall begin from the irrational, indeed the reason-contradicting?'' I
answer to this that I have never assumed this. The context shows on the contrary
that what is at issue is the opinion that all things in existence begin from
something undeveloped, in order to run through an unsurveyable series of
development. If one now would ask me why everything in the world begins from
something in which the rational lies only as a hidden germ, then I answer that I do
not try at all to explain why reason is such as it is: but that nature is so, I
think one will in vain deny. Each single human being begins indeed his existence
first as a wholly unconscious fetus and after birth as an unreasoning infant. If the
author of the Bemærkninger will apply this to the human race, he will hardly come
into contradiction with experience. Indeed he thinks that history should point to an
original state of perfection. On p. 300 it says:

\begin{smallquote}

% --- p. 78 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{78}``As far as history reaches, we never find that a people by its own power has
raised itself from rudeness and barbarism; but the noble germ of culture was always
brought from places where it already was, even if in the new place it found so
favorable a soil that it grew far beyond the tree from which it was taken. We can
imagine the imperfect gradually developing into greater perfection, but on the other
hand I confess that I cannot enter into the line of thought according to which it is
