assumed that life in accordance with the order of nature develops out of death,
reason out of what is contrary to reason, the good out of the evil.''
\end{smallquote}

On this I must remark that history, I do not speak of legends, does not reach back
to the peoples' first time of formation; their mutual influence on one another loses
itself in obscure times, about which we gradually learn to guess something by means
of their languages, the likeness of their legends and of their opinions; but to
their first formation we hardly attain. But when we assume that the germ of reason
lay hidden in the oldest human race as in the child, then we thereby by no means
assume that the rational is developed from the irrational, but rather that a
conscious reason is developed from something as yet unconscious of itself. In the
oldest human race there was, according to this way of conceiving, surely no foreign,
more developed reason present that could begin the education; but the assertion that
this should be a necessary condition seems to me unproved. It follows from the laws
of nature that man's capacity for reason must be developed by the reciprocal action
with the surrounding world; when through the eye he receives an impression of an
object, he will like the child stretch out his arm toward it; after manifold trials,
the memory of the visual impressions, of the figures and distances found by feeling,
will imprint itself in his memory, and his capacity for reason will produce thoughts
thereupon. The different animals will each produce a particular impression on him;
from the same kind of animal he must receive the same kind of impression, from the
different on the other hand unlike. His memory preserves these impressions, his
capacity for reason works them up. The same holds naturally for all kinds of
% --- p. 79 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{79}other objects: plants, stones etc. Among men the more highly gifted will first
develop, and then work upon the others. Man will by his natural impulse be led to
produce sound on occasion of certain impressions. Even in animals this happens; but
man will in virtue of his higher dispositions distinguish more finely between them,
store them more perfectly in his memory, and use them to make known his feelings and
thoughts. How many generations of men it lasted before from this there arose a
reasonably comprehensive language, need not be decided here; it is enough that the
beginning of the way is seen. If we now consider what has happened in the historical
time, it may perhaps be impossible for us to find a people that has had nothing else
for a teacher; but no one will deny that there are countless examples that men have
found out new truths and everywhere made new spiritual progress. This points to the
truth that such must also have happened before the historical time, and agrees then
perfectly with what we have seen, that the human dispositions need only the
influence of the whole general rational arrangement grounded in the Godhead in order
to be developed.

Theologians have in general been very inclined to assume that nature itself had
become worse through man's first sin; but this opinion cannot at all be reconciled
with our definite insights. It is certain that the laws of nature were the same, that
matter had the same properties, that living beings were subject to suffering and
death, before man came into being. I have said things belonging here in several
places in my book here treated, and particularly pp. 127 and 128, but in the greatest
brevity, since I regarded it as an admitted matter; but now I feel called upon to
state that our numerous investigations into the inner structure of the globe and its
laws of development have shown that, long before man came on the earth, many great
and shattering changes have taken place, in which whole animal species, indeed even
animal genera
% --- p. 80 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{80}perished, that many animals also in those times devoured others, indeed in the
bones of animals of the former world distinct traces of disease have been found. So
evident proofs one has that bodily evil, destruction, sickness and death are older
than the Fall! In so far as any Bible passage might seem to conflict with this, it
will no doubt by a right interpretation be brought out of this conflict; but if the
opposite were the case, which I do not at all believe, one would have to let such
passages stand as unelucidated obscurities until higher insight was gained. I submit
to the dogmaticians to consider how far the doctrine of their science on sinfulness
in all ways must be regarded as indisputably correct, or could gain by a new
treatment.

\begin{intro}

2. \textsc{Anders Sandøe Ørsted} (1778---1860), the great jurist, was in his youth
strongly influenced by Kant and Fichte and acknowledged even in his old age the
lasting significance of this influence. But both as jurist and as philosopher he
soon came to lay greater weight on experience, especially on the great differences
between individualities, than the German philosophers were accustomed to. He was
strengthened in this by a treatise by Treschow, of which a piece is to be quoted
further below. The conception of ethics to which A. S. Ørsted was thus led he has
expressed in a treatise ``Om Grændserne mellem Theorie og Praxis i Sædelæren''
(read in the Scandinavian Literary Society 1811), of which two pieces are
communicated here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. THE ETHICAL SIGNIFICANCE OF INDIVIDUALITY}


It lies in the fundamental nature of the human faculty of cognition that it cannot
embrace the whole reality immediately, but only mediately, by means of concepts, and
that these will in all eternity not penetrate and exhaust reality.
% --- p. 81 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{81}Nature consists of an infinite series of objects, of which none is perfectly like
the other, but between which also never any abrupt transition takes place from one
opposition to another, whereas they, by infinitely small differences of degree,
approach one another. This infinite richness, infinitely great and infinitely small
difference, we become aware of, not only when we turn our gaze to the great whole,
but even when we follow a single, however insignificant, object in its different
parts. Reason also sees that the absolute unity, which is the root of all existence,
cannot reveal itself otherwise than as something in all respects infinite, just as
we also comprehend that, since all objects of nature are products of the same
forces, only distributed in a different way, nature cannot be otherwise constituted
than stated above. But how should the finite understanding be able to grasp this
infinity, in its whole fullness? Besides that we, by reason, see \emph{in general},
that it must spring from a unity, we can do nothing else than grasp some of the most
striking points, setting all that lies within two such points as alike, and all that
lies on the other side of the same as essentially different therefrom,
notwithstanding that from the one to the other of these two boundary points there
can be a greater distance than between the one, and what, on that side of it, lies
nearest it. The concepts thus formed of the different kinds of objects must
therefore necessarily be insufficient to distinguish things that lie at or near the
points of transition. Investigators of nature also admit that even the greatest and
most striking differences in nature, e.g. between organic and inorganic beings,
between animal and plant, melt together so in the points of transition that at these
one is not in a position to determine with reliability to which class the object is
to be referred.


If it were possible to break through that boundary,
% --- p. 82 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{82}if we were able to dissolve reality into the concept, then this opposition would
fall away immediately thereby, and with it also the opposition between outer and
inner, between possibility, reality and necessity, as well as all other forms of
finitude. We would then come into possession of an infinite knowledge, and thereby
our whole being would become infinite, or we would become, each for himself, God
himself.

This confirms itself thus, what one of the greatest investigators of all times, with
his usual great and deep gaze into the realm of truth, but as it seems to me without
a wholly satisfactory development, has taught\footnote[1]{See Kants Kritik der reinen Vernunft, 4te Auflage S. 755---760.}, that outside mathematics,
which deals with concepts that it itself creates (by a pure construction), no proper
definition, or sharp determination of boundaries for concepts, can be given, neither
in the sphere of pure philosophy nor in that of experience. This truth it would not
be difficult further to support by a comprehensive induction, in that one sought out
from all branches of knowledge some of the most recognized supposed definitions of
their most essential concepts, and showed that they, however serviceable they may be
to give preliminary and partial concepts of their objects, are yet far from
exhausting and precisely delimiting these. But to carry this out here would be too
far from the aim we have set ourselves. It is certain and known to everyone versed
in science how almost always he who with his own examination lectures on any science
whatever, dissatisfied with the definitions his predecessors have given of its
fundamental concepts, himself attempts new ones, by which his successors yet are
just as little satisfied.

Our purpose at present is, partly by means of what has already been developed about
the human faculty of cognition in general, to cast a light on the science that is to
teach us to arrange our life as our rational vocation demands. The foremost teachers
of this science will have
% --- p. 83 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{83}it that it shall draw such a sharp boundary line between the commanded and the
forbidden that one, provided with right insight into its principles, and animated by
good will, needs no other compass in order always to find the right way in the
bewildering labyrinth of life. Those who are not content with the present state of
the science, but believe that it has taken a wholly wrong flight, ground their
discontent chiefly on this, that it does not satisfy that demand. This is especially
the case with the newest and most important work of this tendency:
\emph{Schleiermacher's} Grundlinien einer Kritik der bisherigen Sittenlehre. Berlin
1803. In this work, elaborated with rare learning and dialectical strength, this
demand is stretched to the very highest, and pursued with such strictness that the
author, chiefly on account of it, denies nearly all that has hitherto been worked
out in ethics all value. \emph{The Stoics}, \emph{Kant} and \emph{Fichte} have,
according to his judgment, known as little how to build up an ethical system as the
\emph{Eudaemonists}. Only the two, \emph{Plato} and \emph{Spinoza}, receive a milder
judgment, not without perceptible influence of partiality.

I venture on the contrary to assert that no human science is in a position to draw
that sharp boundary line, and what has been stated above will come to my aid in the
proof of this assertion.

Undeniably every rule for rational conduct dissolves into the fundamental law that
we ought to make our whole life an imprint of the eternal reason which reveals
itself in us. Reason presents itself, however, in every human individual not merely
as \emph{reason in general, but in a certain determinate}, this individual's
alone own, and his being as rational individual determining shape. This reveals
itself already, in a manner visible even to the dullest eye, in the outer, which,
according to the idea of a nature, cannot but stand in necessary connection with the
inner, although a veil, of which only small parts can be
% --- p. 84 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{84}drawn aside, conceals the more determinate nature of this connection. That
peculiar shape which reason has assumed in each person in particular is indelible;
for it belongs to the necessary conditions under which the eternal can present
itself in time; could he strip himself of this peculiar dress, he would no longer be
a single individual in whom reason dwells, but the pure absolute, substantial reason
itself. Whoever seeks to destroy this his peculiarity, striving to transform himself
into an abstract rational being, he is at bottom working to uproot
life's root, and making himself an empty, marrowless shadow-image. It is thus an
eternal law for the activity of rational beings that they cannot deliver their
contributions to the execution of the common plan of reason otherwise than each in
the peculiar form of his rational nature. It is also easily seen that the whole
force of reason, which, in its revelation to finite beings, has scattered itself in
a countless multitude of single individuals, can present itself in its totality only
thereby, that each develops the lot of the force of reason allotted him to the
highest degree of perfection\footnote[1]{One of this Society's most honored members, who is at the same time one of the fatherland's most thorough thinkers, Professor \emph{Treschow}, has, in a treatise inserted in the Writings of the Royal Danish Society of Sciences for 1807, which has as its title: Is There Any Concept or Any Idea of Singular Things, etc.?, developed with insight the essence of individuality, and with force pronounced its rights.}.
But from this it follows that every single rational being has a double ideal or
model, according to which he has to form himself, the one determined by the basic
form of all force of reason dwelling in a finite being, the other determined by the
peculiar form in which reason reveals itself through him. The first he has in common
with all others, the latter on the other hand gives him a particular calling of
reason, that no other has in common with him, although he, by following it,
intervenes, as a necessary link, in the great chain of rational beings, which, taken
together, present the one, eternal reason. There
% --- p. 85 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{85}is thus, besides the general ethics, a particular one for each single individual.
It goes with our moral nature in this respect in exactly the same way as with the
physical. There is something which, on account of the general laws of the organism,
must have a beneficent or disturbing effect on every organism, and consequently on
each single one. On the other hand there is here also much whose influence is not
determined by the general laws of the human organism, but rests on the particular
constitution of this in this or that individual. Besides the general rules of health
for all, there are consequently also particular ones, which have their origin in the
peculiarity of each one's organism. So also with regard to man's moral nature. There
is a certain activity which the rational nature common to us all either demands or
rejects, but there is also much which each one's peculiar character of reason
declares either as contradicting the same or as promoting its proper development.

This peculiarity of each single rational being comes especially into consideration in
the very important part of one's morality which consists in the choice of the
particular part of the great realm of reason to whose cultivation one chiefly
sacrifices one's life and strength, as well as in the manner in which one works in
this one's sphere. This peculiarity is especially prominent in the more eminent
natures who are called, by science or art, to give the world of ideas new clarity,
fullness and force, or by statecraft and state administration to give the ideas a
more perfect imprint in the arrangement of social relations. Such heroes of humanity
are incessantly occupied with producing something new, of which even the smallest
parts bear a mark, recognizable to every connoisseur, of the author's peculiarity,
but naturally of a peculiarity that, in the manner determined by the fundamental laws
of finitude, strives toward the infinite. But also outside the actions that stand in
relation to each one's particular sphere of activity, the duties, especially those
that demand a
% --- p. 86 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{86}positive activity, receive manifold modifications from the subject's
peculiarity. I will merely mention man's dutiful conduct in opposing follies and
wickednesses. Whether gentle mildness or crushing force ought to be used depends
indeed, for a great, indeed perhaps for the greatest part, on the nature of the
object dealt with, and he who would act dutifully to check evil ought to procure for
himself a clear knowledge of what this demands and endures, and then arrange his
measures accordingly; but much also depends, although the object dealt with is the
same, on the character of the subject dealing with it; for if he acts in a strange
character, he necessarily becomes inconsistent and accomplishes nothing. If the
gentle man will take upon himself the role of the bold, and this one that of the
former, they can both be sure to speak and act in vain.

Only the spiritually blind could hit upon the idea that those expressions of
individuality in human life were not significant enough to stand under the moral
law. One would then have to assume that it was the beneficial or harmful influence of
actions on society that stamped them as morally good or morally bad, and even then
one must have a very short outlook to make that assertion. But should it still be
necessary to show how infinitely superficial this way of representing is? The
concepts of usefulness and harmfulness have the most evident stamp of relativity.
They indicate nothing that immediately and for its own sake ought to be chosen or
avoided, but something that stands as means to another, which is regarded as a good
or evil. They can thus not serve as the measure for the original and proper good and
evil, but await, when rightly understood, their determinateness first from the
preceding development of certain higher concepts. If there is now no other reality
than the fleeting one that is in time, and thus that which maintains our sensible
existence in the few moments that are granted us, in the most pleasant or at least
least unpleasant way, is
% --- p. 87 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{87}the only and most real good, then those expressions of each one's peculiar force
of reason may perhaps be regarded as less significant. But if we, seeing that that
which is only in time has properly and in itself no existence, acknowledge that
there is something eternal and constant, and that sensible reality is only the
reflection of this, and only as such a reflection has worth and significance, then
that which brings us into kinship with this eternal must be the true good, just as
that which removes us from it the true evil; and then it must be one's noblest
endeavor that each faithfully develops and presents the eternal in the form in which
it will be presented through him.

With more show, though not with better ground, one could object that all that thus
flows from each one's peculiar character follows therefrom by the laws of necessity,
and thus lies outside the sphere of freedom and morality. But our opinion is by no
means that each shall follow his individual nature as he once finds it. To make this
the rule for his actions would be an absolute lack of all morality. But we ought to
search out the basic form of our rational individuality, fathom what idea it is that
the Eternal One has sought preeminently to reveal through us, and how He has willed
to reveal it; and the calling of reason thus found out we shall remain true to, but
all the impure and foreign constituents that we have taken up into our being we have
to separate and cast off. For this much exertion and self-struggle is surely
demanded, namely struggle with our lower self, not with the higher, spiritual self,
which is destined to rule not to be subdued. Nothing is indeed more common than that
men, even those who are preeminently endowed by nature, from laziness, vanity or
self-interest, neglect their proper calling of reason, or reach for an apparently
greater goal than that to which their nature pointed. When we had once become
finished with that purification, then indeed all struggle was over, and what then
remained of our being was a pure and
% --- p. 88 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{88}unsullied source, from which our moral life, without further trouble and care,
would well forth. But so it is everywhere with morality. When we had once, by
freedom, soared up to the summit of morality, so that alone the will to be true to
the plan of reason remained with us, and determined each of our movements, the moral
law would become a law of nature, and rule over us with absolute necessity, so that
there could no longer be talk of choice, struggle or self-mastery. But a perfect
morality, without all possibility of relapse, the finite rational being can only
strive after in the infinite, but never take possession of; for the force that
desires something other than what the law demands can indeed in the infinite be
weakened and subordinated, but never wholly annihilated or subdued.

We do indeed see now, as stated, \emph{in general}, that the eternal and absolute
reason must reveal itself in a countless multitude of different individuals, each of
which has its own peculiarity, without any individual ever being able either to melt
together with another, or with the absolute reason itself, and that the Eternal
One's general plan with all rational beings must receive manifold modifications and
closer determinations through this peculiarity of each one. Meanwhile we are,
however, in no way in a position to construct a \textit{priori} any real
individuality, or to sketch the course of life determined thereby, for we, who are
bound by the limitations of individuality, cannot penetrate into the absolute reason
itself, which would be necessary if we were to be in a position to derive the
individualities from this. Could we bring it to this insight, the individualities
would no longer show themselves to us as something real, as they necessarily must
for the finite understanding, but only as phenomena, as revelations of the absolute
reason. Indeed it is not even possible for us to refer our own or any other
determinate individuality, given by observation, to a concept; for every
individuality contains an
% --- p. 89 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{89}infinity. However long I go on adding mark to mark, I still have no individual,
for of all these marks it is conceivable that they are found in several, but it is
impossible that one can have the other's whole individuality. The differences
between the different individuals are besides more \emph{quantitative} than
\emph{qualitative}, for every being provided with the complete human organization
bears all human dispositions within it, and only the infinitely different degree in
which one possesses this or that disposition makes the difference. But how should
one by concepts weigh or measure magnitudes for which we have neither weight nor
measure? The logicians had therefore rightly set concepts and individuals against
one another.

Thus individuality sets an insurmountable boundary to our reason's striving, as well
in the domains of nature as of freedom, to see through the connection between unity
and manifoldness. What the moral theory can teach about the duties of individuality
consists chiefly in what has been developed above, which concerns only individuality
in general. Yet it might perhaps, supported by a deeper anthropology, go somewhat
further. As certain as it is, namely, that every man's rational nature has its
particular peculiarity which he shares with no other, and that this peculiarity is
not exhausted by any concept, so certain it is nevertheless at the same time that
these different peculiarities, which, as grounded in the eternal reason, taken
together must form one whole, do not separate from one another by leaps, but that
several individualities, besides what is common to all rational individuals, still
have more or less in which they in particular agree. When those of these points of
agreement that have the most important consequences for the system of the actions
that reason demands performed by each, are grasped with a sharp and penetrating
glance, there can be produced a doctrine of the different circle of duties
determined by the different dispositions, in which, to be sure, no
% --- p. 90 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{90}individual can read his way to his whole vocation (for those general
propositions must naturally be more closely determined by each one's real
individuality) but which yet can be of excellent guidance. Further: Nature has set
great masses of men with uniform basic traits in such connection with one another
that each of them, in order to use his force expediently and with the greatest
result, must work with and upon the others to whom he is set in this relation. Every
people, naturally not the multitude of men whom an arbitrary policy has bound into
one body, but what has been made so by nature, has its determinate peculiarity,
already announcing itself in the physiognomies, just as well as each single
individual, and again several peoples stand in such a natural kinship that they in
turn form a greater whole. When one thoroughly knows this one people's nationality,
then one sees what part of the common plan of reason the Eternal One has in
particular wanted to entrust to this people, and when one has further fathomed the
mutual kinship of the nations, one knows which nations are destined to join
themselves to one another, in order, by common application of force, to co-operate
in a greater part of the realization of the plan of reason than that which has
fallen to each one's separate lot. For each single individual, who is not destined
merely to work in the forms once introduced, but who, with independent force, will
work for the higher aims of humanity, this must have the most important consequences
for his course of life, not only because the peculiarity of the nation of which he is
a limb has influence on his own, but also because it is with and upon this nation he
is to work immediately, in order to work mediately with and upon the whole
brotherhood of man.


% --- p. 91 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{B. ETHICS AND EXPERIENCE}\opage{91}

From all that has been set forth I believe I am entitled to draw the conclusion that
the concepts and formulas which the theory of morals is able to supply are not
sufficient to solve all the tasks which the manifold complications of life bring with
them. Hereby the value of science's endeavors to find out the highest unity for all
moral commands, and to bring the formulas derived therefrom as near to the
individual conditions of life as possible, is by no means diminished. Only that the
goal which ethical science thus sets itself, just as well as that which is assigned
to our moral life itself, is an ideal, whose complete attainment is in all eternity
impossible. Of the highest importance it will always be to bring the fundamental
idea of morality to the greatest possible clarity, and to procure for ourselves the
most complete insight, our ability permits, into what flows from this idea, when it
is combined with what investigation and experience teach us about human nature in
general. Often this insight will immediately teach us whether something, in the given
case, ought to be done or left undone. But with this one must, if it is to give any
real result for the rational conduct of life as a whole, combine undaunted and
imperishable striving to know ourselves and our particular vocation of reason, as
well as the world and the conditions in society wherein we have to exercise our moral
force. In particular we must seek to sharpen our spiritual aim, to develop our tact
for the apprehension of the real conditions and their right reference to the idea of
morality. Without procuring for ourselves skill in this, we shall not be in a
position, in the right manner and to the due degree, to fulfill any moral
\emph{command}; and even the moral \emph{prohibitions} we shall, at the boundaries of
the forbidden kind of actions, often not be able to understand or apply. The moral
life is thus not merely \emph{science}, but also \emph{art}. What science
% --- p. 92 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{92}can do, in order to form this art, is among other things to supply a purposeful
elaboration of moral casuistry, whereby moral tact is guided in judging the cases
that lie on the boundaries of the ethical concepts and formulas. Kant has in later
times surprised by the casuistical questions which he has placed at the treatment of
every duty. One must admit that it is, according to his doctrine of the
unconditional duties, and especially with regard to the express utterance in
Tugendlehre S. 56, strange also at these to find casuistical questions; just as his
casuistry not seldom contains less significant tasks. But in itself, and according to
the way of representing here developed, moral \emph{casuistry} will serve as guidance
where moral dogmatics gives out.

Moreover, even provided with the most thorough ethical knowledge, the most lively and
cultivated ethical tact, and the best insights into the nature of the conditions of
life whose treatment falls within our sphere of action, we shall often, where moral
calls of opposite kinds meet, be in uncertainty as to what ought to be chosen. In
this uncertainty we have to act according to the most reasonable insight we know how
to procure, and must then comfort ourselves with the thought that, even if we do not
hit upon the right, it has yet not been lacking in us of honest and active will to
procure the best knowledge, and to perform the duty recognized. An absolute certainty
in our acting we cannot, in the relative sphere in which we have to move, expect,
because for this would be required an absolute certainty in our whole knowledge of
God, men and the world, on which knowledge the correctness of our acting is
conditioned.

To be sure some inquirers into wisdom speak of an unerring tact, excluding all doubt
and wavering as well as all error, which in every given case is to tell us whether
our judgment on what we have to do in it is right or not. Kant and \emph{Fichte}, not
to mention the adherents of the English school, speak especially, with much
enthusiasm and energy, about
% --- p. 93 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{93}this infallible tact, under the name of conscience, and reject all probabilism,
by which one determines oneself in moral matters, as absolute
% print: Vernuft (misprint for Vernunft, in the footnote)
unconscientiousness\footnote[1]{See \emph{Kants} Religion innerhalb die Grenzen der blossen Vernunft, p. 287 ff., \emph{Fichtes} Sittenlehre S. 214 ff.}. But however much I have been carried away by the moral force and boldness that express
themselves in what they have said on this, it can yet by no means convince me.

The existence of that feeling they assume because they regard it as necessary if a
moral action is to be possible, since it is unscrupulous to undertake anything of
whose dutifulness one is uncertain \textit{(qvod dubitas, ne facias).} \emph{Fichte}
seeks at the same time to prove the possibility of the feeling thus postulated by the
moral law, whose infallibility, according to him, rests on this, that it indicates a
perfect agreement between the real and the original I, and thus is grounded in the
consciousness that is judge over all other conviction, and is not itself subject to
any higher judgment.

In so far as those philosophers postulate an infallible conscience, as necessary
condition for the moral conduct of life, they presuppose that a perfect moral acting
is man's vocation. As soon as one assumes that an eternal striving to find the truth
and to act in the truth is all that can be demanded of man, then it may very well
hold with this that we often, notwithstanding the most honest search, do not find the
true; indeed that we, at bottom, can never attain perfect certainty about with what
undertaking we can most worthily and with greatest moral benefit fill a given time,
because for this there would be required a complete survey of life, its significance
and conditions. That we, although we are conscious of the imperfection in our moral
insight, nevertheless venture to act, is necessary; for otherwise the thought that
there might possibly still be something better to accomplish would never permit us to
carry out any actual call, and we should thus spend our life in an eternal
inactivity, which surely
% --- p. 94 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{94}would be most immoral. When those ethicists advise us to postpone acting until we
have attained perfect conviction of the dutifulness of the action in question, then
there betrays itself here the fundamental trait of their system, otherwise not
concealed either, that it is more negative than positive, that it presses more toward
the omission of what is forbidden by the law than toward the carrying out of what is
demanded. But the moral law is not satisfied by a mere non-acting, it demands that
there shall be action for the furtherance of the plan of reason. Besides, every
moment of time in which the moral does not happen is indeed filled by another acting
which, if not in itself, yet with regard to the morally necessary left undone
thereby, is contrary to the law, and thus there is contained, strictly speaking, in
every omission of the morally demanded also a positive acting which the law
disapproves. The command not to undertake any action without perfect certainty that
it is the right one must thus be mitigated to this, that we have to procure for
ourselves the best possible insight that lies in our power. If we look to actual
life, we shall also easily find a multitude of cases where it is obvious that one
cannot postpone one's decision until one has become free of every doubt. This holds
not merely where it is a question of directing the great human and national affairs;
even in the most insignificant life such cases meet frequently.

If one next considers more closely the mark that Fichte has given of the evidence by
which one in moral matters, without fear or danger of error, can let it rest, one
will not find oneself satisfied thereby either. Where it is a question of the moral
principles according to which I in general have to arrange my life, I shall be able
to refer them to the basic source of all conviction, and thus procure for myself a
clear feeling, admitting no doubt, that it is truth I possess, and yet the guarantee
for this feeling's objective infallibility, which, according to \emph{Fichte}, is to
lie in the feeling itself, is hardly so wholly
% --- p. 95 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{95}to be believed. But where it is a question of a determinate moral call occurring in
actual life; where the premises for the judgment by which I have to resolve on a
certain action rest on a right apprehension and combination of complicated
conditions of life; where what one assumes about a certain fact and its motives must
determine what ought to be done or left undone; where it is necessary, in order to
act rightly, to reckon the future, to foresee the physical or psychical consequences
of a certain action; there it will be impossible to put one's judgment on what, in
the given case, ought to happen in connection with the truths that have an absolute
necessity and clarity with them; for the connection of the single with the absolute
can never be fully fathomed. How one can thus admit that man, with the best will,
may err, where it is a question of setting up rules in general for the moral life,
but yet assert that all error about what, in a given case, ought to happen, is
self-incurred, is inconceivable. Indeed there is even contained in it no small
self-contradiction; for, when the ethicist presents a system for human actions, this
is no doubt also an activity that stands under the moral law; and conscience would
thus have to be, in this undertaking, as in every other, an infallible guide, so that
he would teach the perfect truth and in the most perfect manner.

Still I must, before I leave \emph{Kant's} and \emph{Fichte's} doctrine of conscience
as an infallible judge over our actions, touch on the example they have both used by
way of illustration. The question is namely: whether he who pronounces sentence of
death upon a heretic can be excused by an erring conscience, or whether one can
straightforwardly charge him with unconscientiousness; and it is asserted that one
must necessarily assume the latter, since the duty that in general forbids depriving
a man of life is certain, but the historical system of faith that is to justify
condemning the supposed heretic from life can never have the inner
% --- p. 96 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{96}necessity with it, that excludes the possibility of error. The most zealous
adherent of such a system of faith would also, think those philosophers, be easily
able to see the injustice of condemning the one who thinks differently, if he laid
before himself the question: whether he dared to venture to confirm the truth of his
system, under eternal loss of all that was dear and precious to him? at which
question he would surely draw back. That every religious enthusiast would really be
frightened back by this question, one cannot but call in doubt, since history shows
us so many examples of martyrs who, with victorious joy, have received the most
agonizing death, grounding their firm hope of rich compensation for this sacrifice
solely and alone on the truth of the faith for which they gave up their lives. On the
other hand the calmer and more sober man would hardly let himself in for that
dreadful condition, even where it was a matter of confirming what he teaches as
truth of reason, but still less where it is a question of the factual presuppositions
he lays at the ground of his actions. Let one think of the example that lies nearest
to that. Can one in general demand that the judge who finds anyone guilty of a crime
shall be willing to stake his eternal salvation on this, that the convicted has
really committed the misdeed attributed to him, and that this is imputable to him in
the degree the sentence assumes? I think that such a demand would dissolve all
administration of justice; for what sober man would indeed on the correctness of his
conviction about a fact, and that about so isolated and secretly committed a fact as
it ordinarily is, which falls under the cognizance of the criminal tribunals, submit
himself to eternal exclusion from communion with God. That what he declares true is
his real conviction, he ought surely to be able to swear, but an oath that this
conviction is also correct would be impudent levity. If now, however, the existence
and maintenance of a penal legislation is a necessary condition for the rational
% --- p. 97 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{97}arrangement of human society, thus a moral demand, then the moral law cannot but
permit that the courts, in judging facts, content themselves with such a degree of
certainty as the nature of these makes possible.

Our moral tact is thus not sufficient either to remedy the incompleteness in our
moral knowledge. From all uncertainty and error in our moral affairs it is thus not
possible for us to free ourselves. From this arises the great deal that in life
appears to us as indifferent, and which is carried out by us without the least
thought of the moral law. That there is not in itself any indifferent action is as
clear as day; for every event, however slight, must, in its great connection with
the whole, contribute to furthering or holding back the completion of the plan of
reason. Meanwhile the circle of the actions which we, in life, do not know how to put
in any connection with our moral vocation, is far greater than that of the deeds we
think of as fulfillment or transgression of a moral command; for we cannot survey or
fathom every action in its infinitely many relations, and must even, for a great
part, give up all striving thereafter, because it would fill the time that is already
demanded by other more important and more fruitful moral endeavors.

What has hitherto been shown about the incompleteness of ethical science and its
inadequacy to solve its great task, one would perhaps weaken by separating from
ethics proper the technics of duties, which would teach us the rules of art that
ought to be observed in the execution of the aims commanded by duty. That one, in
presenting the whole of ethics, does not enter into the technical execution of the
particular aims of duty, I find just as natural as that one does not in universal
history present all the particular events, perhaps important for a single state, or
single family, or for a single art or science; but one cannot therefore deny that the
sciences that teach us rightly to execute a moral aim, such as jurisprudence and
pedagogics, belong to
% --- p. 98 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{98}ethics in its whole extent, and in particular are parts of applied ethics. But
however one may think about this, it becomes, in all circumstances, clear that the
prudence that finds out the best means for the right execution of the moral aims is
itself a part of the dutiful relation, and that ethics, when it does not concern
itself with the rules of this prudence, by no means completes the system of the
actions improved by reason.

When we, moreover, on the one side, find in us a need, grounded in our rational
nature, to derive all that is manifold in life as well as in nature from its highest
unity, and therefore unwillingly acknowledge the distance, which we have found
ourselves compelled to assume, between theory and practice in ethics, then it cannot,
on the other side, be denied that there is also an equally indelible basic trait in
our nature, which abhors squeezing the whole of human life and its movements under
standing formulas, excluding all peculiar and free treatment of the conditions that
meet us. These two conflicting drives fit also well together in a being whose
vocation is an eternal progressing development, not a fixed possession.

% --- p. 99 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: IV without a stop (as p. 60 III; the Contents has IV.)
\anthsection{IV} {THE PSYCHOLOGICAL SCHOOL (TRESCHOW, SIBBERN, HOWITZ).}\opage{99}

\begin{intro}

1. The interest in experience, and in the problems that arise on the ground
of experience, by which Holberg and Sneedorff had been animated, found voice
again in a series of men who in the nineteenth century cultivated psychology
and sought partly to bring it into close connection with natural science,
partly to treat more far-reaching philosophical questions from standpoints of
experience. The first in the series is \textsc{Niels Treschow} (1751---1833),
author of the first independent Danish psychology. In his philosophy he
maintained --- in opposition to German philosophy's absorption in general
and abstract concepts --- the individual and the concrete as what is properly
real (``Gives der noget Begreb eller nogen Ide om enslige Ting?'' Videnskabernes
Selskabs Skrifter 1807), and it was therefore natural for him to suppose that the
differences between the species had developed historically and successively. The
``moral'' misgivings about this supposition he has refuted in a fine manner
(Elementer til Historiens Filosofi 1811).
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. THE INEXHAUSTIBILITY OF THE INDIVIDUAL}


Before I proceed to the investigation of the question here proposed, it is
necessary to explain its meaning and likewise its importance. By concept
I understand, with many recent philosophers, more than the mere representation.
The latter may be sensuous, drawn from immediate experience,
which comprehends only the surface of things, not what is inward and abiding
in them. The objects of concepts are, on the contrary, the essential and
unchangeable properties of things; and concepts themselves are either,
% --- p. 100 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{100}as genera and species, formed by mere abstraction from experience, or have,
through some other activity of the power of thought according to its own laws,
acquired a form at once fixed and proper to them. This form consists in this,
that concepts, together with a certain matter, exhibit a relation between what is
known from experience and something unknown: likeness, e.g.\ a property
common to several things otherwise different; cause or force, an inward
constitution that contains the ground of what is outward and visible. Ideas,
again, represent the highest principles, which may be partly the ultimate end of
our actions, partly the most general and first real grounds of the objects
themselves. Both are infinite, and therefore attainable only by an unceasing
approximation, the one in our theoretical investigations, as we pursue the long
series of causes up to the highest link, the other in our actions, which, however
excellent they may be, still fall far short of the ideal's perfection.

Both concepts and ideas have this in common, that they contain something abiding
or essential, whereas the representations of the senses inform us only of the
changes to which we ourselves, as well as all outward things, are subject. But
singular objects are the proper aim of experience, that is, of the senses: what is
abiding and general in them the understanding or reason appropriates to itself, and
leaves to science to order, to investigate, to work up; what remains it is wont,
like a useless shell whose kernel has been picked out, to discard.

The singular human being is no exception to this rule. The sciences that make our
own species their proper aim, such as anthropology, morals, politics and
legislation, consider only humanity in general, not human beings. The most
practical among them make the whole, the general, their end, and set the parts
aside, or hold themselves entitled to regard them as mere means to the former.
Hence so many thousands are sacrificed for a so-called general good: on
% --- p. 101 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{101}no altars does human blood smoke more often than on those that are consecrated to
humanity itself. For singular things, it is thought, are perishable; the species
or the genus alone does not perish. The properties by which one individual
distinguishes himself from another are merely empirical; but if they have no
unchangeable and essential character, one can form no fixed concept of them; what
are they, then, but a kind of mirage or phantasm, pleasant enough perhaps to look
upon for a short time? But why spare them, if they stand in the way of any plan
for eternity, seeing that they are destined to vanish in the next moment?

Against this it has been said, on the other side, that genus and species
nevertheless do not subsist except through singular things, and that the former
must therefore perish along with the latter. This answer, which agrees entirely
with the opinion adopted by most philosophers, is perhaps less satisfying than it
is fit to stop the mouths of those who, without any deeply thought-out theory,
would defend the wrong they inflict, under the appearance of the good of the
whole, upon so many of their fellow men. To give out the species as a mere
concept without a particular object would be the easiest way of settling the
matter, if it fitted entirely all the singular appearances one means thereby to
explain. To these may be reckoned, first, the striking likeness one cannot avoid
noticing between plants and animals of the same species; next, the forces that in
all, or in many kinds of beings, always act according to the same laws. For the
describers of nature will not admit that their natural species have any other
ground than the mind's abstraction; nor, on the supposition that natural forces
are merely general concepts, will one be able to understand how the same force,
e.g.\ that of attraction and repulsion, according to the various ways of
representing it that have been devised, can either be found by a kind of division
in many singular things at once, or how of a force that is the same only in
respect of its species there can be as many individual ones as there are
substances that
% --- p. 102 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{102}possess it. The last seemed to \textit{Aristotle,} among several such examples, so
unthinkable that, although he otherwise rejected Plato's opinion concerning the
origin of concepts and agreed with the empirical philosophers cited above, he
nevertheless assumes a single universally active understanding, which in each
human being expresses itself in a particular manner, but is not numerically
different. One only fails to grasp how he did not see that the same must then
hold just as well of every other force that lies either in human nature or in the
nature common to all.

It cannot, consequently, be regarded as so settled a matter that the concepts of
the understanding, e.g.\ that of humanity as a whole, can have no real objects
outside the individuals themselves. The Platonic Ideas, so far as they are supposed
to be forms or patterns after which all that belong to a species are formed like
impressions, neither Aristotle, Locke nor Kant has sufficiently refuted, indeed
has not even expressly contradicted, although they have shown clearly enough the
incorrectness of many partly false or distorted interpretations of this theory. In
practical life, in the judgments of common human understanding itself, in the plans
of legislators and statesmen, the influence of this opinion shows itself even
then, when those who adopt or presuppose it are scarcely conscious of this
themselves, or have formed any distinct concept of it. If one asks after the
objects of these ideas, it does not seem unreasonable either that they may lie
buried both in ourselves and in the whole of outward nature, as though beneath a
mist or a blinding glare, and that to become distinctly aware of them, not merely
to divine them, as most do, a sharper sight is required, which, whether given by
nature or acquired by one's own diligence, is nevertheless found in only a few.

But even if this theory, so far as it extends, is correct, might it not
nevertheless be far too narrow, and false, if it is moreover exclusive? Are there
not likewise both ideas and concepts of singular things, or are those about
% --- p. 103 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{103}species and genera alone possible? This question is, especially when applied to
human beings, exceedingly important. It amounts, namely, to this: has every human
being, just as much as the species itself, a certain fundamental form or essential
character that remains the same under all the circumstances of life? Is
individuality anything other than a play of changing shapes or new determinations,
in which nothing is imperishable except what each has in common with all others,
that is, with the general human nature? Or are these changes only successive
developments of a kernel which, whether it is just beginning to grow or has
already become a fruit-bearing tree, or after several transformations has become
unrecognizable even to itself, nevertheless always retains the original
fundamental features? Is, finally, the goal at which everyone aims, even
unconsciously, the pattern after which he must be formed, in truth an infinite
idea which, notwithstanding all multiplicity, indeed apparent conflict, has
inward unity and unshakable fixity in itself? It is clear that only in this case
has every individual an indescribable worth, whereas in the other the species
alone has it. That this difference of opinions must necessarily have especially
important practical consequences it is almost superfluous to recall.

\sectionrule

Self-consciousness is the foremost expression of individuality, the highest and
best-known step that any finite being has ascended. The more clearly and distinctly
we distinguish ourselves from others, and know what marks us off from them, the
more perfect we are. All philosophy consists in knowledge of ourselves, not
according to the common human nature alone, but especially according to the
individual one. The fruit of learning and reflection is chiefly a kind of
self-recollection. All development and resolution of concepts, all composition and
connection of them, serves only to make consciousness entirely clear, to put us in
a position to give ourselves a complete account of ourselves. Conscience and
% --- p. 104 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{104}morality rest on nothing else either. To act not from impulse but from principles, is
that not to act with distinct consciousness of them? For even in those who are
determined by feelings, fundamental principles are at work, though according to
obscure and indistinct representations. The whole excellence of our being consists,
therefore, in individuality, the aim of all striving is to attain it in the very
highest degree: it is consequently, in the first place, legitimate, it is neither
necessary, nor useful, nor possible to suppress it: in the second place, it is the
pattern after which everyone must form himself, the idea one ought always to seek
to approach: from which it follows that ideas can be individual, and that their
objects, just as much as the species, are eternal, infinite and unchangeable.

One may even raise the question whether there are indeed other ideas of real
things than those adduced, and whether the general opinion, that these must either
necessarily be general concepts, or that they can at least just as well have species
and genera for their objects as singular things, is not founded upon a confusion of
the logical and ideal essence of things with the real, which only individuals can
actually possess. The dispute as to whether genera and species truly exist outside
our thoughts is, as is well known, very old. Those who hold them to be real appeal
to the fixed boundaries that nature seems to have set between beings of different
species, which make any transition from the one to the other impossible. As
concerns the more perfect organisms in the plant as well as the animal kingdom,
the matter is held by most naturalists to be settled; the human race especially is
in their view a striking proof of it; since even the lowest among our species so
plainly distinguishes itself from the most excellent among the neighboring species,
or, if one will, genera, that no confusion or intermixture, except through gross
ignorance, seems possible here. Nonetheless there are facts and other grounds that
might awaken doubt of this. I will not even adduce the many uncertain ones that have
been
% --- p. 105 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{105}employed here by various famous men, nor the conjectures and hypotheses that
have been advanced, but not sufficiently proved, by considerable authors such as
Monboddo, Hallen, Lamarck, and even our own Fabricius. In the first place, it is
nevertheless certain that the more general concepts become, and the further they
remove themselves from the individual ones, the more their boundaries run together,
and the more difficult they become to determine. The criteria of genera are in
general more wavering than those of species, and among the former most of all the
higher ones, namely those of orders, families and classes, of organic as well as
inorganic bodies. As our knowledge of nature, even the merely empirical, extends
itself, we see gradually vanishing those boundaries which we believed we had
determined not arbitrarily, but according to nature's own direction. In the second
place, even when not even the longest time seems able to displace them, it is
nevertheless doubtful whether this permanence is absolute and essential, or merely
relative, since we are compelled to regard as unshakable whatever a few thousand
years, to which our experience, when it reaches its utmost, must after all confine
itself, have let stand. We find, in the third place, varieties just as well, e.g.\
Negroes, or among dogs pugs and greyhounds, as proper species, which, although
they have presumably become so divergent through accidental circumstances, have at
last taken on an indelible character. How, then, will one make good that the same
may not hold equally of the latter?

It is, finally, not so difficult to conceive how species may have come into being
through the general laws of attraction and affinity, which in the plant and animal
kingdoms acquire several new determinations and a higher significance, even if from
the beginning nothing but individuals were brought forth, in many though not yet so
distinctly determined gradations and forms of the same force. For since like
everywhere seeks like, and since connections, societies, the reproductive drive and
the drive to imitation must always bring several still nearer to one another than
they already are by nature, all singular beings must thereby at length have formed
groups or clusters, which may indeed at the
% --- p. 106 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{106}boundaries stand in a double and ambiguous kinship, namely both to those within
and to those outside the same, but of which the greatest number must nevertheless be
recognizably enough separated from those belonging to the surrounding ones.
Originally, therefore, there are only individuals; species and genera seem, on the
contrary, like societies or works of art, to be of a different and later creation.

\textit{Linnaeus,} who by a kind of flash of genius discovered much that in others
would have been the fruit of long reflection, held that in the beginning there were
only genera, from whose combination the species subsequently arose. Kant's
hypothesis concerning the origin of the different human races, the kernel of which
is supposed to have lain in the first human pair, has in all likelihood had the
same ground in his case, although neither of them has distinctly developed it. I
should think that both meant, or at least ought to have meant, roughly the following
by it: Neither genera nor species were in the beginning as determinate as they have
since become through mixtures and combinations. The forms were at first, as in the
works of sculptors, entirely rough. Nature has, so to speak, contented itself with
drawing the great outlines, the finer ones, as well as the final polish, have come
gradually. It may be conceived, e.g.\ that the first animals existed in a condition
in which it might have been difficult to determine whether the form was meant to
represent human beings or an irrational animal. After that shape had begun to reveal
itself, it might still be uncertain which person in particular it was the master's
intention to portray. One does indeed find everywhere physiognomies in which the
characteristic and individual seems to be lacking. But the nearer the work came to
completion, the more recognizable the distinguishing features became. Here too
nature proceeds only gradually. But it is clear that individuality is its utmost
aim. As concerns the human race, experience teaches that culture itself contributes
to this, and that for that reason one finds among civilized peoples a
% --- p. 107 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{107}multiplicity and likewise a determinateness in the form of singular persons that
is never so great among barbarians and savages. It must consequently be nature's
intention to individualize itself; the particular or the individual is not a means
and instrument for the general, but the end in itself.

But even if it were settled that individuals are the real, indeed perhaps the only,
objects of ideas, has this proposition then any important consequence? Does one
thereby obtain a nearer knowledge of individuals, or of the things themselves? Can
one now form a distinct concept of it better than otherwise, and determine it so
that we are put in a position to distinguish the one from the other? If not, what
use can the result be that we have sought so laboriously? For as easy as the
empirical criteria of singular things must be to find out, which are sufficient for
use in daily life, so difficult is the fundamental form itself either to fix in the
mind or to express in words. Nonetheless there hovers before our eyes from it a
clear though confused image: otherwise we should not learn to know things better
from acquaintance and intuition than from mere descriptions. The art of painters
and sculptors consists in grasping these fundamental forms and in so presenting them
that even by beautification they are set in a greater light rather than obscured.
Thus it is possible, out of every human face, out of every not entirely perfect
character, to form both an ideal and a caricature without effacing the fundamental
features or making them less recognizable in the one way than in the other. Neither
the roses of youth nor the wrinkles of age alter this form: if only we are properly
attentive, we become aware of it in both. Age and culture develop it more and more:
in early childhood it is entirely indeterminate; rawness and wildness likewise make
it difficult to observe: in which conditions individuals also seem most to resemble
both those reckoned to the same species and those belonging to different ones, e.g.\
human beings and apes.

It makes no difference to the main point that we are not in a position,
% --- p. 108 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{108}either in description or in definitions, to present this form. The first are taken
from the sensations of the moment, or from the impressions things make upon our
senses, in which the essential and the accidental, the objective and the subjective,
are always mixed. For definitions, language lacks the requisite wealth of words,
since of necessity it must confine itself to designating general concepts: and
although it also has names for singular objects, it has none for the individual
constitutions that make them such. The limitation of the human understanding must
content itself with knowing things in general and in the large: but one ought not
on that account to forget that the general concepts are only aids to survey, not to
exact knowledge of things in themselves. For in the real world there are found only
individuals, and their essence is not what is expressed by the criteria of the
species or the genus, but what lies in the eternal idea, of which innumerable
modifications, showing themselves in time, are possible. This idea is not general in
the sense that it comprehends several things; but insofar as it expresses all
possible states of the same one, one might perhaps give it this name: properly,
however, it is right to make a distinction between this abstract representation of
the singular and the concrete one.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. THE DOCTRINE OF DEVELOPMENT}


We gladly see the singular appearances that occur in experience explained in some
natural way. Our desire for knowledge is thereby satisfied, whereas it is rebuffed
or thrust back when omnipotence or another supernatural cause is put forward. In the
childhood of reason one does not know the distinction between the natural and the
supernatural: the two mingle in the less cultivated person's manner of
representation, and are the same. It is perhaps less a real error than an idea not
distinctly thought. But however that may be, therein lies the whole reason why the
ancients
% --- p. 109 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{109}in all cases usual and unusual alike turned their thoughts to the influence of a
god or of a hidden power; whereas we always believe that the nearest causes must be
found in something visible and known, but regard only the higher causes as
concealed and supersensible.

As concerns the first origin of the world, of animals and of plants, we are
therefore inclined to assume an inconceivable creation, although we grant that in
their subsequent changes and propagation nature's own forces, according to laws once
determined, are chiefly at work. If one carries the merely physical investigations
somewhat further, one does seem to become aware of a kind of possibility of seeing
how the so-called lifeless materials and bodies may have formed themselves by an
innate force, but one despairs of grasping the production of organic bodies in the
same way. From this arises the evolution-system, adopted until the most recent times
with almost universal approval, whose ultimate ground is the many kinds of shoots
or germs that seemed able to have received existence only by an immediate creation.
That these could have been produced by a raw matter's gradually self-developing
force, this opinion seemed to most as unreasonable as it was dangerous and bordering
on explicit denial of God. How deeply, it was further thought, is human nature
thereby degraded, indeed every living being, although the ancients did not find it
degrading, but reverently worshipped the earth as their mother. Yet we see that such
is nature's course. Of omnipotence one forms the conception that it can bring forth
everything at once, the greatest along with the least. One wonders, therefore, and
rightly, on learning that everything comes to be out of something preceding which,
when we consider the whole, is always, as far as form goes, inferior to what
follows. The seed or germ, even of human beings and animals, is in the beginning
nothing but a wholly formless fluid: if we pursue it to its origin, with our
thoughts, when the eye can no longer do so, then it loses itself at last in
materials that we
% --- p. 110 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{110}cannot distinguish from the common ones. But how little are these known to us!
How glorious is that dust which we use as an emblem of lowliness and out of
ignorance despise! Is the common matter anything other than the visible God, than a
revelation of the highest being itself?\footnote[1]{By matter one must here
understand not the phenomenon, which is an object of experience, but its
supersensible substrate, the ultimate subject of the same, its eternal essence
itself.} Just as the Ideas develop out of his infinite understanding by an eternal
birth, so the seed, the fundamental forms of all things, lies enveloped in matter's
womb, but becomes only in time, and consequently only gradually, intuitable images
of those patterns which they ever more nearly strive to express.

The first human beings did not arise immediately either from the sea or from the
earth. Yet in both lies the mediate ground of both our matter and our form. Of the
earth we are formed. For before the solid separated itself from the fluid, nothing
living yet existed, least of all the most excellent creature among all living things.
Nature's forces are not different from the Godhead's own force, except insofar as
the latter considered in itself is infinite, while in the former it reveals itself in
a finite manner, and in its infinity only as an unceasingly increasing magnitude. In
this sense one may say that matter has formed itself, advancing gradually from the
less perfect to the most perfect human form. One may likewise say with truth that
God, after having brought forth matter itself, or, by its properties, having made his
own being as intuitable as it could become, has out of it again brought forth all
things. These turns of speech, which most hold to be as different as heaven and
earth, mean at bottom the same, and indicate only two human manners of
representation.

In a still more mediate way water is the origin of all things. The ancients, who out
of an easily excusable impatience leapt past the nearest and intermediate causes in
order to
% --- p. 111 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{111}soar up to the highest, misunderstood these propositions about our origin, which
are in themselves quite correct. Either they let human beings and gods arise
immediately out of chaos, which is the original fluid, or else they held that by the
united forces of the sun, the air, the water and the earth, the most perfect animals
and human beings themselves were begotten of the last, just as mold and maggots are
of putrefied bodies. The first opinion rested on a mythical tradition and a poetic
presentation of it. The poet's imagination resembles the chaos they sing of, and in
the same way contains the seed of reason's most glorious works. It comprehends the
whole at a glance, but does not notice that what in this flash of genius, as in the
divine understanding, exists all at once, can become actual for the senses only
gradually. The second hypothesis is indeed a fruit of experience and reflection, but
an unripe one. For although the newest experiences seem in this to agree with the
most ancient, that some among the lowest plant and animal species can come forth in
this way without the usual propagation from their like, indeed even wholly new ones
that did not exist before, we have nevertheless not the least ground, from nature's
regular course, to suppose the same of the larger and more developed organisms in
the two kingdoms of living things. Such giant strides, as a sudden transition from
the lowest to the highest degree of development, are no less at variance with
philosophy's fundamental principles than with experience and analogy. For from the
concept of time and finitude the opposite manifestly follows. How was any time
possible without order and a continuous advance in which there can be no leap? How
can one think the infinite being expressed in finite forms except by an eternal
approximation to the former; or conceive time as eternity's image, unless it is
unbroken and uniform throughout?

From materials that seem raw, because one becomes aware of no form in them yet,
there occurs, then, an imperceptible transition to more determinate shapes, whose
regular sequence or
% --- p. 112 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{112}series one can for that reason only indicate in general, as I have also done
above. To separate them by sharply marked boundaries will therefore not do, because
nature's course, in those deviations or degenerations by which the former genera and
species are transformed into wholly new ones, is not merely creeping, but at the
same time so slow that neither our own experience nor history's far too small
compass puts us in a position to follow its almost imperceptible traces. After the
lapse of millennia, the least ambiguous remains of animals and human beings, which
may be regarded as ancestors of those now existing, though rather different, still
give occasion for objections and doubts. I shall therefore adduce none here. One
will find them described in abundance in the writings of naturalists, but most of
them uncertain, all of them disputed. What may be right or wrong with respect to
these one cannot, as everyone sees, decide \textit{a priori}; but the matter itself,
which one would prove from them, is nevertheless not doubtful.

An immediate creation, whether of actual animals and plants or of their nearest
germs, is empty words. If, on the other hand, the question is of a mediate or
natural production, then no other is possible than the one adduced. The human race
has consequently not existed from the beginning in its present form and shape,
although every history, the sacred as well as the secular, must naturally proceed
from this point. The specific criteria by which natural history now distinguishes
this being from others, it has not always had, but has taken on gradually. I venture
boldly to advance this opinion, not as a mere hypothesis, but as an undeniable
consequence of an order of nature that is not accidental but necessary, whose series
one cannot break off without absurdity and contradiction. It would, on the other
hand, be temerity if I were to maintain that one can with the same certainty depict
the human shape in every preceding condition, or determine to which species of
creatures now known we formerly belonged, or from which we have perhaps sprung. I am
convinced
% --- p. 113 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{113}that the human being constitutes a species or genus for itself, and by no means
believe that the ape, as the Tibetans maintain according to \emph{Pallas's} report, or
any other animal, however high they may rise through all eternity, will ever become
human, or just the same as, even if more than, what we now are. But I am indeed of
the opinion that our first ancestors, whose memory no history and no archive can
have preserved, ran through a course of life in which neither could the higher gifts
of mind come to that maturity which they reach in the beautiful and fully developed
body we now bear, nor was mere sensibility provided with all the requisite
instruments for so many kinds of sensations, so fine and so vivid. Nature's
manifoldness, the inexhaustible wealth of Ideas in the Godhead's bosom, their
destination to an eternal and ever-progressing actuality, does not permit us the
thought that all beings are nothing but the same thing at different degrees of the
height they must ascend. What, then, does the inequality among creatures of one
species mean, which despite this individual difference can each in its own way be
equally perfect? Why should such manifoldness not have place among species as well?

Have we perhaps reason to be ashamed of such an origin? This question can be
answered quite briefly. We need not go so far back in time to find stronger grounds
for our humiliation. How many years is it since each of us stood far below the life
of the animals, indeed of the meanest plant? Is it not more important to think of
what we now are, and shall one day quite certainly become, than of what we were: as
if the proud \emph{cedar} would look down with scorn upon the young shoot that
springs up at its roots, or we upon the fetus whose heart has scarcely begun to beat
in the mother's womb. Everywhere all that lives has the same root: divine is our
descent, eternal and infinite our destiny.

\begin{intro}

% --- p. 114 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{114}2. In \textsc{F. C. Sibbern} (1785---1872), who along with Holberg and Kierkegaard is the most
distinctive figure in Danish philosophy, the romantic current proceeding from
% print: Forskerperslonighed (misprint for Forskerpersonlighed)
Steffens united with the influence of Treschow's clear personality as an inquirer.
A great enthusiasm and a deeply moved mind went together in Sibbern with the need
for observation and with the sense for the individual forms of life. Behind the
peculiarity and stiffness of his language the reader finds a warm humanity and an
untiring striving for clarity. The pieces chosen here will illuminate his line of
thought from different sides.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. MENTAL DEVELOPMENT AND STRUGGLE \\ (Psychologisk Pathologi. 1828).}


The human being finds himself here on earth in a mental becoming, as regards both
his existence in his own inner life and the outward conditions in and under which he
steps forth as a personal being. Only gradually, and under manifold reciprocal
action and reciprocal struggle, not merely with the surroundings but also with that
which comes forth more from within and up into consciousness, does the human
being's life win for itself, in spiritual respects, a determinate, fixed mode of
existence. The greater the content that lies in an individual for development, that
is: the more the individual is set and ordered (disposed) so that the ground of life
can bring forth in him much of all that can come forth in humanity at all, the
stronger, the more captivating, indeed the wilder, can this \emph{struggle of life}
become. Although strongest in the youthful period of life (in the age of young
manhood), which for that matter can fall at different times in different
individuals, it nevertheless continues, more or less, throughout the whole of life.
There are also individuals who in this respect remain as it were perpetually young;
indeed there are some who even throughout their whole life remain so entangled in
the ferment naturally connected in youth with this struggle of life that it is as if
their whole life were a spiritual struggle of birth, though at times with such a
depth, and with
% --- p. 115 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{115}such honesty in the endeavor, that the most glorious existence may seem therein
announced for a world to come.

On the one side it is a matter of growing properly into the great whole, but on the
other side of enjoying advancement for one's own personality in all its peculiarity.
If it looks on the one side as though it were now a task for the great whole, for
the all-embracing order of things, how this will know to assimilate to itself this
its fetus stepping forth for itself, which is now to be taken up again as member and
part into the whole from which it stepped forth as a being for itself, then on the
other side it nevertheless becomes a task for the self-conscious individual himself
how he, as he is and finds himself, with these his endowments and dispositions, and
moved by the fundamental impulses that work in him and drive him in certain
determinate directions, is to get things settled and clear, to come to equilibrium
and harmony, both in himself and with the surroundings. The individual, who with
self-consciousness perceives what goes up and down in him, cannot stop at merely
looking on; he must himself live the life that moves in him, and cannot avoid
taking its burden upon himself; nor does he feel at rest until he has done so, as
indeed he must. Nature in the human being works toward the completion of the
formation or creation begun in the human being, but it is the human being himself
who, with self-consciousness, in intelligence and freedom, is to carry the work
further. And so the individual himself has to fight the struggle, both with all that
works in his own inner life and seeks to come forth but pulls toward the most
various sides, and with the many circumstances, in respect of the outward
surroundings, that will not fall into place. Yet these are only objects of this
struggle insofar as the individual feels himself, in and through them, halted,
indeed entangled in his inner life. This remains, then, always the field on which
the struggle is properly fought.

Here reflection enters, then, not merely in general, as the individual is
continually concerned with getting things settled for himself, but also in
particular as
% --- p. 116 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{116}\emph{self-reflection}, or as the individual's constant looking back upon his own
ego, as he is constantly concerned with himself. In this there is, for a being such
as the human being, nothing in and for itself that ought not to be. For if healthy
self-feeling is certainly to show itself in a healthy \emph{self-forgetfulness}, it
shows itself on the other side no less fully in a healthy self-remembrance and
\emph{going inward into oneself} at certain moments and times of life. Without such,
the being that lives and works for the greater whole could not, in its own inner
life, rightly become something, promote its own growth of soul, and bring to
ripeness the creation, or the fruit on the tree of life, that it is itself to become
and be. True, the right and full health of soul shows itself in this, that the
individual on life's course, while he turns constantly and primarily about himself,
\textit{eo ipso} nevertheless goes, uninterruptedly and without halting, his
constantly advancing way about an eternal sun, or conversely, that in doing this he
