\textit{eo ipso} also does that; and Christianity lets us see this mode of existence
in a still higher light. But what is thus in every moment to merge into one another
can, in the life of the human being in time, do nothing other than each find its
times in which it especially comes forth; but especially in a being conscious of
itself, which is still in its becoming, and which can become nothing except insofar
as it is itself a party to making itself so, that going inward into oneself, that
self-occupation and having to do with oneself, must necessarily find its times in
which it must have room.

This holds even if life goes a pure, quiet, healthy and even course forward. But to
this it does not come so easily. The more deeply life opens in a heart, the sooner
it may come to pass through a chaotic mixture and stirring of the elements in its
inner life, and the world that is to build itself up in it it must now take part in
creating, as if it were to be re-created from the ground up. And since in this not
merely the content of life in general stirs according to its essence, though in its
various fundamental effects, as from various sides, and as
% --- p. 117 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{117}in conflicting elements that first seek harmony, but now also the ego stirs
according to its own egohood, it is quite natural that in the inward struggle a
certain morbid entanglement in oneself, with alternating self-confidence and
despair, can easily gain the upper hand in certain periods of life, as a naturally
critical state of transition. This must then be passed through with trust and
self-encouragement, but also with resigned waiting and surrender to the
all-embracing all-working power that shows its might precisely here in the
individual, and whose vessel one feels oneself most to be precisely in this; with
indefatigability in solving the tasks that lie immediately before one, but with a
feeling that the burden of life as a whole is not to be borne by ourselves
\emph{alone}, least of all all at once and straightway in its whole extent. But this
critical state of transition we shall come to consider more closely in what
follows, where the passionate emotion that we have called restless love will be
dealt with. Here, where we merely follow the human drives and inclinations in their
egoistic or idiopathic direction, we have only to consider this morbid entanglement
in itself, insofar as it appears especially as a constant self-reflection, in which
not so much the content of life in its many stirrings debates itself out in the
individual's inner life, but rather the individual himself wrestles with his inner
and outer world.

It cannot escape us that a \emph{reflection-sickness} of this kind, a
\emph{brooding} ego-sickness, as we, having brought it under the concept of
ego-sickness in general, have called it, seems especially to prevail in our time, in
which partly the greater struggle for physical and civil subsistence, which
circumstances now often bring with them, has done so much to call forth, nourish,
and as it were accustom to, self-reflection at all, partly individuals no longer, as
formerly, feel themselves, neither in religious nor in civil respect, borne by a
fixed order of things in life, a firmly established disposition in society, and a
course of things that readily incorporates the
% --- p. 118 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{118}individuals into the larger whole, and lets them find their place therein. In an
age that is generally an age of upheavals, and in which a new order of things is
trying to form, reflection on life and on life's circumstances and nature naturally
spreads more than it otherwise would, and a greater struggle of life for each
individual in particular then naturally has to be gone through. Often it is,
moreover --- this must not be overlooked --- evident that nervous weakness or other
bodily weakness is the cause of the incessant thinking about oneself.

It is then as if everything in life were a task; scruples and stumbling-blocks are
found everywhere; the smallest trifle can occupy the mind, irresolutely or
anxiously, before the matter is done, disturbingly, when it is over. It is as if the
whole of life, as Kant somewhere expresses himself, were sown all over with duties,
or else with countless considerations that seem essential and necessary, or with
other incessantly entangling halts in the free course of life. Healthy
self-forgetfulness is almost never reached. Everything becomes an incitement to
scrupulous self-admonition. It is as if all the world were out to get the
individual.

But this last expression makes us attentive to the twofold turn that constant
self-reflection can take. \emph{Either} the individual feels himself under it more in
struggle with himself, in order to bring himself to a pure and clear living. Then
all the occurrences and encounters of life that present themselves, especially its
demands, work entangling, ensnaring, \emph{distressing}. Education often lays the
first ground for this, when the child does not grow up so as to get freedom to look
about and to move and romp freely in its own way. \emph{Or} the individual feels
himself more in struggle with things and circumstances, which he cannot get ordered
according to his opinion and will, cannot get brought to be quite comfortable for
him; then the surroundings act more \emph{irritatingly}.

If this direction gains the upper hand, ego-sickness takes on more the
\emph{selfish} character proper, and we shall then
% --- p. 119 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{119}especially call it \emph{ego-sickness proper}. The individual lives then as if he lay
in a constant war with the whole world, as if everything were, or at least could be,
uncomfortable for him or in his way; as if everything were so out to get him that he
must be at his post against it. Soon this, soon that person is then against one or
not to one's liking. Incessantly the mind is occupied with whether things too are now
such as one would have them. Not even a gift or a piece of good fortune finds one
open and ready, when it is not exactly entirely after one's own head. While the
brooding reflection-sick see tasks everywhere in all occurrences, the selfish are
more inclined to see attacks, aspersions upon their person in every act of others,
every occurrence in the course of life that refers to their person but does not
agree with their momentary representations. Even our Lord would have difficulty in
making everything to their liking. If the former do not so readily take them in
jest, for which a certain self-forgetfulness, such as belongs to one who lets
himself go, is required, the latter are rather of the kind who cannot bear jest. Even
in trifles their own justice is very important to them. Even with regard to God they
would be capable of insisting upon it. The temper that is to recall Goethe's words
about Mieding: ``Und auch ein Bock macht ihm kein graues Haar'' one must not seek in
them. While the former, brooding ones take everything more as a vehicle for
scrupulous self-occupation, the latter rather hollow out, by constant criticizing of
life and circumstances, more and more the abyss that lies between them and the
world, and end by seeing themselves against the whole world and the whole world
against themselves. Therefore, while with the former's reflection-sickness a measure
of goodwill may very well unite, the latter are more envious. Precisely the word
\emph{envy}, as a desire to move about in the feelings of discontent with the world
and to sate one's mind in outpourings of bitterness against it, for which occasion
is then sought everywhere, as if with diligence, designates the state of soul of the
selfish ego-sickness. It can even lead to \emph{cruelty}, merely from desire to sate
its
% --- p. 120 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{120}envious mind, by giving its hatred full scope to destroy, indeed torment, the
object against which the hatred is kindled. That it, when pride joins itself
thereto --- and a certain pride perhaps always lies therein --- can rise to the most
dreadful state of mind, will be easily seen; but of pride we shall speak further on.
Whether we should reckon ego-sickness merely among the actual moods, or whether it
is to be called a passion proper, might still be asked. That in many respects it
shows itself especially only as a source of all kinds of envious, obstinate, proud
moods and expressions of mind is certain; but as abiding ego-sickness it is
nevertheless an abiding striving to put through its ego, to make it count, to find
satisfaction for its egoism, to see it upheld, nourished, tended, and thus has its
determinate object, for which one labors and strives, and is thus a passion proper.

Against ego-sickness in general, both the brooding kind, which we shall especially
call reflection-sickness, and the selfish kind, which we think of in particular by
the word ego-sickness, it seems that, next after or beside a knowledge of its true
nature constantly nourished by consideration of one's condition, especially
\emph{rest} for the mind is the remedy one must apply. But this is here to be sought
in several ways: first quite simply by trying to guard oneself against every
occasion, partly of disturbing reflections in general, partly of the irritating and
provoking emotions in particular. But since the world is full of such occasions for
one who is easily irritated or inclined to brood, and since complaints that the
surroundings will not grant one the rest one needs never lead to the goal but lead
farther from it, because by them one loads oneself with new vexations, whose source,
once the mind is in unrest, is inexhaustible, so long as help is sought in the
surroundings' accommodating themselves to one's mind, the right rest is to be
sought not merely outwardly, by fleeing everything disturbing, but also, and
especially, in an
% --- p. 121 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{121}inward resting, by a renunciation of everything that formerly occupied thought and
% print: Atttraa (misprint)
desire, but occupied it only to unrest and discord. One must resolve no longer to
take a thousand things to heart, to let life go as it will, and for a time to care no
more whether it goes contrary to one's representations of what is right and correct;
for not even the most well-founded complaint or resentment may one let have room. The
main thing must be to grant mind and constitution rest, until one, after the
necessary resting, can again attain to taking life and life's concerns to heart in a
right and healthy way, and letting them matter to one as much as is otherwise in
general fitting, but which that person sick in mind must just as well refrain from
doing as many a bodily sick person must refrain from it, because now something else
and more important demands the refraining. We may here be reminded of Christ's
words: If your eye offends you, tear it out! In general a human being ought to spare
his eyes and take care to preserve them as precious organs; but if the eye
\emph{vexes} one, then surely the salvation of the soul is more important than that
of the eye itself. Thus the care not to harm one's soul, by constantly feeding one's
ego-sickness, is more important than the care for all the rest that, during our
incessant occupation therewith, irritates the heart once fallen into this sickness.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. THE SOUL IS ACTIVITY (Philosophisk Archiv. 1829).}

We see manifestly in the human being a life subsisting, with a certain unity and
wholeness for itself, and this is the properly true, constant being in the body,
since it makes the body be and persist, whereas the latter under its working
constantly becomes another through a constant exchange of the material, as the body
also, when life ceases, ceases to be able to
% --- p. 122 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{122}subsist as this body, in \emph{this} form. Nothing can be objected to this. But this
life in its progress brings forth in the human being, or rather itself steps forth
in, a sum of sensation, feeling, etc., which I have called the purely mental or the
spiritual, and in this a manifold activity reveals itself, as in the functions going
on for themselves in the body, but in such a way that this purely mental activity can
by no means with any right be said to occur in a bodily manner, or to occur in any
part of the body, so soon as one will keep to the immediately factual and not set up
a hypothesis by which something is posited that no one has either seen or can
imagine; for who has seen a piece of reasoning take place in the brain, a matter
that must not be confused with the fact that, during the reasoning, something
corresponding may well take place in the brain as well? This sum of the life
appearing in the body and of the purely mental or spiritual life now manifestly
forms a unity, and the human being steps forth as one living being, in which there is
also one such life as that described in the definition of soul given before. That
this life \emph{subsists} in this wholeness, and can be called a
\emph{subsisting thing}, and that it is therefore also permissible to call it a
\emph{living thing}, one will surely also concede. For the rest, it suffices that
there is here recognized to be one life, subsisting with perfect unity in itself.
Should that expression, \emph{living thing}, be able to lead to the misunderstanding
that it were thought as though it were a something that was something other than
that life itself, which something now merely had this life, then it had better be
avoided. (Even of God himself Holy Scripture says in one of its most significant
utterances not that God \emph{has} love, but that God \emph{is} love, something that
also teaches us how spiritually we ought to understand the words: God is a spirit,
and they who worship him ought to worship him in spirit and truth). But that life,
thus subsisting or persisting as a perfectly single \emph{One} for itself, is
nevertheless constituted by a course of things occurring according to a total order
of
% --- p. 123 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{123}things, in which an all-working shows itself; it has become as it became because
this all-working in this place so brought it with it. I do not see what can
reasonably be objected to this, and to what has been said further in the foregoing
for the elucidation of this point. But from the all-working power by which it was
constituted from the first, the individual life cannot tear itself so loose as to
become a really \emph{altogether} self-subsisting independent whole; it continues
always only to be a link in the whole chain of things. But if that all-working
reveals itself, with its inner content, to which among other things that which we
call the laws of nature belongs, in the total whole in its wholeness, it must also
reveal itself in everything single, indeed in each individual living subsistence;
none such can therefore be thought to stand in any other relation to the all-working
than a relation like that in which every special working, in which a life reveals
itself, stands to this life; and just as such a special working or function has two
sides, the one that it steps out into its organ as something for itself, the other
that in it in turn that whole life steps out, as in one among the many, so also must
the subsistence of the whole individual life, that is, the subsistence of the soul,
have two such sides from which to be regarded. Whether this life, subsisting as
soul, is now an immortal one will then depend on whether the function of the
all-working that steps forth in it is a merely temporary one, or a perennial one,
and that one continuing constantly as the altogether selfsame; whereby it then comes
into consideration at all whether the total order of things is one that in its
course carries with it both kinds of functions, or both kinds of individualizations
of life. Thus I think the matter is to be regarded, when it is to be taken purely
physiologically.

What is to be answered to the question what the soul properly, or in its ultimate
ground, is, has now been indicated already sufficiently in general by the foregoing.
The
% --- p. 124 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{124}closer pursuit of this would require deliberations as to why there exists at all
an organizing and individualizing life, and what \emph{this} is in its essence;
whereby it then depends chiefly on seeing that only individual self-conscious
subsistences are those in which the divine can reveal itself \emph{as such}, and that
\emph{this} now in its working and creating course equally definitely constitutes for
itself its material, the substrate for its revelation in the finite, in order
thereupon to constitute itself as the organic life that is in the individual, in that,
e.g.\ striving toward a seeing, it constitutes an eye, in order thereby to constitute
itself as a seeing life. Yet this would lead us into quite other series of
considerations than the one we have before us here. At bottom it is more the real
concept of a mental subsistence that is at the same time an ego, than of a soul in
general, that I here sought to indicate. The question why not all living and sentient
subsistences are such self-conscious subsistences that they can feel themselves in
relation to the ultimate ground of their existence, and thereby become dwellings for
the life of the divine that reveals itself \emph{as such}, that is, in its
spirituality and according to the fullness of its spirituality, in finitude,
nevertheless always remains, and we can so much the less answer it, as we must admit
that we do not know what the animals, each in its own kind, are properly in their
ultimate ground. Of \emph{ourselves}, on the other hand, we may well dare to know it
and to speak of it. But this matter belongs then no longer to the concept of the soul
in general, but to the concept of egohood and personality in particular.

But there remains still to justify, somewhat more closely than has been done before,
the reality of the whole concept of soul, for which the foregoing may now well put us
in a position, since it has shown us what the soul is. Whereby we naturally do not
wish to justify it in any other sense than that in which it has previously been set
up. When the sensualists in France\footnote[1]{See the review of Broussais's
\textit{L'irritation et la folie} in Revue encyclopédique for December 1828, p. 586.}
% --- p. 125 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{125}declare themselves against the concept of soul, this cannot but remind us how
strongly Fichte in his time expressed himself against it. If the former do so because
\textit{l'homme physique} is for them the whole human being, Fichte did so because
he contested the notion that attached itself for most people to the concept of soul,
that it was a thing, a something that \emph{had} life and \emph{had} consciousness,
and so on, a notion by which he believed that it was substantialized in such a way
that it was basically materialized thereby, since it is always thought of as being
somewhere in the body, indeed in space; as indeed the proper place of the soul has,
as is well known, been sought at more than one point in the brain.

But why, then, when the soul is at bottom nothing other than the fundamental life
subsisting in the human being in its unity and totality, do we not stop at this
expression, but go over to the new expression \emph{the soul}, as if that concept
were not enough for us? Manifestly because this life, in its subsistence for itself,
over against other subsistences, or other objects subsisting for themselves, steps
forth as a living being subsisting for itself. In its individualization into unity
and wholeness in itself, different from other subsistences, or other objects at all,
with which it enters into a relation of reciprocal action, it shows itself over
against them not merely as a life, but also as a \emph{living thing}, as was remarked
above, and now the concept of soul enters: a concept that for the rest is so
connected with the concepts of individualization and individual that it gets the same
dialectical character as these have.

% --- p. 126 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{C. THE DOCTRINE OF DEVELOPMENT (Spekulativ Kosmologi. 1846).}\opage{126}

So that, when creation is spoken of, a received, half-obscure concept may not
confusingly thrust itself up before us, we shall clarify for ourselves more closely
\emph{what creation} means, and how it is to be thought. I believe I can do so best
by setting forth the following:

With the very first stirring in that cosmic primeval mist from which our solar and
planetary system has proceeded, there began, as far as our earth is concerned, the
process of creation that has constituted the first human being, and from so early a
point of time the birth of the first human being is therefore to be dated. It must
surely be clear that we, who live to this day, are God's creatures just as much as
the first human being. But how are we created? \emph{Mediately}, as productions of
the whole telluric process of creation, coming forth at their times and under their
conditions. Precisely so, then, is the first human being also created. He came forth
in the fullness of his time, when that whole period of creation in and on the globe
of the earth reached the point at which the time for humanity had come. If the first
human being came into being in a different way from us, he is nevertheless not
created in any other sense of the word ``created'' than we are.

How, then, did the first human being come into being? Without doubt in this way,
that a certain animal formation, from a first slight beginning, through a series of
generations or offspring, as the process of development in the progeny went
constantly further and further, reached the point at which its last offspring became
a being that, so to speak, opened the eye of spirituality, in that it said I to
itself and felt itself in its egohood, but now also felt an urge in itself to name
all animals, i.e.\ to stamp them as its own, and to regard itself as standing in
relation to them all and as master over them all. With this humanity dawned in
animality. But now the life that had thereby dawned developed further, until in the
fullness of time in the series of offspring another individual came forth who felt
himself in his
% --- p. 127 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{127}inmost identity with God and therefore did not say to himself: I am I myself, but
said: I am God himself and God himself am I. This offspring was Christ, and in him
the Godhead thus dawned in humanity. But as we dated the birth of the human being
--- as far as our globe is concerned --- from that first stirring in the cosmic mist,
so we must also date the birth of Christ from there, as far as our globe is
concerned. But \emph{more closely} we can (and that in agreement with the manifest
allusions in John 1:1---14) date it from the point of time when conscience, as God's
holy, commanding, warning and judging voice, first began to work in humanity; for
with that the kingdom of holiness began, and with that, therefore, the Word, the
Life and the Light also began, in a \emph{higher sense}, to speak, work and shine in
the inner life of humanity.

I add: the first human being was thus not created in any other sense than that in
which Christ too must be said, with respect to his earthly nature, to have been
constituted; for although his conception was a special one, his life nevertheless
proceeded from a formation of the fetus that started from an ovum, and he was indeed
born of a certain definite human stock, so that weight is even laid upon this. As he
then came forth in the successive course of development of a series of human beings,
as a link in this, so the first human being came forth in a certain series of animal
development, as a link in this; but as now thereby something new dawned in the midst
of animality, so there too something new dawned in the midst of humanity.

The first human being has thus had a mother and a father. If we now go back in
thought, we must find their first parents in certain elementary animals, which again
have come forth as links in the total working of the process of formation of the
globe.

\sectionrule

It is through the very struggle or debate of the moments of life or existence that
life seeks its firm foundation. But
% --- p. 128 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{128}this has led us to the important main remark that everything in nature forms
itself from the first \emph{sporadically}, to which all experience bears witness in
many ways. In our time the sporadic has even been found in the first formation of our
bodily organism, namely in the beginning of the formation of the fetus, in that it
has been shown that the formation of the fetus in several respects begins from
several points of organization, from which the formations work toward each other,
until, when no disturbances or halts at any point occur (which then entail
malformations), they join together into one in such a way that it then looks as
though everything had not been formed in this way from many points of organization.
If we look further to how crystallizations form, e.g.\ ice upon the freezing of
water, we see the crystallization-needles (ice-needles, quite small six-sided
columns, shooting out in the form of six-rayed stars) spring out from innumerable
points, but nevertheless join together into the compact, so to speak solid, unity
that we see before us at last. The sporadic we see further in human consciousness,
where the many drives (and therefore many drives can be named, in place of the one
drive of life) assert themselves each from its own point, and often move partly
chaotically, partly at any rate in a separated way, among one another, as they all
first seek the right middle, that is the right harmonious merging into totality.
Many cycles of ideas form in such a sporadic way side by side; it is recollection
through which they are to find full unity, so that what is sporadically separated
comes to lose itself in the organic, in which it is then in a state of being
overcome, but yet so that the separateness remains and essentially belongs too.
--- In the greater humanity we see this sporadic in many ways. From innumerable
points human beings, human views and endeavors, meet one another, under much debate,
seeking union, but not yet finding it. So it is within the states, each for itself,
so in the sporadic existence of states and nations
% --- p. 129 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{129}opposite one another. And if we now go back to the first formation of the planet
from that cosmic primeval mist, then, just as the total attraction must equally have
proceeded here from all points at once, the other effects cannot be thought otherwise
than as proceeded from innumerable points at once, since each particle has from the
first had its determinate real significance, or its determinate role to play, and has
straightway made it count. \emph{Now}, however, just as in every bodily organism, when it is
once constituted as a formed whole, so without doubt also in the totality of the
earth (with the exception of humanity), everything that comes to be is to be regarded
as so constituted by the totality and proceeding from it that the sporadic may here
now, on the whole, be said to be dissolved in the total. In organisms this sporadic
is also seen only in their very first beginning, and then already in such a way that
a fundamental type, as a fundamental idea, gathers it into such a correspondence of
the one to the other that by this the sporadic already appears more only in the
outward than in the deeper movements of life, although many monstrosities, greater
and smaller, down to such as are never called so, can here prove the reality of
sporadism. In the organism, once it is formed, it is overcome in the normal state.
Meanwhile diseases \emph{perhaps after all} might teach that the unity, although it
is a unity in overcome manifoldness, nevertheless still contains the sporadic in such
a way that the individual organs and organic systems can assert their self-subsistence
against one another in a certain sporadic way.

In healthy organisms, on the other hand, we see how the movements, proceeding from
the separated toward unity, lead to the separated subordinating itself and losing
itself as a member in the forming unity. But so we see further that subsistences
already constituted can constantly become as a material for a new higher forming,
which in making \emph{itself} count takes them up and assimilates them to itself, and
thereby takes them into
% --- p. 130 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{130}its greater unity, its greater whole. The plant is thus the food of the animal,
i.e.\ becomes in its dissolved parts material for the formations of animal life; and
our souls we learn readily to regard as those we are to surrender to the spiritual
creating of a higher power, that it may shape and form them into its organs and
thereby into constituents of its kingdom.

But as ever greater unities thus form, there forms at the same time in each of them
a single thing subsisting in them, as the inner total forming in each of them, which
now in a certain relative sense so becomes One and All in \emph{them} that it now
constitutes the properly existing being in its little outer so forming itself.
Already a \emph{system of operations} complete in itself, which forms a
certain cycle rounded in itself, can be regarded as such a subsisting thing. But
especially we see such an inner being and subsisting thing in all \emph{life}, which
however holds properly only \emph{then}, when in this life a \emph{living thing} can
be said to subsist, i.e.\ when it is so centralized in itself that it can be called a
\emph{soul}; and here we first speak of \emph{beings}.

Souls are thus \emph{formed}. This we must wholly assert of them. They \emph{become}.
--- As an organism is developed, the life expressing itself in it is constituted so
as to form such a unity in itself that a soul is thereby constituted in this life,
which shows its center in its egohood. The process of creation that goes on as the
organism is formed is at the same time that by which the subsisting one stirring in
it, or the inner subsistence appearing in it, is established or constituted, that is,
created. But this must not be thought as though the soul now consisted only in the
unity, centralized in itself, in the system of the operations taken together, as
though the soul were merely \emph{their} result, or \emph{their} coming together into
unity. No! as an ideal-real inmost infinite and all-embracing being in its inner
unity in itself (that creative realm of the individualizing tendency, that
\textit{natura naturans}) stirs everywhere at every point, so it is also \emph{hereby}
that souls
% --- p. 131 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{131}are constituted, and \emph{from here} that their becoming proceeds, as a
\emph{now} entering, under such and such conditions, new constituting and
establishing from the inner depth or momentum of that inner ideal-real fundamental
source or fundamental movement. Just as life in the single organism not merely
organizes its outward (the body), but \textit{eo ipso} organizes \emph{itself} as the
life subsisting in this body, so also by the all-constitutive realizing itself in the
whole, insofar as it, as a \textit{natura naturans,} aims at the formation and
development of individualizations, not merely this great whole becomes organized, but
this all-constitutive --- this all-life --- also thereby organizes itself, as now
stirring in a system of individual living subsistences as in its functions and
organs, where the souls themselves can then be regarded both as the organs in which
that all-constitutive functions (namely in respect of these subsistences' stepping
out), and as the functions in which it steps out, but in such a way that, just as in
the single organism the single organ with its function can step forth with a certain
self-subsistence over against the very totality of life in which it alone is and
subsists, so now those souls, especially as egos, step forth still more with such a
self-subsistence, notwithstanding that their whole subsisting is to be regarded only
as an immersion in --- or also as a stepping out of --- that all-totality, in which
they do not subsist in an isolated monadistic way, but precisely only as functions,
or as ideas exist in consciousness. For from these too, namely ideas, a comparison
can be taken. Just as my seeing and hearing subsist with a certain self-subsistence
in me, so the same holds also of my musical sense and interest, likewise of the life
of the idea of honor in me, etc.; but further this same holds of my own subsisting in
the all-being, insofar as this shows itself as stirring in the realm of
individualization.

% --- p. 132 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{D. PERSONALITY AND DOGMA \\ (Universitetsprogram. 1846).}\opage{132}

In general, when we go through the history of the Church, we meet on many points
the following peculiar thing: Great and important questions arise concerning
important points, first of all in such doctrines as concern precisely that which the
Apostle has especially called the mysteries of the kingdom of God, mysteries of which
he is set to be the steward. (1 Cor.\ 4, 1.) About the divinity of Christ, the
personality of the Holy Spirit, the double nature and double will of Christ, further
about human ability and inability, human freedom and divine all-determination;
later: about the proper nature of the redemption and reconciliation accomplished
through Christ, about the proper significance of that which is imparted in the
sacrament of the altar, etc., deeply penetrating questions arise. These are now
resolved by means of debates, deliberations, final cognitions, whereby the
utterances of Holy Scripture and its connection as a whole are made the nearest and
first and essential foundation and guide, but yet the whole prevailing manner of
view --- both with respect to what is handed down in Scripture and with respect to
the spiritual conditions of life in which every human being who goes into himself
finds and feels himself --- gets an unavoidable, deeply penetrating influence. From
such a ground, so full of life, there then emerges --- we will at least assume it,
and for a great part we must and dare assume it --- the final decision, that is, the
final coming to clarity. But now? what happens then? Scarcely has one in such a
living way brought the truth out, so that it --- really or at any rate supposedly
--- by itself and its own inner power has come to fix and ground itself, than one
dogmatizes it, tears it loose from its living root, its inner ground of life, kills
it, makes a mummy of it, and in this its mummy form it is now to be preserved,
accepted by all, pass
% --- p. 133 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{133}from generation to generation. It disclosed itself during and in consequence of a
living deliberation, and has proceeded from it, but it may not go on exercising its
dominion by virtue of the same, let alone proceed further. The matter is now to be
regarded as concluded and decided once for all, and the bare result is to count and
be believed as result. To be sure, this may still be elucidated, explained, made
accessible by a certain thinking; but the free movement of thought from which it
arose may no longer enter in the way it prevailed when that result sprang forth; for
if one were constantly to go back to it anew, the authorization to arrive at another
result, and thus to deviate, would have to stand constantly open, at least outwardly
and in form, indeed it would then have to be not only expected but wished that sheer
doubt would arise, as testimony that the conviction in those in whom it formed itself
really formed itself in a living way, in consequence of living movement of thought
and heart. --- But to this comes the following: The knowledge had formed itself only
under a dissent which was not resolved but remained standing, although the
dissenters \emph{taken as a whole} could be regarded and had to be regarded as just
as Christian-minded as the victorious party, unless one by an enormous
\textit{petitio principii} would seek the proof of their un-Christianness precisely
in their dissent. Also in this respect everything ought then to call upon one to let
the question continue to stand open, whereby alone the dissent could be thoroughly
smoothed out, and the schismatizing avoided against which the Apostle Paul had
spoken so strongly, but to which precisely the Church now surrendered; for by
casting out those who thought otherwise (only opinion can here be in question, and
therefore one rightly says hetero\emph{dox}, ortho\emph{dox}) it schismatized itself
over against that, and thus established the principle of schismatizing. Truth could,
in outward respect, only have required that one had set up one's knowledge as
\emph{one's own}, whereby, when this had been done solemnly, all the authority would
have been put into it that one could demand, if one otherwise even did right in
% --- p. 134 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{134}demanding so much. For the rest one ought to have left it to the truth to penetrate
by its own power to all those who during the deliberation had had full right to
speak and to vote, and whose dissent could not cease to be a dissent, but on the
contrary had to go on begetting dissent from itself, when the only true way of
dissolving dissent was blocked, that which goes through the living movement of truth
itself and its spiritual activity. But one did, in the highest degree, the opposite.
One established a \textit{fides ex opere operato,} namely \textit{ex opere fidei
operato.} Faith --- so one thought at least --- had now completed its work; now this
was henceforth to be believed as such, with humble acceptance. The Catholic,
however, added that it was to be done out of veneration for the Church, namely the
outward Church. The Protestant, on the other hand, wanted it to be accepted on
account of its being grounded in Scripture, only that this must not come to show
itself as such --- for then the doors would indeed be opened to constantly renewing
investigation and testing, thus also to constant doubts, and these would then have
to receive their justified intervals and receive their present representatives in
certain individuals. While Catholicism demanded only veneration, Protestantism, by a
strange self-contradiction, laid the yoke upon thinking itself. It would obviously
have been more lenient and milder if, when one did not want to take courage to let
the same evangelical freedom that one had assumed stand just as open to coming
generations, but in the confidence in and assurance of the infallibility of the
doctrine now fixed thought one had to fence in one's Gospel with dikes, one had been
able to establish a necessity of the same kind and nature as that Catholic
veneration for the Church, and had warned against the free, freely devoting reading
of that collection of holy writings, full of life and movement and unfathomable
depth, containing much more than the Epistles to the Romans and Galatians, which two
themselves moreover have too much the expression and effect of a fresh and free
outpouring of spirit to be able, with
% --- p. 135 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{135}the reflective person, to stand as unassailable foundations for that dogmatizing.
Yet surely the inconsistency and the timid half-heartedness would then have become
all too conspicuous. For the rest it may well be that the new Gospel might need those
tutors or child-minders (cf. Gal. 3, 24 and 25) in the beginning, and until it had
become firmly established. But afterward one should then have opened freedom instead
of fastening the fencing and sharpening the supervision. But precisely the opposite
happened. Instead of that old Judaism's doctrine of law and gospel of law and faith
of law --- for Judaism too had a gospel, since it had its promise, and it had a
faith, since it had its trust --- there came a similar legalism as dogma-gospel and
dogma-faith. To what an enormous degree this was moreover against Luther's spirit,
mind, heart and way of thinking, everyone must be able to see. Protestantism now
went so far that, although the doctrine concerned the deepest and most difficult
points, for whose simple understanding a penetration was presupposed that could not
be expected in many, let alone in all, the matter was nevertheless so pressed that
those dogmas were, even in the general simple Christian instruction, not merely to
be communicated but to be impressed as those whose even literally correct
apprehension was essentially what mattered; whereby death must surely come to beget
death from itself, and that above-mentioned new kind of holiness by works had to
appear, namely dogma-holiness.

Without the name of heretic and the branding as heretic such great things would
hardly have been accomplished and carried through. It was the way of terrors that
was taken. We know well that fear and trembling (Phil. 2, 12), with which the soul
can and must at its times work out its salvation; the anxiety of the heart-sick is
not without its good ground and its good fruit. But it is of quite another nature
than such outward terror, which precisely suppresses the inner holy thing that stirs
in that anguish and trembling; for it does not permit one to go back to this ground
of the heart, in order to fetch in it, in the most genuine
% --- p. 136 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{136}way, the most genuine chastisements and consolations. Luther has (in the Preface
to the Epistle to the Romans) said significantly and deeply that faith consists in
this: ``dass wir des Geistes Anfang haben''; but that stiff insistence, prevention
and terrorizing may be said to aim at ``dass wir des Geistes Anfang unterdrükken'';
for the \emph{spirit} comes precisely not \emph{as} \emph{spirit} when one will force
it into certain forms or formulations.


Yet what one would not permit happened nevertheless. The Lord will himself rule in
his kingdom, and the spirit lets itself not be fettered. It does not demand that we
in its service should advance in the form of a lord, but in the form of a servant.
Did not rationalism break forth mightily, and may anyone say that humanity, care for
human beings, care for feeding the hungry and refreshing the thirsty, etc., has not
had place just as well and just as warmly on its side as on that one? Yes, was not
rationalism precisely in the beginning bound up with a striving for such care for
human beings? Only let one not confuse rationalism with indifferentism. Without
Christian spirit rationalism truly was not, in many respects and in many ways. But
besides, many sects arose, which, dissatisfied with the Church, felt themselves in
their evangelical freedom, and either withdrew from or wholly separated themselves
from the ruling Church. In them there was even saved, during the Church's struggle
with that its chief opponent, rationalism, gradually much of that which was too
Christian, too cosmic, too world-historical, too deep of heart and too deep of
conscience, for it to be taken up into rationalism, which could thus become only a
partiality beside the others, but which for the rest may not be denied to have been a
partiality both natural, necessary and fruitful beside them, and to have, even if
itself arrogant, not only broken much ecclesiastical pride and self-sufficient mind,
but also brought that whole doctrine of the Church, which had worked so much at
% --- p. 137 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{137}schismatizing itself, to be able now, too, to count for nothing other than a
partiality beside so many others.

\sectionrule

With spiritual weapons one is to contend, to fight; spiritually one is to enlighten,
clarify, calm, carry through and bring to an end; and therefore, precisely
\emph{therefore}, no worldly weapons may be used, no halts that taste of the worldly
may enter, no cutting-off decisions may take place; in the same degree as these
occur, the inward clarification and the inner decisions of the spirit are halted, and
even regarding these one must not hope or believe that, when everything is left to
them, one may one day come to see the matter finally decided and brought to an end.
No, these inner decisions of the spirit are so full of life that they never decide
once for all; for here too there comes the everlasting resurrection and inner
rebirth. Nor does the spirit let itself be determined in the end; its kingdom is
inexhaustibility and therefore everlasting renewal, and the constant change of
individuals is here the constant material and realm of its renewals.

Of the wrong we spoke, but also of the one-sided there must be much talk. --- Whence,
then, both? Do they come from nothing?

If they are of any significance and penetrating activity, and have not directly the
lusts of the flesh, but the reflections, inquiry and deliberation of natural reason,
being gripped by one thing or another in Holy Scripture, indeed care for human beings
and care to stand against those lusts of the flesh, and other such things as source,
origin and first mover, --- and how often has this not undeniably been the case? ---
then let one consider that it is out of the inner deeper wellspring of life that all
this, more or less deeply, comes; it is \emph{the moments of life} that we must see
stirring in it, it is the dialectic of life, the inner process of development and
living of life, that will come forth in it, and to this we must pay heed.

% --- p. 138 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{138}These moments are of two kinds: the one sum we will call the worldly, or rather the
moments of life of the side of the world --- the other the spiritual, those of the
spiritual kingdom. As for the former, it is indeed the human being, humanity, in which
the kingdom of the spirit is to be --- I may well say it --- incarnated. But then
everything that belongs to the nature of humanity, the organ, must also come up and
be set in motion, so that it may come to share in the benefit of that incarnation,
and thus the whole human being in his wholeness may come to benefit from it. All this
is to be taken up and leveled out, and what belongs to that which is to be put to
% print: undre (misprint for under)
death must also be brought in under the process of dying off, which it cannot be
unless it comes forth; but then it must, if it is not to come forth as plain
sinfulness, so that either the authorities or at any rate the worldly general human
judging gets to do with it, have the right to come forth as view and opinion, and
much can then also come forth in this way, without there being, within that domain of
the authorities, any occasion to forbid it. But then the Church has to do with it
only spiritually, and must then take it solely in the free spiritual way, in order
that it may be, not the outward authority of the outward Church, but the spirit's own
might and power, that is left to accomplish and complete it, whereby alone, indeed,
its kingdom is furthered.

But now we come to the second main sum of moments: the spirit's own inner ones; for
here too there are manifoldness, richness and fullness. And that in two respects. For
first the spirit bears its original fullness in itself, destined to come wholly to
all, namely to everyone. Next, the many individuals are not alike, and innumerable
special guidances and fulfillments are given here, in which also in this respect the
spirit can unfold its manifoldness and fullness, solving each individual's peculiar
task of life in a correspondingly peculiar way. And so one must in the highest degree
beware lest one wish to prescribe to the spirit certain definite ways, as if it
% --- p. 139 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{139}might not come, when it did not come by these and through the doors one has
prepared for it, but would come where it pleased, that is to say, where in each case
it belonged. But has one not gone to work as if one wished to prescribe laws to the
spirit? For in what did all those denunciations as heretical, expulsions, rejections,
declarations of impropriety, etc., consist and do they consist, other than in this,
that one --- while one will not work at thoroughly solving \emph{one's own} task, by
constantly leaving the debate open and taking it as a purely mutual matter of
clarification, --- now even wants to hinder the spirit from solving its; for to that
one will not give it room. Ought one not, next, also to have left the debate open and
free for the reason that one must not believe oneself to have ``laid hold of it''
(Phil. 3, 12) to such a degree that one might not oneself need what the spirit
through the endeavors of opponents may perhaps wish to bring to one. And does this
not hold to this day of all? With double force must one emphasize this, when one
remembers that many who have nourished and spread deviating views or attempted new
solutions of the fundamental Christian task, be it in a dogmatizing, philosophizing or
practical direction, have been driven by a sense of truth and concern for truth no
less strong, and by a feeling for humanity and concern for human beings no less
strong, than that which may have animated the best among the so-called orthodox. If
one then holds to that which the Lord himself has given as the chief mark: ``By their
fruits you shall know them,'' the fruits speak no more for and against on the one
side than on the other; unless one by an enormous \textit{circulus in probando,}
which one would even attribute to the Lord himself, will make precisely the nature of
the tree in itself the test for the goodness of the fruits, which are then in turn to
serve as test for the tree itself. But why not let the one voice sound just as well as
the other, when the one does not have that testimony more on its side than the other?

% --- p. 140 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{140}If we look more closely, then everything spiritual that is to come to dominion in
the world goes precisely the way of so-called subjectivity. How, to begin with, did
that which went out from Christ come to the individuals: John, Peter, etc., and later
to Paul? how, then, afterward through these individuals to the many? Was it in a
manner of outward captivation? was it by a mastering of souls, by which, as one says
in German, ihnen angethan ward? Even if so, the real working was nevertheless one
coming from within, which from without was only awakened. Many have perhaps been
captivated in this way, but in them, as long as the effect remained at this, so that
they remained as it were stamped as Christians, no true living Christianity has been
able to arise. Such a one goes only through another kind of being gripped. This has
proceeded from the consciousness of sin, the knowledge of sin. But to what degree are
we then not immediately here on the domain of subjectivity, but to what degree at the
same time on the domain of the deep objectivity working in subjectivity. The
punishing, anxiety-producing, vigilance-urging element in me, is it I myself? or is it
not immediately the working of so-called grace, thus a working of the All-Objective?
Moreover, all those who hold to the historical-ecclesiastical given, can they hold to
it except because they are moved by it, or at least moved by a living knowledge of
and feeling for the importance of holding to it? But then in them too that which forms
itself in their innermost becomes the real foundation, and only through this does all
that other get significance, thus true objectivity, for them. Or do they themselves
wish to hold to the historical purely historically? Then only a so-called historical
faith, no faith of the heart, no true living faith, no faith in which the Holy Spirit
stirred, would be the result. And yet their foundation nevertheless becomes such that
we must call it subjective; for it then becomes the assurance of the validity of such
a historical faith. --- One cannot flee the Omnipresent, however one may wish it; but
the
% --- p. 141 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{141}Omnipresent proclaims himself first and last in each individual in his innermost.
He is the only true ultimate Objective, \textit{sensu eminenti,} the fund and fullness
of all objective truth, but he works in spiritual respects solely from within, in and
through subjectivity.

But surely one can and must, taking the matter from another essential side, still
recall in reply the following: Is not Christ's work in the Church a great work of the
world, which does not go in isolation to each individual, but goes through the whole
to the individual? Does it not go from the center of humanity to the wholeness of
humanity, as the total organization, and \emph{through this} to the individual? I
answer: this reminder is of great significance and importance. But do not the first
Apostles teach, does not Paul's later appearance teach, indeed the resistances he
found before he, or what came from him, finally broke through, does not Luther's
appearance and activity, etc., etc., teach that precisely individuals constantly form
as it were the new points of organization from which the kingdom, as an organization,
develops more and more? Are not the individual subjectivities here constantly just
the newly opened wellsprings of life through which what is destined for many, indeed
for all, first comes to several individuals and then to more and more? Let one then
not accuse anyone of subjectivity in this respect either!

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

3. To the psychological school must also be counted the physician \textsc{F. G.
Howitz} (1789---1826). In a treatise on insanity and imputation (1824) he contested
the sharp distinction drawn by jurists and moralists between insanity and reason,
accountability and unaccountability. Supported by observations he maintained the many
degrees and shades in human nature and in human conditions.

\end{intro}

% --- p. 142 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{THE MANIFOLDNESS OF \\ PSYCHOLOGICAL EXPERIENCE}\opage{142}

Sound human understanding sometimes confuses the uncommon with the insane, and
conversely, the ridiculous with the sublime. The history of Diogenes, Galileo, Joan
of Arc, Muhammad and nearly all founders of religions furnishes examples of this. Nor
is it well possible to fix the boundaries of fundamental likeness; they let
themselves be better felt than described, the knowledge of them rests on tact and on
sound understanding, which arrives at truth through a twilight and half-conscious
inferences, and not through any strict demonstration. Besides, these boundaries vary
according to the special cases; what e.g.\ during the storming of Syracuse was in
Archimedes admirable eagerness for knowledge would in a simple citizen be insane
insensibility; what on the battlefield is martial enthusiasm is in peacetime raging
bloodthirstiness; what in St.\ Teresa is called divine inspiration is called in an
ordinary sinful human being a paroxysm of madness.

One will perhaps object that insanity thus becomes a wavering concept. But I hope to
be able to prove that it just thereby becomes truer; that insanity really is no wholly
separated condition, and that between insanity and non-insanity, far from there being
any constant chasm, there are gradual transitions, which the jurist perhaps cannot
take into account, but which the philosopher and moralist must recognize. It is not
without good reason that sound human understanding assumes half-mad people and fools.

Besides this psychological side, insanity also has a somatic or, so to speak, medical
side, from which it can be known. It is the bodily causes that have produced
insanity, and the bodily cases of illness that so often accompany it, that furnish the
materials for this kind of
% --- p. 143 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{143}knowledge, and the possibility of attaining the same in this way is the only valid
reason why physicians are consulted by judges in order to give certainty whether
insanity takes place in a criminal or not. Whenever, namely, a lack of rational
self-determination shows itself either after preceding or in conjunction with present
deviations in the physical state of health, the physician, on the strength of his
knowledge, acquired by experience, of the causal nexus between diseases of the brain
and of other organs, can establish with a high degree of probability both whether
insanity takes place and how and at what time it arose.

\sectionrule

An insane act can hardly be a rational act, but every irrational act is not therefore
insane; if it were so, simple-mindedness on the one side and passion on the other
would both have to be kinds of insanity. The difference between these two conditions
and insanity consists in the ground of the latter in a bodily illness. The correctness
of this is also confirmed by this, that when either passionateness or simple-mindedness
are grounded in so significant a deviation of the usual organization of the brain that
the natural human relation is permanently or regularly disturbed, they are rightly
reckoned as insanity.

Although some kinds of real insanity rest on a lack of reason grounded in the
physical, either as a whole (idiocy) or in certain directions (fixed ideas), there
are other kinds of this disease that seem to rest rather on the lacking influence of
reason than on its own erroneous nature. Reason here counsels the right thing, but
its counsel can either not be heard or not be followed. For there is a morbid
\emph{dejection of the heart}, which without false ideas merely expresses itself in
altered feelings, inclinations and
% --- p. 144 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{144}passions, and these, so to speak, \emph{fixed affects}, which are likewise caused by
physical faults of the brain, but in other organs, are far more dangerous than the
mere fixed ideas. To this belongs the so-called \textit{iracundia morbosa,} a
condition in which the human being is brought by the most insignificant occasions to
an exceedingly high degree of anger and violent acts following upon it, (it is not
uncommon, and examples are cited among others by Hencke in his Abhandl. aus dem
Gebiete der gerichtlichen Medicin, vol. 2, p. 280 etc.). To this belongs also Pinel's
so-called \textit{mania sine delirio} or \textit{furor transitorius,} which consists in
% print: lingende (no Danish word known to us; lynende?)
a sudden, temporarily erupting frenzy, with a desire to destroy and kill.
\textit{(Sur l'alienation mentale,} Paris 1809, p. 155 to 161). The persons attacked by
it are well aware of their condition, they make the bitterest reproaches to
themselves over it, they warn others to keep away when they notice the attack
approaching\footnote[1]{Or they seek to prevent the outbreak by the constraint that
the presence of others imposes on them. A mother entreated that she not be left alone
with her child, since otherwise she would have to kill it. (cf. Mende in Henckes
Zeitschrift f. die Statsarznk. 1. Jahrg. 2 H. XII.). Under such drives, he says,
reason is often active, it abhors the satisfaction of the drive, but yet performs the
act with deliberation.}, indeed even during it they are able to answer coherently and
give no other sign of derangement of mind than that murderous desire, so that they
are sometimes able to deceive strangers\footnote[2]{The same occurs in patients with
fear of water (hydrophobia), they sometimes exhort the bystanders not to approach the
bed, since in that case they feel that their urge to bite becomes irresistible.}). In
others such paroxysms express themselves in an irresistible tendency to steal (ibid.
§ 158), without the understanding for the rest seeming in any way or at any time to
be clouded. What makes these kinds of insanity easily distinguishable from moral
corruption is, according to Pinel, a certain contrast in which they most often stand
to the usual character of the individuals attacked (§ 116); a strictly honest man
will e.g.\ in such a paroxysm steal, an otherwise good son will maltreat his mother,
the
% --- p. 145 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{145}compassionate becomes malicious, the peaceable violent and the modest unchaste.

\sectionrule

Everywhere where our systems are to be applied to concrete cases the nature of things
does not quite correspond to our divisions and definitions; instead of the sharply
separated concepts that the human being forms and must form, he finds in the real
world that the boundaries of things, so to speak, melt together, and by the
incessant transitions that maintain the connection of the chain he is convinced of
the arbitrary and subjective character of the distinguishing marks adopted by the
ordering understanding.

When we thus distinguish between accountability and unaccountability, freedom and
unfreedom, possibility and impossibility of a psychological compulsion, insanity and
the human rational normal state, these concepts may well have reality and be
perfectly applicable when regard is had to the distant extremes in the opposite
states and e.g.\ the criminal with sober deliberation is set in parallel with the
raging man; but the same is no longer the case when we consider the transitions and
compare the slightly insane person with the so-called free man whose understanding
is to a high degree restricted by innate organization, blinded by prejudices,
clouded by intoxication, morbidly dejected, or by habit subordinated to physical
drives, passions and affects. We meet here the same difficulties in fixing boundaries
as meet the systematic naturalists at the transition from plants to animals or from
alkalis to earths; and it is comprehensible that these difficulties must appear both
earlier and in greater number when the objects that are to be distinguished withdraw
from our immediate sensory intuition, as here where the investigation concerns the
psychological nature of the human being.

% --- p. 146 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: V without a stop
\anthsection{V} {HEIBERG AND POUL MØLLER}\opage{146}

\begin{intro}

1. The Gospel of Romanticism had in its time been proclaimed here at home by
Steffens, mainly in adherence to Schelling's half-poetic philosophy. As we have seen,
the elements that the Romantics thought they had harmonized soon began to separate
out. In Germany Hegel sought to remedy this. Whereas Schelling had left the relation
between science, poetry and religion standing in mystic unity, Hegel firmly asserted
philosophy as the central organ of truth, while art and religion expressed truth in
the more imperfect forms of the image and of fantasy. Hegel nevertheless steadily
maintained that the content of the different spiritual endeavors was the same, and
that the difference rested only on the forms. It was \textsc{Johan Ludvig Heiberg}
(1791---1860) who first introduced Hegelian philosophy into Denmark, led by the
conviction that it alone could remedy the ailments of the time, a conviction he has
developed in his treatise ``Om Philosophiens Betydning for den nuværende Tid''
(1833), of which a piece is given here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{PHILOSOPHY, ART AND RELIGION}


Critical transitions occur in the development of humanity when the objects of
knowledge have expanded so that they no longer fit into the form or the system that
formerly comprehended them; they thus burst the old form, and work now blindly and
chaotically until the new has formed itself. And really this is the case in which we
at the moment find ourselves. It is sufficient to mention the expansion that
% --- p. 147 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{147}the natural sciences and politics have received, and which could not be attained
except by a restriction of religion and a setting aside of art and poetry; for
natural science and politics, these two endeavors around which the present scientific
life turns (although the first of them is itself already beginning more and more to
be regarded as subordinate to the second), are endeavors in the finite, whereas art,
poetry and religion are realizations of the infinite; and although these three
activities do indeed present the infinite as realized in the finite, it lies
nevertheless in the accidental nature of their forms that the finiteness which they
take as a basis can only be the abstract, or finiteness in general, so that they can
never exhaust the infinite multitude of finite determinations that make up the
finite. The \emph{reconciliation} of the infinite with the finite which religion
presents, and which art and poetry likewise present, though in other accidental
forms, can therefore, with regard to our present endeavors, not become more than a
reconciliation of the infinite with nature and state in general, but not with the
infinitely many determinate details in which those finitudes have their existence.
The special investigation of the laws of nature and of the circumstances of the state
must necessarily remain foreign to art, poetry and religion, and consequently these
likewise remain foreign to that. Here, then, the case mentioned occurs: that the
expansion of the objects of knowledge (which, since they can receive an expansion,
are finite) bursts the form of the existing, and since the form itself is the
infinite, the infinite in this way recedes from the finite, and becomes a land lying
beyond, unknown, undiscoverable and unattainable, just because it is infinitely far
from what is known, discovered and attained; for by making the infinite the opposite
of the finite, one makes it the \emph{same} determinateness as this, thus the finite
itself, thus that in whose ground we are rooted and in whose
% --- p. 148 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{148}presence we live. With greater truth we can therefore say that the infinite is
annihilated, or that God really does not exist in the age or in the land or in the
human being who does not believe in him; for when God is not in consciousness, he can
merely exist as a slumbering God, thus only in the blind reason which fills
unconscious nature, whose eternal laws are the last place of refuge to which human
arbitrariness cannot penetrate.

If, therefore, the infinite driven away from our finite circumstances is to be
regained, this must happen through those circumstances being seen in their
\emph{truth}, i.e.\ being recognized as striving toward philosophy. What formerly was
able to bring about a reconciliation of the divine with the human can no longer do
so, because the knowledge of the human has, so to speak, outgrown the knowledge of the
divine. Religion, and likewise art and poetry, must, because they are not able to
\emph{enter into} the endeavors of the age, necessarily \emph{set themselves beyond}
them; but with this the age would not be satisfied, for what it fights for it will
not let itself be robbed of, and in this it is right, since the infinite must now, as
before, be won through the own development of the finite endeavors, not through their
sudden halting. Only philosophy is able to enter into the many details of our finite,
especially political aims; only it is able to see their direction toward the
infinite, and by this knowledge to clarify what is obscure in them. Hereby it alone
becomes able to sublate them, without annihilating them; on the contrary, in their
sublation into the infinite it precisely confirms their validity. In this way our
finite striving becomes grafted with the infinite, the human with the divine, and the
limitation has vanished; our sciences become philosophy, and our states regain the
ordering form.

But just as philosophy confirms the legitimacy of our finite endeavors by showing how
the
% --- p. 149 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{149}infinite is their goal, so by the same act it in turn installs the infinite in its
rights, namely by determining it as the goal, and thereby, as it were, bestowing on
it a property in the real world. It is so far from philosophy's wishing to make art,
poetry and religion superfluous that on the contrary it will procure recognition for
them in reality, and do this for its own sake, since it cannot do without them. If at
the moment they lack this recognition, the reason is not that one doubts truth as
substance, but only that one doubts that it is contained in the accidental forms in
which those activities present it. It therefore depends on apprehending those three
spiritual spheres as a real relation of substantiality, that is, on
\emph{comprehending} them, or grasping them in their \emph{concept}; but in raising
oneself to the concept one has raised oneself above substantiality; one has then
passed beyond the changing, accidental forms, and has reached the one and necessary
form which is identical with its content, namely the form of the concept, which is the
concept itself, truth, which is itself the form of truth. In philosophy art, poetry
and religion thus receive their justification; and philosophy documents its own
validity by documenting theirs, which is why it was also said above that it could not
itself do without them; for if it is precisely thereby to show itself as philosophy,
that it sees in \emph{the substantial} the \emph{concept}, then it can no more do
without the former than without any other moment in its development.

But --- one will perhaps object --- if art, poetry and religion are to regain the
significance which they formerly had for humanity, must this not happen through their
own infinite power? How is it conceivable that they should not through this, but
through an outside authority, philosophy, be installed in their lost rights? --- To this the answer is
that philosophy is not outside them, but in them, that it is their own immanent
% --- p. 150 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{150}power, which they need only themselves develop. They all three contain truth, and
consequently philosophy; this is their common substance, and it is through this that
they have formerly proclaimed their divine origin. But substance is the obscure
truth, veiled under its accidents. In preceding ages, when so much that has now
developed was still enveloped in an obscure germ, the obscure substance itself could
be a sufficient light in this darkness. But just as the light that shines for us at
night is scarcely noticed by the rays of day, so in an age in which so much else has
developed from its germ, truth, so long as it is substance, and consequently in
accidental form, must shine with a dim light; and the age therefore rightly demands
that it develop out of its swaddling, in order to become the \emph{naked}, the
transparent truth, which has cast off its accidental dress, so that its form no
longer \emph{veils} it, but, as nakedness, shows, indeed \emph{is} it itself. By this
act it documents that it really was the substance; for if art, poetry and religion
could not develop into philosophy, this would mean in other words that substance
could not develop into the concept, and that it consequently was not substance. Then
those higher activities would be without substance, i.e.\ empty of content, and thus
not be what we have taken them for, but on the contrary be false directions of the
human spirit. If, then, in an age that demands to \emph{know} what the preceding ones
have \emph{felt} and \emph{believed}, they are to show their valid title, they must
bring their truth \emph{to light}, i.e.\ develop into the form of the concept, in
which they themselves can be comprehended; only thereby do we gain certainty that our
feeling, our faith was not empty, but really filled with the substantial content; and
it is consequently so far from knowledge's wishing to put an end to feeling and faith
that on the contrary it will make them firm, for only when we know that they contain
truth can we surrender ourselves undisturbed to them.

% --- p. 151 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{151}As regards religion in particular, it coincides with philosophy insofar as it itself
contains it; but insofar as this is a \emph{content} in it, against which it itself
constitutes the form, it is thereby different from philosophy, which presents the
same content, but in its own form, not different from the content, and thus not as a
content. In regarding religion as different from philosophy, one regards it in its
own \emph{determinate} form, whereby it distinguishes itself from this. The form of
religion is thus, so far, religion itself, that in which it consists, or its essence;
but on the other side the form, as accidental, and as opposed to the substantial
content, is itself inessential; so that the essence of religion can be said to be the
inessential. Since, further, the form of religion is a \emph{determinate} form,
against which other equally determinate forms must be set, whereas that of philosophy
is rather form itself, in its infinity, or as the sole form, in which all determinate
differences are sublated; and since, moreover, the finite is precisely the
determinate, i.e.\ that which excludes all other determinations: it follows from this
that religion, although it, like philosophy, has the infinite for its object,
nevertheless, insofar as it is \emph{different} from philosophy, presents the
infinite from the standpoint of \emph{finitude}. Since, then, what is characteristic
of religion (in its opposition to philosophy), its nature or its essence, is posited
in its determinateness, finitude, limit or restriction, it is the nature of religion
to be \emph{a determinate religion}. To be sure, philosophy too in each of its
systems is a determinate philosophy, but this accidentality is inessential for
philosophy, whereas it is essential for religion. The difference of the systems is
sublated in philosophy itself, and every system is philosophical only in that it
contains more than its own determinateness; but the different religions are not
sublated in religion itself, since this, which precisely
% --- p. 152 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{152}consists in determinateness, would thereby itself be sublated. One can deny all
the philosophical systems, but in this denial there lies that one concedes philosophy
itself; on the other hand one cannot deny all the individual religions without
denying religion itself; for only he who has a certain determinate religion has
religion in general. Just as the different philosophical systems are --- as remarked
before --- all the same philosophy at different stages of development, so also all
religions contain the same God, and consequently the same truth, and in this religion
is identical with philosophy. But since the difference between the two lies in this,
that religion makes the accidental into the essential, the difference between the
different religions becomes an essential difference (since it is grounded in the
accidental), so that when a certain determinate religion is declared the true
religion, the others must necessarily be declared false, --- a relation which by no
means takes place between the different philosophies. When, therefore, one extols the
tolerance of the Christian religion, one can be right insofar as it is far from
demanding that the adherents of the other religions be persecuted with fire and
sword; but if by tolerance one understands that it concedes to the other religions
the same substantial essence that it itself possesses, one is obviously mistaken; in
this sense it is not tolerant, and \emph{cannot} and \emph{ought} not be so, for it
must, like every religion, posit its essence in the accidental difference that takes
place between it and others.

Different, on the other hand, is the relation in which art and poetry stand to
philosophy. These do not present --- as religion does --- the infinite from the
standpoint of finitude. In them, therefore, the accidental form is the inessential.
The Muses are sisters: one art, one kind of poetry does not stand hostile to the
other; all are equally good, for they all have the same substantial essence. In this
they distinguish themselves from religion, and agree with philosophy. But
% --- p. 153 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{153}their difference from the latter must not be overlooked either: The accidental and
inessential form by which the kinds of art and of poetry distinguish themselves is
for each of these in turn the essential form (the form that is set against matter,
not against substance); thus it is in each of them the sole or necessary form, and
just as essential as their very essence. (Colors, tones, language, etc.). But none
the less it can be imperfect, i.e.\ separated from the essence (the form separated
from the material), so that skill and virtuosity are needed to unite them. Hereby the
work of art or of poetry is placed under the category of the infinite approximation
to an ideal; and hereby art and poetry are different from philosophy, in which
material and form, thought and its organization cannot be separated, because they are
One, and the ideal consequently is not sought, but is originally realized.

Philosophy is thus the ground in which all our aims, finite as well as infinite, find
their truth, and thereby their justification and claim to validity. When one
recognizes that truth is the common, substantial content which unites them, whereas
what separates them is only their accidental form --- and this one recognizes by
\emph{comprehending} them, i.e.\ by grasping their common substance as \emph{concept},
in which the accidental form with its differences is sublated, and this is philosophy
--- then they will all subsist peacefully side by side, for then each of them is
determined by its boundary, and in this limitation it has its freedom, its security
that it will no more be overstepped by the others than it will itself overstep
theirs; and the only unlimited is then truth, in which all those limitations rest in
sublated independence, and therefore in tranquil existence. For just as in the
bodily organic world disease sets in when a single activity, a moment in life, will
assert itself as the whole, whereas life itself is the only activity that has this
absolute right, so also the moral
% --- p. 154 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{154}world is sick, when finite activities, though finite in different ways or to
different degrees, e.g.\ politics and religion, each for itself will make itself the
infinite, the sole, whereas only the moral \emph{life}, truth, is really the infinite
and the sole. Every disease is a crisis, and under this latter designation the
character of the age was also indicated, as it was remarked that, as long as it was in
its crisis, it could not have its philosophy. But on the other hand its crisis too
cannot be sublated except by philosophy. A disease cannot cease until health has set
in, but health can no more set in until the disease has ceased. This circle takes
place in all real activity; and it is only a misunderstanding when one believes that
something is the absolutely first therein; for what is the first must express this
precisely by being the last; and conversely. Every true result is its own
presupposition, every true inference its own premise. Philosophy could not be the
last, in which all finite activities lost themselves, if it were not the first, from
which they all sprang, as from their substantial foundation. Only through this
insight can one understand that art, poetry and religion contain the same substance as
philosophy; for whence should they have got it, if not from this? From philosophy
