they have received it, and to philosophy they deliver it back. If, then, we have
formerly regarded the present crisis of the age as striving toward philosophy, and
seeking its sublation in this, we must now, in order to complete the truth, add that
it is philosophy itself that has called forth this crisis, or that it is philosophy
that leads the deviating directions back to their source. The critical transition of
the age is thus one that is to produce the philosophy of the age; but since it is this
that has called forth that, it must itself be present therein; or in other words: what
is produced is here the producing, and what is sought is the found. It is \emph{found},
% --- p. 155 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{155}because a pair of the supreme representatives of humanity in our times have
already presented it in the name of humanity; it is \emph{sought}, because the
majority of the cultured have not yet appropriated it, are not conscious of it.

\emph{Goethe} and \emph{Hegel} are indubitably the two greatest men that modern times
have produced; no others deserve to such a degree to be called the representatives of
our age, for in their works the whole spiritual life of our age, as \emph{ex}istent
and \emph{pre}sent, i.e.\ comprehending the future in unity with the past, is
concentrated. Therefore, although they have now both been called away from their
earthly life --- and that almost simultaneously, as by a strange coincidence, which in
this last circumstance would complete the parallel between them, even though the one
seemed only to follow the bidding of regular nature, whereas the other was perhaps
snatched away at the culminating point of his activity --- it is nevertheless so far
from the case that they already belong to the past, to the dead, that on the
contrary, as the most present of all spirits, they will be fully understood and
grasped only by the coming generation.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

2. \textsc{Poul Møller} (1793---1838) was for a time absorbed in Hegel's system, whose
significance he found especially in the order and coherence it demanded in our world
of thought. That he left it, or, as Heiberg said, deserted, came partly from his
finding in speculative philosophy no solution of the riddles, but only a reshaping of
them, partly from his missing in it recognition of the reality of individuality, a
reality that only experience lets us catch sight of. He did not produce a coherent
philosophical work; but in his last years he was occupied with reflection on the
relation of our thoughts to our personality, what he called personal truth, and he
made a draft of a treatise on affectation, of which a piece is communicated here.
\end{intro}

% --- p. 156 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{ON PERSONAL TRUTH AND AFFECTATION}\opage{156}

Various profound people in our time make use, in their writings and conversations,
of the word truth, which they seem to take in such a sense that they thereby denote
almost everything that is estimable in human life. On the other side they then
condemn as untruth, or indeed even as inner lie, so many kinds of moods of mind
conflicting with reason that one is naturally brought to raise the question whether
truth, with them, is merely one of the essential sides of morality, or whether all the
determinations of morality are united in it, or whether it is a special virtue
alongside other virtues. This the many who make use of that concept in aphoristic
utterances seem not really to have made clear to themselves; and there is hardly any
systematic work in which that point of view is so carried through that the
indeterminate concept which hovers before those people can be assumed to have its
adequate presentation therein.

A human being's life has a kind of truth when, without dissimulation, it follows its
natural desire; a higher truth it has when it has attained virtue (in the ancient
sense of the word), so that it still draws the content of its actions from the
natural drives, but has yet won such a mastery over them that in their satisfaction
it observes a certain moderation. A still higher stage of personal truth is had by the
life that with pure rational autonomy determines all its resolves. (That this cannot
happen by a merely subjective thinking, but in such a way that the subject recognizes
the rational order, which without its co-operation is in its development in
existence, to be a work of the same reason that is the proper truth of its will, it
does not belong here to prove.) Insofar, finally, as the human being's pure
self-determination is the will hallowed by religiousness, he acts in perfect harmony
with the whole world of reason; he is what he should be, and his life cannot attain
any higher truth. But this truth is nothing other than morality,
% --- p. 157 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{157}and every deviation from it is immorality. Affectation is certainly a kind of this
untruth; but we have to determine more closely what place it occupies in this larger
circle.

The human being who has affectation in his life does not, to that extent, determine
himself with perfect moral freedom; his actions do not have their origin in the true
self, which is his free moral will. His will is determined by some merely natural aim
or other, whereby he is brought to represent a foreign person or to take upon himself
a false role that has not been assigned to him in life.

It belongs, then, in the first place to the essence of affectation that it is a
falsity. But not every falsity is affectation. He who, with clear consciousness that
he is lying or dissembling, makes himself guilty of lie and dissimulation, does not
thereby display any affectation. We by no means say hereby that affectation is any
lesser vice than the dissimulation that is accompanied by clear consciousness. It
will appear in what follows that one degree of affectation presupposes less
immorality than deliberate dissimulation, whereas in another degree of it greater
immorality is displayed. Affectation, then, is not unmixed falsity, but always has an
admixture of self-deception; for it lies in the concept of affectation that the
person strives to be what he cannot be; but he cannot strive after this without at
least for a while imagining to himself that he can be it. But the wholly inculpable
self-deception, which is without any falsity, can just as little be called
affectation, so that the two constituents here indicated, falsity and self-deception,
are always found combined in it.

If we consider the degrees of affectation according to its greater or lesser
connection with the subject's character, then it has the least possible share therein
when it conflicts with the direction of the will, but in the single moment escapes the
person because his virtue has not become a facility. Such an expression of affectation
stands in the same relation to affectation that has become a habit as an intoxication
has to habitual drunkenness, and
% --- p. 158 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{158}there is hardly any mortal who does not, at least in youth, sometimes make himself
guilty of small, half-involuntary dishonesties of this kind. If there is anyone who
even at the beginning of his period of development so preserves his independence that
he even avoids that form of affectation which lies near the border of amiability,
that he e.g.\ never, out of a desire to be in perfect agreement with a friend, male
or female, forces himself to sympathize more with them than he can, then he must
certainly by nature have a very strongly marked individuality; but whether he
therefore has the happiest natural disposition is another question. His gift for
keeping his individuality pure and free of extraneous admixtures may also have its
ground in an egoistic bent to enclose himself merely in his own circle of thought,
and a lack of capacity to open his mind to foreign influence. He who is not able in
intercourse so to give himself over to others that for a while he becomes one with
them, that he goes quite out of himself and loses himself in a foreign circle of
consciousness, can certainly by his reserve save himself from being overwhelmed by
any spiritual power; but the individuality that is rescued only in this way always
becomes highly one-sided and poor. Spiritual perfection can, like physical growth, be
furthered only by the individual's regularly melting together with what is foreign to
it, and apparently sacrificing his self in order to return home enriched to himself
again. But then it may certainly happen that a well-endowed youth, from such a
fruitful self-forgetfulness, in which he has let another dispose over his line of
thought, returns to himself with the semblance of representations and feelings which
are repudiated by his true ego, but which he forces upon himself in order to enjoy
the ravishing pleasure of sympathy. The expressions of affectation that come forth in
this way may often precisely become the ground of a strengthening of the truth of the
personal life, in that he who through inadvertence has overstepped the boundary of his
personality is brought, by dissatisfaction with himself, to a clearer
% --- p. 159 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{159}consciousness of what he feels, wills and knows, and to a more vigorous
self-movement in similar cases. He who has been on the other side of the boundary now
first knows properly where the boundary is. --- Such a passing expression of
affectation, which has its source in a power that blinds feeling, which the human
being, by a subsequent reflection, disowns as something that does not belong to his
essence, and against which he guards himself in future, is a lesser offense than the
lie proper. (\emph{The momentary affectation}.)

A higher degree of affectation takes place in him who has acquired the habit of a
certain kind of false expressions, in that he imagines himself to have certain
opinions, interests or inclinations because for some outward reason or other he
wishes to have them. As when one out of vanity lies himself into a love of some art
or other for which he has no sense, or when relatives and friends of a zealot count,
to themselves and others, as being of like mind with him, although his zeal is
foreign to their heart and under altered circumstances falls away of itself. The
possibility of the self-deception rests here on the logical consistency with which the
assumed role is pursued; but the center that bears such a circle of representations
falls outside the subject himself. This second degree of affectation is in moral
respect not at all less imputable than the lie and dissimulation accompanied by clear
consciousness; for the fact that it is not brought to full consciousness comes merely
% print: itl (misprint for til)
from the party concerned not willing to bring it to consciousness. This is remarked
because a highly incorrect concept of it has come into circulation, the multitude
believing that in self-deception there lies sufficient justification, or at least
excuse, even for open badness --- an opinion whose incorrectness is easily evident
when one pursues it into some of its undeniable consequences. From it would namely
% print: ta (misprint for at)
follow that one could drive one's falsity so far that it again became honesty, and
that he who lies so long that he himself believes his invented tales would at
% --- p. 160 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{160}this stage become better, because his lie is transformed into self-deception.
According to this view a judge could also, in all innocence, let himself be bribed,
since he would merely need, by sophisms, to bring himself to believe that his
benefactor was in the right. The conviction that has its origin in inclination cannot
be particularly well grounded, which already shows itself from the fact that the
effect in general does not last longer than its cause. It is not improbable that he
who finds a literary work bad because he believes that his enemy has written it will
find it very good if he learns that his friend is the author of it. But that in the
first case he found it bad was certainly no innocent error, however much
self-deception there might be in it; for one ought as little in one's assumptions as
in one's actions to follow one's inclinations against reason and truth. Under this
second degree of affectation the human being takes a false element into himself and
distorts his personality, so that its expressions do not hang together with his real
self. Insofar as he thus has a double inner life, one real, which is accessible, and
one apparent, which he wills shall count for himself and others, he leads with
respect to the latter only a life of semblance. (\emph{The fixed affectation}.)

The affectation last mentioned, even where it is driven furthest, can still allow the
person to have a lasting semblance of inner coherence, both for the subject himself
and for others. But such a semblance of consistency can be only momentary in the third
and worst degree of affectation. It takes place where the human being does not have
some feigned trait or other in his character, where he does not have the habit of a
certain kind of affectation, but has a facility in affectation in general, which now
assumes this, now that definite shape. This badness approaches, according to its
greater or lesser development, more or less to complete untruth in the personal life.
If a human being could reach its culminating point, then there would be no abiding
kernel in
% --- p. 161 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{161}his thinking and willing, but there would form itself in every moment of his life a
temporary personality, in order to abolish it in the next. He would probably for the
most part, like certain animals, change color according to his surroundings, and to
that extent be the passive product of his circumstances; but since this is only one of
the shapes of affectation, his course could nevertheless not be calculated by the
simple rule that he should resemble his surroundings, since affectation can also show
itself in a striving to present in one's conduct the peculiar, the unusual. To this
completed lie in the inner life no one can bring it; but if anyone could, it would be
a moral suicide, whereby the human being would have wholly annihilated himself as a
peculiar figure in the moral world. (\emph{The changing affectation}.)

% --- p. 162 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: VI without a stop (as IV and V; the Contents has VI.)
\anthsection{VI} {SØREN KIERKEGAARD}\opage{162}

\begin{intro}

It was above all on theological and aesthetic questions that the Hegelian philosophy
was applied here at home. In purely philosophical terms, apart from Poul Møller,
Sibbern and Kierkegaard made a stand against it. But from the theological side the
attempt was made to apply Hegel's method without paying homage to his system.
Theology was proclaimed as ``a higher unity'' of philosophy and religion. H. L.
Martensen set up (in his dissertation of 1837) the dogma of creation --- in which the
unity of knowledge and power is secured --- as the foundation of a new theory of
knowledge, which was to make it possible to let all spiritual culture, both science
and art, attain its harmonious perfection with the revealed religion as its center.
It was the ideal of Romanticism in its third form, different from the conceptions of
both Steffens and Heiberg. While Martensen, from his theological chair, sought to
lead his hearers through Hegel up to the highest unity of all, to which he believed
he had found the way, his philosophical colleague Rasmus Nielsen, who at this time
was his collaborator, proclaimed theology to be ``the proper and true central
science, in which the theory of philosophy and the practice of religion meet''
(Propædeutisk Logik 1845).

\textsc{Søren} \textsc{Kierkegaard} (1813---1855) asserted, in opposition to this
endeavor, a critical theory of knowledge in the spirit of Kant. But he attacked
theology not merely from the purely scientific side. The idea of that higher unity
rested, according to him, on a volatilization of the seriousness of life and of
existence. The human being who is moved by the unrest and tension of life and gripped
by religion has neither time nor strength for theological speculations. Kierkegaard
protested, then, both on behalf of thought and on behalf of life, and this protest
led him to develop ideas of lasting significance. Not only in philosophical but also
in poetic and religious form he has found expression for his inward and passionate
intellectual life. Of his poetic and religious writings a selection (by Carl Koch)
is already available in the first volume of the present collection.
% --- p. 163 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: Beysning (misprint for Belysning)
\opage{163}Here, then, selected passages are to be given to illuminate his philosophy.
% print: Tii (misprint for Til)
To this, however, there also belongs his doctrine of the stages, i.e. of the
different ways of taking life, the different practical views of life.
By this doctrine he has made an interesting contribution
to descriptive psychology.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. THE PASSION OF THOUGHT AND THE PARADOX \\ (Philosophiske Smuler. 1844).}


Although Socrates applied himself with all his might to gathering knowledge of human
beings and to knowing himself, indeed although for centuries he has been praised as
the human being who knew human beings best, he nevertheless admits that the reason
why he was disinclined to think about the nature of such beings as Pegasus and the
Gorgons was that he was not yet quite clear with himself whether he (the knower of
human beings) was a stranger monster than Typhon or a gentler and simpler being,
by nature partaking of something divine (Cf. Phaedrus § 229 E). This seems a
paradox. Yet one should not think ill of the paradox; for the paradox is the passion
of thought, and the thinker without the paradox is like the lover without passion:
a mediocre fellow. But the highest potency of every passion is always to will its own
downfall, and so it is also the highest passion of the understanding to will the shock\trnote{Danish \textit{Anstød} (verb \textit{støde an}) joins \textit{an-}, `against', to \textit{Stød}, a blow or shock (\textit{et elektrisk Stød}); in everyday use it is also `offense' (\textit{tage Anstød}). It is rendered `shock' to keep both the jolt suffered by the understanding and, behind it, the sense of being scandalized, which Kierkegaard names \textit{Forargelse} in the appendix to this chapter.}, although the shock must in one way or another become its downfall.
This, then, is the highest paradox of thought, to will to discover something that it
cannot itself think. This passion of thought is at bottom present everywhere in
thought, also in the thought of the single individual, insofar as he, thinking, is not
merely himself. But because of habit one does not discover it. Thus the human gait,
as the natural scientists explain, is a continual falling; but a respectable and
sedate man who goes to the office in the morning and home at noon presumably
thinks this an exaggeration, for his
% --- p. 164 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{164}forward striding is after all mediation; how should it occur to him that he is
constantly falling, he who goes straight ahead of his nose.

But let us now, in order to get started, make a bold proposition; let us suppose that
we know what a human being is. Thereby we have the criterion of truth that in the
whole of Greek philosophy was \emph{sought}, or \emph{doubted}, or \emph{postulated}
or \emph{made fruitful}. And is it not remarkable enough that this is how matters
stand with the Greeks; is it not, as it were, a brief epitome of the significance of
Greekness, an epigram it has itself written, and with which it is better served than
with the sometimes very prolix things that are sometimes written about it? Thus the
proposition is well worth assuming, and for another reason also, since we have
already explained it in the two preceding chapters, whereas if one wishes to explain
Socrates otherwise than we have done, one must take good care not to fall into the
snares of the older or the later Greek skepticism. Unless the Socratic theory of
recollection, and of the fact that every single human being is the human being, is
held fast, there stands Sextus Empiricus, ready to make the transition that lies in
``learning'' not merely difficult but impossible; and Protagoras begins where he
left off, that everything is man's measure, understood in such a way that he is the
measure for others, by no means understood Socratically, that the single individual
is the measure for himself, neither more nor less.

So we know, then, what the human being is, and this wisdom, whose value I least of
all shall rate low, can continually become richer and more significant, and thus
the truth also; but then the understanding too stands still --- as Socrates did; for
now the paradoxical passion of the understanding awakes, which wills the shock,
and wills, without rightly understanding itself, its own downfall. Thus it is with
the paradox of erotic love. A human being lives undisturbed in himself, then the
paradox of self-love awakes as love for another, for one who is missed.
% --- p. 165 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{165}(Self-love lies at the ground of, or goes to the ground in, all love, wherefore, if
we wanted to imagine a religion of love, this, as epigrammatic as it would be true,
would presuppose only one condition and assume it as given: to love oneself, in
order then to command: to love the neighbor as oneself.) Just as the lover is changed
by this paradox of love, so that he almost no longer knows himself (the poets bear
witness to this, who are the spokesmen of erotic love, the lovers themselves bear
witness to this, since they permit the poets to take from them only the word, not the
state), so that dimly sensed paradox of the understanding reacts in turn upon the
human being and upon his self-knowledge, so that he, who believed he knew himself,
now no longer knows with certainty whether he is perhaps a more strangely composed
animal than Typhon, or whether he has in his being a milder and more divine part.
(Phaedrus 230 A).

But what, then, is this unknown against which the understanding in its paradoxical passion strikes, and which even disturbs the human being in his self-knowledge? It is
the unknown. But it is, after all, not any human being, insofar as he knows this, nor
any other thing that he knows. So let us call this unknown \emph{the God}. It is
merely a name we give it. To want to prove that this unknown (the God) exists hardly
occurs to the understanding. For if the God does not exist, it is of course
impossible to prove it, but if he does exist, it is folly to want to prove it; since I,
precisely at the moment the proof begins, have presupposed it, not as doubtful ---
which a presupposition cannot be, being a presupposition --- but as settled, since
otherwise I would not begin, readily understanding that the whole would become an
impossibility if he did not exist. If, on the other hand, by the expression ``to prove
the existence of God'' I mean that I want to prove that the unknown that exists is the
God, I express myself less happily; for then I prove nothing, least of all an
existence, but I develop a determination of a concept. In general, to
% --- p. 166 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{166}want to prove that something exists is a difficult business, indeed, what is still
worse for the bold who venture into it, the difficulty is such that renown by no
means awaits him who occupies himself with it. The whole procedure of proof constantly
becomes something quite different, becomes a further development of the inference I
draw from having assumed that what is asked about exists. Thus I constantly infer not
to existence but from existence, whether I move in the world of sensuous palpability
or in that of thought. Thus I do not prove that a stone exists, but that the something
that exists is a stone; the court of law does not prove that a criminal exists, but
proves that the accused, who after all exists, is a criminal. Whether one wants to
call existence an \textit{accessorium,} or the eternal \textit{prius,} it can never be
proved. We shall give ourselves plenty of time; there is, after all, no reason for us
to hurry as there is for those who, from concern for themselves or for the God or for
something else, must hurry to get it proved that it exists. If so, there is indeed
reason to hurry, especially if the person concerned honestly made clear to himself
the danger that he himself or the thing asked about did not exist before he got it
proved, and did not treacherously nourish the secret thought that it nevertheless at
bottom exists, whether he proves it or not.

If one of Napoleon's deeds were to prove Napoleon's existence, this would be most
strange, since his existence explains the deeds, but the deeds do not prove his
existence, unless I have already understood the word ``his'' in such a way that I have
thereby assumed that he exists. Yet Napoleon is only the single individual, and to
that extent no absolute relation obtains between him and his deeds, so that another
also could have performed the same deeds. Perhaps it comes from this that I cannot
infer from the deeds to existence. If I call the deeds Napoleon's deeds, then the proof
is superfluous, since I have already named him; if I ignore this, then I can
% --- p. 167 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{167}never prove from the deeds that they are Napoleon's, but (purely ideally) prove
that such deeds are a great general's etc. Yet between the God and his deeds there is
an absolute relation, God is not a name but a concept, perhaps it comes from this that
his \textit{essentia involvit existentiam.} God's deeds, then, only the God can do;
quite right, but which, then, are the God's deeds? Immediately the deeds from which I
want to prove his existence do not exist at all. Or does it perhaps lie right before
one's nose to see the wisdom in nature, the goodness, or the wisdom in the governance?
Do not the most terrible trials meet one here, and is it not impossible to be done
with all these trials? But from such an order of things I do not want to prove God's
existence, and even if I began, I should never finish, and should besides have to live
constantly \textit{in suspenso,} lest something so terrible suddenly happen that my
little bit of proof be spoiled. So from what deeds is it, then, that I prove it? It is
from the deeds considered ideally, and as they do not immediately show themselves. But
then it is not from the deeds that I prove it, but I merely develop the ideality I
have presupposed; trusting in it I dare even to defy all objections, even those that
have not yet come into being. As I begin, then, I have presupposed the ideality, and
presupposed that I shall succeed in carrying it through; but what is this other than
that I have presupposed that the God exists, and trusting in him it is really that I
begin.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. CRITICISM AND THE PHILOSOPHY OF EXISTENCE \\ (Uvidenskabelig Efterskrift til Philosofiske Smuler. 1846).}


Lessing has said: \emph{Wenn Gott in seiner Rechten}
\emph{alle Wahrheit, und in seiner Linken den einzigen
immer regen Trieb nach Wahrheit, obschon
mit dem Zusatze mich immer und ewig}
% --- p. 168 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{168}\emph{zu irren, verschlossen hielte, und spräche}
\emph{zu mir: wähle! Ich fiele ihm mit Demuth in}
\emph{seine Linke, und sagte: Vater, gieb! die reine
Wahrheit ist ja doch nur für dich allein}!
(cf. Lessing's S. W. 5th vol. p. 100). --- At the time when Lessing said these words
the system was presumably not finished; alas, and now he is dead! Had he lived now,
now that the system is for the most part finished, or at least is in progress and will
be finished by Sunday: believe me, L. would have seized it with both hands; he would
not have had the time and propriety and high spirits to play odd-or-even with God,
as it were, for a joke, and in earnest to choose the left hand. But the system also
has more than what God has in both his hands, already at this moment it has more,
let alone on Sunday, when it will be quite definitely finished.

The words are found in a little essay (Eine Duplik. 1778), occasioned by a pious
man's defense of the history of the resurrection against the attack in the fragments
published by Lessing. It is well enough known that one could not at all figure out
what L.'s purpose was in publishing these fragments. Not even the well-read Chief
Pastor Götze could say definitely which passage in the Apocalypse fitted, indeed
was fulfilled, in Lessing. To that extent Lessing has in a singular way compelled
people to adopt his principle in relation to him. While in those times too there was
otherwise result and readiness enough, one could not at all get Lessing's life taken
and world-historically slaughtered and salted down in a §. He was and remained a
riddle. If anyone now wants to call him forth again --- he gets no further with him.

Here now first an assurance concerning my humble person. As readily as anyone I am
willing to fall down in worship before the system, if only I can catch sight of it.
Hitherto I have not succeeded in this, and though I have young legs, I am nearly
weary of running from Herod to Pilate. A few times I have come quite close to
worship; but behold, in the
% --- p. 169 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{169}moment I had already spread out my handkerchief so as not to soil my trousers
when kneeling, and then quite guilelessly said for the last time to one of the
% print: ogrigtigt (misprint for oprigtigt)
initiated, tell me now truly, is it now also wholly finished, for in that case I
will throw myself down, even if I should ruin a pair of trousers (owing namely to
the heavy traffic to and from the system the road is not a little muddy), I always
got the answer: no, it is probably not quite finished yet. And so it was
postponed again --- with the system and with the kneeling.

System and completeness are about one and the same thing, so that if the system is
not finished it is no system. I have already reminded elsewhere that a system that
is not quite finished is a hypothesis; whereas a half-finished system is nonsense.
If someone now says that this is only a quarrel over words, that the systematizers,
on the contrary, themselves say that the system is not finished, I would merely ask
why they call it a system. Why are they in general double-tongued? In presenting
their summary they say nothing about something being lacking. They thereby also
lead the less expert to assume that everything is finished, unless they write for
readers who are more expert than they themselves, which would presumably seem
unthinkable to the systematizers. But if the building is touched, the builder steps
forward. He is an extremely agreeable man, courteous and kind to the visitor, he
says: yes, we are indeed still building, the system is not yet quite finished. Did
he not know it before, did he not know it at the time when he sent out his
beatifying invitation to all human beings? But if he knew it, why did he not say
it himself, i.e. why did he then not refrain from calling the achieved fragment a
system? For here it is again: a fragment of a system is nonsense. A continued
striving after a system, on the other hand, is a striving; and a striving, indeed a
continued striving, is what Lessing speaks of. And yet surely not a striving after
nothing! On the contrary, Lessing speaks of a striving after truth; and he uses a
% --- p. 170 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{170}remarkable word about this drive toward truth: den einzigen immer regen Trieb. This
word einzig cannot well be understood otherwise than as the infinite, in the same
sense as that it is higher to have one thought, one single thought, than many
thoughts. So they both speak, Lessing and the systematizer, of a continued striving,
only the difference is that Lessing is stupid or true enough to call it a continued
striving, the systematizer clever or untrue enough to call it the system. What would
one judge of this difference in other relations? When Agent Behrend had lost a silk
umbrella, he advertised for a nankeen one; for he thought as follows, says he, if I
say it is a silk umbrella, the finder is more easily tempted to keep it. The
systematizer perhaps thinks as follows: if I call my achievement on the title page
and in the newspaper a continued striving, alas, who will then buy it or admire me;
but if I call it the system, the absolute system, then everyone will buy the system
--- if only the difficulty did not remain that what the systematizer sells is not the
system.

Let us then go on, but let us not make fools of one another. I, Johannes Climacus,
am neither more nor less than a human being; I assume that the one I have the honor
of speaking with is also a human being. If he wants to be Speculation, pure
speculation, then I must give up speaking with him; for in the same moment he
becomes invisible to me and to a human being's weak mortal eye.

Thus: a) \emph{a logical system is possible};
\emph{b) but there can be no system of
existence}.

\begin{center}a.\end{center}

α. If, however, a logical system is to be constructed, care must chiefly be taken
that nothing is admitted which is subject to the dialectic of existence, which thus
only is in the process of coming to be, or of having been, is not in the process of
being. From this
% --- p. 171 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{171}follows quite simply that that peerless and peerlessly admired invention of Hegel's,
to bring movement into logic (quite apart from the fact that at every second place
one misses even his own attempt to make one believe that it is there), is precisely
to confuse logic. It is indeed also odd to lay movement at the foundation in a sphere
where movement is unthinkable, or to let movement explain logic, while logic cannot
explain movement. On this point, however, I am so fortunate as to be able to refer to
a man who thinks soundly and is happily formed by the Greeks (rare qualities in our
time!), a man who has known how to free himself and his thinking from every dragging
and crawling relation to Hegel, whose renown everyone else will profit from if not in
some other way then by going further, i.e. by having taken Hegel up into himself; a
man who has preferred to content himself with Aristotle and with himself --- I mean
Trendelenburg (Logische Untersuchungen). His merit is, among other things, to have
grasped movement as the inexplicable presupposition, as the common element in which
being and thinking are united, and as the continued reciprocity. I cannot here make
any attempt to demonstrate the relation of his conception to the Greeks, to the
Aristotelian, or to what, oddly enough, in a certain sense, though only popularly, has
much similarity with his presentation: a little passage in Plutarch's treatise on
Isis and Osiris. It is by no means my opinion that the Hegelian philosophy has not had
a beneficial influence on Trendelenburg, but the fortunate thing is that he has seen
that it does not do to want to improve upon Hegel's building, to go further etc. (a
mendacious way in which many a bungler in our time arrogates to himself Hegel's
celebrity and fraternizes with him in the manner of a lazzarone); and on the other
hand, that, sober as a Greek thinker, without promising everything, without wanting to
beatify mankind, he accomplishes much and beatifies him who needed his guidance in
% print: ttil (misprint for til)
the knowledge of the Greeks.

In a logical system nothing must be admitted that has a relation
% --- p. 172 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{172}to existence that is not indifferent to existence. The infinite preponderance that
the logical has over all thinking by being the objective is limited again by the fact
that, seen subjectively, it is a hypothesis, precisely because in the sense of
actuality\trnote{Danish \textit{Virkelighed} (adjective \textit{virkelig}, from \textit{virke}, to act, to be at work) does duty both for `reality', as against illusion or fancy, and for `actuality', the word by which the Danish Hegelians rendered Hegel's \textit{Wirklichkeit}, as against possibility (\textit{Mulighed}); \textit{i Virkeligheden} is also the everyday `in fact'. Kierkegaard plays these senses against one another. In his excerpts the noun is rendered `actuality' throughout, so that its opposition to possibility, and to Hegel, stays visible; elsewhere in this volume it is usually `reality'.} it is indifferent to existence. This duality is the difference of the
logical from the mathematical, which has no relation at all to or from existence, but
has only objectivity --- not objectivity and the hypothetical as the unity and
contradiction in which it relates itself negatively to existence.

The logical system must not be a mystification, a ventriloquism, in which the content
of existence emerges slyly and surreptitiously, where logical thought gives pause and
finds what the Herr Professor or Licentiate has had up his sleeve here. A stricter
judgment between these can be had by answering the question in what sense the
category is the abbreviation of existence, whether logical thinking is abstract
after existence or abstract without any relation to existence. This question I should
like to treat somewhat more fully elsewhere; and even if it were not satisfactorily
answered, it is still something that one asks in this way.

β. The dialectic of the beginning must be made clear. The almost amusing thing about
it, that the beginning is and again is not, because it is the beginning, this true
dialectical remark has now long enough been like a game played in Hegelian society.

The system begins, it is said, with the immediate; some are even, for lack of being
dialectical, oratorical enough to say the most immediate of all, although precisely
the comparative reflection contained in this could become dangerous for the
beginning. The system begins with the immediate, and therefore without presuppositions,
and therefore absolutely, i.e. the beginning of the system is the absolute beginning.
This is quite right, and has also been sufficiently admired. But why has one not,
before beginning the system
% --- p. 173 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{173}put the other equally important, precisely equally important question, and made it
clear to oneself, and respected what plainly lies in it: \emph{how does the system begin}
\emph{with the immediate, i.e. does it begin immediately
with it}? To this one must surely answer unconditionally No. If the system is assumed
to be after existence (whereby a confusion with a system of existence is occasioned),
then the system comes afterward, and so does not begin immediately with the immediate
with which existence began, even if in another sense it did not begin with it, because
the immediate never is, but is sublated when it is. The beginning of the system that
begins with the immediate \emph{is then itself reached through a reflection}.

Here lies the difficulty, for when one does not deceitfully or thoughtlessly or in
short-winded hurry to get the system finished let go of the one thought, then in all
its simplicity it is capable of deciding that there can be no system of existence,
and that the logical system must not boast of an absolute beginning, because such a
one is, like pure being, a pure chimera.

For when one cannot begin immediately with the immediate (which would be to think it
as an occurrence or as a miracle, i.e. which is not to be thought), but this
beginning must be reached through a reflection, then it is asked quite simply (alas,
if only I am not put in the corner of shame for my simplicity, because everyone can
understand my question --- and therefore must be ashamed of the questioner's popular
knowledge): how do I get that reflection stopped which was set in motion to reach
that beginning? For reflection has the remarkable property that it is infinite. But
that it is infinite means in any case that it cannot be stopped by itself, because in
being supposed to stop of itself it uses itself, and thus is stopped only in the same
way as a disease would be cured if it were itself permitted to determine the
medicine,
% --- p. 174 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{174}i.e. the disease were nourished. Perhaps this infinity of reflection is the bad
infinity --- then we are soon done, for the bad infinity is supposed to be something
contemptible that one, the sooner the better, must give up. May I not on this occasion
put a question. Whence does it come that Hegel and all Hegelians, who are otherwise
supposed to be dialecticians, become angry on this point, indeed angry as Germans?
Or is it a dialectical determination: the bad? From where does such a predicate get
into logic? How do scorn and contempt and scare tactics find a place as permissible
means of motion in logic, so that the absolute beginning is assumed by the single
individual because he is afraid of what neighbor and next-door neighbor will think
of him if he does not? Is ``bad'' not an ethical category? What am I saying in
speaking of the bad infinity? I accuse the individual concerned of not wanting to stop
the infinity of reflection. I demand something of him, then? But in genuinely
speculative fashion I assume, on the contrary, that reflection stops itself. Why do I
then demand something of him? And what do I demand of him? I demand a resolution. And
in this I do well, for only thus can reflection be stopped, but on the other hand a
philosopher never does well to make fools of people, and in one moment to let
reflection stop itself in the absolute beginning, and in the next moment to scorn a
human being whose only fault is that he is stupid enough to believe the first, to
scorn him, in order in that way to help him to the absolute beginning, which thus
takes place in two ways. But if a resolution is demanded, presuppositionlessness is
given up. Only when reflection is stopped can the beginning take place, and
reflection can be stopped only by something else, and this something else is
something quite other than the logical, since it is a resolution. And only when the
beginning by which reflection stops is a breakthrough, so that the absolute beginning
itself breaks forth through the infinitely continued reflection, only then is the
beginning presuppositionless. If, on the other hand, it is a break by which
reflection is interrupted so that the
% --- p. 175 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{175}beginning can come forth, then this beginning is not absolute, since it has taken
place by a μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος.

When the beginning with the immediate is reached through a reflection, then the
immediate must mean something other than otherwise. Hegelian logicians have rightly
seen this, and they therefore define the immediate with which logic begins thus: the
most abstract that remains after an exhaustive abstraction. Against this definition
there is nothing to object, but there is against the failure to respect what one says
oneself, for this definition says precisely, indirectly, that there is no absolute
beginning. How, I hear someone say, when everything has been abstracted from, is it
not then etc. --- yes --- when everything has been abstracted from. Let us be human
beings. This act of abstraction is, like that act of reflection, infinite, thus, how
do I get it stopped --- and it is indeed only when\dots{}. that\dots{}. Let us even
venture a thought-experiment. Let that act of infinite abstraction be \textit{in actu},
the beginning is not, after all, the act of abstraction, but it comes after. But with
what do I then begin, since everything has been abstracted from? Alas, here perhaps a
Hegelian would sink moved to my breast, and blissfully stammer: with nothing. And that
is indeed what the system says, that it begins with nothing. But I should have to put
my second question, how do I begin with this nothing? For if the act of infinite
abstraction is not one of those foolish pranks of which one can well do two at once,
if on the contrary it is the most strenuous work that can be done, what then? Then all
my strength goes to holding it fast. If I let go any part of my strength, then I am
not abstracting from everything. If I then begin under this presupposition, I do not
begin with nothing, precisely because in the moment of beginning I did not abstract
from everything. That is to say, if it is possible for a human being, thinking, to
abstract from everything, then it is impossible for him to do more, since it, if it
does not altogether surpass human strength, in any case absolutely exhausts
% --- p. 176 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{176}it. To grow weary of the act of abstraction, and thus come to begin, are mere
grocers' explanations, which do not take it so precisely with a small difficulty. The
statement itself, to begin with nothing, quite apart from its relation to the infinite
act of abstraction, is deceitful. To begin with nothing is, namely, neither more nor
less than a new paraphrase of the dialectic of the beginning itself. The beginning is,
and again it is not, precisely because it is the beginning; this can also be expressed
thus: the beginning begins with nothing. It is only a new expression, not a single
step further. In the one case I think the beginning merely \textit{in abstracto,} in
the other case I think the relation of the equally abstract beginning to a something
with which one begins; and quite rightly it turns out that this something, indeed the
only something that corresponds to such a beginning, is nothing. But this is merely a
tautological paraphrase of the other proposition: the beginning is not. The beginning
is not, and the beginning begins with nothing are altogether identical propositions,
and I do not get off the spot.

What if, instead of speaking or dreaming of an absolute beginning, we speak of a leap.
To want to be content with a mostly-as-good-as, with being able to say that one almost
has it, with sleeping on it till tomorrow and then being able to say it, shows only
that one is akin to Trop, who little by little came so far as to assume: that having
been close to getting a juridical examination was having got it. Everyone laughs at
this, but when in the realm of truth, in the sanctuary of science, one reasons
speculatively in the same way: then it is good philosophy, genuine speculative
philosophy. For Lessing was no speculative philosopher, he therefore assumed the
opposite, that an infinitely small distance makes the ditch infinitely wide, because
the leap itself makes the ditch so wide.

It is odd enough: Hegelians, who in logic know that reflection is stopped by itself,
that a doubting of everything turns over by itself into its opposite (a true
% --- p. 177 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{177}sailor's yarn, i.e. truly a sailor's yarn), know on the other hand for everyday use, when
they are agreeable people, when they are like the rest of us, only, as I shall always
willingly concede, more learned, more talented etc., know that reflection can be
stopped only by a leap. Let us dwell a moment here. When the individual does not stop
reflection, he becomes infinitized in reflection, i.e. no decision occurs. By thus
losing his way in reflection the individual becomes really objective, he loses more
and more the decision of subjectivity and the return into himself. And yet one will
assume that reflection can objectively stop itself, whereas it is the other way
around; objectively it is not to be stopped, and when it stops subjectively, it does
not stop itself, but it is the subject who stops it.

As soon as Rötscher, for example (who in his book on Aristophanes does understand the
necessity of transition in world-historical development, and otherwise has presumably
understood in logic reflection's passing through itself to the absolute beginning)
sets himself the task of explaining Hamlet, he knows that reflection is stopped only
by a resolution, he does not assume (shall I say strangely enough?) strangely enough,
he does not assume that Hamlet, by continuing to reflect, finally came to the
absolute beginning; but in logic he assumes (shall I say strangely enough) strangely
enough, there he presumably assumes that reflection's passing through itself stops at
the absolute beginning. This I do not understand, and it pains me not to understand
it, precisely because I cherish admiration for Rötscher's talent, for his classical
culture, for his tasteful and yet primitive conception of psychological phenomena.

What has been said here about the beginning of logic (for that the same shows that
there is no system of existence is to be pursued more closely under b) is very simple
and plain; I become almost quite embarrassed in saying it, or embarrassed that I must
say it, embarrassed at my situation, that a poor pamphleteer,
% --- p. 178 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{178}who would rather lie worshipping on his knees before the system, should be forced
to say something like this. What has been said could also be said in another way,
whereby it might make an impression on one or another, in that the presentation more
specially recalled the scientific conflicts of a moment now past. The question would
then become what significance the Hegelian Phenomenology has for the system, whether
it is an introduction, whether it stands outside; if it is an introduction, whether
it is then taken up again into the system; further, whether Hegel has not even the
astounding merit of having written not merely the system, but two, indeed three
systems, for which there always belongs a peerless systematic head, and which
nevertheless seems to be the case, as the system becomes finished more than once etc.
All this has at bottom been said often enough, but it has also often been said in a
disturbing way. A big book has been written about it, saying first everything Hegel
has said, then taking account of this and that later thing, all of which merely
distracts and casts a distracting prolixity over what can be said quite briefly.

γ. It would be desirable if, in order to cast light on logic, one oriented oneself
psychologically in what the state of mind is of him who thinks the logical, what kind
of dying-away from himself is required for this, how far imagination plays a role
in it. This is again a poor and highly simple remark, but for that reason it may be
quite true and by no means superfluous: that a philosopher has by degrees become such
a fantastic being that hardly the most extravagant imagination has invented anything
so fabulous. How does the empirical I relate in general to the pure I-I. He who
wishes to be a philosopher presumably likes to know a little about this, and wishes
above all not to become a ridiculous being by ein, zwei, drei Kokolorum being
transformed into speculation. If he who occupies himself with logical thinking is
also human enough not to forget that he, even if he were finished with the system, is
an
% --- p. 179 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{179}existing individual, then fantasticality and charlatanry will gradually vanish.
And even if an eminently logical head is needed to remold Hegel's Logic, only true
human understanding is needed in him who once with enthusiasm believed the great
thing Hegel said he did, and showed his enthusiasm by believing it, and his
enthusiasm for Hegel by crediting him with it; only true human understanding is
needed to see that Hegel in many places has conducted himself indefensibly, not
toward grocers, who after all believe only half of what a man says, but toward
enthusiastic young men who believed him. Even if such a young man has not been
distinguished by outstanding gifts, when he nevertheless has had enthusiasm to
believe the highest, which was indeed said of Hegel, when he has had enthusiasm in the
moment of difficulty to despair of himself in order not to give up Hegel --- when such
a one comes to himself again, he has a right to demand this Nemesis, that laughter
consume in Hegel what laughter has a rightful claim on, and such a young man has
nevertheless upheld Hegel quite differently from many an adherent who by deceitful
asides made Hegel now everything, now a trifle.

\begin{center}b.\end{center}

A system of existence cannot be given. Is such a one, then, not? By no means. That
does not lie in what has been said either. Existence itself is a system --- for God,
but cannot be so for any existing spirit. System and completeness correspond to each
other, but existence is precisely the opposite. Seen abstractly, system and existence
cannot be thought together, because the systematic thought, in order to think
existence, must think it as sublated, thus not as existing. Existence is that which
spaces out, which falls asunder: the systematic is the completeness that closes
together.

In actuality a disappointment now occurs,
% --- p. 180 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{180}an illusion of the senses, which the Crumbs also sought to point out, and to
which I must refer, namely to the question whether the past is more necessary than the
future. For when an existence has been traversed, it is after all finished, it is
after all concluded, and to that extent it has fallen to the systematic conception.
Quite right --- but for whom? He who is himself existing cannot win that completeness
outside of existence which corresponds to the eternity into which the past has entered.
If a thinker graciously consents to be absent-minded enough to forget that he is
himself existing, speculation and absent-mindedness are nevertheless not quite the
same. On the contrary, the fact that he is himself existing hints at the claim of
existence upon him, that his, indeed if he is great, that his existence in his own
time may again as a past have the validity of completeness for the systematic
thinker. But who, then, is this systematic thinker? Why, he is the one who is himself
outside existence and yet in existence, who in his eternity is eternally concluded,
and yet includes existence in himself --- it is God. To what end the deception!
Because the world has now existed for 6000 years, existence therefore does not at all
have the same claim upon the existing person as it has always had, which is not that
he in a fancy should be a contemplating spirit, but in actuality an existing
spirit. All understanding comes afterward. While the now-existing person undeniably
comes afterward in relation to the 6000 years that went before, if we assumed that he
came to understand these systematically, the singularly ironic thing would result that
he did not come to understand himself existing, because he himself got no existence,
because he himself had nothing that was to be understood afterwards. From this it
would follow that such a thinker would have to be either our Lord or a fantastic
quodlibet. Everyone surely sees the immorality in this, and then surely everyone also
sees that what another author has remarked about the Hegelian system is in order: that
through him one got a system, the absolute system finished --- without having an
ethics.
% --- p. 181 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{181}Let us only smile at the ethical-religious fantasies of the Middle Ages in
asceticism and the like, but let us above all not forget that the speculative low-comic
extravagance of becoming I-I, and then nevertheless qua human being often such a
Philistine that no enthusiast would care to have led such a life --- is just as
ridiculous.

Let us then, concerning the impossibility of a system of existence, ask quite simply
as a Greek youth would ask his teacher (and if the lofty wisdom can otherwise explain
everything, but not answer a simple question, then one sees that the world is out of
joint): who is to write or finish such a system? Presumably a human being, unless we
are to start again on the singular talk that a human being becomes Speculation,
becomes the subject-object. Thus a human being --- and presumably a living one, i.e.
an existing human being. Or is speculation, which produces the system, the common
striving of the various thinkers: in what last conclusion does this community close
together, how does it come to light? presumably through a human being? How, then,
do the individual thinkers in turn relate to this striving, what are the intermediate
determinations here between the individual and the world-historical, and what kind of
person, again, is he who draws them all on the systematic thread? Is he a human being
or is he Speculation? But if he is a human being, he is existing. For the existing
person there are now in general two ways: he can either do everything to forget that
he is existing, whereby he succeeds in becoming comic (the comic contradiction of
wanting to be what one is not, e.g., that a human being wants to be a bird, is no more
comic than that of not wanting to be what one is, as \textit{in casu} existing, as one
also finds it comic in ordinary speech when someone forgets what his name is, which
does not so much mean forgetting his name as the peculiarity of his being), because
existence has the remarkable property that the existing person exists whether he
wants to or not; or he can
% --- p. 182 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{182}turn all his attention upon this, that he is existing. It is from this side that
the objection must first of all be made against modern speculation, that it has not a
false presupposition, but a comic presupposition, occasioned by its having in a kind
of world-historical absent-mindedness forgotten what it is to be a human being, not
what it is to be a human being in general, such a thing one might indeed still get the
speculators to concede, but what it is that you and I and he, we are human beings each
for himself.

The existing person who directs all his attention to the fact that \emph{he} is
existing, to him that saying of Lessing's about the continued striving will also smile
as a fine utterance, by which the inventor presumably did not acquire immortal
renown, because it is very simple, but which every attentive person must find true.
The existing person who forgets that he is existing will become more and more
absent-minded, and as people sometimes deposit the fruit of their \textit{otium} in
writings, so we may expect as the fruit of his absent-mindedness the expected system
of existence --- presumably not all of us, but only those who are nearly as
absent-minded as he. While, then, the Hegelian system in absent-mindedness goes on and
becomes a system of existence, indeed, what is more, becomes finished --- without
having an ethics (where existence precisely has its home), that simpler philosophy,
which is expounded by an existing person to existing persons, will especially bring
forward the ethical.

As soon as it is remembered that to philosophize is not to speak fantastically to
fantastic beings, but that one speaks to existing persons; that it is thus not to be
decided fantastically \textit{in abstracto} whether the continued striving is something
lesser than systematic completeness, but that the question is what existing beings
must content themselves with, insofar as they are existing, then the continued
striving will be the only thing that does not contain a disappointment. Even if
someone has attained the highest, the repetition by which he must fulfill his
existence, if he is not to go backward (or become a fantastic
% --- p. 183 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{183}being), will again be a continued striving, because here again completeness is
removed and postponed. It is with this as with the Platonic conception of loving, that
it is a want, and that not only he feels want who craves something he does not have,
but also he who wishes the continued possession of what he has. Speculatively-fantastically
and aesthetically-fantastically one has a positive completeness in the system and in
the fifth act of the drama, but such completeness is only for fantastic beings.


The continued striving is the yield of the existing subject's ethical view of life. The
continued striving must therefore not be understood metaphysically; but there is indeed
no individual either that exists metaphysically. Thus one could, by a misunderstanding,
form an opposition between systematic completeness and the continued striving after
truth. One could then, and has perhaps even tried, to recall the Greek idea of always
wanting to be a learner. This is, however, merely a misunderstanding in this sphere.
Understood ethically, on the other hand, the continued striving is the consciousness of
being existing, and the continued learning the expression of the incessant realization,
which at no moment is finished as long as the subject is existing, which this subject
is precisely conscious of and therefore not deceived by. But Greek philosophy had a
constant relation to ethics. One therefore also did not find that always wanting to be
a learner was a great discovery or an enthusiastic undertaking of a single eminent
person, since it was neither more nor less than the understanding that one is
existing, which is not meritorious to become conscious of, but thoughtless to forget.

\sectionrule

\emph{When the question of truth is asked objectively,
reflection is objective on the truth
as an object to which the}
% --- p. 184 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{184}\emph{knower relates. Reflection is not on the relation, but}
\emph{on its being the truth, the true, to which he relates. When}
\emph{what he relates to is merely the truth, the true, then the subject is in the truth}.
\emph{When the question of truth is asked subjectively},
\emph{reflection is subjective on the individual's}
\emph{relation; when merely the how of this relation
is in truth, then the individual is in truth},
\emph{even if he were thus to relate to untruth}.
Let us take as an example the knowledge of God. Objectively, reflection is on its
being the true God; subjectively, on the individual's relating to a something
\emph{in such a way} that his relation is in truth a God-relation. On which of the two
sides, then, is the truth? Alas, must we not here take refuge in mediation, and say:
it is on neither of the sides, it is in the mediation? Excellently said, if only
someone could say how an existing person goes about being in mediation; for to be in
mediation is to be finished, to exist is to become. An existing person also cannot be
in two places at once, be subject-object. When he comes nearest to being in two places
at once, he is in passion, but passion is only momentary, and passion is precisely the
highest of subjectivity. --- The existing person who chooses the objective way now
enters into the whole approximating deliberation that would objectively bring God
forth, which in all eternity is not reached, because God is subject, and therefore
exists only for subjectivity in inwardness. The existing person who chooses the
subjective way grasps in the same moment the whole dialectical difficulty in that he
must use some time, perhaps a long time, to find God objectively; he grasps this
dialectical difficulty in all its pain, because he needs God in the same moment,
because every moment is wasted in which he does not have God. In the same moment he
has God not by virtue of any objective deliberation, but by virtue of the infinite
% --- p. 185 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{185}passion of inwardness. The objective person is not troubled by such dialectical
difficulties as this: what it means to devote an entire time of inquiry to finding
God --- since it was after all possible that the inquirer died tomorrow, and if he
lived he could after all not regard God as something one takes along on occasion,
since God is precisely something one takes along \textit{a tout prix}, which in the
understanding of passion is precisely the true relation of inwardness to God.

It is at this dialectically so difficult point that the way turns off for him who
knows what it is to dialecticize and to dialecticize existingly, which is something
other than to sit as a fantastic being at the writing desk and write what one has
never done oneself, which is something other than to write \textit{de omnibus
dubitandum,} and oneself existing be as credulous as the most sensuous human being ---
here it is that the way turns off, and the change is this, that whereas the objective
knowledge goes comfortably forward along the long road of approximation, not even
spurred on by passion, for the subjective knowledge every delay is perilous to life,
and the decision so infinitely important that it is immediately as urgent as if the
opportunity had already gone unused.

Now when the sum to be reckoned is this: where is there most truth, whether on the
side of him (and to be at once equally on both sides is, as said, not granted to an
existing person, but is only a beatifying fancy for an imagined I-I) who solely
objectively seeks the true God and the approximating truth of the representation of
God, or on the side of him who is infinitely concerned that he in truth relates to God
with the infinite passion of need: then the answer cannot be doubtful for anyone who
is not utterly muddled by means of science. If someone who lives in the midst of
Christendom goes up to the house of God, to the house of the true God with the true
representation of God in knowledge, and now prays, but prays in untruth; and when
another lives in an idolatrous land, but prays with the whole passion of infinity,
although his
% --- p. 186 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{186}eye rests on the image of an idol: where is there then most truth? The one prays
in truth to God, although he worships an idol; the other prays in untruth to the true
God, and therefore worships in truth an idol.

When one inquires objectively into immortality, another sets the passion of infinity
upon the uncertainty: where is there then most truth, and who has most certainty? The
one has once for all entered upon an approximating that never ends, for the certainty
of immortality lies precisely in subjectivity; the other is immortal and fights
precisely for that reason by striving against the uncertainty. Let us consider
Socrates. Nowadays everyone, after all, bungles together a few proofs, one has more,
another fewer. But Socrates! He leaves the question objectively problematic: if there
is an immortality. Was he then a doubter in comparison with one of the modern thinkers
of three proofs? By no means. Upon this ``if'' he stakes his whole life, he ventures
to die, and he has arranged his whole life with the passion of infinity in such a way
that it had to be found acceptable --- if there is an immortality. Is there any better
proof of the soul's immortality? But those who have three proofs do not arrange their
life according to them at all; if there is an immortality, it must be disgusted by that
way of living: is there any better counterproof against the three proofs? The
``crumb'' of uncertainty helped Socrates, because he himself helped with the passion of
infinity; the three proofs profit the others nothing at all, because they are and
remain bores, and for lack of proving anything else have by their three proofs proved
precisely that. Thus perhaps a girl, too, with a faint hope of being loved by the
beloved, has possessed all the sweetness of being in love, because she herself staked
everything on this faint hope: many a married lady, on the other hand, who more than
once has succumbed to love's strongest expression, has probably had proofs, and yet,
oddly enough, has not possessed the \textit{quod
erat demonstrandum.} The Socratic ignorance was thus the expression, held fast with
the whole passion of inwardness, of the fact
% --- p. 187 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{187}that eternal salvation relates to an existing person, and therefore must become a
paradox to him as long as he exists, and yet it might well be possible that there was
in the Socratic ignorance in Socrates more truth than in all the objective truth of
the system, which coquettes with the demands of the time and accommodates itself to
Privatdocents.

\emph{Objectively the accent falls on}: \textit{\emph{what}} \emph{is said};
\emph{subjectively}: \emph{how} \emph{it is said}. Already aesthetically
this distinction holds, and is expressed definitely thus,
that what is truth can in this one's and that one's mouth become
untruth. This distinction is particularly to be heeded in these times, for if one were
to express in one single proposition the difference between antiquity and our time,
one would have to say that in antiquity only a few knew the true, now everyone
knows it, but inwardness stands in an inverse relation to this.
Aesthetically the contradiction that arises from truth's becoming untruth in this
one's and that one's mouth is best grasped comically.
Ethically-religiously the accent falls on how, again; yet this is not understood of
decorum, modulation, delivery etc., but is understood of the existing person's
relation to what is said in his very existence. Objectively one asks merely about the
determinations of thought, subjectively about inwardness. At its maximum this how is
the passion of infinity, and the passion of infinity is itself the truth. But the
passion of infinity is precisely subjectivity, and thus subjectivity is the truth.
Seen objectively there is no infinite decision, and thus it is objectively correct that
the difference between good and evil is canceled by the principle of contradiction,
and thereby also the infinite difference between truth and lie. Only in subjectivity
is there decision, whereas wanting to become objective is untruth. The passion of
infinity is the decisive thing, not its content, for its content is precisely itself.
Thus the subjective how and subjectivity is the truth.

But the how that is subjectively accentuated is at the same time,
% --- p. 188 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{188}precisely because the subject is existing, dialectical in the direction of
time. In the moment of passion's decision, where the way turns off from objective
knowledge, it looks as if the infinite decision were thereby finished. But in the same
moment the existing person is in temporality, and the subjective how is transformed
into a striving, which is impelled and repeatedly refreshed by the decisive passion of
infinity, but which is nevertheless a striving.

When subjectivity is the truth, the determination of truth must at the same time
contain in itself an expression of the opposition to objectivity, a reminder of that
parting of the ways, and this expression indicates at the same time the elasticity of
inwardness. Here is such a definition of truth: \emph{the objective}
\emph{uncertainty, held fast in the appropriation of the most passionate
inwardness, is the truth},
the highest truth there is for \emph{existing persons}. There where
the way turns off (and where it is cannot be said objectively,
since it is precisely subjectivity), objective knowledge is
put to rest. Objectively he then has only the uncertainty, but precisely
this tightens the infinite passion of inwardness, and
the truth is precisely this venture, with the passion of infinity to choose the
objectively uncertain. I contemplate nature in order to find God, I do also see
omnipotence and wisdom, but I see at the same time much else that frightens and
disturbs. \textit{Summa Summarum} of this is the objective uncertainty, but
precisely for that reason the inwardness is so great, because the inwardness
embraces the objective uncertainty with the whole passion of infinity.
In relation to a mathematical proposition, e.g., objectivity is given, but therefore
its truth is also an indifferent truth.

But the given determination of truth is a paraphrase of faith. Without risk no faith.
Faith is precisely the contradiction between the infinite passion of inwardness and the
objective uncertainty. If I can grasp God objectively, I do not believe, but precisely
because I cannot do so, I must believe; and
% --- p. 189 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{189}if I want to keep myself in faith, I must constantly take care that I hold fast the
objective uncertainty, that in the objective uncertainty I am ``over the 70,000 fathoms
of water'', and yet believe.

In the proposition that subjectivity, inwardness, is the truth, is contained the
Socratic wisdom, whose immortal merit is precisely to have respected the essential
significance of existing, of the knower's being an existing person, for which reason he
in his ignorance was, in the highest sense within heathendom, in the truth. To grasp
this, that speculation's misfortune is precisely that it again and again forgets that
the knower is an existing person, can already be difficult enough in our objective
time. ``But to go further than Socrates, when one has not even grasped the Socratic,
is at least not Socratic.''


\sectionrule

\excerpthead{C. THE THEORY OF THE STAGES (Uvidenskabelig Efterskrift).}


``Enten --- Eller'', whose title is already suggestive, lets the existence-relation
between the aesthetic and the ethical come into being in existing individuality. This is
for me the book's indirect polemic against speculation, which is indifferent to
existence. That there is no result and no final decision is an indirect expression of
truth as inwardness, thus perhaps a polemic against truth as knowledge. The Preface
itself says something about this, yet not teachingly, for then I could know something
with determinateness, but in the merry form of jest and hypothesis. That there is no
author is a means of distancing. --- ---

\emph{Part One} is a possibility of existence that cannot win existence, a
melancholy that is to be worked up ethically. It is essentially melancholy, and so
deep that it, although autopathic, deceptively occupies itself with the sufferings of
others (``Silhouettes''), and moreover deceives by the veil of pleasure, of prudence,
of depravity, but the deception
% --- p. 190 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{190}and the veil are at once its strength and its impotence, its strength in
imagination and its impotence in winning existence. It is imagination-existence in
aesthetic passion, therefore paradoxical and foundering on time; it is at its maximum
despair; it is thus not existence, but possibility of existence in the direction of
existence, and brought so near that one as it were feels how every moment is wasted
where a decision has not yet come. But the possibility of existence in the existing A.
will not be conscious of this, and keeps existence away by the finest of all
deceptions, by thinking; he has thought everything possible, and yet he has not
existed at all. This has the effect that only the Diapsalmata are purely poetic
effusions, while the rest richly has thought-content in it, which may easily deceive,
as if having thought about something were identical with existing. Had a poet laid out
the work, he would scarcely have thought of this, and would perhaps by the work itself
have brought forth again the old misunderstanding. For the relation is not to be
between an immature and a mature thinking, but between not existing and existing. A.
is therefore as a thinker developed, he is far superior to B. as a dialectician, he
has received all the seductive gifts of the understanding and of the spirit; thereby
it becomes clearer in what B. differs from him.

\emph{Part Two} is an ethical individuality existing by virtue of the ethical. Part
Two is also what brings Part One forth, for A. would again conceive being an author as
a possibility, actually carry it out --- and then let it lie. The ethicist has
\emph{despaired}; he has in despair \emph{chosen himself}; he remains in this choice,
and in this choice \emph{manifest}; he is a husband (A. was familiar with every
possibility within the erotic range, and yet not even actually in love, for in the same
moment he had in a way been on the point of consolidating himself), and collects
himself precisely against the hiddenness of the aesthetic upon marriage as the deepest
form of life's manifestation, whereby
% --- p. 191 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{191}\emph{time} is taken to the credit of the ethically existing person, and the
possibility of \emph{acquiring a history} is continuity's ethical victory over
hiddenness, melancholy, illusory passion, despair. Through juggling mist-pictures,
through the distractions of a luxuriant thought-content, whose execution, if it is any
good at all, is the author's absolute merit, one arrives at a quite simple human being,
existing by virtue of the ethical. This is the scene-change, or rather, now the scene
is there: instead of a world of possibility, shot through with imagination and
dialectically arranged, there has come to be an individual --- and only the truth that
edifies is truth for you, i.e. truth is inwardness, mark well, existence-inwardness,
here in ethical determination.

Thus that matter was got through. The book's merit, or whether it has any, does not
concern me; if it has any, this must essentially lie in not giving a result, but in
transforming everything into inwardness; imagination-inwardness in Part One into
conjuring the possibilities with potentiated passion, dialectic into transforming
everything into nothing in despair; ethical pathos in Part Two into embracing with
quiet, inviolable and yet infinite passion of resolution the modest task of the
ethical, edified thereby, manifest to God and human beings. --- There is no teaching,
but it does not follow from that that there is no thought-content, thus thinking is
indeed one thing, and existing in what is thought another. To exist is in relation to
thinking as little something that follows of itself as it is something thoughtless. It
is not even a conviction that is communicated and expounded, perhaps, as one says,
with inwardness; for a conviction one can also have in the representation, whereby it
so easily becomes dialectical in the direction of more or less true. No, one exists in
thought, and the book or the writing has no final relation to anyone. This
transparency of thought in existence is precisely inwardness. If speculation, for
example, instead of teachingly propounding \textit{de omnibus dubitandum} and getting a
% --- p. 192 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{192}chorus that swears to swear \textit{de omnibus dubitandum,} had instead made an
attempt to let such a doubter come into being in existence-inwardness, so that one
could see down to the smallest detail how he went about doing it --- yes, had it done
so, that is, had it begun on it, then it would have given it up again, and put to shame
would have seen that the big word which every rattler has bound himself by oath to
have realized is not merely an infinitely difficult task, but an impossibility for an
existing person. And this is precisely one of the sad sides of all communication, that
the good communicator, now to win people, now from vanity, now from thoughtlessness,
takes his mouth so full that he has not only in a twinkling done all that is possible
for an eminent existing spirit in a long life, but what is impossible. One forgets that
existing makes the understanding of the simplest truth for the common man in
existence-transparency exceedingly difficult and strenuous; one claims without ado
everything by means of result (I have heard people, who are so stupid that one could
