run into doors with them, say: one cannot remain standing at the Socratic ignorance),
and ends like all windbags by finally having even done the impossible. Inwardness has
become a matter of knowledge, existing a waste of time; hence it comes that the most
mediocre person who in our time writes something together speaks so that one would
think he had experienced everything, and only by heeding his parenthetical clauses does
one see he is a rogue; and hence it comes that a person in our time who exists merely
with as much energy as a mediocre Greek philosopher is regarded as demonic. The rattle
of pain and sufferings one knows by heart, likewise the glorious praise of
steadfastness. Everyone rattles on; if there exists a person who for the sake of an
opinion exposes himself to a small unpleasantness, one regards him as a demon --- or as
stupid; for one knows everything, and in order not to remain standing there, one knows
also that one is not to do the slightest thing about it, for by
% --- p. 193 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{193}means of external knowledge one is in the seventh heaven, and when one is to
begin to do it, then one becomes a poor single existing human being who again and again
stumbles and from year to year gets forward only very slowly. Yes, if one can sometimes
with a certain relief think of Caesar's having had the whole Alexandrian library
burned, one could really, well-meaningly, wish for humanity that that superfluity of
knowledge might be taken away again, that one might again get to know what it is to
live as a human being.

\sectionrule

``From Enten --- Eller'' ``Stadier paa Livets Vei'' is noticeably different by a
threefold division. There are three stages, an aesthetic, an ethical, a religious, yet
not abstractly, as the immediate, the mediate, the unity, but concretely in
existence-determination as enjoyment-perdition; action-victory; suffering. Yet
notwithstanding this threefold division the writing is just the same an Either --- Or.
The ethical and the religious stage namely stand in an essential relation to one
another. The difficulty with Enten --- Eller was precisely that the work concluded
ethically, as was shown. In the Stages this has been made clear, and the religious has
asserted its place.

The aesthetic and ethical stage are brought forth again, in a certain sense as
recapitulation, and yet as something new. It would also be a poor testimony to
existence-inwardness if every such stage could not be rejuvenated again in
presentation, even though it may be risky in the attempt to scorn the apparent
assistance of externality in bringing out the difference, as by choosing new names and
the like. --- The ethicist concentrates again on marriage, as actuality's dialectically
most composite manifestation. Yet he draws forth a new side, and asserts especially
the category of time and its significance as the medium for the beauty that grows with
time, whereas aesthetically seen
% --- p. 194 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{194}time and existence in time is more or less retrogression.


By the threefold division the existence-position among the stages is changed in
relation to one another. In Enten-Eller the aesthetic standpoint is a possibility of
existence and the ethicist existing. Now the aesthetic is existing, the ethicist
fighting, fighting \textit{ancipiti proelio} against the aesthetic, which he
nevertheless easily overcomes again, not by the seductive gifts of the spirit, but by
ethical passion and pathos; and against the religious. In concluding, the ethicist does
his utmost to ward off the decisive form of a higher standpoint. That he thus defends
himself is in order, since he is not a standpoint, but an existing individuality. It is
also a basic confusion in modern science that one without ado confuses the abstract
contemplation of standpoints with existing, so that when someone is knowing about the
former he is therefore existing; whereas every existing individuality, precisely as
existing, must be more or less one-sided. Seen abstractly there is indeed no decisive
strife between the standpoints, because abstraction precisely removes that in which the
decision lies: the \emph{existing subject}, but nevertheless the immanent transition is
a chimera, a fancy, as if the one standpoint determined itself necessarily by itself
over to the other, since the category of transition is itself a break with immanence,
is a \emph{leap}.

The aesthete in Enten-Eller was a possibility of existence; he was a young, richly
gifted, partly hopeful human being who tried himself out on himself and on life,
``with whom one could really never get properly angry, because the evil in him, as in
the medieval conception of it, had an admixture of the childlike''; and because he was
in truth no actuality, but ``a possibility of everything'': thus the aesthete went, so
to speak, around in the Assessor's living room\footnote[1]{Even ``Forførerens Dagbog''
was only the possibility of terror, which the aesthete in his groping existence had
conjured up, precisely because without actually being anything in possibility he had to
try himself out in everything.}. The
% --- p. 195 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{195}Assessor was, in relation to him, genial, ethically secure and essentially
admonishing, as one somewhat older and more mature relates to a younger one whose
talents, whose superiority of spirit he in a way acknowledges, while he has
unconditional power over him by security, experience and inwardness in living. In the
``Stadierne'' the aesthetic emerges more definitely in existence, and therefore it
becomes latent in the presentation itself manifest that the aesthetic existence, even
where a milder light falls on it, as it is always essentially glittering, is
perdition; but it is not a foreign standpoint, like the Assessor's, that makes this
clear as a warning to a young person whose life is not yet in the deepest sense
decided. To admonish against a decided aesthetic existence is too late, to want to
admonish Victor Eremita, Constantin Constantius, the Dealer in Fashions or Johannes
the Seducer is to make oneself ridiculous, and to produce as comic an effect as a
situation I once experienced: a man snatches in the haste of danger a little child's
stick from his child, in order with it --- to strike an enormous bandit who had forced
his way into the room. Though involved in the danger, I involuntarily burst out
laughing, because it looked as if he were beating clothes. The relation between the
Assessor and the aesthete in Enten---Eller made it natural and psychologically right
that the Assessor was admonishing. However, there was also in that work in the final
sense no decision (the Preface), so that the reader could say: see now it is decided.
A reader who needs the reliability of a sermon of reproof for a standpoint to be
misguided, or an unfortunate outcome (e.g. madness, suicide, poverty etc.), nevertheless
sees nothing, he merely imagines it; and as an author to go about it so is womanish,
writing for childish readers. Take such a figure as Johannes the Seducer. He who needs
% print: Fortabelse. han (misprint)
him to go mad or shoot himself, in order to see that his standpoint is perdition. He
does not see it all the same, but imagines it. He who namely grasps it, he grasps it,
just let the Seducer open his
% --- p. 196 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{196}mouth, he hears in every word the perdition and the judgment on him. The reader who
needs the external punishment is merely a fool, for one can indeed take a quite decent
man and let him go mad, then such a reader believes that it is an unjustified
standpoint.

The aesthetic stage is represented by ``\textit{in vino veritas}''. Those who step
forward here are aesthetes, to be sure, but by no means ignorant of the ethical.
Therefore they are presented not merely but at the same time as those who clearly know
how to give an account of their existence. In our time one believes that knowledge
decides, and when one merely gets to know the true, the shorter and quicker the better,
then one is helped. But to exist is something quite other than to know. --- The young
man is nearest to being merely a possibility, and therefore still to be hoped for. He
is essentially thought-melancholy. Constantin Constantius is hardening of the
understanding. Victor Eremita is sympathetic irony. The Dealer in Fashions is demonic
despair in passion. Johannes the Seducer is perdition in coldness, a ``marked'' and
extinct individuality. All are consequent to the point of despair. As one will find in
the second part of Enten --- Eller the answer and correction to every misdirection in
the first part, so one will also find here the explanation in the ethicist, only that
the ethicist essentially speaks out, and nowhere directly takes account of what,
according to the design of the work, he cannot be assumed to be aware of. It is thus
left to the reader himself by himself, if it so seems to him, to put it together, but
nothing is done for a reader's comfort. It is indeed just what readers so gladly would
have, they want to read books in the royal manner, as a king reads a petition, where a
marginal summary exempts him from being troubled by the petitioner's prolixity. In
relation to the pseudonymous authors this is surely a misunderstanding on the reader's
part; for from the impression I have of them, I do not know that they seek anything at
all from the lofty majesty of the reading world's majority.
% --- p. 197 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{197}It would also strike me as most odd. I have namely always thought of it thus, that
an author is someone who knows something more or knows the same thing differently from
the reader, therefore he is an author, and otherwise he should not concern himself with
being an author. On the other hand it has never occurred to me that an author was a
suppliant, a beggar at the reader's door, a peddler who, by means of a devil of a
chatter and a little gilt finery on the binding, which properly stuck in the daughters'
eyes, foisted his writings upon the families.

Johannes the Seducer ends with this, that \emph{woman is only}
\emph{the moment}. This is in its generality the essential aesthetic proposition, that
\emph{the moment} is everything, and to that extent essentially nothing again, just as
the sophistic proposition that everything is true is that nothing is true. The
conception of time is in general the decisive thing for every standpoint right up to
the paradox, which paradoxically accentuates time. To the same degree as time is
accentuated, to the same degree one advances from the aesthetic, the metaphysical, to
the ethical, the religious and the Christian-religious.


Where Johannes the Seducer ends, the Assessor begins:
\emph{that woman's beauty increases with}
\emph{the years}. Here time is accentuated ethically, but yet not otherwise than that
the taking back of recollection out of existence into the eternal is possible.

The aesthetic stage is indicated very briefly, and in order to place the accent properly
on the religious, it is presumably why the author has called the first ``a
recollection'', in order by pressing this back to bring the ethical stage and
especially the religious forth the more.

As regards the content of the writing more closely, I shall not enter further. Its
significance, if it has any, will lie in the existence-inwardness of the different
stages, differently made visible, in passion, irony, pathos, humor, dialectic. Such
things naturally do not occupy docents. Am Ende it was perhaps not unthinkable that a
docent carried
% --- p. 198 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{198}courtesy so far that he en \textit{passant} in a comma, in a note to a paragraph
in the system, said of this author: he represents inwardness. Then the author and an
ignorant reading world have got to know everything. Passion, pathos, irony, dialectic,
humor, enthusiasm etc. are regarded by docents as something subordinate, which every
human being has. When it is therefore said: he represents inwardness, then with this
brief word, which everyone can say, everything is said and much more than the author
has said. Everyone knows what he is to think by it, and every docent could easily have
accomplished everything in this direction, but left it to reduced subjects. Whether now
really everyone knows more concretely what inwardness is, and everyone as an author
can accomplish something in this direction, I shall leave undecided. Of everyone who
keeps silent, I shall be willing to assume it, but the docents do not keep silent.

Yet, as said, with the content of the writing I have nothing to do. My thesis was that
subjectivity, inwardness, is the truth. It was for me the decisive thing with regard
to the problem of Christianity, and with the same regard I have thought I ought to
pursue a certain striving in the pseudonymous writings, which to the last have honestly
refrained from teaching, and to take a preferential regard to the last, because it
appeared after my Crumbs, freely reproducing recalls the earlier ones, and through
humor as borderland determines the religious stage.

% --- p. 199 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: VII without a stop (the Contents has VII.)
\anthsection{VII} {RASMUS NIELSEN AND HANS BRØCHNER}\opage{199}

\begin{intro}

1. Under the influence of Kierkegaard's criticism \textsc{Rasmus}
\textsc{Nielsen} gave up theological speculation and appeared (1849) with a
sharp criticism of Martensen's Dogmatics. From this time on he stressed the
opposition between experience and speculation and sought by eager studies in natural
science to find a new philosophy. In ``Grundideernes Logik'' (1864---66), which was not
completed, he asserts the ideal principles of knowledge as the constant presupposition
for the conception of reality that science can give us. But his original conception of
science continued to make itself felt, only somewhat faded in comparison with the time
when, together with Martensen, he speculated in the light of the divine unity of
knowledge and power. ``Grundideernes Logik'' therefore did not become any continuation
of Kierkegaard's critical theory of knowledge. Nevertheless he asserted, with a
somewhat different motivation from Kierkegaard's, the sharpest opposition between
religion and science. ``The Problem of the Heterogeneous Principles'' concerned for
him partly the relation between idealism and realism (``Knowledge and Power''), partly
--- in its strongest form --- the relation between faith and knowledge. From a treatise
of his later years, which may be regarded as his philosophical testament
(``Philosophiske Grundproblemer''. The Philosophical Faculty's Festschrift on the
occasion of the University's 400th anniversary), some pieces are given here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. PHYSICS AND METAPHYSICS}


Whereas Hegelianism has sought to transform the world of realities into a system of
metaphysical concepts, Positivism has, by way of change, thrown all metaphysical
ballast overboard, in order to hold exclusively to realities and
% --- p. 200 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{200}facts. It is, as is well known, not the realities that conform to our
concepts, but on the contrary our concepts that must conform to the realities; the
wisest thing philosophy can do is therefore to give up the a priori problems, to leave
its old leaky hulk, to go aboard physics' steamer as a passenger, and to take the title
of Positivism. In justification of so important a step the celebrated founder of the
school, Auguste Comte, has pointed out three stages of development
\textit{(trois états),} which the human spirit on its way to the knowledge of truth
must necessarily pass through, \emph{the theological, the metaphysical and the
scientific}. At the theological stage one seeks the cause of the phenomena in a
supernatural will and lets the arbitrariness of the supernatural will determine the
course of things without regard to fixed laws. At the metaphysical stage arbitrariness
disappears and makes room for such supersensible, self-valid entities as the Absolute,
Substance, Cause, the Idea etc. This intermediate stage has been necessary as a stage
of transition from the theological to the scientific
; but in itself the metaphysical standpoint is unfruitful, as can be seen in particular
from the endless disputes without result into which it dissolves. Positive philosophy,
which stands at the scientific stage, now characterizes itself by regarding the
phenomena as subject to unchangeable laws, seeking to reduce these laws to the
smallest possible number of fundamental laws, and considering every attempt to
penetrate either to the first causes or to the so-called final causes utopian and
meaningless.

To exclude metaphysics is fully justified when by the word metaphysics one denotes a
science that sets imagination in the place of experience in order to discover the
essences and \textit{qvalitates occultæ} of which real things must absolutely consist,
unconcerned whether it agrees with reality or not. But against so superficial a
conception of the concept of metaphysics protest must be made in the name of reality.
Hegel's
% --- p. 201 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{201}``Wissenschaft der Logik'' is essentially metaphysics. Whatever imperfections
may be pointed out in this Logic, one should nevertheless not accuse the author of
fetching his concepts from the world of imagination. Concepts are so inherent in
things that no existing thing can avoid corresponding to its concept. The ``formation
and discipline'' of consciousness that Hegel demands on behalf of logic he can with
full right demand on behalf of all the sciences. --- The relation between physics and
metaphysics we can determine thus: Physics forms its concepts after the observed
facts, metaphysics develops its concepts from the conditions necessary for all being
and existence. To deduce the concretions of the concepts of existence from pure
thought is impossible; but to give an account of empirical phenomena without going
back to the a priori concepts is also impossible.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. MATERIALISM AND IDEALISM}


Among the most conspicuous problems of the human understanding belongs without doubt
the one that revolves around the relation between life and organism, between soul and
body. This problem presses itself upon one as if of itself, and it is then no wonder
that both in older and in more recent times it has given occasion to a multitude of
different hypotheses, and that these hypotheses, each in its turn, have competed for
the dignity of being exalted to dogma. Nor has any problem lain nearer to hand when it
was a question of a striking example of the unfruitfulness of philosophical
speculations and the imperfection of all human knowledge. If one grapples with this
problem, one will soon make the discovery that there is something Protean about it,
and that it easily escapes him who does not rightly know where to grasp it in order to
hold it fast. One may proceed from the presupposition that appeals to sound sense,
that life is different from the organism, that the soul is a relatively independent
% --- p. 202 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{202}being in opposition to the body, and yet in the further course of the
investigations be led to the recognition that soul and body, and then of course also
life and organism, cannot each have its own subsistence, but must be assumed to develop
from one and the same fundamental principle; the problem \emph{is then this: is the
fundamental principle originally to be placed in the ideal or in the real}?

If one makes the ideal principle absolutely valid, conceives life as Idea and the soul
as the life-Idea's self-reflection in its immediate other, then the organism becomes a
phenomenon of life, the body a phenomenon of the soul. If one conversely makes the real
principle absolutely valid, then life becomes a resultant of the forces acting in the
organism, and ``the soul-specter'', a phantom formed by brain functions, shows itself
only as a revenant from the darkness of Scholasticism. What above all complicates the
matter is the circumstance that however irreconcilable the opposition between idealism
and materialism is, it is nevertheless unavoidable that the idealist must express
himself materialistically about the psychical, and the materialist idealistically about
the bodily, when it comes to the point. \emph{``The brain}
\emph{thinks, just as the stomach digests,''} this proposition is materialistic,
insofar as it places the activity of digestion on a level with the activity of thought.
The activity of thought is ideal. The idealist knows no purer expression for ideal
activity. In wanting to make the ideal activity material, the materialist comes
precisely to make the material activity ideal. The idealist, who on account of the
unity of being and thought lets matter think, must of course also let the stomach
think, conceive the activity of digestion as a dialectical process of concepts, and
thereby comes, in his efforts to make a material activity ideal, to make the ideal
activity material. Insofar as the idealist is in any way to enter upon particular
determinations of organs and organic conditions, he must necessarily
% --- p. 203 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{203}speak as if matter were really material, and of such categories as bones,
muscles, nerves use words and expressions that immediately correspond to our
representations of the bodily. The same holds in the reverse relation for the
materialist when he speaks of the psychical. However materialistically the psychical
functions are conceived, he is nevertheless compelled to use words such as sensation,
feeling, representation, consciousness etc.; but already the immediate and natural
understanding of these words transports us with a jerk from the material sphere over
into the ideal. When the materialist treats of the consciousness of the brain, the
spinal cord, the ganglia, he cannot avoid distributing the lower and higher
consciousnesses among organs, so that it looks as if consciousness were nevertheless
something ideal for itself; he lets the different consciousnesses fuse into a kind of
unity of consciousness, without being able to fuse the organs into a similar unity.

On account of this one might be tempted to the assumption that the opposition between
idealism and materialism was not essential, that the strife was only a strife about
words; but this is by no means the case: the opposition is one of principle. From the
idealist side the matter is understood thus, that the material activity is semblance
and appearance of the ideal; materialistically it is understood thus, that the ideal
activity everywhere becomes semblance and appearance of the material. That there is
semblance and appearance here on both sides proves that reflection is in motion, that
there is a fight for a monistic decision. The merely monistic unity is, however, an
ambiguous unity, and as long as there is strife for an ambiguous unity, the strife can
be continued in all ambiguity. The old dualism was mechanically miraculous, but it was
not ambiguous. The miraculous science has thoroughly overcome; but the modern monism,
which has set itself in the place of dualism, suffers from an ambiguity that will
hinder every true scientific progress as long as it is allowed to remain standing.

% --- p. 204 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{204}To remove the ambiguity it is first of all necessary to come to clarity about how
far there is at bottom any difference in principle between the lifeless and the
living, and, if such a difference undeniably takes place, then to examine in what it
properly consists and what the distinguishing marks are by which it is to be known.
Without dwelling on comparisons and descriptions which from a natural-historical
standpoint may be orienting for practical use, we shall try to hit the kernel of the
matter
% print: op (misprint for og)
and draw attention to that which, in our conception, must be the decisive thing. As
the distinguishing mark of lifeless activity of whatever kind, mechanical, magnetic,
electric, chemical, we hold fast to what physics has demonstrated as law, that action
and reaction in all interactions of material members must correspond exactly to one
another, so that no self-governing center can be assumed. As the mark of life-activity,
on the other hand, it is emphasized that in the mutual action and reaction of the
material points an ideal center forms and with it a conditioned self-activity which
alters and governs the interaction of the parts. The facts corresponding to this are
metabolism and formation of shape, not immediately each by itself, but in active
union. A crystalline form can maintain itself when metabolism is excluded, a metabolism
can go on and the form be disturbed; in this there is not a trace of life-activity.
But when a metabolism goes on in such a way that a typically determined development
of form is maintained during it, then we regard this as an unfailing sign of life's
organizing activity. The ideal center of the activity cannot be localized
mathematically like a sphere's center of gravity, which is only a resultant of the
weight of the parts; nor can it be either a separate material point or a metaphysical
real, for in that case it would have to act according to its properties in the manner
of other material points or reals. Every whole that rests solely on the interaction of
real parts according to properties must, as the theory of potential functions proves
exactly, be
% --- p. 205 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{205}strictly subject to the law of action and reaction: here there is not a trace
either of self-active unity or of governing center. Everywhere, on the other hand,
where cells form and life expresses itself, there is an intimation of peculiar need,
peculiar demands, which require a household of their own; life-activity has the stamp
of a governing something which through its other, its material, will assert itself and
work for its maintenance and self-development. Characteristic of lifeless things is
their complete indifference to the state in which they find themselves. No one will
assume that there is in water a hidden yearning to become ice, or in ice a quiet urge
to pass over into vapor; on the other hand a plant germinating in the dark, which
stretches toward the sunlight, may well be said to betray a striving and yearning. It
is a distinguishing mark of life that it has an aim: self-development and
self-maintenance is the aim. To struggle for one's life is to have an end in view. It
must greatly surprise anyone who has not yet given up all hope of rational insight
that researchers of positivistic zeal for an exact knowledge of conditions and causes
have rejected every notion of final causes, and yet, as concerns the development of
organic forms, have chosen the slogan: \emph{struggle for life}.

With these distinguishing marks of the lifeless and the living we stand before
\emph{the problem of the heterogeneous principles}.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{C. KNOWLEDGE AND FAITH}


Scientific investigations into the essence of faith lead so deep into the mysteries of
subjectivity that the problem of the heterogeneous principles in the last instance
transforms itself into a decisive boundary problem, \emph{the problem} of
\emph{religion and} \emph{science}, \emph{faith and knowledge}.

It is remarkable in this respect to see how Comte, the founder of Positivism, after
having begun
% --- p. 206 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{206}with an objectivity of facts that excludes the subjective, ends by calling feeling
and subjectivity to his aid and, in the name of religion and morality, complaining of
the spirit's modern revolt against the heart \textit{(l'insurrection moderne
de l'esprit contre le cœur)}. His learned and clear-thinking disciple, Littré, has,
certainly not without a certain melancholy that does honor to his devotion to the
master, pointed out this regrettable inconsistency\footnote[1]{\textit{Auguste Comte et la philosophie positive.} Paris 1877. p. 539 ff.}. Feeling, says Comte, ought always to
rule over intelligence; thus, objected Littré, as in the Middle Ages philosophy was
theology's handmaid, so the spirit is now to be the heart's slave. There is something
ambiguous in this. If Comte with his expression means nothing other than that the
spirit, intelligence in unity with feeling, is to work for the good and the beneficial,
then no one will contradict him in this; but if he thereby means that the whole
government is to proceed from the heart, then he blinds the heart and gives morality
over to all manner of excesses. If he means that intelligence ought not to work for
itself or strive after pure truth and abstract theory, then he mutilates humanity and
robs it of its mightiest instrument for ennoblement and progress in perfection. It was
not the heart, but faith, the blind faith in the authority of revelation, that held the
spirit in medieval subjugation. Were the spirit's rising against this subjugation to be
ruinous for human souls, then the Middle Ages would, historically seen, have to have
more virtues and fewer vices than modern times; but history shows that precisely the
opposite is the case. Comte sets the scientifically grounded faith (la foi démontrée)
against the revealed faith (la \textit{foi révélée)} and demands then that intelligence
shall submit to religion; but what does that mean other than that the spirit shall
submit to its own work, thought to its own proofs\footnote[2]{\textit{Work cited, IIIme partie. Chapitre IV. Examen de l'opinion que l'esprit doit être subordonné au cœur. p. 545. En preténdant unir les principes du régime positif avec les conséquences du régime théologique, on tombe dans des contradictions implicites. Subordonner l'esprit á la foi révélée est une notion parfaitement claire; car la révélation émane de la divinité, et, quelque inconcevables que soient les mystères qu'elle ordonne de croire, il n'importe, une autorité devant laquelle l'homme n'est rien a parlé, et il doit soumettre. Mais, dans le régime positif, que peut signifier la subordination de l'esprit à la foi démontrée, puisque cette foi démontrée n'est pas autre qu'une œuvre de l'esprit lui-même et le résultat d'une longue enquête qui a mis en lumière les lois immanentes du monde? C'est dire le même par le même que l'esprit se subordonne à une foi qu'il a faite, et énoncer une impossibilité que d'énoncer qu'il s'insurge contre cette foi.}}.

% --- p. 207 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{207}That the principle of freedom cannot find recognition in Positivism goes without
saying. By taking the fact to be something in itself objective and holding to
realities without real concepts, positivistic philosophy has from the beginning
excluded every attempt at a more penetrating knowledge of the subjective essence of
freedom. The only scientific way from the objective to the subjective, from necessity
to freedom, goes through the real concepts. \emph{Things
are determined by their concepts, concepts
are determined in being comprehended, and they are comprehended
originally by the all-comprehending
self-unity, the ontological subjectivity}.
If this assumption can be shaken, if it can really be demonstrated that there can be
things without concepts, facts without laws, objects without objectivizing selfhood,
then there can be talk of holding the necessary fast without freedom and of finding out
the really true, the in-itself independent objective, by eliminating the subjective.
But if every attempt in this direction, in light of what has been clarified about the
problem of knowledge and of reality, is found to be unreasonable and meaningless, then
it stands unshakably fast that the whole objective existence is borne by objectivizing
subjectivity, that the necessary order of things has its ground in self-determining
freedom. Things, facts, which for our empirical investigations are the first
determining, are then not in themselves the first; they are a derivative, they exist
only
% --- p. 208 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{208}as outcome of power. The real concepts, which for our rational analyses are
logically determining, are not the first logical: the system of concepts points to an
eternal logic in an eternal self-thinking unity. From all sides, through all
determinations, whether real or ideal, logical or antilogical, the investigations
point as in converging lines toward the all-uniting center, the determining point of
departure, the inner ideal causality of real causes and series of causes in outward
infinity, i.e. toward the original freedom.

But what and how much the divine freedom can do, no human being can know. Science has,
as regards content, to hold to what is actually at hand, what has been produced and in
accordance with the laws is demonstrably produced; we must, in other words, know
freedom by necessity. There may then lie in the depth of freedom possibilities of which
science has and can have no inkling. The self-conscious life is in the highest sense
interested in everything that concerns freedom; since it is now impossible for human
knowledge to measure the depths of freedom, there must on that account be questions of
life, decisive questions of freedom, which science cannot answer. What does the eternal
spirit want with this nature, what is its proper meaning with the endless swarm of
animate organisms that, as it seems, are born only to suffer, and live to die? Above
all, what is its intention with the human being? Are the human souls destined to
vanish with the dissolution of the present organs, or do they bear in themselves a
disposition to a development infinitely continued under other conditions? Science
cannot enter into an answer to such questions, for possibility stands against
possibility. --- ---

Are we done with this? By no means. On the neutralizing equilibrium of opposite
possibilities it is impossible to live. No free rational human being can lead his life
without a rationally
% --- p. 209 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{209}determinate view of life; but a determinate view of life is possible only by
virtue of a decisive choice between opposite possibilities; here is the boundary
between religion and science, faith and knowledge. The highest to which scientific
insight can attain is to clarify the possibilities and show us the necessity of choice;
but for the choice itself science itself cannot be responsible: the responsibility
rests on the choosing subjectivity. He who chooses to live on a view that sacrifices
the individual to the universal, bends freedom down under necessity and lets the self
expire in infinite resignation, can appeal to scientific grounds just as little as he
who chooses to fight for freedom's personal ideals and to rely on the omnipotence of
love. Both choose in faith, and the choice of faith may just as little extinguish the
scientific consciousness in the latter as in the former. If we must now admit that only
those principles whose claims are justified by scientific grounds are scientific, does
it not follow with irrefutable consistency that the principle of faith cannot be a
scientific principle?

It is not enough to present the principle of faith and the principle of knowledge as
heterogeneous; for even when we hold to Newton's rule:
\textit{principia præter necessitatem non esse multiplicanda,}
it cannot be denied that within the domains of science there are heterogeneous
principles. Some of these, like those of materialism, spiritualism, positivism,
idealism, combat each other, expose by mutual criticism each other's one-sidedness and
prepare in this way their own dissolution; but there are others that cannot be
neutralized in this way. The heterogeneous special sciences have not only each its own
material, its own aim and its own method, but also, in consequence of the course of the
investigations, each its own peculiar principle: no encyclopedist has yet been able to
derive all the sciences from one single principle; but since the sciences nevertheless,
insofar as they are sciences, are homogeneous, the
% --- p. 210 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{210}heterogeneity of the principles can only be relative. If we limit ourselves now to
calling the principle of faith and the principle of knowledge heterogeneous principles,
we make ourselves guilty of an ambiguity. There must after all be a qualitatively
decisive difference between principles which with all their heterogeneity fall within
science, and a principle which on account of its unconditionally subjective, personal
peculiarity falls outside all science; in order therefore to remove the ambiguity we
must expressly impress that the principles of faith and knowledge are absolutely
heterogeneous.

It might indeed seem as if the dualistic separation of faith and knowledge had
significance only for those who choose to believe in the supernatural facts of a
positive religion, but not for those who hold to reason and build their religious
conviction on an objective, scientific foundation. This is a great error; Comte's
utterance about ``the spirit's modern revolt against the heart'' is from the standpoint
of science a forceful statement of subjectivity's undeniable claim to primacy in the
religious. Let one say: ``I cannot believe in an almighty, free and fatherly God, for
science has convinced me that such a being cannot possibly exist;'' another: ``I must
necessarily believe in a personal God, for science has in speculative dogmatics
irrefutably proved that God is personal;'' then both make themselves guilty of one and
the same failure to recognize subjectivity's share in the proof.

\emph{No personal coherent view of life is possible
without faith, no essential}
\emph{faith is possible without decisive self-determination,
no decisive self-determination is}
\emph{possible without a} choice, \emph{no choice is possible without}
\emph{a self-conscious choosing subjectivity, responsible for its choice}. Can such a
preponderance of subjectivity in the religious really stand in conflict with science
and sound reason?

% --- p. 211 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{211}Science cannot do more than lay before consciousness the possibilities among
which choice must be made, and illuminate the real grounds; but to transform the
grounds into motives, to determine the specific weight of the motives and to relieve
subjectivity of the responsibility of choice it cannot do. It might indeed seem as if
the responsible subjectivity now suddenly became so independent and self-willed that it
could prefer a view of life as mythically fantastic as it wished, without owing either
science or reason any account for it. This is indeed also the case with such
individualities as do not see what science really is, and in whom reason is still an
immature child; but it by no means holds of those who have gained a deeper insight into
the essence of science. Even when from inner personal need they choose to believe in
the revealed religion and preserve the inherited reverence for the Church and its
institutions, they must constantly give science an account, in giving themselves an
account; in no case may the human being arbitrarily extinguish the light of reason, but
the pure light of reason burns clearest in science.


It thus becomes a life task to \emph{unite religious faith with scientific
consciousness}.
How is this possible? The positive content that is appropriated in faith cannot, as
Littré assumes, be taken from science; and yet the scientific consciousness must, as
far as it can, take part in subjecting this content to a critical examination. We meet
here at the highest stage of intellectual life again the double-sided functions. The
purifying criticism is at one and the same time a criticism of faith and a criticism of
knowledge. The criticism of faith separates out all that does not originally concern
the essence of faith; the whole apparatus of tradition in external historical form it
regards as a clothing, a sheath in which the holy content is preserved. It will by no
means disdain the clothing or break the sheath; but it will nevertheless appropriate
only what the believing subjectivity from inner
% --- p. 212 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{212}experience needs as an indispensable condition for the development of its life of
freedom. How the criticism of knowledge is everywhere involved can be known by the
conception and the valuation of what is handed down. We can illuminate the interaction
under the point of view of the two categories: possibility and probability.

\emph{The improbable must be believed, but the impossible
cannot be believed}, this is a fundamental proposition for
all faith in revelation. Faith in revelation presupposes the possibility of the miracle,
science stands fast on the improbability of the miracle; but faith and knowledge agree
in distinguishing between possibility and reality. The possibility of the miracle points
to what we must call the perennial wonder, i.e. to the general presupposition that the
eternal self-power continues to stand in a relation of freedom, an actual relation of
will, to the natural order of things, in which everything proceeds according to laws.
That rational knowledge cannot raise any warranted objection to this has been shown in
the foregoing. ---

No real miracle has ever been demonstrated by compelling historical testimony. The
miracle is an external sign of an essential inward thing, of the power of freedom over
necessity, of life's victory over death; the miracle is sign and symbol, and does not
become more, even if its reality were confirmed by ten thousand sworn eyewitnesses. The
given sign is at once an inner and an outer fact; the inner is the really ideal, the
essential content of faith, the outer is the wondrous phenomenon that makes visible the
presence of the inner. The inner, the essential, is the extraordinary of freedom and
love, the improbable for the finite understanding, which can be appropriated
subjectively, tested subjectively and be present to consciousness at all times; the
external, the phenomenal, is the past, it belongs to a determinate time, and the age's
more or less adequate conception of it is to be determined by approximation according
to probability. The criticism of faith can thus appropriate
% --- p. 213 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{213}the improbable but yet possible, and the criticism of knowledge, as regards the
outer appearance of this possible, hold to the probable, and both be agreed that what
is miraculous in the sign is \emph{spirit-reality}, \emph{reality}
\emph{for the spirit}. Spirit-reality means that here there really is a content of
faith, an unambiguous revelation of the divine will; reality for the spirit means that
the outer may not be held fast in so sensuously immediate externality that there can
be talk of a breach of the laws of nature. ---

The religious faith is in its first immediacy a faith in something historical, a faith
in authority that stands fast on a holy, infallible tradition; the naive faith will
hear nothing either of the probable and improbable or of the difference between outer
and inner, it demands certainty, historical certainty. Now when some external authority
goes surety for the historical certainty without entering into critical investigations,
it happens that faith from the beginning acquires an admixture of superstition, an
admixture that is extremely difficult to separate out when the truth of faith itself is
to be preserved. If an external authority is to vouch for the truth, then the criterion
of truth is lost; for authority does not work convincingly but imposingly. Every
community of faith will then come forward with its myths and its miracles and aim at
imposing on the unfortified soul; here there is then no question of which of the many
religions has the deepest truth-content, but which of them by its signs and symbols has
the best grip on impressing. It is a distinguishing mark of the revealed, i.e. the
\emph{subjectively}
\emph{spiritually clear} religion, that it through the external authority everywhere in
the name of truth points to the inner, to the authority of the spirit, the witness of
the spirit.

The doctrine of two absolutely heterogeneous principles is thus by no means any
instruction to detach religion from science, or faith from reason; the dualistic
separa-
% --- p. 214 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{214}tion is precisely the condition for attaining a reconciling unity: also of faith
and knowledge it holds that opposite poles attract each other.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

2. While R. Nielsen had begun as a conservative (``right'') Hegelian, \textsc{Hans}
\textsc{Brøchner} (1820---1875) began as a radical (``left'') Hegelian. But his life of
thought too received great influence from Kierkegaard, with whom he stood for many
years in a close personal relationship. He strove to lead Hegel's speculative ideas
back to psychological and ethical points of departure. In his doctrine of ``Stadierne''
Kierkegaard had wished to show that a personal life stands the higher, the deeper
oppositions it is able to contain without being burst apart. Brøchner found, in
accordance with this, the peculiarity of the human in the urge to shape impulses and
drives into a harmonious unity, and found the presupposition for this in the capacity
to raise oneself above the single, isolated impulses and sensations. This development
takes place with inner necessity, and even where isolation and defiance, the
characteristic marks of evil, prevail, a reconciliation is nevertheless possible, in
that an inner contradiction will be felt in going wholly up in something finite and
limited. The spiritual development, which is thus always possible, rests on science's
understanding of existence. But since existence is never exhausted by experience, a
faith is necessary in which a more perfect unity and coherence is anticipated. The
object of faith is a principle of unity, which can be designated as spirit or thought,
but not as personality, since only a finite being can be personality. --- This
conception Brøchner developed --- in his very abstract language --- in his writing
``Om det Religiøse i dets Enhed med det Humane'' (1869), whose concluding section is
to be given here. Of Brøchner's other writings his works on the history of philosophy
are especially to be noted.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{HUMANISM AND RELIGION}


1. From all the earlier developments it follows as a result that there can be an
essential unity between the religious consciousness and the humane consciousness,
% --- p. 215 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{215}``cultural consciousness'', and this unity can and will, because it is grounded in human
nature, assert itself notwithstanding that the religious, where it still has the form
of the \emph{positively religious}, comes into \emph{opposition} to the humane, as the
essence of both begins to become clear to consciousness. In this opposition the
religious consciousness holds fast the dogma of the supranatural, the supranatural
principle of knowledge and the supranatural principle of will, whereas the humane
consciousness holds fast the essentiality of nature as the foundation of human life,
and the capacity of the unaltered, complete human nature to attain, out of its own
inner being, to the truth of knowledge and of action, whose source the human being
harbors in himself. This opposition, which within Christianity came forth to
consciousness at the close of the Middle Ages, became clearer and clearer, under the
unfolding of philosophical thought, in the following centuries, and there developed,
from realistic as well as from idealistic presuppositions, an opposition to the
Christian view of the world and of life, and philosophy's strife against the religious
either determined directly the common consciousness or at least indirectly influenced
the development by which in modern times this consciousness removed itself from the
Christian and from the positively religious in general.

There might indeed, during the development of the opposition, and while this
development partly led to open struggle, still for a time be \emph{partly} preserved an
uncertainty. A single form of philosophy could, by acquiring through its one-sidedly
idealistic principle an abstract likeness to the Christian view of the world, deceive
itself and seek a unity where the opposition was yet the fundamental thing. And since
the Christian had constantly had alongside itself a development which was influenced
by it, even if this had its root in another principle of life and was borne by the
conception of nature and of human life inherited from antiquity, which the new age
independently reacquired, that which had developed simultaneously
% --- p. 216 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{216}with Christianity, partly under its influence, but from another principle, could
be taken as something that had proceeded from Christianity itself, so that it was thus
seen as the principle which from its inner fullness of life could unfold a content of
reality, in science, in art, in the forms of the moral and political life. And thus
even in our time the view which, by positing a relative unity of the Christian and the
humane, sees in Christianity the principle for the shaping of a world-reality in the
religious spirit, could stand over against the view which conceives Christianity as
relating itself only negatively to the natural and to the unfolding of human life in
real reality, and has its representatives just as much within the positively religious
as within the humane. But there can be no uncertainty as to which of the two opposite
conceptions is the true and the historically secured, if one does not let oneself be
confused by the unavoidable inconsistencies into which a religion that posits the
denial of the world, the denial of nature as its principle, must fall, when it is to
\emph{continue}
\emph{to exist in the alien} world, whose destruction within a short time was the
original expectation. One need only analyze the Christian representations of the purely
spiritual God, of creation out of nothing, of the God-man, of the state of salvation
and of the end of the world, and the
\emph{abstractly spiritualistic} character of Christianity,
its \emph{denial of the natural} and thereby
also \emph{of the reality-content of human life}, will
become indubitable. And when to this is now joined what Christianity must share with
every positive religion: \emph{the relegation
of the principle of truth, of theoretical} and
\emph{practical truth, to a transcendent
being}, over against which the human being stands as the merely finite, as that which
by its nature is powerless to find the truth, and when this conception is seen as
sharpened by the Christian representation of the state of sinfulness
% --- p. 217 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{217}and the consequences of the first sin, then the opposition between the Christian and
the humane view of the world and of life must show itself as complete.

But under this opposition between the positively religious in its highest form and the
principle and content of the humane consciousness there shows itself nevertheless not
only the possibility, but the necessity, of a reconciliation in the spirit, by which the
humane and the religious are brought to unity, and to such a reconciliation the
development of the modern age itself has led.

As the modern consciousness became clear about its opposition to the Christian-medieval,
the opposition to the transcendent supranatural could, by its abstraction, lead to a
\emph{finite conception of nature} and
\emph{of human life;} but this conception ends in consequences that must lead the spirit
back to the recognition of the religious according to its \emph{essential}
significance, according to its
\emph{content of infinity}. The humane consciousness
is forced by the finitude of ``real reality'' and by its own finitude to seek an
infinite, and finitude harbors in itself the trace of the infinity by which it has
being. Consciousness rediscovers the infinite in that \emph{nature},
which for materialism became contradictory and inexplicable,
shows itself to the spirit in the light of \emph{life and ideality}, and \emph{in that
consciousness deepens itself into}
\emph{itself,} and recognizes its relation to the \emph{living} reality's
\emph{ideal-real principle}. In this deepening
the self rediscovers the \emph{religious} relation, rediscovers it by the road of
\emph{knowledge}, and rediscovers it \emph{without giving up itself}
\emph{and without denying a fundamental activity of}
its being. The humane consciousness becomes, in seeking down to the ground of its
being, in truth \emph{ethical} consciousness, and as such \emph{religious}
consciousness. Only through this relation of unity does the humane consciousness
receive its truth, as surely as the human spirit first becomes conscious of itself in
the deepest sense when it becomes conscious of itself \emph{as grounded in the}
% --- p. 218 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{218}\emph{divine}, and as freely subordinate to it. And the religious receives in unity
with the humane concretion and
\emph{reality}, and has in this its concrete reality
its \emph{truth as principle of the totality of real}
\emph{human life}. In this unity the religious reconciliation becomes \emph{complete},
because the life of religion is not lived by a flight from the reality of human life,
by a denial of essential forms of expression of it: a
denial that must always become self-contradictory, since it becomes
possible only by the very powers of human nature; --- but is lived in
\emph{every} essential form of the life of the spirit, and has its positive
expression in these forms of life, so that the religious relation
becomes an \emph{absolute} relation, embracing the \emph{whole} of life,
and joining the human spirit with its \emph{essential}
Absolute, with \emph{the truly infinite}. And in this
unity there is preserved, with the \emph{absolute relation} and with the \emph{reconciliation},
--- the spirit's reconciliation \emph{with its principle} and in
\emph{its own inner being}, --- \emph{what in the positive religions}
\emph{is essential}, while the historical form is dissolved,
the transcendence of representation sublated in the relation of immanence,
in which transcendence becomes a moment.

2. The \emph{essential task} of the present writing can thus be defined as this,
\emph{to point to the} form of \emph{religiosity},
\emph{which is present, though}
\emph{often unclear and half-conscious, in the humane}
\emph{consciousness} of our time, which, more clearly or more dimly, has proclaimed
itself in this consciousness \emph{from the beginning of modern times},
which has made itself felt in the social and moral
relations, in the endeavors of humanity, in the form of intellectual life in general,
and which needs only to be \emph{liberated and clarified}
through consciousness's self-deepening, in order to show itself in
its essentiality, as one with what is deepest moving in the positive religions
and as one with the innermost essence of the human spirit. It is the
\emph{religion of reality}, which is not to be founded or created, but only pointed out as
% --- p. 219 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{219}being and as grounded in the law of development of the human spirit;
--- which is not to be a rationalizing transformation of a positive religion or even a
new positive religion, but
\emph{the expression of the eternal spirit of religiosity}
as \emph{the principle of life of the whole of human life}. Therefore
the task has been to show this true and eternal religion of reality as that in which
the \emph{total} human life is in unity and harmony, and in which therefore the
\emph{humane}
\emph{consciousness} can find \emph{religious reconciliation}. The solution
of the problem of the relation of faith and knowledge has therefore been sought from that which in the infinite human being has the primacy of the concept, but in such a way that what is peculiar in the essential
forms of expression of the human spirit is recognized, that the working of the human
spirit according to its concrete determinateness has come to its validity. What was
intimated in the introduction to the writing:
``Problemet om Tro og Viden'', has received its closer execution. In the \emph{essence}
\emph{of the human spirit the necessity of the religious relation} has been sought, and it
has shown itself as the necessity of a truly \emph{absolute}
\emph{relation}, of a \emph{true relation of infinity}, of
such a relation in which the \emph{full reconciliation}: the reconciliation
\emph{within reality}, can be found, in that
the human spirit, in the \emph{absolute devotion to}
\emph{the principle}, comes to be in \emph{inner unity with itself}. In this
relation \emph{religion, science} and \emph{morality}
are seen in inner unity. From the recognition of the natural ground of the human spirit
as essential through the relation to the absolute principle, reference has been made to
an \emph{ethical} action, which receives a \emph{content of reality} through this natural ground,
and which, in taking on the stamp of the \emph{religious}, \emph{fills}
the \emph{religious} with \emph{reality} and, more closely, with
a \emph{thought-determined, rational reality}.


3. The standpoint that has been asserted in this writing thus does \emph{not set an abstract metaphysical} in the
% --- p. 220 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{220}place of \emph{religious life-reality}. It finds in
the principle itself the guarantee for the essentiality of the real, and
by accentuating this it provides room for a \emph{relation of freedom}
as opposed to the abstract metaphysical relation of necessity, and secures the
essential significance of the necessary form of being of the real for the
ethical-religious relation;
and thereby, and by letting this relation embrace a concrete
totality of life, it asserts it precisely as a \emph{reality}-relation.
That there is everywhere a going back
to metaphysical determinations does not conflict with this; for the metaphysical
retains its \emph{universal} validity; but it must only be determined
in such a way that it points \emph{to the real}, to the conditions of life of the finite
\emph{reality}.

The standpoint that has been asserted cannot rightly either, as some have wished, be
designated as a negative, and as such be brought together with the standpoints
from which the attacks on the Christian have been made that belong to our time and the
one immediately preceding. Its relation to the most significant representative of the
negative tendency of our time, to \emph{Feuerbach}, has been presented in detail above.
Through that presentation, and through all the earlier developments in general, it will
have been shown that it relates itself negatively to the positive religions only in that
it negates the determinateness of \emph{finitude} in them, but that through
this negation it precisely strives to assert \emph{the infinity of the essentially}
\emph{religious}. From this point of view the standpoint is precisely to be designated
as \emph{positive}: the negative becomes a determination for one who, in that he
relates himself polemically from a \emph{one-sided} point of departure,
\emph{can only tear down}, and for one who \emph{consciously}
\emph{holds fast the determinateness of finitude}. ---

Should anyone nevertheless, from the negative relation I take to the moment of
finitude in the positive religions, while I strive to assert what is essential,
eternal and infinite in the religions, want to take occasion to fling out, in the
manner of sycophants,
% --- p. 221 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{221}the accusations, calculated on ignorance and always welcome to fanaticism, of
atheism, smoldering hatred of Christianity, and whatever else the catchwords are, then
it shall on my part be answered only with the silence that is that of contempt. I have
once in another writing expressed myself on my personal relation to the positive
religion, and it is meaningless to repeat a statement of that kind.

% --- p. 222 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
% print: VIII without a stop, as IV--VII (the Contents has VIII.)
\anthsection{VIII} {THE LAST GENERATION}\opage{222}

\begin{intro}

1. \textsc{Sofus Heegaard} (1835---1884) developed as a philosopher under the strong
influence of Rasmus Nielsen, whose line of thought underlies his ``Indledning til den
rationelle Etik'' (1866). Later, through the study of Kant and the more recent English
philosophers, he was led to a critically searching standpoint. In his book ``Om
Intolerance'' (1878), from which a piece is given here, he expressed a pronounced
individualism, particularly in the settling of religious problems. That was not to be
his last word. When sorrow and illness broke in upon him, he found his only refuge in
the faith of the church, as he expressed beautifully and inwardly in the preface to the
second edition of his book ``Om Opdragelse''.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{THE DOGMATIC AND THE CRITICAL TYPE OF MIND}


\emph{We live on possibilities.} However firmly we grow on the trunk of society; however
securely we rest in the present, in our daily work, in business and friendship, in the
familiar, in custom and usage; however confidently we trust that this outer, sensible and
palpable reality is the solid thing, of whose reality only fools can doubt; however
trustfully we live in the comfort of the home and under the shelter of the notion that in
this our privatissimum we are unassailable, that here the possibilities have no power over
us --- it all avails nothing: the ground sways, and each day leads us surely and
inexorably one step nearer to the border where the land of riddles begins. The thoughtful
person is aware that these are hard terms to live on; but
% --- p. 223 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{223}he knows also that the world is not arranged for his convenience.
The strict school of possibilities need not, however, make him despondent; it often
wakens the best in his nature and calls forth a nobility in the way of thinking and a
spirit of reconciliation that under less hard terms would perhaps not have come forth.
Thus the thoughtful person takes up his share of the burden, or thus he can at least
take it up.

The less thoughtful person, by which of course is not meant the less gifted person,
goes about it differently. He \emph{will} not know of these possibilities; he \emph{will}
not see them or hear them spoken of; they \emph{must} not be there at all. He therefore
chooses, like the ostrich, to stick his head into a bush and pretend that nothing is the
matter. But whoever you are, I give you this friendly advice: Take care! For however
secure you feel, and however surely you tread the earth, do not forget that the earth
too floats. Do not forget that \emph{if} you are mistaken in your self-assurance, you
will sooner or later get the consequences to the last farthing!

The difficulty consists in one's not being willing to take on the burdensome task that
life lays upon us, of living on \emph{possibilities}.


Here society steps in and consoles the terrified: Just come to me, says society; I shall
not frighten you with possibilities; I have reality in full measure. Here you see first
the highway of tradition, fenced in with railings on both sides; it has now been traveled
for millennia, is free of robbers and extremely convenient to travel on. Here, further,
are the institutions, practical forms that have been worked out to the finest detail;
here are the church system, the school system, the theater system, the health system, the
poor-relief system, etc. I give you, society goes on, everything your heart desires; I
feed you, I amuse you, I instruct you, I console and edify you; I have a police force
that protects you, and a military establishment that steps in when the danger
% --- p. 224 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{224}becomes imminent. Just fit yourself into my forms, and no one shall touch a
hair on your head. And why should you hesitate? I know you, after all, as a respectable
man and honest citizen, who abhors both what stands out and what disturbs the peace. So
do not trouble yourself about those restless heads who make a stir on every occasion;
they achieve nothing anyway. But this much I will tell you, that I do not bargain. Either
you must belong to me wholly and entirely, or else I will know nothing of you at all.
Once you have joined me, you cannot move a foot, stir a finger, write a line or utter a
thought without carrying it out inside my system and under my control. There is only a
single case where I cannot control you, and that is when you \emph{think} your thoughts.
Therefore I have also once for all declared thoughts duty-free; but I candidly confess
that in this I have only made a virtue of necessity; for if I \emph{could} control you
there too, I would do so. Besides, you need not fear my control. Only be like all the
others, and you shall never notice any compulsion.

Thus speaks society, and its offer is tempting. Yet, in order not to be unjust, I ought
to add that here I am speaking of the strivings of society according to their ``ideal
average'' or in principle, whereas reality makes many an exception to this. It is solely
the interest in characterizing the opposition between the demands of society and those of
personality that makes both appear in such sharp outline.

On the one side, then, the possibilities of life, which rise up as grave and admonishing
figures in the background of personality; on the other side society with its living,
colorful reality, without doubt or weighing of alternatives, but everything definitely
formulated and registered. Between these two great powers every human being is placed,
and at this parting of the ways are formed the manifold \emph{types of mind}, which
together make up
% --- p. 225 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{225}the human world, and which, despite their numerous shades, can nevertheless
all be referred to two basic forms. For brevity's sake I shall call these two basic
forms the dogmatic and the critical type of mind, for which I unfortunately do not know
sufficiently descriptive Danish expressions; but the reality of the matter will emerge of
itself in what follows.

The \emph{dogmatic type of mind} pays homage to society. It prefers to have its affairs
clear. There must be order in things, it says, and therefore we must beg to be excused
from all swaying and floating possibilities. If a question concerns me, then this
question must put up with being definitely formulated, definitely answered and then
entered in the ledger. If there are questions that cannot be treated in this way, then
for that very reason they do not concern me. To express oneself in this way is called
speaking \emph{absolutely}; and the propositions in which one expresses oneself are
called \emph{dogmas}. A barracks regulation, a police notice, all the accepted laws of
society are in fact so many dogmas, although as a rule one does not use the word in this
connection. No society can exist without dogmas. This is in itself no sign of the
perfection of society, but is called forth by hard necessity. We cannot wait until the
order of society has been scientifically grounded, since in the meantime people would
kill one another; therefore we take a shortcut and arrange ourselves with dogmas, in that
in the affairs of society we decline to have the individual play with possibilities. If a
colonel were to engage a battalion of free personalities and drill with possibilities, we
should get a charming military establishment. The police do not bargain with every Tom,
Dick and Harry, but quite dogmatically take all ``free personalities'' in the police
sense by the scruff. It holds everywhere here that the law is the same for all, and that
everyone who wants to form an exception to the rule gets ground down. It is constantly
one and the same main thought that recurs under different forms:
% --- p. 226 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{226}the basic dogma of society rests on uniformity and sameness of kind.
Understood in principle, society does not merely stick its soldiers and policemen into
uniform, but wants throughout to put all its members in uniform also in spiritual
respects. Entirely it does not succeed in this; special groups or parties form, as for
example in political life; but within the party the society-forming power works on
restlessly. The political party again has the essential marks of society. One political
party speaks absolutely in these dogmas; the other political party speaks absolutely in
the directly opposite dogmas; but absolutely they all speak.

If one turns to science or art, one finds the dogma again \textit{in optima forma};
in science, moreover, in two forms. The \emph{positive} dogmatists of science are known
from so-called speculation. To concede the limits of their knowledge, to admit without
reservation that there is infinitely much they do not comprehend or grasp, they regard
half as a disgrace, or as something beneath their dignity; and even if they sometimes, in
a fit of modesty, make this concession in hazy generality, they take the concession back
in each particular case. These positive dogmatists know the secret of force and matter,
know perfectly well what cause and effect are, explain to us the nature of the soul from
the ground up and guarantee us a multitude of things about which otherwise no human being
knows anything.

The \emph{negative} dogmatists also have their specialty; they know especially thoroughly
what is not; and that is always something. Now if the positive dogmatists with their
scientific omniscience can tell us what is, and the negative dogmatists with \emph{their}
scientific omniscience can tell us what is not, and one then puts both together, science
has a fine future. The negative dogmatists know, for instance, that there exists no God,
and conclude with logical sharpness that there exists no personal God either. They also know
% --- p. 227 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{227}for certain that there is no existence of the soul after death. They present proof that moral
freedom is a fancy, that remorse is a folly and that moral evil vanishes in the totality
of the world. I am not speaking here of those who treat these views as possibilities; for
that they are, scientifically speaking, fully entitled. I speak only of those who present
them as if they were secured through proof.

These positive and negative dogmatists in turn represent the formula of society
correctly. They speak absolutely and express themselves in dogmas. Not a single one of
their dogmas can they prove, but sure of their cause they are.

The result of all these assertions is the following: The value of all scientific dogmas
for the existence of God, for the immortality of the soul, for freedom, plus the dogmas
against the same, is in science = zero. Of all these things nothing is \emph{known}.

Thus we see the circles of society formed after the same type of mind: Here one speaks
absolutely, here one commands through dogmas, all and everything shall be uniform. In
this way the great machine of society works, and each section of the machinery carries
out the order correctly. Offer a party man, no matter within which sphere, the tip of a
finger, and he takes your whole person. Make him in good nature the slightest
concession, and he entangles you in the net of consequences, strips you of all
peculiarity, all independence and in a twinkling transforms you into a means. He may
himself be extremely honorable, but the formula of society works through him and bleeds
you. He whom experience has taught this sees the necessity of always being cleared for
action.

Thus far we have still slipped through so graciously in the negotiations between
personality and society. Certainly at the stages just mentioned there occur many sorts of
conflicts, and often of a serious nature, but none of them
% --- p. 228 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{228}is so deep-going that it acquires decisive significance for the question we are
treating here. I have therefore left them out of consideration so far and held the
formula of society in abstract purity.


But as soon as we come to \emph{the religious domain}, abstractions no longer help.
It is indeed the same concepts and the same oppositions that we meet again here, but the
reality is changed. The spiritual atmosphere we here enter refracts the light in another
way, and life shows itself in new colors\dots{}.

The \emph{civil} dogma personality submits to even in such cases where it conflicts with
his views, because this dogma addresses only the \emph{common} human element in him, or
at least that which he has \emph{in common} with the other members of his society.

Against the \emph{religious} dogma personality protests, of course on the assumption that
his view conflicts with the command of the dogma; and he protests because \emph{this}
dogma, unlike all other dogmas, addresses that in him which he \emph{has not in common}
with any other human being in the whole world.

He protests next because the religious dogma speaks absolutely, and this time the
absoluteness is in earnest. Here it is not hidden, as with the civil dogma, beneath
manifold relativities, which in turn could lead to manifold compromises. No, this time
the dogma commands without reservation, and is to be understood literally. The first
thing personality does, then, is to reject the dogma as dogma. Perhaps it is found later
that he is in reality in agreement with what the dogma bids; perhaps not; but first of
all he rejects the dogma, because it presents itself \emph{as dogma}, and he cannot do
otherwise. In its innermost being personality is namely as manifest to itself as it can
ever become; here neither dissimulation nor defiance is possible. As personality
inexorably sits in judgment on itself in conscience, so it decides
% --- p. 229 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{229}for itself what it will believe and hope. I say ``will'', but could just as well
have said ``can'' or ``must''; for will, can and must coincide here. Here we are, that
is, at the end of the ``blind alley'', at what the philosophers call \textit{Causa} sui,
and however much we strive to get behind personality, all our exertion is in vain.

Personality must and will be self-determining in its own affair; but the religious dogma
speaks absolutely and will not concede to personality the right of self-determination.
Here is the first decisive point of tension between personality and society.

The personality that in the religious relation asserts the right of self-determination
to the utmost, that does not let any dogma be prescribed to it, but, if it comes under
the absolute command of the dogma, again does so in virtue of self-determination, belongs
to the so-called \emph{critical type of mind}. Here solitude is the decisive thing, the
forms of society stand in second rank; yet not so that personality should despise society
or withdraw from any duty to society. On the contrary, it recognizes that in society it
has an indispensable point of passage, if it is otherwise to acquire a content in its
solitude, which again is only an abstract expression of the fact that it remembers a long
series of benefactions, direct and indirect, that it has received, and which, if one is
not especially thoughtless or ungrateful, call forth the feeling of being at home, the
quiet joy in the traditions of one's people and the certainty that in the hour of danger
one would stand by one's countrymen through thick and thin and, if occasion offered, be
shrewd and brave if one could thereby benefit society, all of which is summed up in the
beautiful word love of country. This alone personality guards itself against: losing
itself in the universal, becoming ``uniform''. The critical mind is vividly aware that
its relation to society is only at an oar's length, and that the moment will come when it
and society part; after this moment society
% --- p. 230 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{230}may be society as much as it will --- personality has left it forever. If such a
personality is fortunately placed, he will not be lured into the snares of vanity,
pleasure or lust for power, because he has a clear notion that in the midst of society he
\emph{is} alone, and that in time he \emph{will become} so still more. He will not let this
thought come forth merely as a figure of speech, but will in stillness and earnest let it
gain power over his life.

If he is placed under unfavorable conditions, feels himself unhappy, he will not despise
the consolation that \emph{this life} comes to an end. Thereby he does not give up his % print: „Ævret“ (PRINTED AS IS; Iveren?), translated as exertion
exertion and become dull; that the critical mind never becomes. He asserts only his right
to complain when he suffers; he asserts his right not to delude himself that he is glad
when he is sorrowful. But this much is certain, that whatever allurements on the one
side, whatever sufferings on the other side are held out to the critical mind, it never
denies solitude as its true fatherland. It maintains strictly that further than to
possibility we do not get in this life; and since the religious dogma puts absolute
reality in place of possibility, it protests against the dogma \emph{as dogma}, regardless
of what it may otherwise contain. It \emph{believes} that by whatever line on the compass
personality may steer, if only it does not show false colors to itself, the way leads
toward the spirit of truth.

When therefore the time comes that my vessel pushes off from land, and I set out on the
long journey, I want to have aboard only what is stamped with my own mark, and whether on
this journey I have a fair wind or struggle with stiff storms; whether in the murk of
night I capsize and go down, or I descry the stars of eternity and sail toward a new
dawn, \emph{I myself sit at}
\emph{the helm}.

% --- p. 231 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{231}
\begin{intro}

2. \textsc{Harald Høffding} (b. 1843). Principal works: Psykologi i
Omrids paa Grundlag af Erfaring (1882. 5th ed. 1905). Etik
(1887. --- 3rd ed. 1905). Religionsfilosofi (1901. 2nd ed. 1906).
Filosofiske Problemer (University program 1902). Den nyere Filosofis
Historie (1894---95. --- 2nd ed. 1903). --- Here a psychological and a
philosophy-of-religion piece are given.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. REPETITION AND THE LIFE OF FEELING}


Frequent \textit{repetition} weakens the vehemence of feeling. The background against
which it originally unfolded necessarily becomes more and more indistinct; a distribution
of light and shadow sets in, so that the contrast gradually falls away. This process is
only a form of the general \textit{adaptation,} which belongs to all life. Under all
conditions the living strives to come into equilibrium with its conditions. For feeling it
often has the effect that freshness and enthusiasm are replaced by indifference and
routine and at last stand as an inexplicable memory. Adaptation damps, in that it causes
the functions to be performed with less expenditure of energy. But this, which is a
saving and therefore a gain when the subject is orientation in the surrounding world and
active doing, is a loss when the subject is feeling. The element of wonder that is in all
