living feeling seems to be lost entirely through repetition. --- That there must come
repetition lies in the fact that the experiences of a finite being are always limited; the
changes must necessarily form a cycle.

S. Kierkegaard has taken this psychological law as the starting point for demonstrating
the boundary between ``aesthetic'' and ethical conduct of life. In awakening and
enthusiasm we are in an attitude of enjoyment, in that we are seized by the strong
influence. The ego lets itself be carried away by the waves of feeling that break forth
arbitrarily. But in daily work, under the depressing and damping influence of repetition
% --- p. 232 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{232}it shall be shown whether feeling possesses any other strength than that
momentary flaring up. Every firm and lasting relation to other human beings, every labor
on the successive realization of an idea entails repetition and seems bound to exclude
the immediate surrender to the movement of feeling that was so easily awakened when the
relation was established, or when the idea was conceived. Characteristic of the
``aesthetic view of life'' is it, according to Kierkegaard, that every relation of life
is only an object of enjoyment; it is touched upon, in order to get the fresh influence,
but repetition (in constant activity or constant living together) is shunned, because it
has nothing stimulating in it, but causes dullness. Enjoyment is seized in the single
moment, but one breaks off in good time, so that the following moments too may get their
fresh enjoyment. With playful arbitrariness one therefore holds oneself hovering in
relation to all relations. The ``ethical view of life'' chooses repetition, in that a
calling is chosen that demands constant work, or in that a constant relation of living
together is entered. The problem for Kierkegaard was now how a transition from one stage
to the other was possible, --- how it was possible to ``will repetition''\footnote[1]{``He who will only hope is cowardly; he who will only recollect is voluptuous; but he who wills repetition is a man\dots{}. When one has sailed around existence, it shall be shown whether one has the courage to understand that life is a repetition, and the desire to rejoice in it.'' \textit{Gentagelsen.} Et Forsøg i den experimenterende Psykologi. Af Constantin Constantius. Kbhv. 1843. p. 5. --- A characteristic expression of the aesthetic view of life is the essay \textit{Vexeldrift} in the first part of ``Enten---Eller''. --- On the significance of repetition in Kierkegaard's thought see further my book \textit{Søren Kierkegaard som Filosof}. p. 83---98. --- In a manner analogous to Kierkegaard, \textsc{Fr. Nietzsche} has made use of repetition to illuminate his view of life. See my book \textit{Moderne Filosofer,} p. 145 f. 156 f.}. To will
repetition presupposes that one neither fears it (like the ``aesthetes'') nor is dulled by
it (like the ``philistines'').

But while Kierkegaard poses the problem in a way that betrays his brilliant psychological
eye, it is striking
% --- p. 233 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{233}to see with what haste he turns his back on psychology.
The problem, by his assertion, cannot be solved with the help of psychology. To live in
repetition without feeling's being weakened is, according to him, possible only through an
inexplicable act of will, supernaturally grounded. He has overlooked that there is a
psychological law of nature on which the ethical demand can rely, --- as indeed ethics,
if it is not to hover in the air or be a constant appeal to the supernatural, must build
on what is psychologically possible. Every act of will must be psychologically possible.

Two kinds of strength must be distinguished in the domain of the life of feeling. There
is a strength of vehemence, and there is a strength of inwardness. It is only the first
that is weakened by repetition; the second kind of strength can precisely be produced by
repetition. Vehemence rests on the sudden release of accumulated energy. Here the effect
of contrast plays a great role; surprise, wonder, the feeling of novelty are essential
elements in the state. But the energy can also be released successively, distributed over
a longer time and over several different points. This can happen when the object of
feeling has a manifold content that successively reveals itself, and which perhaps is
discovered only when a certain adaptation to the content has taken place, just as small
streaks of light in a dark room are discovered only when one has stayed there for a
longer time. The constant occupation or the constant living together produces a new
level, a new real self, in relation to which fluctuations of feeling become possible that
otherwise could not arise. This is a main point in the \textit{psychology of
fidelity.} When a relation to another human being is established, or when an idea is
conceived, feeling is determined especially by the contrast to the preceding state, and
the element of wonder becomes the predominant element. But after this relation of
contrast has fallen away with the novelty, attention can get time to turn to the single
sides of the object. There takes place, so to speak, an analysis, which
% --- p. 234 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{234}makes it possible that feeling can be determined by the single sides and properties
of the object, not merely by the total impression of the object. The inner wealth (if it
is there) takes the place of outer splendor. In the course of adaptation the object of
feeling opens up its nature and presents itself under different points of view, and on
the other hand the different elements in the individual's own nature are brought into
interaction with the object. Within the fundamental relation there may thus be found a
manifoldness of changing relations. Feeling is therefore spread over an ever greater
domain of life and can be fed by many more sources than at first. So it goes, e.g., in
the relation to a human being with whom we live together. The inner growth the feeling has
undergone often first becomes clear when the relation is put to the test; there may then
again take place a conversion from the distributed to the concentrated form, whereby it
appears that the capital invested has borne interest.

Here it depends on \textit{how rich and comprehensive a content} the feeling is attached
to. The narrower our ego, that is to say: all that to which our interest is attached, the
more quickly the possibilities for a new and fresh feeling are exhausted. Here the
sympathetic feelings show their superiority over the egoistic. A comprehensive sympathy,
the attachment of interest to great and significant objects, preserves freshness or makes
new freshness possible despite the depressing influence of repetition. Such a feeling is
a rebirth of the original sanguineness, which begins by attributing value and reality to
every arising representation, but easily turns into dullness or discouragement under the
influence partly of repetitions, partly of disappointments. ---

From Goethe's \textit{Briefe} aus \textit{der Schweiz} a beautiful example is put forth
here to illustrate the influence of repetition on feeling. --- The subject is the sublime
impressions of a mountain journey in Switzerland. --- ``A young man whom we took with us
from Basel said that he did not have the same feeling as the first time, and gave novelty
the credit. I would, however,
% --- p. 235 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{235}say to this: when we see such an object for the first time, the unaccustomed soul
first expands, and this brings about a painful joy, an overflowing fullness, which moves
the soul and draws voluptuous tears from us. Through this operation the soul becomes
greater in itself without knowing it, and is no longer able to have that first feeling.
Man believes he has lost, but he has gained: \textit{what he loses in delight, he gains
in inner growth.}''

Something is thus unavoidably lost that cannot be regained. It goes with the budding
feeling as with the first breath of the newborn, in which the lung is first distended in
such a way that it is never afterwards entirely emptied; no later breath can therefore be
like the first. To that extent repetition is impossible. But pessimism wrongly regards
this as a great loss; seen from another side it is a great gain. It depends on each
individual's view of life from which side he comes to see the matter.

The different significance that repetition or adaptation can have for the vehemence of
feeling and for its inwardness leads us to bring forward the opposition that the older
psychology (especially after \textsc{Kant}'s excellent presentation in
\textit{Anthropologie} § 71) set up between ``affect'' and ``passion,'' or, as I prefer to
call them: emotion and disposition. By \textit{emotion} is understood a sudden surging up
of feeling, which for a while overwhelms the mind and checks the free and natural
connection of the elements of knowledge. \textit{Disposition,} on the other hand, is the
feeling that has become nature, woven in. It may have arisen from a series of emotions;
but it may also have formed by continuous growth through changes that each by itself
attracted no attention. What emotion is in the single moment, with violent, striking
movement, disposition is in the depths of the mind as a saved-up sum of force that lies
ready for use. Therefore disposition does not exclude deliberate reflection; on the
contrary, it finds its expression in a thought dominating the whole
% --- p. 236 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{236}circle of representations. ``Affect'', says Kant, ``acts like water that breaks
through a dam, passion like a stream that digs itself ever deeper into its bed. Affect is
like an intoxication that can be slept off, passion like a madness that broods over a
thought that strikes ever deeper root.''

Feeling often begins with emotion and passes --- if it finds constant nourishment ---
over into disposition. Anger and grief are emotions, vindictiveness and melancholy are
kinds of disposition. The deepest and most central current in a human being's nature is
his ruling disposition, which is conditioned and introduced by his innate nature, fed,
developed and clarified by all movements of feeling and experiences. --- Repetition has a
different influence on emotion and on disposition: the former is weakened by it, the
latter can be nourished.

Just as emotion can pave the way for disposition, the latter can give itself vent as
emotion, although it can also find its satisfaction in a well-considered and calm
fashion. --- By the way in which disposition gives itself vent, emotions of another kind
than itself may be awakened. Thus when love of country in the moment of danger awakens
warlike courage and lust for battle. Or when in the carrying out of a plan of murder that
springs from cold self-interest cannibal impulses are awakened, so that the murderer
maltreats his victim in a way that is not necessary for his purpose. Between emotion
(affect), which is a momentary flaring up, and disposition (``passion''), which rests on
an ingrained disposition of feeling fixed by repetition, lies \textit{mood,} the
preliminary attunement of consciousness to a certain kind of feeling. It designates a more
lasting state than emotion, but has not, like disposition, grown strong. It does not lead
immediately to outward action, neither to sudden outbursts, like emotion, nor to constant
activity, like disposition. --- Within one and the same mood
% --- p. 237 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{237}there may again take place \textit{ripples,} e.g., by the changing tones in a
piece of music, which nevertheless throughout awakens the same mood, or by the different
elements in a series of memories which nevertheless throughout maintain one and the same
mood. The ripples are feelings of relation in higher degree than the mood, just as the
emotions are so in higher degree than the disposition.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. THE RELIGIOUS PROBLEM AND ITS FUTURE}


In the course of the last centuries it has become clearer and clearer that the
significance of religion cannot consist in the scientific understanding of existence which
it was supposed to be able to afford. Negatively this is grounded in the fact that
science leads to understanding by other paths and in other forms than religion.
Positively it is grounded in the growing psychological insight into the nature of
religion, which leads to stressing the elements of feeling and will as the essential in
religion, whereas the significance of representations is relatively subordinate. The
conclusion of this whole series of changes can only be the acceptance of the symbolic or
poetic character of all religious representations.

If now the need to maintain the persistence of value holds on after the poetic character
of all mythical and dogmatic representations has been seen through, then the continuity
of human spiritual development can be preserved at a very essential point. It is not a
philosophy that thereby comes to take the place of religion. It is one kind of faith that
takes the place of other kinds of faith. Faith can only ever be the \textit{object} of
philosophy, not the \textit{product} of philosophy, except in so far as philosophy can
demonstrate the psychic possibility of a certain faith under certain spiritual conditions
of life. The philosophy of religion investigates the epistemological, psychological and
ethical conditions to which every kind of faith is subject. But it can construct no
faith; it can only describe, analyze
% --- p. 238 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{238}and evaluate the faith that life itself at different standpoints leads to develop.
The philosophy of religion is a comparative science; it investigates the standpoints of
faith and the forms of faith in their relation to the spiritual conditions of life and in
their struggle with these, just as comparative biology investigates the organic beings in
their relation to and their struggle with the material conditions of life. And it finds
--- as far as I can see --- the possibility and the progressive development of a
religious standpoint without myth, dogma and cultus, a fruit of personal experiences that
give themselves expression in a poetry of life, which forms itself differently according
to the peculiarity of the individual. Even if images and symbols from the classical times
of the positive religions are retained, this happens at such a standpoint with historical
and psychological justification. First, such reinterpretation as is necessary here is
constantly undertaken within the positive religions. And second, the great poetry that is
often deposited in myths and dogmas is not the special property of the single people, the
single church or sect, but belongs to humanity and is at the disposal of anyone whose
experience of life goes in the same or an analogous direction as the experience to which
those images owe their first origin or formation. But there are those in whom the image-
and symbol-forming process goes on at first hand, whether its fruits can acquire
significance for the individual alone or for more with him. ---

The positive religions that history knows can, psychologically considered, be conceived as
concentrations, syntheses, in which men's representations of existence have been worked
together, interwoven with their deepest experiences of life. No proof can be given that
the time of such spiritual concentrations should be past, even if for the present we live
in the time of criticism and analysis. And neither can any proof be given that such
concentrations necessarily must have the mythological or dogmatic character that the
positive religions known hitherto have had. It
% --- p. 239 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{239}takes spiritual freedom and power to live on the conviction that our highest ideas
can only be parables. Conditions have hitherto been less favorable to the development of
this capacity; but who dares to set a limit to the freedom and power that may develop in
the human race?


It therefore seems to me unjustified to narrow the concept of positive religion so that
the mythical and dogmatic personifications come to stand as a necessary accessory. And it
would be especially unjustified if our earlier psychological investigation, which led to
recognizing the derivative significance of representations in the religious domain, is
correct. Moreover, ``natural'' religion has as a rule also had that accessory. But still
more unjustified it would be if one would regard such personifications as necessary
constituents of all religion, whether it appears as ``positive'' or not. One will not be
able to dismiss the question: what value does it have that the personal beings one
believes in exist, and that one believes in them? And then the idea of the persistence of
values will show itself as the fundamental religious idea, the one that also first gives
the representations of the personal gods a real content and a real significance. And this
idea will be able to preserve its
significance even if all personifications get only symbolic significance, and even if
there could be found other symbols than those taken from human life and the human form.
There will then be preserved a continuity in the intellectual life of human beings, even
where it leaves the positively religious standpoint. Whether the standpoint attained when
positive religion is abandoned is really a higher one is a matter for itself. It does not
become a higher one without further ado because myths, legends and dogmas are pushed
aside. As I once said in another place: \textit{not} believing something is about the
least that can be said of a human being. The negative: abandoning a standpoint, says
nothing about the value of the new standpoint, or about whether
% --- p. 240 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{240}a new standpoint is attained at all. The measure for ``lower'' and ``higher'' in the
transition from positive religion to a free religious standpoint must be the same as is
applied within the series of the religions when one religion is said to be higher than
another. In the ethical philosophy of religion we shall return to this point.

As I have remarked several times, I do not lay great weight on the name religion. The
danger in using the word religion in a more comprehensive sense might be that dishonest
accommodation would thereby be favored. But on the other hand religious life in the
present often takes --- and not least in such circles as believe they have the exclusive
right to be called religious --- forms of such a character that it truly takes no great
resignation to renounce using the name religion.

But however one may then name a standpoint that holds fast the faith in the persistence
of value without clothing it in dogmatic form, it is a task for the philosophy of
religion to investigate more closely to what extent such a standpoint is possible, and
what difficulties it has to contend with. ---

When the scientific conception of the world is spoken of, one often lays too great weight
on the general laws of nature, which indeed are also what strikes the eye most when the
modern picture of the world is compared with the old. But the laws are really
abstractions. They designate the forms and rules according to which motion and
development in existence proceed. But the essential thing is that motion and development,
in the spiritual as well as the material domain, always have a certain definite direction
and with it a certain definite individual character. Existence is like a great
individuality; it stands before us as one of countless possibilities; we must take it as
we take the single human being, who is always more than an example of a general law. An
individuality arises through an interplay of laws; it is (to use H. C. Ørsted's word) a
manifold unity (in
% --- p. 241 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{241}opposition both to ``simple unity'' and to manifoldness), which we must take as
experience gives it. As far back as the boldest hypotheses go, we constantly find certain
definite forces working in certain definite directions. Even if one could derive all
later forces and directions from these --- for us --- original ones, the original forces
and directions still stand for us as mere facts, which must be taken as facts, just as
the original dispositions with which a human life begins must be taken as factually given
in their individual determinateness. Existence is unique, and if we have the right to
compare it with a drama, then it is a drama with a definite, special exposition and
sequence of scenes, which must be taken as they once for all are.

The investigator of nature and the historian seeks to find his bearings in the course of
this drama. But since only a single scene, perhaps even only a single line, of this great
drama falls within our experience, we cannot construct the whole drama for ourselves.
And if we tried it, the drama that we got out would again prove to be only a part of a
still greater drama. Every mythology, every dogmatics and every metaphysics leaves ---
however it may go with the inner coherence and consistency in their constructions ---
always unanswered questions, partly with regard to what lies before the beginning of the
drama they depict, partly with regard to what follows after its end. Neither the
foundation nor the consequences are exhausted.

A human individuality too is inexhaustible in its peculiarity. It is impossible for us
to find and connect all its elements so that the ``manifold unity'' comes forth in its
special character. But still more does this hold with respect to existence as a whole,
where we do not even, as with a limited individuality, have the interaction with a
surrounding world to learn from. The problem of existence gets its sharp sting precisely
from this, that existence, when we try to conceive it as a whole, must have a special,
individual character, and
% --- p. 242 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{242}that the individuality we try to ascribe to it cannot stand as conditioned by the
relation to anything different from it, as is otherwise the case with all individual
forms of existence.


For our general theoretical and practical principles this has its great significance.
Were they to be completely demonstrable as valid, existence would have to stand before us
as an individual whole whose inner law those principles were. Thus the proposition of
causation could be strictly proved only if all phenomena could be referred to their
definite place within a great whole; the same holds of the proposition of the persistence
of energy, which can be proved only for closed and isolated wholes. The unprovability of
the principles is connected, as has also appeared in what precedes, with the unfinished
character of existence --- for us and perhaps in itself. The proposition of the
persistence of value shares the fate of the other principles; only if we could grasp
existence in its whole ``manifold unity'' would it be possible to become clear what
validity it has. And in every attempt to assert it, it must not be forgotten that every
such general proposition means only an incursion or an insight into reality from a single
side, not an exhaustive determination. Both the individual character of existence and its
infinity here constantly set us limits. ---

There thus stand more possibilities open for the validity of the religious principle than
is often assumed. Both empiricism and dogmatism can inhibit thought. Yet the most serious
difficulty for holding fast to that principle rises rather from the fact that existence
presents too many possibilities than from the fact that it offers too few. There goes on
in existence a constant friction and struggle between elements and individuals. Countless
germs are cast out, but only few take root and carry life and development further. And
where development is promoted, it happens under mutual inhibition, through displacement
and pushing aside. Development proceeds from
% --- p. 243 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{243}a multitude of different starting points, and the tendencies of development that
these starting points possess stand by no means from the first in harmonious relation to
one another. Most frequently a result is attained at one point only by nothing being
attained at other points. Resistance and hindrance could certainly have their great value
in being releasing forces that set free an energy that would otherwise slumber; but it is
very far from being the case that the resistance and hindrance that experience shows us
are limited to this.

It is the sporadic character of all development that causes the running costs in
existence to seem greater than the yield. Existence shows itself wasteful and therefore
merciless. And the wastefulness brings about, so it seems at least, a loss of value.

The problem of the disharmonious in existence leads back to the problem of unity and
manifoldness. It has proved impossible to derive the manifoldness from the unity, and
even if it were possible, the problem of disharmony would thereby be able to appear all
the more glaringly: for if the manifoldness --- the many sporadic starting points --- had
really sprung from a principle of unity, how would it then be possible to explain that
there is not always a harmonious but often a disharmonious relation between the different
tendencies of development in the manifold elements and individuals? There could then well
be frictions between them, but not greater than the release of energy required\footnote[1]{Cf. \textit{Den nyere Filosofis Historie.} The sections on Böhme, Bayle, Leibniz, Butler and Schelling.}.
It is of no use here to refer to the myth of the Fall; it is only putting several riddles
in place of the one. For how is a fall possible when every element and every individual
in existence has originally sprung from a harmonious principle? And a Fall explains at
most the disharmony within the human world, not the other disharmonies in existence. If
one will say that the possibility of a fall
% --- p. 244 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{244}must be there if man was to be a personality, since such a one develops only through
choice between possibilities, then one falls into a serious self-contradiction when one
ascribes personality to the Godhead and yet will not admit the possibility of a Fall in
its case. The very possibility of a fall is besides already an expression of a
disharmony: if I have the possibility of committing a scoundrel's trick tomorrow, then I
am already a scoundrel today. If one admits an original possibility of a fall as posited
by the Creation, then one has also admitted that the disharmony is produced by the
Creation itself, which indeed also from another point of view must consistently stand as
a depreciation of value. No scholasticism can avert this consequence.

There is one side of the matter that is usually overlooked in the discussion of this
problem. One is inclined to assume that it is the totality of existence, the principle of
the whole, that has ``right'' on its side, and that the single element or individual,
whose tendency of development leads to a breach of this totality, has ``wrong''. But it
is a great abstraction when the principle of the whole is in this way set in opposition
to the single elements or individuals. The force that stirs in each of these is indeed a
part of the force of the whole, and the laws that work in the single being in a
``manifold-unitary'' way are individualized interplays of the general laws of existence.
Each single element of existence has in this respect the same ``right'' as the whole, and
it is not rebellious because it follows its own law, its own tendency of development. It
is an oriental way of thought that makes one inclined beforehand to find the ground of
disharmony in the elements and not likewise in the principle of the whole. It was against
this way of thought that Goethe reacted in the magnificent poem ``Prometheus'': it was
precisely the inner divine germ in the single finite being that he wanted to assert in
opposition to the usual external
% print: ud-/udvortes (the syllable ud doubled over the line end; translated as external)
concept of God. Only from a dualistic religious standpoint could this poem be regarded as
irreligious. The profound
% --- p. 245 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{245}religious conception, according to which God himself suffers, has (however it may
settle the matter mythologically or dogmatically) in reality given dualism its deathblow,
without itself knowing or wishing it. --- But the settling of the whole account becomes of
course so much the more difficult when regard must be had not merely to the possibility
of a total harmony, which might arise by the interplay of the finite harmonies and
disharmonies, but also to those regions in existence where disharmony reigns, and where
one cannot hear how it fades into an all-embracing harmony. Such regions are after all
independent parts of existence and cannot be dismissed with the claim that they represent
only subordinate parts in the great world concert. Only an oriental ``superman'' (of whom
the supermen constructed in the most recent times are impotent plagiarisms) could sit in
his heaven and find harmony in his existence under these conditions. Here lies the great
justification and the beneficial effect of the protests of Voltaire and Schopenhauer.

When existence, even from a purely theoretical and intellectual standpoint, which asks
only for scientific understanding, can come to stand as irrational or as unfinished,
then it holds no less from the standpoint of evaluation. It is confirmed here too that
religion does not solve the riddles that science cannot solve. The advantage that
religion perhaps has here over science is that it can more easily lead thought away from
the insoluble riddles, so that their sting is less felt; this is the advantage where there
is to be willing and acting, --- where the struggle is to be taken up, and where courage
is to be kept up. --- In order to escape the sting that is in the problem we stand before
here, one must --- to use an image of Bayle --- keep thought from seeing its own shadow,
just as Bucephalus could be controlled only when it was not frightened by its own shadow
on the ground. Consciousness must be occupied by an ideal content, concentrated on a
luminous thought, so that it is not inhibited in its striving by the notion
% --- p. 246 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{246}of the dark and disharmonious sides of existence: this is a condition for the very
struggle against the dark and disharmonious being able to be carried on. But to look away
from the problems is not to solve them. And it is then most consistent to admit
insolubility, especially when it is the case that life can be led greatly and beautifully
even if one does not get a solution to all riddles. It is not the whole factual
constitution of existence that really concerns us as striving beings, but the conditions
for continued development that it offers. Naturally the strength of resistance and the
degree of disharmony will determine the character of our experience concerning the
relation between value and reality. But the proposition of the persistence of value is in
itself independent of how little or how much value there is in existence; it says only
that the value which is there persists. Only if one lets reality spring from value does
the sting of the problem become unendurable. If there is a possibility of asserting and
perhaps increasing the value that once for all is found in existence, then the religious
axiom can be maintained.

There are two thoughts that will always be of far-reaching significance for our position
toward the problem that the disharmonies in the world cause.

First, our experience is limited in relation to the unsurveyable existence, and it must
therefore constantly be tested whether an extension of experience might not bring about
another result. Already in purely theoretical problems this thought plays an essential
role. When our experience by strict inference leads to self-contradiction, we do not
therefore give up the assumption of the validity of the logical principles, but test
whether the ground of the self-contradiction did not lie in our experience's being too
limited. Often it then turns out that the contradiction falls away when we can extend
experience. Thus too objections to the persistence of value will often fall away when we
widen our horizon through discovery or production of values there where hitherto there
did not seem to be possibility
% --- p. 247 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{247}of them. It was the most important and approximately the only valid thought in
Leibniz's well-known attempt to assert an optimistic view of the world, that by means of
the idea of the infinity of existence we are more favorably placed toward the problem of
disharmonies than the thinkers of antiquity (Plotinus, Augustine).


Second, life is constantly rejuvenated. Every year has its spring, every generation its
youth. New germs constantly come forth, and there is no trace that the world should have
grown old. A new section of the history of existence constantly begins, in relation to
which the earlier series of experiences on which we build our judgments about the value
of reality are only a prehistory; the whole earlier drama now becomes only an exposition
to a greater drama. Existence is not only unsurveyable but inexhaustible.

A faith in the persistence of value is therefore, in spite of all, psychologically
possible. Whether it also really exists, and in what form and what extent, experience
must show.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

2. \textsc{Kristian Kroman} (b. 1846). Principal works: Vor Naturerkendelse % print: „2.“ -- the Contents numbers Heegaard-Feilberg 1.-6.; the §§ print 1, 2 (Høffding), 2 (Kroman), 3 (Wilkens), 4 (Lehmann), Feilberg unnumbered (both readings recorded in the transcription header)
(1883). Om Maal og Midler for den højere Skoleundervisning
(1886). Kortfattet Tænke- og Sjælelære (1882. ---
4th ed. 1905). Etik. (Den almindelige Del). (1904). --- A piece
from ``Tænke- og Sjælelære'' is given here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{THE GROWTH OF KNOWLEDGE}


For the perceiving subject all sensations are from the first only a series of uniform
factual states or partly uncomprehended images that stream past the inner vision. The
important oppositions, well known to the developed consciousness, between the apparently
blind, accidental temporal \emph{alternation} and the firm, necessary \emph{causal}
% --- p. 248 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{248}\emph{connection}, between the immediately appearing \emph{properties} and the thing
lying behind them, which bears them, between the relatively self-sufficient \emph{external
world} and the relatively self-sufficient \emph{ego} or \emph{subject}, are not yet
explained. How these oppositions gradually assert themselves we shall now briefly
indicate.

They are all due to the subject's \emph{belief in causation}, the assumption, illuminated
several times in what precedes, that \emph{every} change \emph{has its cause}, that every
definite \emph{antecedent} gives a definite \emph{consequent} and conversely, and this
assumption arises, \emph{psychologically considered}, like so many others from the
activity of association. If the sense-image \textit{B} is often followed by the
sense-image \textit{A}, then this latter will in future constantly awaken the
association-image b and with it an expectation of the sense-image B; if I have often
seen a man walk past followed by a yellow dog, I shall, the next time I see the man, also
expect to see the dog, i.e., I now believe that there is a firm connection between the
two phenomena. Of course we form in this primitive way a great multitude of expectations
that are never fulfilled; but experience teaches us gradually and involuntarily to
distinguish between the greater or smaller probability of the fulfillment of the
expectation, and our great interest in being able at all to determine what is coming
keeps alive our belief that there is regularity everywhere in the course of events. For
the reflecting individual it will gradually dawn clearly that the belief in causation
thus involuntarily formed is still quite groundless and also cannot be grounded by
experience; at the same time, however, its complete necessity as a presupposition for all
our living and acting will more or less consciously announce itself, and the theory of
causation set forth in the Introduction is thereby brought near. By this latter the
proposition of causation finds its defense \emph{as a valid}
\emph{means of knowledge}; it depicts for us, in other words, the \emph{epistemological}
--- in opposition to its just-described \emph{psychological} --- \emph{origin} of the
proposition of causation.

% --- p. 249 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{249}By means of this mighty weapon (the proposition of causation) our content of
consciousness is gradually transformed and extended to the highest degree. In reality the
different kinds of transformations and extensions go on simultaneously, step by step; we
must, however, here depict them one by one, as if they followed one after another. The
general progress consists in the subject's finding more and more causal connection in the
manifold of representations, originally determined only by time and space. In order that
this may take place throughout, there come, however, at all points peculiar
\emph{supplementary additions} to what is immediately observed as necessary. Our senses
always supply us with only properties, not things. We do not sense the apple itself, we
sense only its form and color, its smell, its taste, its hardness, etc. All these
sense-images appear, however, in a peculiarly regular way in our consciousness; they come
and go frequently together; from the presence of some of them we can with great certainty
infer the presence of the others. By turning and twisting the apple in our hands we get
in a short time a great manifold of sensations of color, extension, sound, pressure and
muscle, which all to a high degree condition one another. To explain this we then form,
by virtue of our belief in causation, the assumption of a \emph{thing} behind the
sense-images thus connected; these latter become \emph{properties, forms of revelation,
expressions} of a present \emph{cause, whose constitution, however, we know only through
all these}
\emph{changing forms of expression or effects}.
For everyday use we certainly also believe that we know the thing itself or the
\emph{essence} in contrast to the properties, the forms of expression. ``The apple
itself'' too seems familiar enough to us; we know indeed that it consists of cells in
such and such an order and of such and such a constitution. It is, however, once again
the form, the arrangement of its elements, that we have described. These elements we can
again dissolve into molecules and these into atoms.
% --- p. 250 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{250}But the atom itself is to us an x, known only through its effects. On the other hand we
must again emphasize that the enormous sum of effects, expressions, properties that we
get to know in different ways in the thing can in itself give us an extraordinarily full
and practically sufficient concept of it\footnote[1]{For the rest we may note that the expressions thing, essence and the like are used in a double way: now of the very kernel or center in contrast to the expressions, the properties, and now of the sum total of the center + all expressions, effects or properties together. Popularly the latter usage is the commoner.}. It is as if only a single point, the center itself,
were left of which we lack \emph{direct} or \emph{positive} knowledge, in that we merely
know that it must be there, if our assumption of causal connection is to be maintained.

In a similar way we gradually form the representation and assumption of a \emph{relatively
self-sufficient external world} over against the subject. From the first this opposition
between an objective and the subject is not present. All our representations, feelings
and other states are comparatively uniform members of the same whole. Soon, however, our
sense-images come into a certain opposition to the remaining members. Already by their
great distinctness and forceful appearance they awaken more attention than the images of
memory and imagination, and soon we notice also that they as a rule stand less in our
power than these; we can as a rule direct and more easily change or interrupt a series of
memories or imaginings than a series of sense-perceptions; our sense-images stand as a kind of
\emph{stubborn representations for us in} opposition to the others, more obedient, and
involuntarily we therefore ascribe to them a certain \emph{independence, a certain}
\emph{self-sufficiency} toward us. On many occasions we notice, moreover, that they can
directly offer resistance, awaken sensations of pressure and muscle in us. Thereby their
self-sufficiency in our eyes is increased; we ascribe to them \emph{force, power of
resistance} (Genstand, Objekt =
% --- p. 251 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{251}Modstand, ``what stands against''). To understand their peculiar coming and going,
which as a rule does not seem to stand in any further relation to our other states of
consciousness, while a certain regularity nevertheless constantly asserts itself here, we
finally assume that they \emph{also exist when they are not sensed}
\emph{by us}, i.e., we ascribe to them \emph{continuance}. But with that the assumption of
an objective external world is finished in its first shape. Still the separation has not
reached its full strength. The members of the external world and our sense-images are
indeed no longer identical, on account of the \emph{continuance} of the former, but yet
quite alike; we still believe that the table itself --- exactly like the table-image in
our consciousness --- is brown, that the lamp --- also when we leave the room --- shines,
that the string --- also when no ear is present --- sounds, etc. Various closer
experiences and reflections (at first developed especially by natural science) come,
however, in the case of a good many of them gradually to assert themselves and teach us
to see that colors, tones, sensations of light and warmth, etc., can only be states in a
perceiving subject and not properties or states of the things themselves, whereas in
these there must be found certain peculiarities such as definite surface constitution,
vibrations of the small parts, etc., etc., that \emph{correspond to} and call forth these
impressions in the subject. The sense-images thereafter no longer become \emph{copies} of
the members of the external world, but only \emph{symbols, signs} of these, and with this
we have then in the main and on the whole reached the conception of the external world
that the great majority of the educated hold and that is likewise asserted by all the
natural sciences. We have thus in brief outline described the \emph{psychological} origin
of the representation of the external world.

It is the business of psychology among other things to describe how we in general
\emph{actually} use our different faculties and powers; the theory of knowledge has, on
the other hand, the task of showing how we ought to use them if true knowledge is to be
the result. It will therefore have its special
% --- p. 252 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{252}interest also epistemologically to look somewhat more closely at the question of the
external world and test whether the procedure just described, or rather the result won,
can on the whole be defended. We have as \emph{epistemological} starting points 1) an
immediately certain \emph{world of representations} and 2) the assumption, secured by our
urge to know: \emph{every change has its}
\emph{cause}. Since we now actually find a manifold of changes in our world of
representations for which we do not succeed in finding causes in ourselves or within the
said world of representations, we must beyond the ego or this world of representations
assume a supplementary world of causes, which can again procure for us the missing
connection and unity. Of the closer constitution of these causes we are, however, for the
present quite ignorant; that they in higher or lower degree should resemble their effects
we have, however, no right to assert. On the other hand we have of course leave to
\emph{test} the hypothesis that there is complete likeness between our sense-images and
--- not their nearest causes in the brain or nervous system, but --- their more remote
causes outside the body. Such an investigation will namely for the present be justified
by the whole character of the sense process as being a means of knowledge. If we test the
said hypothesis in its full extent, we are, however, led to contradictions. Duration,
form, hardness and the like are properties that we can think the members of the external
world, the objective things, as possessing over against one another; colors, tones and
the other previously mentioned determinations seem, on the other hand, to be quite
unthinkable as states of the thing in and for itself or as relations between thing and
thing. That a thing can also ``be hard'', even if no sentient being is present, is
comparatively easy to elucidate and understand; that it should also be red when no one
sees it, with this statement we cannot, on the other hand, connect any clear meaning. If
we limit our hypothesis, however, to the assertion that the members of the external world
\emph{with regard to}
% --- p. 253 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{253}\emph{relations of time and space} essentially resemble our (developed)
representations of them, then we never run into contradictions; every inference from this
hypothesis is confirmed daily by all our experiences, and the view of the world thus
shaped, precisely the one we also came to before, therefore seems to stand as the truest
and surest we can form\footnote[1]{Cf. Vor Naturerkendelse, Kap. 19, 20, 21.}.

For closer assurance we shall nevertheless dwell for a moment longer on the matter. We
can call the view that besides the subject there are found other, coordinate members
(whether living or lifeless beings) in existence: \emph{objectivism}. Over against it
stands another view, according to which the knowing subject is alone in the world, in so
far as everything else, all other subjects and things, are only representations, states
within its consciousness without anything corresponding outside its consciousness or
coordinate with it. This latter view we call \emph{subjectivism}. Only seldom has this
latter doctrine been set up in full sharpness. Now the subjectivist has conceded to the
other \emph{subjects} a coordinate place in relation to himself; now he has conceded that
there \emph{possibly} were other coordinate members, but that by way of thought one could
never demonstrate anything of the kind, and now finally it has been held that subjectivism
and objectivism were two \emph{equally placed} assumptions, of which neither could be
proved or disproved. Not in any of these weakened forms either can the doctrine be
maintained. When the child tears its toy to pieces, it happens not so seldom that it is
unable to put it together again; in a similar way it may now and then go with the
philosophers and the universe, and one of the cases we surely have here. Many steps can
nevertheless be taken without arriving at an objective (coordinate with the
subject) external world. I command forth different series of thoughts in my consciousness
or let different series of associations have free course; hereby I am not pointed beyond
myself or my world of consciousness. I ``walk out along the highway'' and am interrupted
% --- p. 254 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{254}suddenly in my thoughts by the sight of an uncommonly beautiful country house on my
right hand. This too I can explain to myself without going beyond my world of
consciousness. The sense-image of the country house had, it is true, apparently no causal
connection at all with my other prevailing representations. But it is still possible that
it popped up as a consequence of the conditions I myself introduced, in that I --- as it
is called in everyday usage --- strolled out along the highway. I am strengthened in this
supposition, in that by introducing the same condition twenty times I get the same result
each time anew. The twenty-first time, however, I do not succeed. I now get the image of a
burnt-down country house. The same antecedent quite plainly no longer gives the same
consequent. This well-known phenomenon the subjectivist cannot master. The objectivist,
on the other hand, solves the riddle with ease by maintaining: So the given antecedent
consisted, besides myself and my conditions, also of a series of \emph{objective}
conditions (highway, country houses, etc.), and in these objective conditions there had
occurred on the twenty-first time a change (a country house had burned). Therefore my
sense-perception too had to become a different one.

Sometimes the subjectivist objects that all the objectivist's ``additions'' and
``explanations'' must themselves be mere representations and thoughts, so that he too
does not in reality get beyond mere subjective states. The previously mentioned
``objective'' conflagration is thus itself merely one more representation in consciousness
like all the others. To this it is to be answered, however, that the objectivist does not
first and foremost form the \emph{representation} of a conflagration or of an objective
member at all; it is first and foremost the \emph{assumption} of such a one that he sets
up, i.e., he makes the assertion: If I am to believe in causal connection in the world,
then I must also believe that besides myself there exist certain other, equally
self-sufficient members of existence, members whose existence is independent of my
representations and thoughts. If the subjectivist
% --- p. 255 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{255}asks again what is meant here by the expression to exist, the objectivist answers: To
exist is here used in the sense of \emph{to act, to give off effects}; the real in the
proper sense is simply the acting. When the subjectivist believes that the whole of
existence can be dissolved into himself, his actual sense-perceptions and his equally
non-self-sufficient ``possibilities of sense-perception'' (all the ``things'' he at the
moment does not sense), this assumption founders precisely on the fact that these
so-called ``possibilities of sense-perception'' incessantly \emph{produce}
\emph{effects}, though they are only ``\emph{possibilities} of sense-perception;'' they
must therefore also in this time be \emph{different}
\emph{from nothing} and thus have an existence independent of the subjectivist's
sense-perception. The untenability of subjectivism becomes most obvious, probably, when
one puts to the subjectivist the question, for example, whether he then also believes
that his own parents are merely two sets of representations in himself.

We thus form not merely the \emph{representation} of an objective external world; but we
also believe --- and rightly --- that this representation is a \emph{true} representation,
i.e., a representation to which a reality corresponds. We now go on to investigate the
corresponding problem for the ego, and first we ask about the \emph{psychological} origin
and development of the representation of the ego or subject. That this representation is
formed later than the representation of the external world and for most individuals never
gets beyond a certain vague indefiniteness, while the more reflective go their several
ways from there, will, in accordance with what has been remarked earlier, be easy enough
to understand. The first representation of egohood is presumably the representation of
one's own body. From the first this is given to us as a set of sense-images or a little
later as a piece of the external world not clearly delimited from the rest of the
external world. Gradually, however, we notice that this piece of external world seems in a
special degree to concern us; its influences and changes awaken pleasure and displeasure
directly in us; its movements come more and more into our immediate
% --- p. 256 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{256}power. Within this domain I act and suffer quite immediately; this domain is therefore
``myself'', the child concludes involuntarily. Gradually this representation of the ego
becomes, however, too broad and tangible, and we choose within the first domain a still
more central but at the same time more indeterminate ``something'', which then becomes
our ego, while the body in its totality now becomes this ego's nearest instrument or
possession, ``\emph{my} body''. Of the nature of this central ego natural consciousness
has at different times and under different conditions of culture formed widely different
views; now it was a more or less material, now a purely spiritual being in greater or
lesser dependence on the organism, and to these hypotheses, now materialistic, now
spiritualistic, the philosophers have then later added various others, more remote and
artificial, of which the previously mentioned \emph{Duplicism}
may be reckoned the most important.

To the question what is then the scientifically justified in these different views we
have already answered in what precedes, since we saw there that the only thing we can
establish with certainty about the ego is this, that it is an otherwise unknown unity with
the capacity to sense, feel, will and interact with the body. For closer illumination of
the circumstances we shall nevertheless still make a brief comparison \emph{between our
conception of the external world} and our
\emph{conception of the ego}. Quite immediately, by direct experience or observation,
there is, as already emphasized, given to us only a world of sense-images and other states
of consciousness. The \emph{con}\emph{tent} of these sense-images makes up the first
elements of our later picture of the external world and could be called our
\emph{primitive empirical or sensory external world}. Of our very sensing, feeling and
willing \emph{activity} too we have, however, immediate knowledge. We not only see and
hear; but we can at the same time \emph{be conscious} that we see and hear. On account of
this peculiar \emph{reflexivity} of the subject one has sometimes spoken
% --- p. 257 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{257}of an ``\emph{inner}'' \emph{sense} in contrast to the outer (and organic sense), a
sense with which the ego immediately perceived its inner states. Besides the direct outer
we thus get also a direct \emph{inner world of sense}. Both together form our whole
original world of sense or experience, a world that thus consists essentially only of
content of representation and acts of representation. Were now sense-perception or
experience our only valid means of knowledge (as consistent \emph{sensualism} or
\emph{empiricism} must maintain), then we could never with scientific right get beyond
this picture of the world. With the subjectivist we should have to stamp our external
world as a sum of contents of representation without necessarily corresponding objective
members, while the subject or ego --- if we may otherwise use this word, belonging to an
entirely different conception of the world --- simply became the sum total of a certain
multitude of acts of representation, feeling and will without a necessarily underlying
representing, feeling and willing something. This conception of the \emph{ego} too has in reality
been maintained at different times, now together with the corresponding conception of the
world and now together with the assumption of a really self-sufficient external world.

As soon as we with full consistency make also the \emph{proposition of causation} a valid
means of knowledge, our picture of the world becomes, however, gradually an entirely
different one. As the cause of all involuntary changes of consciousness we now assume a
world of relatively self-sufficient things, and behind all acts of consciousness we now
assume a really acting something, an ego or subject, which is not the ``sum of'' but the
holder, the producer of all these representations, feelings and expressions of will. From
a wild and uncomprehended manifold with absolute arising and passing away our world
thereby gradually transforms itself more and more into one single coherent, law-governed
and incalculable whole, in which the entire immediately given motley play of representations % print: „uberegnelig“, as printed (u7 C06)
is explained as a simple and
% --- p. 258 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{258}natural consequence of the ego's interaction with the remaining world-elements,
``the things''.

Between these two conceptions the choice is, however, by no means arbitrary. Had
sensualism or empiricism been presented with full consistency but a single time, its
untenability would at once lie in plain view. Through association, however, even the
sensualist is involuntarily led to reckon with the proposition of causation, and thus
there arise numerous sensualistically tinged but never wholly sensualistic pictures of the
world with causal connection now here and now there, where one most immediately feels the
need of it. Thus arises the singular phenomenon that a non-sensualistic conception of the
ego attaches itself to a sensualistic conception of the external world or conversely. How
we epistemologically justify the use of the proposition of causation has already been
mentioned earlier. That it is the very wish to live, to assert our being, that is the
foundation of this proposition, sufficiently explains why no one in the long run can
throughout abstain from building on it, and the only really tenable theory of knowledge
that there is, is therefore the one that besides sense-perception also reckons the
proposition of causation a valid means of knowledge. With these two factors every
non-philosopher reckons and thereby arrives at a conception of the world that no
philosophy has ever been able to exchange for an essentially simpler or more natural one;
with these two factors several philosophers reckon only in a quite illogical way, when
they set up the arbitrary and self-contradictory assertion that we may indeed use the
proposition of causation \emph{between} the immediately given members of the world, but
not by its help infer to new members in existence. For how should we indeed be able to
explain to ourselves, e.g., the arising and vanishing of the many moon-images in our
consciousness, if we might not assume a ``real'' moon out there beyond the subject and
existing also in the intervals in which it was not sensed?, how should we explain to
ourselves the peculiar appearance of the Sirius-image, if we might not
% --- p. 259 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{259}assume a ``real'' Sirius-member, which perhaps has never with full certainty been
sensed by any eye, but which nevertheless must enjoy full citizenship in our picture of
the world, if this is to be a whole connected by causes? And quite in the same way with
the subject! If this is not to become a nothing in every dreamless sleep, it must be
defined otherwise than as a sum or totality of representations. In this way one can define
\emph{consciousness}; but one must then not at the same time assert that consciousness can
see, hear, think, feel, etc.; for this a sum of representations obviously can do just as
little as the single representation itself can see, hear or think. ``When I look into
myself'', says a famous philosopher, ``I never see an ego, but always only definite
representations, feelings, etc.'' This assertion is entirely correct. Quite in the same
way a man could search through his whole house without ever running into himself or the
spectacles he may himself have on his nose. But this means simply that the ego is not
reached by sense-perception, but on the contrary immediately by a simple application of
the proposition of causation, a simple reflection or inference. For who was the searcher
who looked into himself? Surely not again merely a representation, but precisely the ego
that was being looked for. It is an indisputable truth that neither the central nature of
this ego nor that of the objective things, in contrast to their action, is more closely
known by us; they both stand before us as, in the last analysis, unknown X's. But these
very X's we can in no way, without getting contradictions, leap over or simply disregard
in our work with their expressions --- a precarious feat at which modern philosophy has
nevertheless with a certain zeal tried its hand.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

3. \textsc{Claudius Wilkens} (b. 1844). Principal works: Samfundslegemets
Grundlove, en Sociologi (1881). Æstetik i Omrids
(1888), Poesien (1893), Filosofisk Propædeutik (1904).
\end{intro}

% --- p. 260 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{A. THE BEAUTIFUL AND ART (From ``Poesien'').}\opage{260}

If something is to seize me poetically or aesthetically, it must captivate and interest
me. It must meet my mind as new and surprising and by a shock of wonder awaken attention
and set feeling in motion.

But this shock can now lead me into different states of mind and acts of consciousness.
It can threaten my existence and goad my instinct of self-preservation, prove to be of
importance for my personal welfare, awaken longing and impulse and lead me to intervene
actively in the world, into a \emph{relation of will}. Or the shock of wonder can awaken
my logical feelings and interests. The phenomenon strikes me as a riddle, a contradiction,
a question and leads me to investigate its connection and causes and spurs my impulse to
know. I enter into a \emph{relation of knowing} to things, and what I seek is a causal
relation, a law. But besides this double relation to the world, a relation of action,
which strives for a change in the course of things, and a relation of knowledge, whose
goal is a realm of laws, there is also a third relation, in which nothing of this happens,
but in a certain sense something far more glorious. The phenomenon that awakens my
interest and engenders emotion, mood and feeling in me can on the one hand be so clear,
\emph{complete in itself} and \emph{surveyable}, that it does not lead me into a relation
of knowledge. The intuitive image, as it were, says to me: ``Here I am whole, take me as I
am''! and even if manifold representations and thoughts do arise, it is only as a free
play of thought on the ground of intuition. And on the other hand the phenomenon
\emph{awakens} \emph{neither impulse nor longing}; the feeling that involuntarily
surfaces is a desireless feeling: ``Die Sterne, die begehrt man nicht, --- man freut sich
ihrer Pracht'', and since nothing leads me beyond the phenomenon, which nevertheless so
vividly seizes me, I am induced to \emph{dwell} in the mood-laden contemplation, to rest
in the feeling, and now
% --- p. 261 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{261}I stand in an aesthetic relation to things, in an immediate and desireless relation
of feeling. We may then for the present say that art and aesthetic contemplation
designate a \emph{conception of the world} in the \emph{desireless, feeling-toned}
\emph{form of intuition}. In this conception through feeling there lies something of a
valuation of the world, a free, personal, disinterested interpretation of the worth of
life and the world.

An aesthetic relation to things and life is thus the relation of free intuition. We know
the world only as image in us; but when these images are strong, compelling, stubborn, we
interpret them as expressions of an existence lying behind, as symbols of this. We can now
enter a double relation to this image; we can on the one hand interest ourselves in the
existence lying behind, whether we seek to explore its connection and know it, or we from
personal interest will act on it by way of the will and handle it. We are in that case
interested in the image only in so far as it is a symbol of representation for the
existence that lies behind, and forms a bridge to this, and it is really this that we
care about. But we can on the other hand also \emph{abstract}, i.e., \emph{disregard the
existence lying behind} and be interested only in the image, and this mood-laden
\emph{dwelling on the image as image} is free intuition, the joy in it the aesthetic joy. % print: „ved den den æstetiske Glæde“ -- „den“ doubled (PRINTED AS IS)

If we are to apprehend an object aesthetically, we must therefore disregard the existence
lying behind. This comes more easily to us of course with the beautiful in art than with
the beautiful in reality, though to different degrees. It is easiest for us with poetry:
here we indeed have to do altogether not with stubborn images, but only with images of
memory and imagination. We feel ourselves at once here in the world of free intuition and
imagination. It is not hard for us either with music; the real existence here is e.g.
vibrating ``catgut'' (air waves), whose vibrations remind us of something in the cry. But
the catgut we forget, and are greatly astonished when
% --- p. 262 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{262}\emph{Shakespeare} in ``Cymbeline'' (Act 2, Scene 3) has the realistic Cloten
remind us of it, and the recollection of the cry works so instinctively that we do not
know of it, and give ourselves up wholly to the tone-images and their relations. In
sculpture it is especially the absence of color that helps us to perform the abstraction.

But apart from this abstraction from certain sides of reality, which makes it easier for
us to reach the aesthetic state, art for the rest takes precisely pains to be as real,
typical and individual as possible, an image of life in its whole fullness.

And rightly! For that wonderful and mood-rich interpretation of the worth of life and of
what is, which was given to us in the aesthetic valuation by feeling, is not an
interpretation of mere fancies or of any loose creation of imagination, but an
\emph{appreciation} of what really is and of what is possible according to the lines of
development of what is. But only the \emph{living} has \emph{worth in itself for the
living}, and it is man's sympathy with man, with the animal and the plant and all that
lives, that in the last instance is the fundamental condition for man's aesthetic state,
as it is also love of life that is the fundamental motive in artistic imagination and
looses the poet's tongue. Aesthetics can triumphantly show that it is not simply any
perceptible form that moves and interests, but a form of life. Even an apparently purely
lifeless form of space, such as e.g. symmetry, we enjoy as beautiful only because we see
it as an expression of equal measure, but we interpret equal measure as an expression of
equilibrium, equal force, as a symbol of the feeling of firmness or security that we have
learned to value as something good in our practice in the world of gravity, when we
learned to stand straight on our legs. Only such a form in which we involuntarily,
through association, feel life, like the organic forms, or in which we are compelled to
\emph{feel into} life, like certain lines, movements, is beautiful for us. To be sure we
relate ourselves aesthetically only to the image as
% --- p. 263 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{263}image, but the image is to awaken feeling [i.e., an ideal representative of the
feelings corresponding to reality], and the image can awaken aesthetic feeling in us only
when we relate ourselves to a \emph{real image of life seen merely as image}. The image is
at once to \emph{seize} us, to be a seizing image, as if existence lay behind it, as if it
were real, i.e., it is to create illusion, and yet we are to care only about the image as
image. The true work of poetry gives us a piece of real life, an image of life, and yet we
know that there lies no existence behind, we are in full and complete illusion, but we
know ourselves in illusion, i.e., we oscillate incessantly between the captivating
reality and the consciousness that it is not real, we are as in a waking dream.

To this abstraction from existence and being set in a dream that dreams the dream of
life, art helps us by its abstractions (one-sidednesses). But for the rest we can also
come into an aesthetic relation to nature and reality, and it goes without saying that, if
nothing really beautiful existed, man would not have thought of reproducing
% print: noges (misprint for noget)
the beautiful. As long as we feel our dependence on nature and are compelled to know or
handle it, we are not in an aesthetic relation, any more than the sailor in distress at
sea has an eye for the sublime might of the sea. Only when we abstract from existence and
dwell on the nature-image as such is nature beautiful, poetic, sublime. Nature is
beautiful when it is seen as image, when we enter into the relation of free intuition to
it (as on walks, outings in the woods, journeys, or when the change of the season shows
us nature as new and young, or we see it in entirely altered conditions as through
colored glass).

Our analysis of the aesthetic relation to things as the relation of free feeling-toned
intuition has thus given us this result. Free intuition is the subjective state in us
which for the rest, seen more deeply, has its ground in the harmonious interplay to which
our faculties, intuition, representation, feeling, etc., are brought. What awakens this
state in us is always a
% --- p. 264 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{264}real form of life or something that is interpreted as such [as \emph{Goethe} says:
``We have the beautiful where we behold the lawfully living in its greatest activity and
perfection, whereby we are moved to a reproduction and feel ourselves as it were set in
the highest activity''], a form or world of images that by its inner fullness of life,
which we sympathetically live after, involuntarily and gripping preaches to us the
significance of life and thus compels us in that judgment of feeling to assert the
significance of what is as being. The beautiful is always image (world of images, world of
imagination). That follows already from this, that only semblance,
% print: Blilede (misprint for Billede)
image, perceptibility, sensuousness can bring us into a free relation of intuition to
what is, and furthermore from two other psychological principles, that representation is
always the fundamental condition for the release of feeling, and that the more concrete a
representation is, the more it approaches giving us a definite and distinct image, in
contrast to the abstract representation, which at most possesses weak remnants of images,
the higher the degree to which it awakens feeling. ---

The beautiful and poetic in a wider sense, the mood-laden, we find in nature and life as
the real-beautiful. Were there nothing of the kind, man would not have come to create a % print: „kom Mennesket til at skabe en Kunstens Verdens“ -- no negation, and „Verdens“ for Verden (PRINTED AS IS); translated by sense
world of art. The beautiful is not an invention, but a \emph{discovery}, and the
productive find is given in the motif, the emotion-stirring value of life that has seized
the artist, awakened his love and interest and burned itself into his soul as this holy
unrest that begets.

But the real-beautiful is not found in any especially great fullness in nature and
life. If one excepts the beauty of landscape and of color tones one finds the beautiful
only scattered and mixed with the indifferent and ugly. Human bodies are for various
reasons imperfect, everyday speech only a poor expression of feeling, and the human cry
only a raw material for music. The beautiful is there, but scattered in space and time:
the gold is there, but hidden in the veins of the mountains.
% --- p. 265 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{265}Then art steps in. By various means, whose possibility is given in the nature of
imagination, the artist purifies that beautiful element and carries it further in accordance with
the laws of its own nature. He separates out the indifferent, he removes \emph{the} ugly
that he cannot use, he abstracts, intensifies or weakens, combines and thus refines in various
directions. He presents to us the ideal that nature itself has intended,
as \emph{Thorvaldsen} in his Jason gives us the perfect
male body; he idealizes nature by nature;
he purifies the type, he stamps and intensifies the
characteristic, and so gives us a refined, typical re-presentation of the real-beautiful and of the beauty that is possible.

\begin{smallquote}

``Therefore can our eye be gladdened --- By a picture's splendor of color,\\
For the light in which it is clothed --- Is nature's own attire.\\
And the stone whose beauty shone --- Shaped by a master's hand,\\
Became beautiful because he measured --- With our Creator's measuring tape.''
\end{smallquote}

(Richardt).

When the real-beautiful has thus passed through a human
spirit, it acquires something peculiarly captivating, actively gripping,
about it. This lies not only in the joy at the imitation
and at the artist's skill, though this joy is not
slight --- we take a particular pleasure in a good imitation,
and the child knows it early. For we know also that the great
artist, although it is precisely he who always seeks back most deeply to the
living model and to nature's own measuring tape, is nevertheless also
the one who most alters the proportions of things, copies least,
is not a mere imitator but a seer, one who is able, one who masters.
But what appeals to us most deeply in the art-beautiful is
that it is a product of the self-conscious human spirit. Art's
ideal world is \emph{man's} work. Every conquest
in the world of beauty is a deepening of human
self-consciousness and of its consciousness of nature, a new
valuation of the significance of life. It is sculpture which, itself
sprung from joy in human beauty, has truly
% --- p. 266 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{266}taught me that the human body is beautiful; it is
lyric poetry which, itself a child of mood, has truly taught me
the worth of what is full of mood; it is the same lyric, a child of love,
that has truly taught me that it is glorious to love;
it is the song of the fatherland that has truly spun love of the fatherland
into our hearts, it is the tone of the hymn that has
borne my soul toward heaven. The particular delight in the
art-beautiful thus lies not merely in the pleasure at a felicitous
imitation, but stems at bottom from the joy that
attaches to the clearer and deeper consciousness of nature
and life. Habit dulls, and men grow stupid. The artist
helps us to see the old and accustomed as new and
brings out the treasure of beauty and peculiarity that we
would otherwise pass by. He digs up the values of life for us;
``how truly it is seen!'' we exclaim, open our eyes and see.

In the productions of art the real-beautiful is re-presented
for us as if through intermediate links that make it more
accessible to us. Art's abstractions from certain sides of reality
help us to look away more easily from existence. We
receive what is beautiful or characteristic in life and reality
refined and in part intensified. The picture of life brings us more easily
into the immediate and desire-free state of feeling than reality
itself. The feelings that are awakened in us are for the most part
the same as those that reality itself would awaken, but
because the picture is detached from existence, a change
has taken place in these feelings: they have become desire-free,
ideal. Finally, the work of art gives us the real-beautiful or
characteristic as it is when it has passed through
a human spirit of its own, seen by a great and original
person who sees more clearly, loves more strongly and feels more deeply
than others, a peculiarly fine and strong soul with a temperament
of its own and a view of life of its own. In all great art we feel this
individual equation, this congeniality with nature and
life, the genius's X, and the work of art acquires its peculiar
magic from the fact that here we have nature as it has passed
% --- p. 267 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{267}through its highest revelation: man. It is not a
simple duplication of reality that we have before us here, but
a peculiarly deep, spirited interpretation and valuation of
life and reality. It is not an honest collector of natural
and human facts, a faithful copyist, a careful
keeper of minutes, that we stand before, but a seer and a prophet
who brings us tidings from the world of values.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. THE BEAUTY OF MOVEMENT (From ``Æstetik'').}


The first element in the joy in movement is \emph{the pleasure
in difference, change}. The general
fundamental law of the life of feeling, the law of relation, entails that the new,
the surprising, awakens feeling most strongly; change delights,
keeps the life of feeling fresh, while repetition dulls it
to the point of boredom. Now in movement, through the object's
mere change of place in space, there is always novelty, change,
new relations to the surroundings (the speed of the clouds, the birds'
flight, etc.). Into the resting line we read movement;
\emph{here} is movement itself, and ``in movement is life.''
Every movement therefore has aesthetically a great advantage over rest.
The life of feeling is at once set in livelier motion. It was
an extraordinary advance in the artistic representation
of the human body in sculpture and painting when it became able
to suggest movement; it was life itself that impregnated
art.

Through the experiences of life, however, we have learned to read movement
as the expression of force. Another element in the beauty of movement
is therefore \emph{force}. Even a locomotive's
movement acquires a certain austere beauty through the sureness of force.
Since a feeling of pleasure attaches to every activity of
forces and faculties, we feel joy in happily exercising our
energy upon an obstacle, and we feel the same joy in pure
% --- p. 268 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{268}aesthetic form in seeing others do it. Our own pleasure in
the exercise of force may in life be bound up with exertion
and displeasure, even if in dancing, in free games, in light gymnastic
exercises it rises into beauty. For contemplation or in
ideal presence it is freed from these life-inhibiting components.
The feeling-tones come forth more subdued and free,
and we have an aesthetic delight in these exercises of force of life.
Later we transfer this delight by association to our reading
of the movements of inorganic and organic nature,
but originally we understand them only from ourselves.

Not every movement, however, is beautiful. Movement
is beautiful only when it calls forth the idea of the subordination of the manifold
under a unity, when we discover unity in difference,
regularity in the movement, when the force at the same time acts
harmoniously. Difference without unity can at most be
picturesque, painterly; unity without difference becomes monotonous and
tiring. The more difference and change, the greater the demand
made on our powers; the senses and the imagination
are in livelier activity; but beauty requires
partly that the activity take place within the limits of
