its normal exercise, and partly that there be unity, regularity.
A chief mark of the beauty of movement is therefore
\emph{harmony, rhythm, regularity}. We see here
the deep connection of beauty with purposiveness,
for in reality regularity means purposiveness,
the adaptation of the movement to its medium and its purpose.
Everything moving that moves through a certain medium
meets greater or lesser obstacles on its way. From this there follow,
as Spencer and Tyndall have developed it, successive
movements back and forth, more or less undulating lines,
which call forth rhythm. Rhythm or regularity
is thus not really different from force: it is a
means for force to overcome resistance most easily; it is
the plan, the purposiveness in force, the economy of force.

Regularity in movement is a general requirement,
% --- p. 269 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{269}but through the element of difference it can assume an infinity
of forms, and the movements can prove highly composite.
The simplest case is that an uncompounded object
changes place in space, and that a certain regularity shows itself in this.
Here a difference in direction can be conceived, and here too
we are led to find that curvilinear movement, like the
curved line in general, is more pleasing than the straight, since
the moment of difference is greater. There can further be difference
in speed, and the most beautiful movement will in general
be the one that is at once slow enough to give
us time to apprehend each position clearly and definitely, and fast
enough to compel our powers to function lively during this
activity of apprehension. But most often the moved
object is of course composite, and the richest source of difference and
also of beauty is the combination of several simultaneous movements
in the different parts of the same object or in the different
elements of the same observation. If, e.g., it is a question
of the movement of a face, \emph{the beauty of expression}, or
merely of a simple play of features, a gesture, then there is here a
multiplicity of differently moved members in the moved
totality, which compels the imagination to unite a series
of several successive moments in the unity of the total movement. Therefore
the harmoniously moved human face must act
so powerfully on us. It can be graceful or harmoniously beautiful
in joy and tenderness; it can be characteristically captivating
under the impression of stronger emotions. But
it is always the infinite play of movements that pleases us.

Here too we must name, as an element that \emph{psychologically}
explains to us the delight in regularity, the joy
in \emph{the fulfillment of expectation}. During the observation
of regularity a definite expectation forms itself:
the coming movement is anticipated by memory,
and as the expectation is fulfilled, the mind feels a satisfaction,
a joy, which is put into the object as a merit
of the object. The irregular, strange, bizarre, outlandish
% --- p. 270 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{270}movement, which constantly awakens surprise and wonder
(as with the lines of the baroque style), does not have this advantage
and acts only for a moment as a stimulus, later as psychically lacerating;
to which must further be added the \emph{physiological}
moment, that the eye is strained and tired by irregular
or angular movement.

Harmonious movement is beautiful, but it need not
really be graceful, although grace has often been defined as
beauty in movement. One can also speak of an austere
style in the beauty of movement, especially where great forces
are concerned; but beauty in movement perhaps finds
its highest form in grace and strives toward it, and
grace can to that extent be named as one of the marks of this
kind of beauty.

Here too, as on other points, Herbert Spencer has
met with ideas that had been developed by Fr. Schiller,
and has given them a more modern-scientific stamp. He
tells himself how he came to his theory. I was watching,
he says, a dancer one evening, and in my quiet mind
I condemned her exertions as so many barbarous
contortions, which one might have hissed at, if
the spectators had not had the malice to applaud where
they thought applause was due. But I also discovered
that now and then there came movements of true grace, and it struck me
that it was these that cost the least effort. I came
to think of other facts that confirmed my
hypothesis, and developed it into a general theory. The
movement which, when we see it or perform it, makes the
greatest impression of grace on us is the one in which every
tension of force or muscle seems to have vanished,
during which the limbs, so to speak, play freely as if borne by
the air. This also explains the superior effect of beauty
that art has always known how to conjure forth by
curvilinear movement. For the curved line is like the schema
for a movement in which as little force as possible goes to
% --- p. 271 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{271}waste, in contrast to angular movement
with its great loss of force and muscular exertion. For the performer
that movement is the easiest, freest; for the beholder the
most beautiful, and also the easiest for the eye's muscular movements.
Curvilinear movement seems to us floating, light and free;
rectilinear and angular movement either heavy and unbeautiful or stiff and
austere. Here too it turns out that beauty in movement
can be traced back to the economy of force, to perfect
purposiveness.

Playful movement (dancing, free gymnastics) of course
most easily assumes grace, but Guyau is right that even
able, purposive work acquires a certain beauty
when the work goes like play, when we feel in it the same
freedom and lightness that characterizes grace. Through the perfect
worker's complete adaptation to his task and
the feeling of freedom that practice breeds, his movements acquire
the form of beauty; and the fact that here one works for an end, whereas
play is a purpose in itself, diminishes the delight so little
that we can even say that two new
moments of interest are added here: the intellectual activity of
finding the purpose of the application of force, and the aesthetic-intellectual
activity in the harmony between the end and the forces employed as
means. Indeed, at bottom we must also in play
involuntarily make a similar valuation of the relation of the means
to a presupposed end, since lightness can only be understood through
the relation between the forces and the end, in the adaptation of force
to the end while an unused surplus is preserved. Play too
is, as activity, a kind of work, and the boundary
between play and work is not as sharp as Spencer would make
it. Grace consists most often in a kind of conscious or unconscious
work, accomplished with less force, greater precision and greater
lightness, a consequence of complete adaptation to a real
or imagined end, the complete harmony between life
and the surroundings. --- It is something of this that we admire in
consummate technique, the perfect adaptation of the means to
% --- p. 272 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{272}the end, as when Ole Bull handled his violin, while
the spinning Hercules arouses laughter by the disharmony
between the slight end and the means' enormous sum of force. It
is this that we admire in the well-formed lancers, whereas the farmhands'
vigorous hopping in the dance has not attained grace
and does not seem to have stopped at the sublime either.
It is this that Ludvig Feilberg, who here offers us a whole
collection of examples, calls tautness in bodily movement. One
of his examples he takes from skating. ``Without tautness
there is no real manner to the skating. It is tiring. One's
vital force is strained without one's being able to get anything out of it; one
cannot comprehend how people can amuse themselves with it. Only
when one begins to dare to trust oneself to one's legs, when
one dares give oneself up to a full, brisk sweep, stretch out in
the air like the circus rider without anxiety about the prosaic
plunge, only then does the sport begin to yield an interest,
only then can one find something that one might call spiritual
content in it''\footnote[1]{Om størst Udbytte af Sjæleevner. p. 45.}. This tautness, which is a product of
practice, is due, like all practice, to perfect adaptation,
but this consists in reaching the end with the least loss of force,
with lightness and freedom, and we value the movement as beautiful,
graceful, by feeling it as the expression of the inner feeling that
is the essence of the lightness and the freedom.

For it is not only the two elements, the joy in difference
and change and the joy in the fulfillment of expectation,
that are of a psychological nature. The more objective
elements too, force, rhythm, grace, point back to psychological
\emph{principles}. Even if a part of the preliminary elements
in grace, e.g. the delight in the curved line, can be explained
physiologically, yet when it is a question of something so
composite as a human being's movement, and especially in the
highest form of grace, the \emph{expressive movement of the human face,
the beauty of expression}, evidently
other factors into play, and by association they attach
% --- p. 273 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{273}themselves involuntarily to the joy in the simple forms of movement as well.
We read movement as the expression of force and forms of force,
but force we understand only from something in ourselves, from
spontaneous energy and its direction toward a definite end,
from the will; and the associated feelings of security, equilibrium,
courage, lightness, etc. attach to this. The regularity of movement
appeals not only to the intelligence but is also valued as
an expression of the will's consistency; grace is read as an expression
of the feeling of pleasure that perfect harmony with
% print: Millieuet (misprint)
the milieu awakens once the will has reached this end. It is
thus the \emph{mental content} that we value in the movement
we call beautiful. We are accustomed to see this movement
as the constant sensuous companion of a free and happy
feeling, and through habit and association every movement comes
to represent a feeling, a state of consciousness;
outer life is for us a revelation of the inner. It is
the happy feeling that here makes up the spiritual content,
the world of values that we read in the beautiful form. Harmonious
happiness, that is beauty. The angular and awkward movement
displeases us not only physiologically, but essentially because
it is read as the expression of an unfree mind, a mind moved
by a feeling of displeasure because it is not in harmony with its
surroundings, whether the cause of this be a lack of mastery over one's
body or embarrassment at the sudden exertion of
force that danger demands. Only compare
the forms and movements of a dog in anger or fear
with its corresponding movements in joy, and one will see that
the angular and hard forms and movements attach to
danger, struggle, anger, the soft and rounded ones to happiness,
play and freedom. The light, free, graceful, half-floating
movement pleases us as the expression of joy and goodwill,
as the form of the self's harmony with its surroundings and the sympathy
that follows from it. It is the joy of life, the harmony of life, for
which grace is the expression, that pleases us through life's
sympathy with life. Of course there can be more austere and
% --- p. 274 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{274}more ingratiating forms of the beauty of expression and of
movement (compare the Venus de Milo and the Medici Venus),
and even sharp, angular forms can offer a certain aesthetic attraction
through the characteristic; but even in this there must
be a certain harmony, a certain closed bearing, and everywhere
it is in the last instance a content of mind that we read in the form, and
that is associated with the form by experience, and now infects
us and awakens our sympathy. Here again we get a vivid impression
of how psychological the foundation of the beautiful is. It is
the law of self-preservation, to strive for pleasure and shun displeasure,
and sympathy and association, that are the key here.

Besides these nearer, primary associations, by
which beauty and grace in movement are, universally among men,
attached to the mind's happiness, lightness and freedom, there must also be named
a series of \emph{remoter}, highly indefinite, but nonetheless contributing
\emph{associations}. Beautiful and graceful movements
are in life especially attached to cheerful and cultured persons,
whose appearance as a whole gives us satisfaction; to
festive occasions such as dances, balls, etc.; to places that on the
whole give us joy, such as theaters and the like; to the representations
of art; and such associations play an indefinite
part in stressing the joy more strongly. As regards grace in particular,
it must further be added that, since woman, in her whole
build and in the nature of her feelings, is nearer to
grace than man, who is disposed more toward dignity,
there will, especially for man, attach to grace an associative tone
of the enchantment that the difference of the sexes affords human beings\footnote[1]{See my essay: Mand og Kvinde. ``Tilskueren'' for 1887. p. 607 ff. and 693 ff.}.

Finally, to the elements named in the beauty of movement
there can be added an element of the sensuously pleasant.
For it is not only the constant alternation, change and
novelty that please us in the running of animals, the flight of birds,
the swimming of fish, the speed of ships, the flight of clouds, and the
% --- p. 275 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{275}still richer play of movement in human mimicry; but
the sensuous phenomenon itself is also constantly altered and renewed.


Thus there is here a great number of aesthetic elements,
but the center of gravity lies in our reading of the quick
and light movement as the expression of force, rhythm and pleasure in
activity, and in our reading of the measured, rounded
movement as the form of the soul's equilibrium and harmony.
The center of gravity lies in the psychological content, in the soul's
harmony. Even Schiller, who is otherwise inclined to stress
form, states this: ``All movements,'' he says, ``that
proceed from the beautiful, free soul become easily gentle and living;
if the human being is cheerful and free, the eye will shine and feeling light up
in it. No tension will be discoverable in the muscles
(more correctly, no incoordination in the muscles), no
constraint in the voluntary movements, for the soul knows none.''
But the sensuous forms and movements attached to this we value
as beautiful only because by inner experience we know
the value of the feelings, of the freedom and harmony of life, that are
their inner soul. ``And the noble (and happy) content of the heart
does not by good fortune hit upon forms that are beautiful in themselves, but
these forms become beautiful by being a natural and intelligible
expression of that content.'' The aesthetics of form is partly
right to lay stress on form, and deserves honor for its exact
investigations in this field, but the deeper
truth is that in form we read a content of life: happiness.
There is beauty in the world, but in the last instance it is read out
of the most beautiful thing of all, the free, harmonious, happy
human soul, whether this expresses itself directly in the form
or is put into it.

\begin{intro}

% --- p. 276 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{276}4. \textsc{Alfred Lehmann} (b. 1858). Principal works: Om Hypnosen
(1890). Hovedlovene for det menneskelige Følelsesliv (1892).
Overtro og Trolddom (1893---1896). De sjælelige Tilstandes legemlige
Ytringer (3 vols.; the last two volumes only in German).
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. DETERMINATION OF THE VASOMOTOR \\ CHANGES \\ (From ``De sjælelige Tilstandes legemlige Ytringer'').}

\emph{The method}. If one wishes to make out the changes that under
given circumstances occur in the state of the vessels in a
normal, uninjured human being, there is, so far as I
have been able to see, only one way to do so, namely: the determination of
the velocity of the pulse wave in the various vascular regions. The
time by which the pulse beat in a given artery lags behind
the heartbeat varies constantly, and we know precisely the factors on which
the changes depend. It is especially
\textit{Grunmach's} investigations that have brought complete
clarity on this point, since he showed not only the actual
state of affairs but also the causes of the mutually divergent
results of older researchers. By direct physiological measurements
on animals as well as on healthy and sick human beings,
Grunmach\footnote[1]{Ueber die Fortpflanzungsgeschwindigkeit der Pulswellen. Du Bois' Archiv. 1879. p. 417 ff. Ueber die Pulsgeschwindigkeit bei Erkrankungen des Circulationsapparates. Virchow's Archiv. Vol. 102. p. 565 ff. Du Bois's Archiv. 1888, p. 129 ff. Cf. v. Frey: Die Untersuchung des Pulses. pp. 131---39.} found, namely, that the velocity of the pulse wave increases
not only with blood pressure but also with the tonus of the vessel
examined. And by comparative physical investigations
of the propagation of the pulse wave in arteries and various other
elastic tubes he showed that there were essential differences
between the various materials, but that the arteries belong among
the tubes whose coefficient of elasticity --- and with it also the pulse velocity
--- increases with increasing pressure.

% --- p. 277 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{277}It may therefore be regarded as established that the time by which
the pulse beat in an artery lags behind the heartbeat will
be the shorter, the higher the blood pressure is, and the more the vessel wall
has contracted under the influence of the vasoconstrictors.
If, then, one determines the variations of this time difference
for a single artery only, no
inferences can be drawn from it about the changing states of the vessel,
since, e.g., a shortening of the time can be due either to a general
increase of blood pressure, or to a purely local contraction of the vessel,
or to both together. The easiest way
in which this difficulty could be overcome might
now seem to be that one determined, simultaneously with the variations of
the pulse delay, also those of the blood pressure. It is, however, quite
doubtful whether one would really be able in that case to
draw sure inferences; I shall, however, not enter upon a closer
discussion of this point, because we are not able continuously
to determine the changes of the counter-pressure. Possibly
one can, by a suitable use of one of the so-called
sphygmomanometers, measure the blood pressure at a single given moment,
but precisely what matters, namely the constant
variations, we cannot register. Consequently it is
not worth the trouble either to examine what could be inferred
if one knew these quantities.

In general, then, as we see, nothing can be derived
from the pulse delay in a single artery. But if, on the other hand,
one also knows various other quantities concerning
the same artery, the matter stands differently. If one takes,
e.g., a plethysmogram of the radial artery simultaneously with
the heartbeat, the delay of the radial pulse can be determined from it,
and in that case one also knows the pulse frequency
and the variations of the volume and of the pulse height. From these four known
quantities sure inferences must in many cases be
drawable. For it was already shown earlier
that from the pulse frequency and the changes of the volume and of the pulse height we
are generally able to decide whether in
% --- p. 278 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{278}a given case vasomotor changes have taken place
or not. On the other hand it could not be decided whether these
changes consisted in a dilatation of the vessels in the arm or a constriction of the vessels
in other regions; for these changes would
have the same influence on the plethysmogram, but
we lacked the means to decide which of them had taken
place. Now it is evident that such a decision must
be possible when to the three quantities known from the plethysmogram
one adds a fourth, namely the delay of the radial pulse,
which depends directly on the tonus of the vessels. By way of example
we may consider a particular case. Let there be given
a plethysmogram in which the volume sinks with increasing
pulse length and decreasing pulse height, as is the case in
general when a piece of mental work begins. Since a lower
pulse frequency (increasing pulse length) will, other things being equal,
entail a greater pulse height and a diminished volume,
the diminution of volume in question can thus simply
be a consequence of this; but vasomotor changes are
of course not excluded. And since the pulse height actually becomes
smaller, whereas the slower heartbeat, ceteris paribus,
gives a greater pulse height, vasomotor changes must
therefore have taken place. But we cannot decide whether
these consist in a constriction of the vessels in the arm or a dilatation of the vessels
in other organs or both together. This
question can, however, be answered when the pulse delay is determined.
For if it proves to remain constant or to
decrease with sinking volume, there must necessarily
be a constriction of the vessels in the arm. For the slower
heartbeat has as its consequence a diminution of the arterial blood pressure,
and with that the pulse delay must increase. If it proves,
then, to be constant or decreasing in a single vascular region,
there must be a constriction of the vessels in this region.
It is of course not wholly excluded that there may at the same time
be a dilatation of the vessels in other regions; but since this
would only have a further diminution of the blood pressure as its
% --- p. 279 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{279}consequence, the constriction of the vessels in the arm must simply be so much the
stronger, since otherwise the pulse delay could not remain
constant or even decrease.

It is thus clear from this example that one will at any
rate in many cases be able to determine the vasomotor
conditions in a single vascular region when, besides
the pulse frequency, one knows the volume changes, pulse height and pulse delay
of the vascular region in question. If, then,
it were possible to determine these quantities for the brain
in a normal human being, one could, by
a series of experiments, determine the flow of blood to
the brain during various psychical states; and it could
thereby simply be decided whether the blood supply of the brain
stood in proportion to the work that it performed in producing
these states. Now these measurements unfortunately
cannot be carried out on an uninjured human being.
The volume changes of the brain at any rate cannot
be demonstrated by any way known so far, except when there
is an opening in the skull produced intentionally or unintentionally.
But when there is an opening brought about by fracture or trepanation,
one has in the rarest cases
an individual psychically completely uninjured
before one. Of the not few persons on whom over the years
brain curves have been recorded, only Bertino, examined by
Mosso, and \textit{Berger's} subject Str. seem really to
have been quite normal, and of these the former was, for other reasons,
only little suited as an object of psychological
experiments. It will thus always be a very rare
case to obtain a subject fully usable in every respect,
and consequently it would also be very desirable if
whoever once finds such a subject could arrange matters so as
not only to record plethysmograms of the brain but also to determine
the pulse delay in the brain, since this seems to afford
the only sure foothold for an interpretation of the curves.

The circumstance that we cannot determine the volume changes of the brain on an
% --- p. 280 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{280}uninjured human being will, however,
only make difficult, but not wholly impossible, the determination
of the conditions of circulation in the brain; all that is required is a few
more determinations. For if, besides the four quantities mentioned
above, we also know the pulse delay in the carotid,
the vasomotor conditions in the brain can be derived from it in all such
cases where the observed pulse delay cannot
have been caused by the changes in pulse frequency alone.
To understand this we need only consider the example mentioned
above. When the pulse frequency becomes lower at the beginning
of a piece of psychical work, this should, everything else unchanged,
entail a greater pulse delay in all regions.
If the measurements now show an increased pulse delay in the carotid,
it cannot be decided whether this is due to the slower
heartbeat alone, or whether a dilatation of the vessels possibly also takes
place in the brain. But when the pulse thereafter, during
the continued psychical work, begins to beat faster,
the pulse delay should, everything else unchanged, decrease. If
it nevertheless turns out that it becomes greater in the carotid --- but
not in other vascular regions --- there can be no doubt
that a dilatation of the vessels must be taking place in the brain. It is
thus evident that the vasomotor conditions in the brain
can be decided in all those cases where the pulse delay in the carotid
cannot have been caused by the simultaneous changes
in pulse frequency. And these inferences obviously become the
surer, the more vascular regions the pulse delay is determined
for, since one thereby obtains a guarantee that the changes of the
pulse delay in the carotid are really due to vasomotor changes
in this region and are not merely secondary
effects of greater changes in other vascular regions.

According to these considerations, then, the smallest number of recordings
with which one can make do, if reasonably sure
inferences are to be drawable, will be the following: the heartbeat,
the plethysmogram of an arm, the carotid pulse and finally respiration.
The registration of these various phenomena must
% --- p. 281 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{281}moreover be carried out in such a way that it is possible to derive the pulse delay
accurately from the curves. Experiments of this kind I have
carried out on a large scale in the course of the years 1897 and '98 with
several different male subjects, younger and older; not, however,
with female ones, partly because it is often difficult to record % print: „delt“ for dels (PRINTED AS IS)
the heartbeat in grown women, and partly because the placing of the cardiograph
necessitates undressing and a series of
manipulations that would be embarrassing for the female
subjects concerned. It would of course have been very
desirable if, besides the curves named, I had also
obtained the tibial pulse, but for purely practical reasons I had
to forgo this. For the placing of all these instruments
takes considerable time when one does not have at one's disposal a
larger staff of well-trained assistants; but through such lengthy
preparations the subject is tired and put into anything but
a normal state of mind, which is by no means suited as a starting point
for the following experiments. I therefore found it advisable
to confine myself to recording the curves that
were strictly necessary. Only in the spring of 1901, when a couple of
young physicians, who had earlier served as subjects
and were well trained in operating the instruments, again placed
themselves at my disposal for the same purpose, was
a longer series of experiments made in which the tibial pulse was registered
simultaneously with the other pulse curves. This series of experiments
is, by the nature of the case, much more valuable than all
the others, since it puts into our hands the means of interpreting
the less comprehensive experiments with certainty. In describing
the experiments and deriving the inferences that can
be drawn from them, I shall therefore always take these more complete
experiments as the starting experiments.

% --- p. 282 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\excerpthead{B. MAN AS THE CENTER OF THE MAGICAL \\ FORCES (From ``Overtro og Trolddom'').}\opage{282}

Men have at all times believed in the possibility of
magical operations. By these operations one aimed
to attain two essentially different things. Partly an \emph{insight}
or \emph{knowledge} that could not be acquired in the ordinary way,
by observations or communications from others, and partly an \emph{influence}
on the surrounding world that likewise could not be attained
by the means generally available at the various times.
The knowledge sought concerned almost always what was
to come, the future; one can therefore comprise all the
magical methods used for this under the name of the arts of divination.
More diverse, on the other hand, were the purposes
that one strove for through magical influences. The healing
of diseases and the prolongation of life, the acquisition of
riches and sexual pleasures, independence of spatial
distances and in general mastery over nature, a
secret and unlimited power to harm or benefit one's
fellow men --- in short, everything that the human heart could
desire but not attain in the ordinary way, all this one
hoped to acquire by magical operations.

The belief in the possibility of these magical arts is found again,
as said, at all times. Now it pervades the whole of society,
now it is pushed back to the less enlightened classes,
but usually only for a short time; then it flares up
again with renewed force. And it goes the same way with
the theories by which one grounded and explained the magical
effects. If one disregards all inessential side circumstances,
there really remain only two such theories.
In modern terms we may call the one the \emph{spiritistic},
since it assumes that all magical effects
are called forth by higher, intelligent beings; the other the
\emph{occultist}, since it assumes an unknown, all-pervading
% --- p. 283 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{283}force of nature as the true cause. These theories
have most often gone peacefully side by side. The sorcery of the Chaldeans
is thus the art of conjuration, hence spiritistic; but
their art of divination is scientifically occultist. In the original
European magic the relation seems rather to be the
reverse; the magical influences are due to the immediate power of the magical means
over things; predictions and prophecies,
on the other hand, come about, at least sometimes, through
the cooperation of fylgjur and other spirits. After the Chaldean
and the European magic had become mixed, the relation
between the two conceptions became variable, but even in the magical
philosophy of a Cornelius Agrippa, where the scientific-occultist
line of thought must naturally predominate, the belief in spirits
still plays a great part. The learned magus's mastery
over the occult forces culminates only when he
is able, by their help, to draw down the heavenly intelligences
and demons from which the forces have their origin.
So here too the two theories stand peacefully side
by side; it is probably only in our day that the relation has
developed into a strife between two essentially different
principles.

Between the theoretical views and the practical operations
there is of course a constant interaction. Just as
the theories serve to explain the significance of the magical actions,
so the magical arts will also serve to confirm
the correctness of the theories, since they --- at any rate apparently
--- lead to the desired results. Since we now see as well that
both the theoretical views and the belief in the practical
operations have maintained themselves with tenacity through
millennia, from the childhood of the human race to our day,
it is thereby given that something real must lie at the
bottom of all these representations. There must on the one hand
be phenomena that lead naturally to the belief in the existence of higher
beings or secret forces, and on
the other hand something must really be attainable by the magical
% --- p. 284 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{284}operations. For if these never led to the desired result,
the belief in them would at last have to vanish, and it would drag
the theories with it in its fall. And the theories would have to vanish
still faster if they had originally arisen as free
fantasies, neither resting on definite observations
nor confirmed through the magical operations. The very
history of superstition shows us clearly enough that the course of
development is thus. Every assumption that from the outset is
built on definite experiences vanishes at last when the experiences
no longer speak for the correctness of the assumption. A couple of
single examples will suffice to illustrate this.

The astrology of the Chaldeans sprang from the observation that there
seemed to be a relation of dependence between the periodically recurring positions
of the movable heavenly bodies and earthly
events, which also to a certain degree repeat themselves.
This belief in the influence of the stars maintained itself right up into the
19th century, but it began to waver when the astronomical
art of observation had become sufficiently developed.
The astrologers then made the sad discovery that the accurately
drawn-up horoscopes did not agree with the events actually occurring,
and that one was therefore obliged to play
tricks with the horoscopes to bring about agreement.
Doubt about the correctness of the theories thus sets in,
as one sees, at the moment when the magical operations no
longer lead to the desired result. And the experts gave up
the belief in astrology completely when the laws of the planets'
movements had been found, and it followed from them that
no connection whatever could be demonstrated between these
strictly calculable movements and the wholly incalculable
earthly occurrences. --- It goes the same way also
with alchemy. It sprang, as we have seen, from observations
of the changes of the metals. Since one was not master
of these conditions and did not know either what the changes
consisted in, the experiences led naturally to the belief that
one metal must be transformable into another. But
% --- p. 285 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{285}the belief vanished again when it had been realized that the changes
concerned only the physical properties of the metals, and that one
had not come a single step nearer to the desired result, the real transformation of metals,
despite enormous sacrifices of
strength, time and money.

The historical facts thus prove that neither
the theoretical views nor the practical operations can
continue to exist when they have no support in experience.
So something real must lie at the bottom of all the
superstitious representations and magical arts that have
maintained themselves through the ages right down to our day. This is
all the more probable since spiritists and occultists
constantly adduce a swarm of observations in support of
their views. And to this is further added that what
one nowadays believes one observes agrees to an astonishing degree
with descriptions of similar occurrences from older
times. There can thus hardly be doubt that it is the same
phenomena that recur at all times. The belief in the activity of spirits
and occult forces must originally have been
called forth by certain appearances that have kept up
this belief down through millennia, and from which it still
to this day draws its nourishment.

Our task should now be to investigate what kind of
phenomena they are that have given occasion to, and constantly
keep up, all the superstitious representations, the belief in
arts of divination and other sorcery. In our treatment of
superstition this investigation must necessarily become the main point,
because it is its results that entitle us to
brand the various views as superstitious. Only when
it has been successfully shown that the whole theory and practice of magic
rests on bad observation and false interpretation of natural,
more or less well-known phenomena, only then have we the right
to give these incorrect interpretations the name of superstition. And
should it turn out in such an investigation that there really
are points that cannot be explained by the forces known
% --- p. 286 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{286}so far, there is nothing for it but openly and honestly to
admit that the magi have been right here. For it is not altogether
unknown in the history of science that at one time something has been
rejected as superstition which later has proved after all to
have its correctness. The possibility is thus not wholly excluded
that, in going through the whole field, we may come upon
one fact or another to which science has so far not
attached its proper significance.

The first question that naturally arises is now
this: where are we to seek the forces that are active in
the magical operations? Is it in inanimate or in living
nature? Is it purely physical forces, or is it psychophysical
forces, that are in question? Here there can hardly
be any doubt; it is in animate nature, more precisely
in man, that these forces must be sought. The physics and chemistry
of our time stand powerless before the magical
effects; these precisely cannot be explained by the natural forces known
so far. And the great advances of natural science
in our day make it extremely improbable that there should still be found in nature
physical forces that could manifest themselves at
every spiritistic séance, but which the natural scientist never
comes upon in his laboratory. For this reason alone, then,
it becomes probable that the magical forces must be sought in
man, and our historical investigations point in the same
direction. At all times it has been clear
that it was not given to everyone to be able to practice sorcery. It
could not be learned in the way anyone can learn to tend a
steam engine or use a telephone; sorcery made
definite demands on its practitioners. Witches, sorcerers,
magi and mediums have always been people with special
aptitudes, and these aptitudes had moreover to be developed. The unprejudiced
and insightful investigations of recent times in this
field have indeed also led to a definite
emphasis being laid on mediumship. Only some few people, the mediums,
have proved to possess the necessary conditions for
% --- p. 287 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{287}attaining magical effects. It is thus man who
is the center of the magical forces. Consequently we must first and
foremost investigate the psychical activities in order to see to
what extent they can explain the magical effects.


But the historical investigations have taught us one thing more.
From our own time we doubtless have indisputably the best descriptions
of the results that are believed to have been attained by magical
operations. The high standpoint that scientific methods
of research now occupy has not been able to avoid
leaving its mark also on research in the field of magic. In the
last decades numerous natural scientists have indeed taken up
this study, and their descriptions of what they have
observed at spiritistic séances can of course be counted on
to be more accurate and more reliable than similar descriptions
from bygone times. Moreover, only very few
old reports of this kind exist. We know not a little about the
methods formerly used, but careful descriptions
of the results obtained are hardly to be found. There exists
a number of old horoscopes that really seem to agree with
the later fate of the persons concerned; there are reports
of a few successful transmutations of metals, and the claims of some
magi to conjurations and summonings of spirits carried out.
But all these descriptions are very scanty and
moreover given by men who were wholly entangled in the superstitious
representations of the spirit of the age; from the hand of skeptics there are
virtually no older reports of successful magical
operations. It is thus to our own time that we must
keep when it is a matter of getting on the track of what
can really be attained. But now the history of spiritism has
taught us that even the modern investigations in this field
are by no means unassailable. In many cases the testimonies of
different observers stand against one another, and even
where there is agreement about what has taken place, the reports
are by no means always so detailed or accurate that
% --- p. 288 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{288}one can without further ado rely on things having taken place
exactly as they are described.

From this it now follows directly that every investigation of the
magical forces in man must necessarily be introduced by
an investigation of the human power of observation and
a determination of the errors with which human observations
can be burdened. For it is conceivable that
many apparently magical effects arise only from the fact
that man under certain circumstances cannot observe
correctly at all. Quite natural phenomena can then, under
such conditions, come to look magical, even
to the ablest observer. And it would obviously be wholly
mistaken to seek the cause of such magical effects deeper
down in mental life when it lies right on the surface: in the very
imperfection of the power of observation. For a phenomenon that
has never occurred, but that an observer only believes he has
seen, there can of course be found no other explanation than this:
that man under the given circumstances has not been able to
observe correctly. First and foremost, then, we must investigate
what errors of observation a given report must always
be reckoned to be burdened with.

\sectionrule

\begin{intro}

\textsc{L. Feilberg} (b. 1849). Principal works: Om størst Udbytte af
Sjæleevner (1881). Holbergs Træ i Fiolstræde (1888). Om Ligeløb
og Kredsning i Sjælelivet (1896). Om sjælelig Ringhed (1899
--- 1902). Naturlærens Leve-ABC (1906). From the last-named work
some pieces are given here.
\end{intro}

\excerpthead{A. CONDENSATION OF ENERGY}


Seen fleetingly, it seems really quite puzzling that a
human being --- to whom surely no one any longer thinks of attributing
such a free will that he should thereby be able to go
% --- p. 289 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{289}outside the laws of nature --- is able to produce a condensation of energy.
By virtue of what power does he do it? One would
think that it was just as impossible as that a stone could take
to rolling uphill, or that a man could lift himself
out of a bog by his hair. Where does the force necessary for it
come from, seen purely physically?

Physically it comes always from the meeting of
oppositely directed effects of nature. Nature has no other way
of producing condensation. The meeting between the quantity of motion once
present in nature and the resistances
to it (reaction, hammer and anvil). If
a current meets a closedness that bars its way, there will
always form a condensation or a pressure head (wave),
which stops the motion and turns it back against itself.

How condensation (pressure head) can in this way directly
be an object of breeding and accumulation can be illustrated
in a particularly graphic way by a water-lifting apparatus
that goes by the name of the \emph{hydraulic ram} (the impulse siphon).
It consists of a conduit pipe in which water, coming
from a reservoir placed higher up, gathers speed by its fall
until the speed causes a flap or ball valve to slam shut
and bar the way for the water, which is then, by its own inherent
living force, forced up into a rising pipe placed in front of the valve.
To a fleeting view such an apparatus may well look
as if it were able to produce life-activity when,
with its pulse-like strokes, it lies there performing one
lift after another, as long as need be, and without anyone
looking after it or touching it, just like a perpetuum mobile.

A quite similar breeding of condensation can plainly enough
be observed in a human being.

\emph{Ex.} 1. If a boy stands in front of a school with a reluctance to go
in, one ought really to wonder that he is nevertheless
able to do it, what force of nature it really is that
gets him in there. One would think that it was just as physically
impossible that he could act against his inclination as that
% --- p. 290 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{290}a stone can lift itself up from the ground. And so it would
be if he really found it easier to go in another direction;
but that is precisely what he does not; for over there on the road
he sees in his thoughts a schoolmaster with a rod. That closes every
other outlet for the current. It gives an unpleasant condensation
(rising head), in the face of which the way into the school in the end
becomes the one he must prefer, although it is
hard. So he is ``rammed'' in. So he goes in ``of his own free will''
(cf. the soldier who went along ``compulsorily voluntarily''). Freedom and self-determination
simply are not otherwise in nature.

Thus existence runs its course day after day also in the field of
higher mental life.

\emph{Ex.} 2. Suppose that a beautiful impression has set a powerful
current in motion, and that one then meets a cold and unpleasant
gust of wind: then one has the ramming at once. The lively
current is broken off, while at the same time the living force, which
cannot be without effect, gets no other outlet than to produce
condensation. It is the well-known phenomenon of being pressed up
through the unpleasant, which every human being knows so well. It
lies at the bottom of all condensation.

Let us say that on a beautiful autumn evening you stand on the shore
and see the glow from the town mirrored in the water along
the beach, while electric searchlights from the fort cast
cones of light onto the trees on the coast. It sets the mind in
strong motion. It can call forth a strong unsatisfied
craving in the mind, a strong surging. An author who described
an Italian night at sea used the expression that he could
gladly have thrown himself down on the bottom of his boat in tears.
If one now seeks an outlet for this quantity of motion by turning
thought in ordinary everyday directions, one gets
a feeling as if one were running against a wall. It is closedness
and closedness all of it; there is no outlet. But if the inner
system of channels is perhaps such that the thoughts are led in other
directions, be it in the direction of your eternal origin, then
it can happen that despondency is all at once relieved by an inner jubilation.
% --- p. 291 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{291}Then a new way has been rammed up. It is evidently the hydraulic-ram effect,
a strong current that is pressed by closedness up into
outlets that are not reached in daily life.

For such ramming-up, however, by no means such special
conditions are needed. It takes place scores of times every day, when
something has set the mind in motion while at the same time outlets
close themselves to the awakened current. Now it may be a couple of drooping
willow leaves that awaken an unsatisfied craving for life,
now a cozy sunny nook that one has no time to enjoy, an
unnoticed wild flower on the edge of a ditch or a forgotten begonia
in a weed-overgrown bed in the garden. Now it may
be a stormy day of sleet on the ramparts, when all people
keep indoors, now beauty in October together with
the thought that soon it will all be over. Always it is
something that sets the mind in motion together with an unsatisfying
prospect of an outlet. It is just as in the hydraulic ram,
where the current first gathers speed in order then, by
sudden barring, to be forced upward.

In view of the significance that such small rammings-up
have for producing the condensation of daily life, it is
important to accustom oneself to having an eye for the small things of daily
life in such a way that one becomes able to take condensation
from them. Unnoticed small things that can set the mind in
motion without at the same time offering any further outlet
that can take the tension from one again. To be able to fetch motion
from the flies' playing in the sun, from the way in which the
tall blades of grass by the pond nod to one another, etc., etc.
It is this sort of small things that can often teach one jubilation
over life, precisely because they offer so slight an outlet in proportion
to the motion they awaken. They ram life up in one. That
a splendid piece of music as a rule cannot do. It gives
a many times stronger motion, but at the same time opens
such powerful outlets for the current that any rise
is made impossible. People think that one can enjoy oneself upward; but
it is physically impossible. Whoever steps aside from daily
% --- p. 292 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{292}ramming pressure passes by the life of condensation at once.
In the mood of a summer resident in the country condensation is almost impossible. One is
too flatly spread out in the outflow and reaches no higher than the lowest
pressure heads. Things must be closed off at the sides before the current
can be forced upward. On the other hand one feels a powerful ramming
(condensation) in the mind on the day the last leaf has fallen
from the trees in November, and there is nothing whatever left to
look at.

Notice how the whole of nature is arranged for
this sort of ramming-up. In among the lovely green stalks of grain,
between which the sunlight plays, creep hideous predatory beetles
and all kinds of devouring monsters, and beside them stands the farmer
wiping his sweaty brow with a worried mind. These are the apparatuses
by which the condensation of force is brought about, inseparably
bound up with existence as they are. He would have to get up early
who could slip round them. But he is not supposed to at all.
It is this ramming that is to create life. Without these
predatory beetles there would never be anything but coarseness in the soul.

Adversity condenses a human being more than prosperity.

Rainy weather more than sunshine.

Jutland's harsh west coast more than Lolland's fertile
fields.

Fallen leaves in October more than the leafing of spring.

Sometimes one and the same impression can at once call forth
motion and close it off. Then the awakened motion is born
with a strong condensation (high ramming, fine impressions).


Think of a stormy day of sleet on the ramparts, when
the wind blows straight across the path into the great treetops, on
whose branches it turns the undersides upward with a thunderous
din, so that one can hardly hear one's own footsteps,
while falling branches make it dangerous to walk up there,
and the rain drives people indoors. Then there is a ramming in the solitary
wanderer up to fine spiritual moods.

Or think of an old ruin, the strong emotion
% --- p. 293 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{293}it can awaken, and the annihilating way in which
it closes off old times. Few things can ram like
an old ruin, a grave or the like. ---

But regarded as a hydraulic ram, man is surely
more complicatedly arranged than the apparatus described above.
The possibility stands open here that the valves can be moved
in a quite different, composite way under the influence
of a more or less intricate system of previously acquired
tracks, ways and connections, different for different
people, whereupon the whole effect must necessarily
acquire a completely arbitrary appearance, as if one
acted on one's own sovereign authority.

If the lift so produced is to become useful, however,
it must in a self-evident way be combined with obstacles
to the motion's going back of itself.

This can happen by the rising pipe emptying its contents at a certain height
into a pond from which the water cannot run
down again, or in a similar way. Once the water has got out into
such a pond, it stays there; then the lift does not become greater
that time; but then one can later have a new ram that
brings the water from the said pond up to another, still
higher-lying pond, and so forth. In this way there come to be definite
resting places for the motion, at which the effort of lifting
ceases while at the same time going back is prevented, definite
\emph{stories}, arranged for a longer staying without
effort.

So the relation must, rationally, necessarily be; but
so too one can clearly observe that it is.

\emph{Ex.} 1. It is an experience that constantly repeats itself that a
discomfort of condensation, after reaching a certain magnitude (height of lift),
can suddenly pass (the overcoming has been accomplished),
while at the same time it turns out that something has happened in the organism.

After a walk that had been strenuous and had produced
a peculiar restlessness and heat, just like a kind of thirst,
I stood one day outside a tavern and thought of a Tuborg
% --- p. 294 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{294}Pilsner, when it struck me at that very moment that this heat
was probably not thirst at all, but a surging in the nervous system
that would be destroyed precisely by the indulgence: ``You shall not,
Jeppe.'' For a while I stood looking at the little tables covered with red
cloths; but nothing came of it. Suddenly
the heat passed, the restlessness was quite gone, and at that very moment I observed
mental flashes of great beauty. A change of constitution had taken place
through the condensation. Had I given in
to the temptation, I know from earlier experience that it would not have
come. Now a way had been rammed up to new stories in the soul.

Occurrences of this kind will be well known.

\emph{Ex.} 2. When after a holiday stay in the country one again
reports for school lessons and the like, one has a rich opportunity to make
observations concerning such breakthroughs of overcoming.
It may in the first days be as if one went about in a mood
of self-surrender, so unpleasant is it. As if one did not
look at houses, streets and objects at all --- that would only be
a suffering. --- As if one left hope outside. But when a couple of days
have passed, it seems to one as if the whole thing at bottom
was not so bad. It is in fact rather nice with this stronger
mood. There is a certain real form of existence in it
that brings one to look on the summer-resident mood as something
poor, to which only wretched individuals could give themselves up in the long run.
One begins to understand the persons who complain of
the lack of work and occupation. There is something flat and
pitiable about this summer-resident mood. It would not
be bearable to have a holiday always.

It is plain how one has been rammed up to a
new constitution of the soul.

Every high goal that is to be reached has its condensation side
of suffering (suffering of condensation); but it is only poor people
who notice it (compare lying in bed till ten o'clock
to avoid the discomfort of getting up). One has in general
no right to call condensation a suffering; it is on the contrary
a glory. What must a stalk not go through
% --- p. 295 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{295}of condensation and death to produce a little grain of rice. It
is all glory. The whole of life is one single great process of condensation.
But it would surely be a misguided creature
that called life a suffering. It is a glory for which
one thanks the gods. In addition, this sort of suffering
is very much as one takes it. In dancing people often work like
horses without talking of hardship. It is a pleasure.
Suffering comes properly forward as such only in
the one who constantly lets himself be pushed forward by nature and does not
voluntarily go at condensation as his purpose in life. If
one has to be beaten into everything, then life becomes a suffering. Then one comes
backward to everything, gets caught in the valves of the hydraulic ram
and goes to pieces like poor tools.

\sectionrule

\excerpthead{B. LOVE OF NATURE}


A man in a working mood is crossing one of the bridges in Frederiksberg
Garden. This morning he got up by an act of overcoming. Now
he has gradually got into the daily trot that usually
fills life, a certain restless mood in which one rushes along
without rest in the soul, always onward, regarding it as
a kind of fraud against life to lay aside one's speed for
a single moment. Life simply is hard, and so it is meant to be. So
existence goes for most people.

It is foggy autumn weather. Everything is dripping wet, and
as he looks down into the canal, a drop of water falls from a
chestnut branch and forms a large bubble that slowly sends
rings out on all sides.

``I have no time to look at that'' is the first movement of thought
that stirs in him. But he would not have
thought that if there had not been something in the sight that had
tried to captivate him. So it often happens that small things
seek to speak to one and catch one. Then one should always
stop and stand still. There is not so much in life that can speak
% --- p. 296 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{296}finely that one can afford to pass it by. Stand still! Do
stand still! Use the few moments you live to see
the wonderful in small things. Our man kept on walking;
but then an involuntary desire came over him to see how
far such a ring could now get. God knows whether it
was not also an old memory that struck him. Often
memories can lie hidden in such small impressions that
do not manage to become conscious. One does not quite know what. He
stood still with one leg forward, ready to walk on.
Then another drop fell. It set as it were a dot in the mind,
a little puncture, a needle's eye into something that was of value.
But it was as if this needle's eye would slip away the next moment.
One cannot hold on to this sort of thing, cannot force
it up. It was as if it constantly slipped away from him. In his
representation of the bubble it seemed to him that he constantly slid off
a smooth curve and fell down now to one side, now
to the other, whereas what ought to happen was that with
a firm and sure hand one held steady right against a definite point.
This constant sliding off seems irritating to him.
At last, with a mighty effort of force, he
puts a finger on a quite definite spot: ``Now it shall
stay there, and now it does not stir.'' And then it can happen that it
really succeeds --- when he stands quite still, so still, so
still, does not draw breath, does not move a muscle, does not blink
an eye --- that then as it were a little root can sprout that
binds, and that he then no longer slides on the smooth surface. Then
the needle's eye becomes larger and larger, until at last a new track
opens completely with a sigh of relief. Then a way has been won
up to something new. That is what a mental overcoming looks like.

The main thing in such a story is the application of force
to the busy mood as foundation. It is
peculiar; for it is really force twice over. The busy mood was itself a product of force, arisen through
overcoming. Now this product of condensation itself becomes the object
of a new condensation. It is condensation to the 2nd power.
% --- p. 297 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{297}It must necessarily give a still stronger condensation. First
there was a certain measure of condensation (the working mood). Then
a new condensation is added and increases itself upon the preceding one,
as it were on top of it. It must necessarily give something more.
For they are both there. The one works upon the product that the
other has produced. It must give an increased effect.

But at the same time as this increased condensation one notices
that something has happened in the soul, a change of the mental
constitution. A new product has come forth through the breakthrough, a peculiar \emph{warmth of heart} (heartiness, life of the heart,
love of nature), a quite peculiar product, of which there was
nothing at all in what preceded, and which cannot help
reminding one of how strong condensations of energy in
nature can often bring objects to a glow. There can be
something dear in standing and looking at those rings. Life in a wholly different
way, which one can well remember, but which one had wholly
lost sight of. Strange, how it can speak to one.

``I have never experienced anything of the sort,'' the reader says
perhaps. Very well! Then let us try another example.

Let us take the overcoming on a \emph{walking tour}. It has its
definite stages. Already at the end of the first mile
one notices a certain unbearableness, a strong bodily
surging, which often tempts one to stop, go in and drink
coffee somewhere or the like. It is a purely bodily overcoming. If
it is allowed to last its time out without interruption, it passes
of itself (the overcoming has been accomplished), after which one can walk
a long time without the least surging in an extraordinarily pleasant,
trained mood. But then comes the overcoming really characteristic
of the walking tour, a more mental form of
exertion of force, which always ends with warmth (life of the heart) of
great beauty. Everyone will know how things at the end
of such a walking tour can acquire something blessedly dear
about them. It is precisely to attain this heart-warm, splendid
mood that one takes the walk, if one is otherwise a practitioner
in this field. Even the villas, empty of people in winter,
% --- p. 298 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{298}can look so lovable that one can hardly
help patting them. Often a bend of a forest road
or a deep wheel rut can look so blessedly homely and dear that one
almost has to laugh. It is love of nature in its purest and prettiest
form. There can be something about such a road that
leads one away from the world as to an old home. One suddenly
recognizes it. The wheel ruts laugh to one, the puddles
twinkle, a wondrous glory calls one away from the accustomed.
You who would live! Hasten to lose yourself in this love of nature.
Hide yourself in it before human life finds you
again, and stay forever in the old home that was and is. Men
produce glory with motley rags and metallic luster.
Nature teaches one a really living glory, the glory
in the soul that mankind forgot. That is how human life
was meant to look. Wake up, man, from the wretched life
you lead! Can you not hear that there is sometimes something that
positively cries to heaven.

\sectionrule

Do you know it, when you suddenly notice
that it hums so mildly and kindly in the stove, as if
old generations were talking? You have no doubt heard it rumbling all
the while; but suddenly you notice that it sounds
so cozy, so mild and kind, as if there were voices
in the sound, coming from a long way off, far, far back in time.
Br.. rr.. it suddenly flares up with a flame right in
the draught hole. It can be as if you stood nailed fast like
a pillar, so still. What could it be? As if you could not
move so much as an eye. As a rule things seethe and boil
in the soul; it is so strange when it suddenly becomes
quite still. All the remarkable things one seems to hear.
It is really only this that matters for a human being who
would think: to be quite still in the soul, so still that the phenomena
themselves begin to speak, like this rumbling of the stove
% --- p. 299 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{299}here now. Then the phenomena themselves tell the whole thing. As a rule
one flickers about like a candle in the wind, falls upon the phenomena
with trembling hands and talks oneself, instead of
letting the phenomena talk. Kills them at last with one's chatter and
one's ego, so that they stand dead round about and say nothing. One
should not be at all. It should be quite still in the soul as
out in the space of heaven. Then life comes into the phenomena. Then
they begin to speak. It is as if this rumbling stole you
along out into space, into the infinite stillness, and as if there all at
once an infinite life arose. Hush! How remarkable it is!

But if you have ever experienced such a phenomenon,
you will know what power of spirit is needed to keep
the soul quite still in this way. The poorer an individual is in spiritual
respects, the more restless he is, the more noise he makes.
It took force to keep an arm quite still; but it
takes still greater force to keep a soul quite still.

One will see that such an experience is the story of the bubble
in Frederiksberg Garden over again, almost word for word, only
with the difference that the object has changed. Then it was
the busy mood that was the object of condensation; here
it is a phenomenon of warmth, that is, precisely the very thing that we then
worked our way up to as the final result. The rumbling in
the stove contained a rich mood of mental warmth and
coziness. Such a foundation of warmth must in general
always be had wherever richness is felt in the soul.
Warmth is needed as the foundation everywhere one is to go beyond
the simpler moods. It is a poor intellectual life that
is not built on warmth. The researcher who does not love his
substances, his oxygen, his hydrogen and his apparatus, never becomes anything but
a craftsman in his soul. The soul withers for him.
% --- p. 300 --- (no text in the layer)
\opage{300}


% --- p. 301 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\anthsection{} {NOTES}\opage{301}

\textbf{P. 5,} \textit{Metaphysics} is the name, in the widest sense, for an attempt of thought that goes
beyond experience in order to find the innermost ground of existence. The metaphysics
Holberg has in mind was rather a remnant of the scholasticism of the Middle Ages,
which believed it could give proofs of theological representations and
depictions of their objects, which could only be done through subtleties
and fallacies. --- \textit{a priori} = independently of experience. ---
\textit{Tertullian,} a Church Father who lived about A.D. 200. --- \textit{Simonides,}
Greek poet, about 500 B.C. --- \textit{Orbis pictus} --- i.e. ``The World in
Pictures,'' a reader by the famous educator Comenius; it appeared
in 1658. --- \textbf{P. 6,} \textit{Physica coelestis,} the physics of the heavenly bodies. \textit{Cartesius}
(Descartes 1596---1650) held that they were carried round by vortices of fine
matter, each of them being enclosed by its own vortex. \textit{Newton} (1642---1727) % print: „omsluttedes at sin Hvirvel“ -- „at“ for af (PRINTED AS IS)
taught that the great masses ``attract'' the small according to the same law that
holds for falling bodies at the earth's surface. There is not, as Holberg thinks,
any contradiction in the sun's sending out rays of light and ``attracting'' masses
(``blowing and sucking''!). --- \textbf{P. 7,} \textit{Principia gravitatis,} the laws holding for gravitation
(gravity, attraction). --- \textbf{P. 10,} \textit{Deism} assumes a God,
but assumes as well that everything in nature takes place according to laws of nature, so that God
works no miracles and exercises no special providence. --- \textit{Warburton,} English
clergyman (1698---1749), wrote against the deists, whose best-known spokesmen
were (see P. 11) \textit{Collins,} \textit{Tyndall,} \textit{Morgan} and \textit{Chubb.} --- The philosophers \textit{Hobbes} % print: „Tyndall“ for the deist Tindal (PRINTED AS IS)
(1588—1679) and \textit{Spinoza} (1632—1677) had earlier, supported by thorough
philosophical investigations, placed themselves in opposition to the doctrine of the Church. --- \textit{The Sibyl's
prophecies} (or oracles), a collection of words of omen from Greek and
Roman antiquity, which were later reworked and expanded by Jewish and
Christian authors and were often used as testimony from the heathen side to % print: „Vidnesbyrd fra hedensk Side mod Kristendommens Sandhed“ -- „mod“ (against), as printed; translated by sense
the truth of Christianity. --- \textit{Josephus,} Jewish historian who lived
in the time of Nero and Titus. --- \textbf{P. 12,} \textit{The Manichaeans} (adherents of a church
that arose in the 3rd century A.D.) taught that evil had its origin in
an evil being, while good came from a good God. Persian and
Christian ideas are mixed together in Manichaeism. \textit{Pierre Bayle} (1647---
1706) maintained that the Manichaean doctrine contained the only rational
solution of the problem of evil. --- \textit{B. S.} is said to be Bernhard Schaebel,
a friend of Holberg's who had made his acquaintance abroad. He
died as rector in Roskilde. --- \textbf{P. 13,} \textit{Democritus,} Greek philosopher, whose
lifetime falls between the middle of the fifth and the middle of the fourth century
B.C., taught that everything consisted of material elements (atoms). --- \textbf{P. 14,}
The Greek words in the second line mean ``reason and God.'' By reason
is meant the world-reason. --- \textit{Seneca,} Stoic philosopher (4—65 A.D.). ---
% --- p. 302 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{302}The Greek sentence, which (with an error in a single word) is quoted from Plato,
means: ``God wills that everything shall be good, and that, so far as possible, there shall be nothing
evil.'' (The dialogue Timaeus p. 30 A.) --- \textbf{P. 15,} \textit{hysteron proteron} ---
i.e. in the wrong order, so that what should come last comes first.
\textbf{P. 16,} \textit{The oracles of Zoroaster} --- Chaldean prophecies which at a later time
went under Zoroaster's name. They were translated into Greek and later (by Henrik
Rantzau 1598) into Latin. \textit{Veritas localis,} i.e. a truth that holds only
in a particular region (``a local truth''). --- \textbf{P. 18.} \textit{Clovis,} the
Frankish king Chlodevig (465—511). \textit{The Peruvian king:} Atapaliba,
see Heltehistorier by Liebenberg I, 403 f. --- \textbf{P. 19,} \textit{Ars critica,} the art
of distinguishing and judging. \textit{Catecheta,} a man who teaches the religious
instruction of children. --- \textbf{P. 20,} \textit{Transubstantiation,} the transformation that the bread and
the wine, according to Catholic doctrine, undergo at consecration, so that they become
flesh and blood, although they keep their sensible properties. --- \textbf{P. 21,}
\textit{Nullius in verba} (jurare) i.e. ``not to swear by anyone's word.'' --- \textit{Philosophia
carbonaria} --- collier's philosophy (on the analogy of collier's faith). --- \textit{Potamon}
of Alexandria founded at the end of the 2nd century A.D. an eclectic
philosophy, i.e. a doctrine that put together supposedly usable parts of
the doctrines of various earlier philosophers. --- \textit{Philosophiæ naturalis professores,}
i.e. professors of natural science. --- \textbf{P. 22,} The peasant boy in
the comedy: Jacob, Erasmus Montanus' brother. --- \textbf{P. 24,} \textit{Character indelebilis,}
i.e. an acquired property that cannot be lost again. At
ordination a Catholic cleric receives a stamp of this kind. --- \textbf{P. 25,}
\textit{In small characters,} i.e. in small letters. --- \textit{Tillotson,} (1630---
1693), English prelate of the comparatively liberal school. --- \textbf{P. 26,} \textit{Complexion,}
i.e. temperament. --- \textbf{P. 29,} \textit{Mathematical method} proceeds through
inferences from strict definitions and axioms. The philosophers sought
to imitate this method, especially the Wolffian school, which in Denmark won
no small following among jurists and theologians, and whose most important representative
was Jens Kraft (1720---1765), with whom Sneedorff too came
into a sharp controversy. --- \textbf{P. 30,} \textit{A philosophical Spectator,} i.e. Andr. Lundhoff's
Den danske Spectators filosofiske Spectator (1744). --- \textbf{P. 36,} \textit{The} % print: „P. 36“ -- the passage (smukke Videnskaber; Endelig eller nødvendig Umulighed) is on p. 31
\textit{belles lettres} are poetry, literature and aesthetics, which build on the involuntary
and immediate mental experiences. --- \textit{Definitive or necessary
impossibility,} i.e. unconditional impossibility. \textit{Arbitrary impossibility,} i.e.
conditional impossibility. --- \textbf{P. 33,} \textit{Faculty} (Formue), i.e. ability (Evne). --- \textbf{P. 36,} \textit{Swift}
(Jonathan), English satirist (1667—1745). --- \textbf{P. 37,} \textit{Scotus} (Duns Scotus),
English scholastic (1265?---1308?). --- \textit{Occam} (William), English scholastic
(1270---1347). --- \textit{Suárez} (Franciscus), Spanish scholastic (1548---
1617). --- \textbf{P. 38,} \textit{Thomasius} (Christian), German jurist and philosopher (1655—
1728). --- \textbf{P. 40,} \textit{Hesiod,} Greek poet (c. 700 B.C.), in his poem ``Works
and Days'' (v. 202). --- \textit{Shakespeare:} King Lear Act. IV, Sc. 1, (the word
``so'' is not found in Shakespeare). --- \textbf{P. 46,} \textit{pium desiderium} i.e. pious
wish. \textit{Theodicy,} i.e. a defense of God with regard to the fact of evil
(title of a work by Leibniz). --- \textbf{P. 49,} \textit{Donati,} Italian
naturalist of the middle of the 18th century. --- \textit{Bonnet} (Charles), Swiss
naturalist and philosopher (1720—1793). --- \textit{Manis,} pangolin. --- \textit{Dasypus,}
armadillo. --- \textit{Lacerte,} lizard. --- \textbf{P. 50,} \textit{Kielmeyer,} German naturalist at
the end of the 18th century. --- \textbf{P. 52,} \textit{Sacred matters} (hellige Sager), i.e. legends (Sagn). --- \textbf{P. 53,}
The \textit{Greek} mentioned here is Plato (see the dialogue Timaeus p. 40 ff.) --- \textbf{P. 68,}
\textit{The author of the Bemærkninger:} Bishop Mynster, see p. 60. --- \textit{Ad intra,} i.e.
inwardly, belonging to the inner part of the being. The expression is used in theology of
% --- p. 303 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{303}God's inner being in contrast to the world created by him. --- \textbf{P. 83,}
\textit{Eudaemonists} is the name for those who ground morality on the craving for happiness. ---
\textbf{P. 88,} \textit{well forth} (fremqvælde), a Germanizing expression for fremvælde. --- \textbf{P. 89,} \textit{which separate
them from one another.} Here and in two places on the following pages ``dem'' (them) is used
reflexively. --- \textbf{P. 92,} \textit{Casuistry} is the investigation of how one is to conduct oneself
when different duties conflict with one another. Kant declared in the introduction
to ``Tugendlehre'' that casuistry was neither a science nor part
of a science, but nevertheless later in the book set up casuistical questions.
--- \textbf{P. 93,} \textit{Probabilism,} the doctrine that in cases of doubt one is permitted
to keep to the most probable opinion, or perhaps even to any
opinion whatever, provided it is probable at all, even if it
is not the most probable. --- \textbf{P. 100,} \textit{these --- these.} The second ``these''
means ``those.'' --- \textbf{P. 101,} \textit{Singular appearances,} i.e. phenomena. --- \textbf{P. 105,} \textit{Monboddo:}
James Burnett, Lord Monboddo (1714—1799). --- \textit{Hallen} --- probably
a misprint for Haller: Albrecht v. Haller (1708—1777). --- \textit{Lamarck} i.e.
Jean Baptiste de Lamarck (1744---1829). His famous work ``Philosophie
zoologique,'' in which he teaches the origin of species by development, is
later than Troschow's treatise. --- \textit{Fabricius} --- probably Joh. Chr. % print: „Troschows“ for Treschows (PRINTED AS IS)
Fabricius (1748—1808). --- \textbf{P. 113,} \textit{Pallas} --- Peter Simon Pallas (1741---
1811). --- \textbf{P. 119,} Allusion to Goethe's beautiful poem ``Auf Miedings
Tod.'' Mieding was ``master of the theater'' in Weimar. --- \textbf{P. 120,} By \textit{actual
moods} Sibbern understands certain constant feelings that are called forth
by the conditions of life under which the single individual lives. By \textit{passion} he understands
a standing desire and yearning toward certain purposes. --- \textbf{P. 121,} \textit{vexes} (ærgre),
i.e. offends (forarge). --- \textbf{P. 126,} in animality = ``in the midst of animality'' (p. 127.) ---
\textbf{P. 128,} \textit{in our time} --- the reference here is to the French embryologist Serres. ---
\textbf{P. 133,} \textit{Petitio principii} is, like \textit{circulus in probando} (p. 139), a
circular inference. --- \textbf{P. 134,} \textit{Opus operatum} is a work whose value is not
to depend on the disposition; it matters only that it is done. Applied
to faith (fides) it means that it matters only to have one's doctrine of faith
in order, whether the disposition goes with it or not. --- Catholicism
requires of the layman only belief that the Church possesses the truth, not
self-won conviction about this truth itself. Such a belief is called
fides implicita. --- \textit{bleve} is subjunctive (Germanizing). --- \textbf{P. 145,} \textit{distant} (fraliggende)
translates ``distante.'' --- \textbf{P. 163,} \textit{Typhon} --- a fire-breathing monster that
Zeus subdued. --- \textbf{P. 164,} \textit{The Socratic theory of recollection} --- Plato
has Socrates teach that the highest thoughts can be awakened out of man's
interior, as if it were a recollection. Such a self-acquisition of the truth
Kierkegaard now sets in opposition to the supernatural communication of truth
that is given by ``the God.'' --- \textit{Sextus Empiricus} (2nd century A.D.) draws skeptical
consequences from Socrates' statement that he does not know himself, as well
as from the philosophers' various answers to the question what a
human being is. --- \textit{Protagoras} (5th century B.C.) taught that man (i.e.
human nature) is the measure of all knowledge --- not that
``everything is man's measure.'' Protagoras' utterance is interpreted one-sidedly by Kierkegaard.
--- \textbf{P. 167,} \textit{Essentia involvit existentiam,} i.e. his essence necessarily
entails his existence (Spinoza: Ethica I, def. 1). According to Kierkegaard,
this proposition can hold of no existing being; its
application thus presupposes that one has to do only with a concept, not with
something existing. --- \textbf{P. 168,} \textit{Hauptpastor Götze} in Hamburg had
directed a fanatical attack on Lessing because the latter had published the so-called
Wolfenbüttel Fragments (whose anonymous author was Reimarus), in
% --- p. 304 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{304}which the biblical writings were subjected to a sharp historical and ethical
criticism --- \textit{The system} --- Kierkegaard's polemic is directed against Martensen and
Rasmus Nielsen, who had announced a new system, a higher unity of
Hegel's philosophy and positive Christianity. Cf. Kierkegaard's article in ``Fædrelandet''
12 June 1842 (Samlede Værker XIII, p. 397 ff.). --- \textbf{P. 170,} \textit{Agent
Behrend} --- a Copenhagen eccentric well known in his day. --- \textit{Johannes
Climacus} --- the pseudonym whom Kierkegaard lets speak in ``Filosofiske Smuler''
and ``Uvidenskabelig Efterskrift,'' and who stands at a searching and critical
stage, outside Christianity, but acknowledging it as the highest
view of life. --- \textit{coming to be --- being:} Kierkegaard distinguishes sharply between
existing (Tilværen, existence), which presupposes the relation of time, and abstract being,
which can also be predicated of what is merely thought. What he in what follows
reproaches Hegel with is that the latter in his Logic speaks of the ``movement'' of thought,
although movement in the proper sense presupposes time. --- \textbf{Pag. 171,} \textit{Trendelenburg}
--- Adolph Trendelenburg, German philosopher (1802—1872), known especially
for his ``Logische Untersuchungen'' (1840) and for his studies on
Aristotle. He maintains an analogy between analyzing and constructing
thought and real movement in nature. --- \textit{Plutarch} finds in
``Isis and Osiris'' an etymological similarity between the Greek words for thinking
and movement. --- \textbf{P. 172,} The question that is raised here ---
whether our fundamental concepts (categories) are ``abbreviated'' experiences or purely
a priori --- Kierkegaard did not come to treat, since his later
production became purely religious. --- \textbf{P. 173,} \textit{Reflection} is here the line of thought
by which we get back to the concept that can serve as the beginning
of the system. Kierkegaard asks why we stop at a
