particular concept instead of constantly continuing our searching thought (the analysis).
One begins, according to him, with a leap, i.e. arbitrarily, not
necessarily. --- \textbf{P. 174,} \textit{The bad infinity} --- is in Hegel the endlessness
of a series that is constantly continued according to the same law, in contrast
to ``the true infinity,'' in which an infinite content is gathered together
into a totality. A more recent mathematician has called this latter infinite
the transfinite. --- \textbf{P. 175,} The Greek words mean ``transition to a wholly
different matter, a wholly different field.'' --- \textbf{P. 176,} \textit{Trop} --- an old
student in Heiberg's ``Recensenten og Dyret.'' --- \textbf{P. 177,} \textit{Objectively
reflection is not to be stopped} --- because we can never know whether the analysis
(reflection) may not discover new relations and presuppositions that must
be taken into account before ``the system begins.'' Subjectively it can stop, % print: a high opening quote “Systemet (PRINTED AS IS)
either from fatigue or by a resolution. --- \textit{Rötscher} --- one of Hegel's
disciples, author of ``Aristophanes und seine Zeitalter'' (1827) and ``Cyclus
dramatischer Charactere'' (1844). --- \textbf{P. 178,} By \textit{the scientific conflicts of a moment
now past} is probably meant the controversy between Schelling,
who wanted to begin with ``intellectual intuition,'' and Hegel, who demanded a
grounded ascent to the standpoint of speculation. See the preface to Hegel's
``Phænomenologie des Geistes'' and his ``System der Logik'' (Erstes Buch,
whose introduction is headed: ``Wie muss der Anfang der Wissenschaft
gemacht werden?''). --- \textit{A big book} --- perhaps the reference is to Sibbern's
book ``Hegels Philosophie betragtet i Forhold til vor Tid'' (1838) (offprint
from Maanedskrift for Litteratur). In this book Sibbern treats on pp. 33—59
the question of the beginning of the system. --- \textbf{P. 180,} \textit{The Crumbs} --- in ``Filosofiske
Smuler'' (Saml. Værker IV p. 235) the question is raised whether
the possible, by having become real, has thereby become more necessary
than it was. --- \textit{Quodlibet} --- anything whatever, i.e. an accidental example, % print: „Qvodlilet“ for Qvodlibet (PRINTED AS IS)
% --- p. 305 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{305}whose individuality is indifferent. --- \textit{Another author} --- the pseudonym
Frater Taciturnus in ``Stadier paa Livets Vej'' (Saml. Værker VI p. 218). ---
\textbf{P. 183,} \textit{The Platonic conception} --- In Plato's ``Symposium'' (p. 200) it is argued
that possession does not exclude craving and striving, since one can wish
and strive always to possess what one has. --- \textit{The Greek: always wanting
to be a learner} --- a line of an otherwise lost poem of Solon runs:
``I grow old, while yet I constantly learn many things.'' --- \textbf{P. 186,}
\textit{Socrates} --- In Plato's ``Apology'' (p. 40 f) Socrates believes himself well prepared
for another life, if there is another, since there he will continue
the work he practiced here in life: to question and discuss! In ``Phaedo''
(p. 114 D) there is a similar line of thought: if there is an immortality, then
he is well prepared for it who has led a noble life in striving for truth.
--- \textbf{P. 187,} \textit{canceled by the principle of contradiction.} --- According to Hegel
the proposition that the self-contradictory is invalid did not hold, for self-contradiction
was for him only a sign of oppositions that were to be resolved
as members of a ``higher unity.'' He confused contradiction and opposition.
--- \textbf{P. 189,} The sentence quoted is the closing statement of ``Filosofiske
Smuler.'' --- \textbf{P. 193,} \textit{Caesar} did not have the Alexandrian library
burned; the fire was due to an accident. --- \textbf{P. 194,} \textit{ancipiti proelio} i.e. with
a double front. --- \textit{Immanent transition} i.e. a transition through natural
development that presupposes no break with psychological laws. ---
\textbf{P. 197,} \textit{The taking back of recollection} etc. --- By recollection is meant here
the Platonic recollection (see the note to P. 164), a thinking proceeding from man's own
interior, which leads beyond every relation of time (``out of existence
into the eternal''). Such a taking back is what is characteristic of
what Kierkegaard calls irony (see Saml. Værker VII P. 231). --- \textbf{P. 198,}
\textit{``Stadier paa Livets Vej''} appeared later than ``Filosofiske Smuler.'' ---
\textit{Humor as borderland.} --- By humor Kierkegaard understands a standpoint
that feels the tragic oppositions that life presents, but lets
them fade away into wistfulness. It is the border stage (confinium) before the
religious standpoint comes into force. It lies between the ethical and the
religious, just as irony lies between the aesthetic and the ethical.
For the religious stage, according to Kierkegaard, wistfulness is not characteristic;
for that the tension between the eternal and life in time is too strong. ---
\textbf{P. 202,} \textit{The soul as the life-Idea's self-reflection} etc. --- By ``Idea'' Nielsen
understands totality (like Hegel). That life is Idea means that it is a totality,
despite the many organs and cells. Life is soul when stress
is laid on the opposition between the whole of life and the outer organism
(``the immediate other''). --- \textbf{P. 203,} By \textit{the modern monism} is probably
meant a conception like Haeckel's, which proclaims a doctrine of unity,
but now lets the psychical produce bodily effects,
now the bodily produce psychical effects. --- \textbf{P. 204.}
\textit{Potential function} --- The potential means the work done by the interaction
of two masses when they are brought from an infinite distance to a certain
given distance. It is directly proportional to the masses, inversely to
the distance. --- \textbf{P. 207,} \textit{The ontological subjectivity:} since every object
% print: Snbjektivitet (misprint)
presupposes a subject, Nielsen infers that there would be no absolute objectivity,
no reality, if there were not an absolute subject whose
thoughts are expressed as real concepts in things (the facts). It
is by their power that things exist. --- \textbf{P.209,} \textit{Newton's rule: principia
etc.} i.e. to assume no more principles than necessary. --- \textbf{P.218,} \textit{The transcendence of representation}
i.e. the external relation that the positive religions
% --- p. 306 ---
\setcounter{footnote}{0}%
\opage{306}maintain between God and the world, the ideal and reality. \textit{The relation of immanence}
i.e. the inner relation that philosophy maintains between God and the world.
\textit{Transcendence becomes a moment} in the relation of immanence, that is to say that the
inner relation does not exclude a difference, is not a confusion.
--- \textbf{P. 221,} --- \textit{in another writing.} By this Brøchner refers to his writing
``Et Svar til Professor Rasmus Nielsen'' (1868), where it says among other things
(p. 30): ``Because I do not seek the truth in what has now for centuries
borne the life of so many generations of men, but in what \textit{eternally} bears
the life of the human spirit and binds it together in unity with the divine
origin, should I therefore not know how to respect the forms
through which the human spirit strove toward this unity, and
in particular the purest and most spiritual of these forms?'' --- \textbf{P. 236,} By % print: „P. 236“ -- „Værdiernes Bestaaen“ stands on p. 239 (Bestaaen also p. 237)
the proposition of \textit{the persistence of value} the author, who sees in this proposition
an abstract expression of the innermost motive in all religion, means the assumption
that through all changes and upheavals in nature and
history there persists a constant possibility for the development of, and work on,
human values of life, even if the development may lead to the values
that persist not being those we now know and acknowledge, but higher and
more comprehensive ones. --- \textbf{P. 256,} \textit{Duplicism} --- By this the author means
the assumption ``according to which everything that is has a double form of appearance,
inwardly as spirit, outwardly as matter.'' --- \textbf{P. 276,}
\textit{Tonus} i.e. state of contraction (in muscles, among others the muscles in
the walls of the blood vessels). --- \textbf{P. 277,} \textit{Sphygmomanometer} is an instrument for
measuring the pressure exerted by the blood in a blood vessel. --- \textit{Plethysmogram} is the
curve that represents the changes in the volume of an arm or a foot;
it is obtained by means of a plethysmograph, an instrument in which the arm or
the foot can be enclosed. --- \textit{Radial artery} --- one of the larger arteries of the arm,
whose pulse beat is most easily felt. --- \textbf{P. 278,} \textit{ceteris paribus} --- all else being equal.
--- \textbf{P. 280,} \textit{Carotid} --- the arteries of the neck, through which the blood flows to
the brain. --- \textbf{P. 286,} \textit{Cardiograph} --- an instrument for recording the heartbeat. % print: „P. 286“ -- Kardiograf(ens) and Tibialis(pulsen) are on p. 281 (the next entry, P. 286, is right)
--- \textit{Tibial} --- name for the arteries of the lower leg (in a wider sense also
of the foot). --- \textbf{P. 286,} --- \textit{admit that the magi have been}
\textit{right here.} It is evident from the context that the admission concerns only the fact,
not the magi's interpretation of it.
